Long time derivation of the Boltzmann equation from hard sphere dynamics
Yu Deng, Zaher Hani, Xiao Ma

Abstract
. We provide a rigorous derivation of Boltzmann’s kinetic equation from the hard sphere system for rarefied gas, which is valid for arbitrarily long times, as long as the solution to the Boltzmann equation exists. This extends Lanford’s landmark theorem [ 40 ] , which justifies this derivation for a sufficiently short time. In a companion paper [ 24 ] , we connect this derivation to existing literature on hydrodynamic limits. This completes the resolution of Hilbert’s Sixth Problem pertaining to the derivation of fluid equations from Newton’s laws, in the case of a rarefied, hard sphere gas. The general strategy follows the paradigm introduced by the first two authors for the long-time derivation of the wave kinetic equation in wave turbulence theory. This is based on propagating a long-time cumulant ansatz, which keeps memory of the full collision history of the relevant particles, by an important partial time expansion. The heart of the matter is proving the smallness of these cumulants in L 1 L^{1} , which can be reduced to combinatorial properties for the associated diagrams which we call molecules . These properties are then proved by devising an elaborate cutting algorithm , which is a major novelty of this work.



1.
Introduction

Dating back to the year 1872, Boltzmann’s kinetic theory is one of the most profound and revolutionary scientific ideas ever introduced. At the heart of this theory lies Boltzmann’s kinetic equation which gives an effective
macroscopic
description of the statistics of a large system of
microscopic
interacting particles. A significant feature of this theory, which caused some controversy at the time of its inception, is the fact that the macroscopic description is given by a
time-irreversible
equation (i.e. with an entropy functional that increases forward in time), which is derived from a microscopic system satisfying the
time-reversible
Newton’s laws of motion. This is known as the emergence of the
arrow of time
.

Unsurprisingly, this profound physical theory prompted the deep mathematical question, namely to rigorously justify the derivation of Boltzmann’s equation starting from Newton’s laws. This question was asked by Hilbert, in his address at the International Congress of Mathematicians in 1900, as the
sixth problem
1
1

1

This was one of the two questions proposed in Hilbert’s follow-up article
[
33
]
expanding on his ICM address. The other question was to establish the mathematical foundation of probability theory, which was done in the 1930–40s.
in his celebrated list of outstanding mathematical problems for the twentieth century. More precisely, he proposed the question of giving a mathematically rigorous derivation for the macroscopic equations of fluids, such as the Euler and Navier-Stokes equations, from the Newtonian laws governing microscopic particle interactions, with the derivation of Boltzmann’s kinetic equation as a crucial intermediate step. This problem requires justifying two limits: (i) the
kinetic limit
where we pass from the (microscopic) Newtonian dynamics for an
N
-particle system to Boltzmann’s kinetic equation for the one-particle density function in the limit
N\to\infty
, and (ii) the
hydrodynamic limit
where we pass from Boltzmann’s kinetic equation to the (macroscopic) equations of fluid motion in the limit where the collision rate goes to infinity.

Despite being the subject of extensive study in the last century, this problem remained open for more than 120 years after it was first announced. While the second hydrodynamic limit is much better understood (see the discussions in Section
1.4.1
below), the main difficulty in the resolution of Hilbert’s sixth problem lies in justifying the first kinetic limit, which is the subject of this manuscript. In this direction, the state of art can be briefly summarized as follows, see Section
1.2
for a more extensive review. A landmark result of Lanford
[
40
]
in 1975 provides the rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation for
sufficiently short time
. This result has since been completed, improved, and further developed by different groups of authors (see for example Illner-Pulvirenti
[
34
]
, Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Texier
[
27
]
and more recent works
[
49
,
8
,
9
,
11
,
7
]
concerning fluctuations). However, all these works are still restricted to perturbative regimes, either under short-time or near-vacuum conditions or at the exact Maxwellian equilibrium.

The main result of this paper fills this gap, and thus completes the justification of the first limit needed for the resolution of Hilbert’s sixth problem. More precisely, we provide a rigorous derivation of Boltzmann’s equation from hard sphere dynamics, for arbitrarily long time that covers the full lifespan of the Boltzmann solution. This solution may come from any given initial data, which can also be arbitrarily large. In particular, if a solution to the Boltzmann equation exists globally, then our derivation is valid for all finite times, and times going (slowly) to infinity. In the companion work
[
24
]
, we will connect this to the second hydrodynamic limit, thus giving a full resolution to Hilbert’s sixth problem (see Section
1.4.1
).

1.1.
Setup and the main result

In this section we state our main result, Theorem
1
. We start by describing the microscopic dynamics of the interacting particles, the kinetic formalism involving
s
-particle density functions, and the Boltzmann equation governing the effective dynamics of those functions. The microscopic system here is given by the standard hard sphere particle model, under the Boltzmann-Grad scaling law. We shall discuss some other scenarios in Section
1.4.2
.

1.1.1.
The hard sphere dynamics

Consider
N
hard sphere particles with diameter
\varepsilon
.

Definition 1.1

(The hard sphere dynamics
[
1
]
)
.

We define the dynamics for the
N
-particle hard sphere system with diameter
\varepsilon
in dimension
d\geq 2
. This will be referred to as the
Original
or
O-dynamics
when necessary, to distinguish it with the various modified dynamics in Definitions
3.15
,
3.17
,
4.1
and
4.3
below.

(1)

State vectors
z_{j}
and
\bm{z}_{N}
. We define
z_{j}=(x_{j},v_{j})
and
\bm{z}_{N}=(z_{j})_{1\leq j\leq N}
, where
x_{j}
and
v_{j}
are the center of mass and velocity of particle
j
. These
z_{j}
and
\bm{z}_{N}
are called the
state vector
of the
j
-th particle and the collection of all
N
particles, respectively.

(2)

The domain
\mathcal{D}_{N}
. We define the
non-overlapping domain

\mathcal{D}_{N}
as

(1.1)

\mathcal{D}_{N}:=\big\{\bm{z}_{N}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}:|x_{i}-x_{j}|\geq\varepsilon\,\,(\forall i\neq j)\big\}.

(3)

The hard sphere dynamics
\bm{z}_{N}(t)
. Given initial configuration
\bm{z}_{N}^{0}=(z_{1}^{0},\cdots,z_{N}^{0})\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
, we define the
hard sphere dynamics

\bm{z}_{N}(t)=(z_{j}(t))_{1\leq j\leq N}
as the time evolution of the following system:

(a)

We have
\bm{z}_{N}(0)=\bm{z}_{N}^{0}
.

(b)

Suppose
\bm{z}_{N}(t^{\prime})
is known for
t^{\prime}\in[0,t]
. If
|x_{i}(t)-x_{j}(t)|=\varepsilon
for some
(i,j)
, then we have

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} x_{i}(t^{+})&=x_{i}(t),\quad x_{j}(t^{+})=x_{j}(t),\\
v_{i}(t^{+})&=v_{i}(t)-\big((v_{i}(t)-v_{j}(t))\cdot\omega\big)\omega,\quad\omega:=(x_{i}(t)-x_{j}(t))/\varepsilon\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1};\\
v_{j}(t^{+})&=v_{j}(t)+\big((v_{i}(t)-v_{j}(t))\cdot\omega\big)\omega.\end{aligned}\right.

where
t^{+}
indicates right limit at time
t
, see
Figure
1
. Note that
x_{j}(t)
are always continuous in
t
; in this definition (and similarly for all the modified dynamics below) we also always require
v_{j}(t)
to be left continuous in
t
, so
v_{j}(t)=v_{j}(t^{-})
.

Figure 1.
An illustration of the hard sphere dynamics (Definition
1.1
(
3b
)):
(v_{i}(t),v_{j}(t))=(v_{i}(t^{-}),v_{j}(t^{-}))
and
(v_{i}(t^{+}),v_{j}(t^{+}))
are incoming and outgoing velocities, and
\varepsilon\omega
is the vector connecting the centers of the two colliding particles, which has length
\varepsilon
.

(c)

If for certain
i
there is no
j
such that the scenario in (
3b
) happens, then we have

(1.3)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(x_{i},v_{i})=(v_{i},0).

(4)

The flow map
\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)
and flow operator
\mathcal{S}_{N}(t)
. We define the
flow map

\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)
by

(1.4)

\mathcal{H}_{N}(t):\mathcal{D}_{N}\to\mathcal{D}_{N},\quad\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)(\bm{z}_{N}^{0})=\bm{z}_{N}(t)

where
\bm{z}_{N}(t)
is defined by (
3
). Define also the
flow operator

\mathcal{S}_{N}(t)
, for functions
f=f(\bm{z}_{N})
, by

(1.5)

(\mathcal{S}_{N}(t)f)(\bm{z}_{N})=f\big((\mathcal{H}_{N}(t))^{-1}\bm{z}_{N}\big).

(5)

Collision
. We define a
collision
to be an instance that (
1.2
) in (
3b
) occurs. Note that these collisions exactly correspond to the discontinuities of
v_{j}(t)
.

We list some basic properties of the hard sphere dynamics, which hold up to Lebesgue zero sets:

Proposition 1.2
.

There exists a closed subset
Z\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{N}
with Lebesgue measure
|Z|=0
, such that the time evolution defined by the hard sphere system (Definition
1.1
(
3
)) exists and is unique on
\mathcal{D}_{N}\backslash Z
, and satisfies

(1)

No two collisions happen at the same time
t
;

(2)

The total number of collisions has an absolute upper bound that depends only on
d
and
N
;

(3)

The flow maps
\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)
defined in Definition
1.1
(
4
) are measure preserving diffeomorphisms from
\mathcal{D}_{N}\backslash Z
to itself, and satisfy the flow or semi-group property
\mathcal{H}_{N}(t+s)=\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)\mathcal{H}_{N}(s)
for
t,s\geq 0
.

Proof.

This is proved in Alexander
[
1
]
.
∎

From now on we will always ignore the Lebesgue zero set
Z
in Proposition
1.2
(as they do not contribute to Lebesgue integrals), and will not distinguish the set
\mathcal{D}_{N}
from
\mathcal{D}_{N}\backslash Z
. We will do the same for all the modified dynamics below (Definitions
3.15
,
3.17
,
4.1
and
4.3
).

1.1.2.
The grand canonical ensemble

In this paper, we study the hard sphere system with a random number of particles which have random initial states, whose law is given by the grand canonical ensemble.

The grand canonical ensemble is defined by a density function
W_{0}:\cup_{N}\mathcal{D}_{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
(or equivalently a sequence of density functions
W_{0,N}:\mathcal{D}_{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
) that determines the law of the random initial data for the hard sphere system. We state the definition as follows:

Definition 1.3

(The grand canonical ensemble)
.

Fix
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
and a nonnegative function
f_{0}=f_{0}(z)
with
\int f_{0}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z=1
. We define the
grand canonical ensemble
as follows.

(1)

The grand canonical domain
\mathcal{D}
. We define
\mathcal{D}:=\bigcup_{N}\mathcal{D}_{N}
to be the
grand canonical domain
, so
\bm{z}\in\mathcal{D}
always represents
\bm{z}=\bm{z}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
for some
N
. We also define the hard sphere dynamics
\bm{z}(t)
on
\mathcal{D}
, and the flow map
\mathcal{H}(t)
on
\mathcal{D}
, by specifying them to be the
\bm{z}_{N}(t)
in Definition
1.1
(
3
), and the
\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)
in Definition
1.1
(
4
), for
\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}_{N}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
.

(2)

Random data and initial density
. Assume
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}
is a random variable, whose law is given by the
initial density functions

(W_{0,N})
, in the sense that

(1.6)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}_{N}^{0}\in A\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{N}\big)=\frac{1}{N!}\int_{A}W_{0,N}(\bm{z}_{N})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{N}

for any
N
and any
A\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{N}
, where
(W_{0,N})
is given by

(1.7)

\frac{1}{N!}W_{0,N}(\bm{z}_{N}):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\frac{\varepsilon^{-(d-1)N}}{N!}\prod_{j=1}^{N}f_{0}(z_{j})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}}(\bm{z}_{N}).

Here,
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
is the indicator function of the set
\mathcal{D}_{N}
, and the partition function
\mathcal{Z}
is defined as follows

(1.8)

\mathcal{Z}:=1+\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{\varepsilon^{-(d-1)N}}{N!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}f_{0}(z_{j})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}}(\bm{z}_{N})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{N}.

(3)

Evolution of random data
. Let
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}
be the random variable defined in (
2
) above, and let
\bm{z}(t)=\mathcal{H}(t)\bm{z}^{0}
be the evolution of initial data
\bm{z}^{0}
by hard sphere dynamics, defined as in Definition
1.1
(
3
)–(
4
), then
\bm{z}(t)
is also a
\mathcal{D}
-valued random variable. We define the
density functions

W_{N}(t,\bm{z}_{N})
for the law of the random variable
\bm{z}(t)
by the relation

(1.9)

\mathbb{P}\big(\bm{z}(t)=\bm{z}_{N}(t)\in A\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{N}\big)=\frac{1}{N!}\int_{A}W_{N}(t,\bm{z}_{N})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{N}.

Then it is easy to see (using the volume preserving property in Proposition
1.2
) that

(1.10)

W_{N}(t,\bm{z}_{N}):=(\mathcal{S}_{N}(t)W_{0,N})(\bm{z}_{N}).

(4)

The correlation function
f_{s}(t)
. Given
s\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
s
-particle (rescaled)
correlation function

f_{s}(t,\bm{z}_{s})
by the following formula

(1.11)

f_{s}(t,\bm{z}_{s}):=\varepsilon^{(d-1)s}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dn}}W_{s+n}(t,\bm{z}_{s+n})\,\mathrm{d}z_{s+1}\cdots\mathrm{d}z_{s+n},

where we abbreviate
\bm{z}_{s}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{s})
and
\bm{z}_{s+n}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{s+n})
similar to
\bm{z}_{N}
.

(5)

The Boltzmann-Grad scaling law
. The formula (
1.7
), particularly the choice of the power
\varepsilon^{-(d-1)N}
, implies that

(1.12)

\mathbb{E}(N)\cdot\varepsilon^{d-1}\approx 1

up to error
O(\varepsilon)
, where
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}
is the random initial data defined in (
2
) and
N
is the random variable (i.e. number of particles) determined by
\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}_{N}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
. In other words, we are considering the hard sphere dynamics with
\approx\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}
particles (on average). This is referred to as the
Boltzmann-Grad scaling law
, and will be fixed throughout the paper.

1.1.3.
The Boltzmann equation

Starting from works of Maxwell and Boltzmann in 1860–1870s, it is predicted that the one-particle correlation function
f_{1}(t,z)=f_{1}(t,x,v)
should solve the Boltzmann equation in the kinetic limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, which is defined as follows.

Definition 1.4

(The hard sphere Boltzmann equation)
.

We define the Cauchy problem for the
Boltzmann equation
for hard sphere collisions, with initial data
f(0,z)=f_{0}(z)
where
z=(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, as follows:

(1.13)

\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big((v-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big)_{+}\cdot(f^{\prime}f_{1}^{\prime}-ff_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{1};\\
f(0,x,v)&=f_{0}(x,v).\end{aligned}\right.

The right hand side of (
1.13
) is referred to as the
collision operator
, where we denote

f=f(x,v),\quad f_{1}=f(x,v_{1}),\quad f^{\prime}=f(x,v^{\prime}),\quad f_{1}^{\prime}=f(x,v_{1}^{\prime})

and

(1.14)

v^{\prime}=v-\big((v-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big)\omega,\quad v_{1}^{\prime}=v_{1}+\big((v-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big)\omega,

and
\big((v-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big)_{+}
is the positive part of
(v-v_{1})\cdot\omega
.

1.1.4.
The main result

We now state the main result of this paper. First we introduce the following norm for functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
,

(1.15)

\|f(x,v)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sup_{|x-k|\leq 1,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\beta|v|^{2}}|f(x,v)|.

Theorem 1
.

Fix
d\geq 2,\beta>0
, and a nonnegative function
f_{0}=f_{0}(z)\geq 0
, with
\int f_{0}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z=1
. Suppose the solution
f(t,z)
to the Boltzmann equation (
1.13
) exists on the time interval
[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
, such that

(1.16)

\big\|e^{2\beta|v|^{2}}f(t)\big\|_{L_{x,v}^{\infty}}\leq A<\infty,\quad\forall t\in[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}].

Note that
t_{\mathrm{fin}}
can be arbitrarily large. Suppose also that the initial data
f_{0}
satisfies

(1.17)

\|f_{0}\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{2\beta}}+\|\nabla_{x}f_{0}\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{2\beta}}\leq B_{0}<\infty.

Consider the
d
dimensional hard sphere system of diameter
\varepsilon
particles (Definition
1.1
), with random initial data
\bm{z}^{0}
given by the grand canonical ensemble (Definition
1.3
), under the Boltzmann-Grad scaling law (
1.12
). Let
\varepsilon
be small enough depending on
(d,t_{\mathrm{fin}},\beta,A,B_{0})
. Then, uniformly in
t\in[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
and in
s\leq|\log\varepsilon|
, the
s
-particle correlation functions
f_{s}(t)
defined as in (
1.7
)–(
1.11
), satisfy

(1.18)

\bigg\|f_{s}(t,\bm{z}_{s})-\prod_{j=1}^{s}f(t,z_{j})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(\bm{z}_{s})\bigg\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2ds})}\leq\varepsilon^{\theta},

where
\theta>0
is an absolute constant depending only on the dimension
d
.

Remark 1.5
.

We make a few comments on the scope of Theorem
1
.

(1)

Assumptions on the Boltzmann solution
: the Maxwellian decay of
f(t,z)
in
v
is standard for solutions to the Boltzmann equation, and in principle can be replaced by bounds in any space in which the Boltzmann equation is locally well-posed by fixed point arguments. On the other hand, the required integrability in
x
in (
1.15
) for the initial data
f_{0}
is basically at the level of
L_{x}^{1}
, which is the minimal requirement to define and normalize the ensemble in (
1.7
)–(
1.8
).

(2)

The time interval
[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
: if the solution
f(t,z)
to the Boltzmann equation (
1.13
) develops singularity at some finite time
t_{\mathrm{sing}}
, then the
t_{\mathrm{fin}}
in Theorem
1
can be chosen as anything strictly less than
t_{\mathrm{sing}}
. If the solution exists and is uniformly bounded for all time
t>0
, then the
t_{\mathrm{fin}}
in Theorem
1
can be chosen as a function of
\varepsilon
that diverges as
\varepsilon\to 0
; the current proof would lead to
t_{\mathrm{fin}}\sim\log|\log\varepsilon|
(or a power of it). We do not expect this to be optimal, and it is an interesting question whether this can be improved (say to
t_{\mathrm{fin}}\sim|\log\varepsilon|
).

(3)

Parameters and convergence rate
: the restriction
s\leq|\log\varepsilon|
and the convergence rate
\varepsilon^{\theta}
in (
1.18
) are not expected to be optimal, and are chosen primarily for convenience. The method of our proof would cover the case
s\leq\varepsilon^{-\kappa}
for sufficiently small
\kappa>0
depending on
t_{\mathrm{fin}}
; on the other hand, if
s
is as large as
\varepsilon^{-1}
then (
1.18
) might not hold even for initial data, see
[
49
]
.

Next, for
s=1
, the optimal value of
\theta
should be
1-
and can be obtained by refining our proof; as for the non-factorized parts or cumulants for
s\geq 2
, their optimal decay rate is linked to the derivation of the fluctuation equations about the Boltzmann limit, which we will discuss in Section
1.4.3
below. Finally, the value
\mathbb{E}(N)\cdot\varepsilon^{d-1}
(inverse of the Knudsen number) occurring in the Boltzmann-Grad scaling law (
1.12
) can be replaced by any constant
\alpha
independent of
\varepsilon
(by replacing the
\varepsilon^{-(d-1)N}
in (
1.7
)–(
1.8
) with
(\alpha\varepsilon^{-(d-1)})^{N}
), or (a suitable power of)
\log|\log\varepsilon|
as in (
2
).

(4)

The grand canonical ensemble
: we choose to work with the grand canonical ensemble (Definition
1.3
), as opposed to the canonical ensemble where the particle number
N
is fixed, only to simplify some calculations with cumulants. This simplification was observed in several recent works on this subject
[
49
,
8
,
9
,
7
]
. Theorem
1
remains true for the canonical ensemble, but the proof would be much longer due to the extra manipulations needed to reduce the expressions of cumulants.

1.2.
Background and history

We briefly discuss the background and history of the rigorous derivation of Boltzmann’s equation, and the closely related problem of deriving its wave counterpart, i.e. the wave kinetic equation in wave turbulence theory.

1.2.1.
Particle systems and the Boltzmann equation

The study of large particle systems has a long history, starting from the beginning of the theory of statistical physics. The kinetic theory, which lies at the heart of nonequilibrium statistical physics, started with the following genius observation of Boltzmann
[
12
,
13
]
: assuming independence of states between different particles, the full statistics of the system can be described by a single closed effective equation, namely the
kinetic equation
, in the limit of infinite degrees of freedom.

The first instance of what is now known as the Boltzmann equation was written down in the 1870s; since then, the mathematical justification of this limiting process has been a major challenge. The main difficulty comes from justifying the assumption of (asymptotic) independence of different particles, which is known as the
Stosszahlansatz
or
molecular chaos
. The formulation of this problem as known today was first put forward by Grad, and the works of Cercignani and Grad in the 1950s introduced the important idea of the BBGKY hierarchy to this subject. In the mean time, a simplified model was also introduced and studied by Kac (
[
38
]
, see also subsequent and related developments
[
47
,
51
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
). Motivated by these contributions, Lanford
[
40
]
(see also King
[
39
]
) was able to set a first milestone in this subject, by proving the molecular chaos assumption and deriving the Boltzmann equation for sufficiently small time.

Since
[
40
]
, there have been numerous results devoted to the derivation of the Boltzmann equation and related problems. We do not attempt to be exhaustive in recounting the vast literature, but only list some of the most important ones below; see the surveys
[
53
,
6
,
10
]
and references therein for the other contributions.

•

Illner-Pulvirenti
[
34
]
(see also
[
35
]
), which obtained a long-time result but for the near-vacuum case (i.e. the collision rate
\mathbb{E}(N)\cdot\varepsilon^{d-1}\ll 1
in (
1.12
));

•

Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Texier
[
27
]
, which completed and refined the result of
[
40
]
, and obtained explicit convergence rates;

•

Pulvirenti-Simonella
[
49
]
, which obtained explicit decay rates for cumulants associated with the hard sphere system, with the exponent proportional to the number of involved particles, again in the short-time regime;

•

Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
, which studied the related problems of deriving Brownian motion, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, and the Stokes-Fourier system from the hard sphere dynamics, by passing through a linearized Boltzmann equation;

•

Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Simonella
[
8
,
9
,
11
,
7
]
, which investigated the further question of deriving the equation for fluctuations, either for short time, or for longer times but at the exact Maxwellian equilibrium.

Finally, apart from the question of deriving the kinetic equations, there has also been a huge literature concerning the solution theory of these equations (Boltzmann, Landau etc.). In general strong solutions exist in local and perturbative settings, but only weak solutions are known globally (see however
[
30
,
36
]
on recent global existence results in the homogeneous setting). We will not elaborate on this, but refer to the reviews in
[
56
,
57
]
and references therein.

1.2.2.
Wave turbulence and the wave kinetic equation

One of the most important developments in the physics of the twentieth century was the emergence of quantum physics as a theory to describe subatomic particle dynamics. A central idea here is the
particle-wave
duality, which contributed to an exponential rise in the physical and mathematical interest in nonlinear wave systems. This led to the recasting of many classical particle theories in the context of interacting wave systems, including the theory of nonequilibrium statistical physics for waves, known as the wave turbulence or wave kinetic theory.

Same as the particle case, the
wave kinetic theory
aims at describing the statistics of a wave system using a closed equation for the lowest order correlation; usually this is the second moment of the wave field, as opposed to the one-particle density function in the kinetic theory for particles. The role of the Boltzmann equation is then played by the
wave kinetic equation
(WKE). For instance, in dimension
d=3
, when the wave system is modeled by the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation, the corresponding wave kinetic equation takes the form

(1.19)

(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\big((v-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big)_{+}\cdot\big(f^{\prime}f_{1}^{\prime}(f+f_{1})-ff_{1}(f^{\prime}+f_{1}^{\prime})\big)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{1}

(including also the homogeneous variant where the
v\cdot\nabla_{x}
term in (
1.19
) is absent), where we use the same notation as in (
1.14
). The wave kinetic equations, similar to (
1.19
) but for different wave systems, were first introduced in the work of Peierls
[
48
]
in 1929 for anharmonic crystals. This was shortly followed by works of Nordheim
[
37
]
and Uehling and Uhlenbeck
[
55
]
in the context of quantum gases. Since then, the theory has attracted a great deal of attention from mathematicians and physicists, and has developed into a rich and broad subject. In particular, these contributions included the influential works of Hasselmann
[
31
,
32
]
in 1962–1963, which applied the wave kinetic theory to ocean waves and led to the modern methods of ocean surface forecasting, as well as the work of Zakharov
[
58
]
in 1965, which provided a systematic approach to the theory and how it can be used to understand turbulent phenomena for waves. In
[
58
]
, Zakharov also introduced the notion of Kolmogorov-Zakharov spectra, along the same lines as Kolmogorov’s theory of hydrodynamic turbulence. It is for this reason that the theory is also commonly known as wave turbulence theory. We refer the reader to
[
46
]
for a textbook treatment.

Mathematically, the study of the derivation of wave kinetic equations like (
1.19
) started with the work of Spohn
[
54
]
. For linear models, this program was completed in Erdös-Yau
[
26
]
and Erdös-Salmhofer-Yau
[
25
]
. For the full nonlinear problem, an important early milestone was due to Lukkarinen-Spohn
[
41
]
and subsequent partial results were obtained in
[
14
,
19
,
16
,
17
]
. We refer the reader to the introduction of
[
22
]
for a more exhaustive historical recount. The full derivation of (
1.19
) in the short-time regime was obtained by the first two authors
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
, serving as the wave-counterpart of Lanford’s theorem.

Recently, in
[
23
]
, the first two authors have extended the derivation of (
1.19
) to arbitrarily long times that cover the full lifespan of solutions to (
1.19
). We remark that the result of
[
23
]
is parallel to Theorem
1
, not only conceptually but also on a very concrete level. In fact, while the proof in the current paper is self-contained, many of the key ideas in this proof are obtained by adapting the ideas from the proof in
[
23
]
to the particle setting. In Section
2.5
we will make a detailed comparison between the proofs in
[
23
]
and in the current paper, including both similarities and differences.

1.3.
Main ideas of the proof

In this subsection, we present a mostly philosophical description of the proof, its strategy, and its most important ideas. A more elaborate and concrete discussion of these ideas can be found in Section
2
.

To a large extent, the framework of the proof in this paper is the same as that in
[
23
]
. Below we will explain this framework in the current context of particle systems.

1.3.1.
Time layering

The well-known fundamental difficulty in extending Lanford’s theorem to longer times, which is the same difficulty in the long-time derivation of the wave kinetic equation, is the
divergence of time expansions of solutions
. In fact, in both settings, the proof of the short-time result is based on matching two different time expansions: expansion I, given by the Duhamel expansion of the dynamics of the microscopic system (or the BBGKY hierarchy satisfied by the correlation functions
f_{s}
), and expansion II, given by the Duhamel expansion of the kinetic equation. The bulk of the proof
2
2

2

In the wave case, there is an extra level of complication coming from the fact that the series does not converge absolutely, but only conditionally.
goes into identifying the leading terms in expansion I, matching them order-by-order with the terms in expansion II, and proving that all the remaining non-leading terms vanish in the limit. At a basic level, both expansions are bounded like
\sum_{n}C^{n}t^{n}
, which is convergent only for sufficiently small times
|t|<C^{-1}
given by the radius of convergence. Beyond this time both expansions will diverge, and this methods fails in a fundamental way.

To go beyond this threshold and reach an arbitrarily large time
t=t_{\mathrm{fin}}
, a natural idea is to subdivide the long time interval
[0,t]
into
\mathfrak{L}
subintervals (called time layers)
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
with
1\leq\ell\leq\mathfrak{L}
and
\tau
sufficiently small, such that on each subinterval, the solution to the kinetic equation is given by a convergent series expansion with base time
(\ell-1)\tau
. This
time layering
is the first key idea in the proof, which allows to resolve the divergence problem for expansion II mentioned above, regarding solutions to the Boltzmann equation. This leaves us with the task of controlling the microscopic system (expansion I) on
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
using the data at time
(\ell-1)\tau
. An obvious difficulty arises with this approach, as the data at
(\ell-1)\tau
will no longer have exact independence between different particles. Such independence (or equivalently the complete factorization of the correlation functions
f_{s}
) is granted
3
3

3

Strictly speaking, we do not have exact independence due to the
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}}(\bm{z}_{N})
factor in (
1.7
), but the cumulants caused by this factor are easy to deal with (see Proposition
5.18
), and will be ignored here.
at time
0
, and plays a fundamental role in controlling the expansion I of the microscopic system in the short-time proof.

1.3.2.
Cumulants

In order to go from time
(\ell-1)\tau
to
\ell\tau
, we need to propagate some information for the ensemble at time
\ell\tau
that describes departure from independence. This will be given by the
cumulants
. Following
[
49
]
, the cumulants can be defined as the
E_{H}
occurring in the following expansion of the
s
-point density function:

(1.20)

f_{s}(t,\bm{z}_{s})=\prod_{j\in[s]}f^{\mathcal{A}}(t,z_{j})+\underbrace{\sum_{\varnothing\neq H\subseteq[s]}\bigg(\prod_{j\in[s]\setminus H}f^{\mathcal{A}}(t,z_{j})\bigg)\cdot E_{H}(t,\bm{z}_{H})}_{O_{L^{1}}(\varepsilon^{\theta})},

where
[s]=\{1,\ldots,s\}
and
\bm{z}_{H}=(z_{j})_{j\in H}
. Here the first term on the right-hand side represents the fully factorized part of the correlation, with
f^{\mathcal{A}}(t,z)
being a one-particle density function, and the remaining terms involving cumulants
E_{H}
describe the deviation from independence. Similar cumulant expansions have also played a central role in the wave setting
[
23
]
and in a series of recent works on particle systems
[
49
,
7
,
8
,
10
]
.

The physical interpretation of (
1.20
) is as follows: the left-hand side denotes the probability density of finding
s
-particles, say
1,\cdots,s
, with states
z_{1},\cdots,z_{s}
, at time
t
. This depends on the collision history of these particles among themselves and with remaining particles. The first term on the right hand side comes from collision histories where the
s
particles are disconnected, i.e. they do not interact with each other and each of them interacts with a disjoint set of the remaining particles. The second term comes from collision histories where the particles in
H
are connected to each other via collisions.

By (
1.20
), we see that Theorem
1
would follow if we can show that (a)
f^{\mathcal{A}}
converges to the Boltzmann solution, and (b)
\|E_{H}\|_{L^{1}}
converges to zero in the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

1.3.3.
The cumulant ansatz

As discussed in Section
1.3.2
, a natural route of proving Theorem
1
is to approximate
f^{\mathcal{A}}
by the Boltzmann solution (Proposition
6.1
; this is relatively easy, see Section
2.4
for further discussions, and Section
14
for the proof), and prove that

•

For each
1\leq\ell\leq\mathfrak{L}
, we have the cumulant expansion (
1.20
) at
t=\ell\tau
, with some constant
\alpha>0
such that

(1.21)

\|E_{H}(\ell\tau)\|_{L^{1}}\leq\varepsilon^{\alpha|H|},\quad\forall H\neq\varnothing.

This
E_{H}
estimate is stated in Proposition
6.2
. Note that this is a reasonable conclusion to aim for. In fact, for
\ell=0
(
1.21
) is trivial, while for
\ell=1
this follows from Lanford’s theorem and the proof in
[
49
]
. The next natural question is: how to proceed on the intervals
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
, to prove (
1.21
) at time
\ell\tau
.

In
[
49
]
, the authors show that if the cumulants at
(\ell-1)\tau
satisfy the
L^{\infty}
estimate

\|E_{H}((\ell-1)\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{\beta|H|},

then one can recover the
L^{1}
estimate (
1.21
) at time
\ell\tau
. The crucial point here is that one has to start with the stronger
L^{\infty}
bound, and can only recover the weaker
L^{1}
bound, therefore this estimate cannot be iterated.

There is something deep behind this phenomenon: for
any
Banach space
X
, it is
not possible
to obtain smallness of
\|E_{H}(\ell\tau)\|_{X}
(in the form of (
1.21
)) using only smallness of the same norm
\|E_{H}((\ell-1)\tau)\|_{X}
, precisely because of the
time irreversibility
of Boltzmann’s equation.

In fact, suppose the contrary, that such an inductive proof of the
\|\cdot\|_{X}
bound was possible. Take
\ell=2
. Then, by the
time reversibility
of the Newtonian dynamics (Definition
1.1
), we could either go forward from time
\tau
to time
2\tau
, or backward to time
0
, which just differ by flipping the signs of the velocities
v_{j}
at time
\tau
. Since
\|\cdot\|_{X}
is invariant under such sign flipping (which is true for any natural Banach norm such as
L^{p}
norms), we would conclude from (
1.20
) (assuming the approximation
f^{\mathcal{A}}(t,z)\approx f(t,z)
) that

f_{s}(2\tau,z_{1},\cdots,z_{s})=f(2\tau,z_{1})\cdots f(2\tau,z_{s})+O_{X}(\varepsilon^{\upsilon})

as well as

f_{s}(0,z_{1},\cdots,z_{s})=f(2\tau,z_{1})\cdots f(2\tau,z_{s})+O_{X}(\varepsilon^{\upsilon}).

This is impossible, as the leading term in
f_{s}(0,\cdots)
is given by tensor product of
f(0,\cdot)
and not
f(2\tau,\cdot)
.

By the above discussion, we know that (
1.21
) cannot be proved by direct induction in
\ell
. In fact, in order to prove (
1.21
), we need to propagate
structural information
(which goes beyond any Banach norms) for the cumulants
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
by induction in
\ell
. Such structural information will be provided by a
partial time expansion
argument, a second key ingredient of the proof, which we discuss below.

Start from a cumulant
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
, and assume
f^{\mathcal{A}}
has already been approximated by the Boltzmann solution. By performing a cluster expansion (see Section
5
or
[
7
]
) on
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
, we can express this cumulant as a sum of Duhamel time integrals involving
f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)
and
E_{H}((\ell-1)\tau)
. Now, the crucial point in the partial time expansion is that, we
refrain from
expanding
f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)
any further in time (and use instead its proximity to the Boltzmann solution), and only expand the cumulant terms
E_{H}((\ell-1)\tau)
in terms of
f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-2)\tau)
and
E_{H}((\ell-2)\tau)
, and so on. This is very important in our proof as it allows us to avoid the
t^{n}
divergence (discussed in Section
1.3.1
); indeed, such divergence only comes from the aggregation if we were to expand the leading terms
f^{\mathcal{A}}
all the way to time
t=0
, which we are avoiding in this partial time expansion. On the other hand, the cumulants come with power gains (of size
\varepsilon^{\alpha|H|}
, see (
1.21
)), which offsets the
t^{n}
divergence even if they are expanded all the way to time
t=0
.

This structural information constructed above will be further discussed in Section
1.3.4
below. As far as we know, such partial expansion construction has not appeared in previous literature, and is crucial in obtaining a time irreversible equation from time reversible dynamics.

1.3.4.
Representing cumulants by molecules

Recall the physical interpretation of the cumulants in Section
1.3.2
and the partial time expansion in Section
1.3.3
. With these, we know that the cumulant
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
can be written as a sum of contributions corresponding to probability densities of possible collision histories on
[0,\ell\tau]
involving particles in
H
. These collision histories are constructed by the partial time expansions, and they satisfy that the particles in
H
are connected to each other via collisions (cf. Section
1.3.2
).

If we disregard the precise positions, velocities and collision times in the particle trajectories, and focus only on their combinatorial structure, then each collision history will
topologically reduce
to an abstract diagram, called a
molecule
. Such diagrams encode the possible patterns of collisions among the particles in
H
, and disregard the precise geometric information. This process of abstraction is illustrated in
Figure
5
(Section
3.1
), where particle trajectories are depicted as sets of edges sharing the same color, and collisions are represented by nodes, called
atoms
. In addition, the molecule also retains the ordering between different collisions of the same particle, and the layer of each collision (represented by horizontal lines in
Figure
5
).

Now, with the molecular representation of collision histories, we can formally state the structural information for the cumulant
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
in Section
1.3.3
, as follows:

(1.22)

|E_{H}(\ell\tau)|\leq\sum_{\mathbb{M}}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|,

where the sum is taken over molecules
\mathbb{M}
that are constructed from the partial expansion process in Section
1.3.3
(see Definition
3.20
and Proposition
3.25
), and
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
is an explicit expression representing the normalized probability density that the collision history described by
\mathbb{M}
happens (see Definition
3.23
).

The formulas (
1.20
) and (
1.22
) will be stated in Proposition
3.25
and proved in Section
5
. For reasons that will be discussed in Section
1.3.5
below, in this proof we also need a truncation on the dynamics, which is defined in Section
4
. For a more detailed discussion of all the above ingredients, see Section
2.1
.

1.3.5.
Reduction to molecule estimates and truncation of the dynamics

To obtain the bound on
\|E_{H}\|_{L^{1}}
in (
1.21
) using (
1.22
), one has to obtain effective estimates for the
L^{1}
norm of the normalized probability densities
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
. Here, the central quantity in understanding such estimates is the number of
recollisions

\rho
in the molecule
\mathbb{M}
. This is defined as the
circuit rank
of
\mathbb{M}
, viewed as an undirected graph, which is the number of independent cycles in
\mathbb{M}
. Heuristically, one expects that as the recollision number
\rho
increases, the number of molecules
\mathbb{M}
in the sum (
1.22
) also increases. This divergence has to be balanced by gains in the estimates on
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
for molecules with larger
\rho
. This heuristic is quantified by establishing the following two estimates. Given
\rho\geq 1
, we have:

•

Bounding the number of terms in (
1.22
).
The number of molecules
\mathbb{M}
with
\rho
recollisions satisfies the bound

(1.23)

\#\mathbb{M}\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C\rho};

•

Bounding the contribution of each term.
The contribution of each molecule
\mathbb{M}
is bounded by

(1.24)

\big\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|\big\|_{L^{1}}\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot\varepsilon^{\upsilon\rho}\,(\upsilon>0).

Note how the above two bounds exhibit the effect of recollisions: each more recollision leads to
|\log\varepsilon|^{C}
loss in the number of molecules in (
1.23
), which is offset by the
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
gain in the renormalized probabilities in (
1.24
). The cumulant estimate (
1.21
) then follows directly from (
1.22
)–(
1.24
), provided that
\varepsilon\ll\tau\ll 1
.

From our construction, the molecule
\mathbb{M}
consists of
\ell
layers, and can be seen as a concatenation of
\ell
sub-molecules
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\,(\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell)
, where
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
contains the atoms in layer
\ell^{\prime}
. To prove (
1.23
) and (
1.24
), we need to impose an upper bound on the number of atoms and recollisions in each of the sub-molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. To enforce the above restrictions, we introduce the notion of
truncated dynamics
(see Definition
4.3
) within each time layer. More precisely, in the truncated dynamics, at each time where a collision would occur, we check whether or not this collision would cause a violation to the following two conditions:

(1)

The number of particles in each cluster is at most
\Lambda:=|\log\varepsilon|^{O(1)}
, and

(2)

The number of recollisions (i.e. independent cycles) in each cluster is at most
\Gamma=O(1)
,

where
clusters
are the subsets of particles that are connected by collisions. If this collision would cause a violation, then we “turn off” this collision and allow the particles to cross each other; otherwise we allow this collision just as in the original hard sphere dynamics.

Of course, this truncation also leads to an error term
f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}
which occurs when the original dynamics (or in fact the extended version of it, see Definition
4.1
) does not coincide with the truncated dynamics. We will control this error term in Proposition
6.3
. In fact, it can be expressed in terms of
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
, and bounded using similar arguments, in a much similar way as
E_{H}
, see Section
2.4
and Section
15
.

Given (
1
) and (
2
), it follows that the number of atoms in any molecule
\mathbb{M}
can be bounded by
|\log\varepsilon|^{O(1)}
. The estimate (
1.23
) then follows from the well-known combinatorial fact that the number of graphs whose vertices have maximum degree 4, with at most
m
vertices, and
\rho
independent cycles is bounded by
C^{m}\cdot m^{\rho}
for some constant
C>0
. Applying this to molecules yields:

\#\mathbb{M}\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\mathbb{M}|^{\rho}\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C\rho},

as claimed in (
1.23
) (Proposition
7.2
; see Section
2.2
for more details). As a result, we are left with proving the estimate (
1.24
), which is the most delicate part of the proof.

1.3.6.
The cutting algorithm

In the proof, we will reduce the estimate of
\big\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|\big\|_{L^{1}}
to a purely combinatorial problem for the molecule
\mathbb{M}
. To this end, we first rewrite it as
\big\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|\big\|_{L^{1}}=\varepsilon^{(d-1)|H|}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
, where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
is an operator involving integration over all intermediate positions and velocities (associated with the edges of
\mathbb{M}
) and all collision times (associated with the atoms of
\mathbb{M}
), and
Q
is a specific input function. These integrals involve Dirac
\bm{\delta}
functions, which encode the relations between incoming and outgoing velocities at each collision. The reduction to this form is contained in Section
7
, see Proposition
7.5
.

Then, to get optimal bounds for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
, we need to find the correct order of variables in which to integrate. For this, we introduce the key notion of
cutting
, see Definition
8.3
, which provides a systematic way of deciding the order of integration in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
(i.e. applying the Fubini theorem). More precisely, each cutting divides the molecule
\mathbb{M}
into two separate molecules
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
; by first integrating in those variables associated with the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{2}
(with those variables associated with the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{1}
regarded as fixed) and then integrating in those variables associated with the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{1}
, we get that

(1.25)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}.

Now, using (
1.25
), our strategy is to devise a
cutting algorithm
which cuts
\mathbb{M}
into a number of “small” molecules
\mathbb{M}_{j}
with one or two atoms, called
elementary molecules
(see Definition
8.11
). The operators
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
can be directly calculated and estimated (say in the
L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}
norm), so that applying (
1.25
) then yields an upper bound for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
. The two most important types of elementary molecules are the
\{3\}
molecules which represent collisions (i.e. a particle
{\bm{p}}
colliding with a given particle
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
), and the
\{33\}
molecules which represent collisions followed by recollisions (i.e. a particle
{\bm{p}}
colliding with a given particle
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, and then colliding again with another given particle
{\bm{p}}^{\prime\prime}
). It is known that
\{3\}
molecules are
normal
in the sense that integrating it does not gain any power, while
\{33\}
molecules are
good
in the sense that integrating it gains a power
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
for some constant
\upsilon>0
(there are also
\{4\}
-molecules which are usually viewed as
bad
due to “wasting” of dimensions of integration). The discussion of elementary molecules, and associated weight and volume bounds, are proved in Section
9.1
, see Definition
9.4
.

With the above discussions, it is now clear that the main ingredient in the proof of (
1.24
) is to construct a cutting algorithm (see Proposition
9.7
) that produces sufficiently many good molecules. This is a major novelty and main technical ingredient, and is discussed in more details in Section
2.3
. Roughly speaking, the cutting algorithm consists of two big steps: (A) reducing the multi-layer case to the two-layer case by performing a layer refining and a layer selection process; (B) treating the two-layer case by exhibiting a dichotomy between two main scenarios and applying several basic algorithms.

In Section
11
, we start with a simplified case, illustrating the main ideas of the algorithm; then we extend these ideas to full generality, and present the proofs in steps (A) and (B) in Sections
12
and
13
respectively.

1.4.
Future horizons

Here we list some future directions, now within reach, with the proof of Theorem
1
.

1.4.1.
The hydrodynamic limit

After deriving the Boltzmann equation from particle dynamics in the kinetic limit, we would need to match it with the hydrodynamic limit and derive the fluid equations (Euler and Navier-Stokes). Formally, this requires considering densities that are
local Maxwellians
, i.e. those functions that are Maxwellian in
v
but with mass, center and variance depending on
(t,x)
:

(1.26)

f(t,x,v)=\frac{\rho(t,x)}{(2\pi T(t,x))^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{|v-u(t,x)|^{2}}{2T(t,x)}\bigg).

By considering perturbation expansions (i.e. Hilbert and Chapman-Enskog expansions) around (
1.26
), we can derive the compressible Euler equation for
(\rho,u,T)
and the incompressible Euler and Navier-Stokes equations for subsequent terms, in a suitable scaling limit.

Compared to the derivation of the Boltzmann equation, the derivation of fluid equations is much better understood, see for example
[
18
,
29
,
52
,
28
]
. In
[
24
]
, by combining these results with an analog of Theorem
1
on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we can finally execute both steps of Hilbert’s program in tandem, and derive the fluid equations from Newtonian particle particle systems in the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

1.4.2.
The short-range potential case

With the methods in the current paper, a natural problem that should be within reach, is the short range potential case studied in
[
27
]
, namely

(1.27)

\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{i}}{\mathrm{d}t}=v_{i},\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}v_{i}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{j\neq i}\nabla\Phi\bigg(\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{\varepsilon}\bigg),

where
\Phi
is a suitable potential function. We believe that Theorem
1
should remain true in this case, but the proof would need to involve (among other things) extra technical ingredients to deal with the possibility of simultaneous interactions between more than two particles via (
1.27
).

1.4.3.
Fluctuations and large deviations

The rigorous study of this direction was initiated in the recent works of Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Simonella
[
8
,
9
,
11
]
. Here one considers the random variable (i.e. observable)
\zeta:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}F(z_{j}(t))
, where
F=F(z)
is a fixed function and
\bm{z}_{N}(t)=(z_{j}(t))_{1\leq j\leq N}
is the solution to the hard sphere system in Definition
1.1
(
3
). Then up to errors that vanish when
\varepsilon\to 0
, we have

\zeta\approx\mathbb{E}(\zeta)\approx\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(z)f(t,z)\,\mathrm{d}z,

which can be viewed as a “law of large numbers” for the random variables
F(z_{j}(t))
. The fluctuation, which is given by

(1.28)

\eta=\sqrt{N}\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}F(z_{j}(t))-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(z)f(t,z)\,\mathrm{d}z\bigg),

should then be described by the “central limit theorem”. Such results have been established in the short-time regime in
[
8
]
, and for longer times at the exact global Maxwellian equilibrium (i.e. (
1.26
) with
\rho=T=1
and
u=0
) in
[
9
,
11
]
.

With the proof of Theorem
1
, we expect that these results in
[
8
,
9
,
11
]
can be extended to the long-time and off-equilibrium setting, where the initial independence assumption in the short time setting is substituted by the precise description of
f_{s}
and
E_{H}
at any time
\ell\tau
given by (
1.20
) and (
1.22
).

1.5.
Parameters and notations

Before starting the proof, we first note that, by the assumptions (
1.16
) and (
1.17
) in Theorem
1
, and using Proposition
A.1
, it follows that

(1.29)

\|f(t)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}}+\|\nabla_{x}f(t)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}}\leq B<\infty,\quad\forall t\in[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}],

where
B
is a constant depending only on
(d,t_{\mathrm{fin}},\beta,A,B_{0})
. In the proof below we will always use (
1.29
) instead of (
1.16
) and (
1.17
). In this section we fix some parameters and define some notations, which will be used throughout the proof.

Definition 1.6

(Parameters and notations)
.

We define the following parameters and notations.

(1)

Time layering
. Recall that
(d,t_{\mathrm{fin}},\beta,A,B_{0})
has been fixed as in Theorem
1
, and
B
has been fixed as in (
1.29
) depending only on these quantities. Throughout this paper we will assume
t=t_{\mathrm{fin}}
(otherwise replace
t_{\mathrm{fin}}
by
t
). Fix a large positive integer
\mathfrak{L}
depending on
(d,t_{\mathrm{fin}},\beta,A,B)
, and
subdivide the time interval

[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
as follows:

(1.30)

\tau:=\frac{t_{\mathrm{fin}}}{\mathfrak{L}};\qquad[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau].

(2)

Convention for constants
. Throughout the proof, we will distinguish
two types of (large) constants
: those depending only on
(d,t_{\mathrm{fin}},\beta,A,B)
and not on
\mathfrak{L}
will be denoted by
C
, while those depending on
\mathfrak{L}
will be denoted by
C^{*}
. The notions of
\lesssim
and
\lesssim_{*}
etc. are defined accordingly. We assume
\varepsilon
is small enough depending on
C^{*}
.

We also fix an
explicit sequence of large constants

(C_{1}^{*},C_{2}^{*},\cdots,C_{15}^{*})
such that
4
4

4

In practice, only in the layer selection part (cf. Definition
12.12
) do we need growths like
C_{8}^{*}\gg(C_{7}^{*})^{10\mathfrak{L}}
and
C_{6}^{*}\gg(C_{5}^{*})^{10\Gamma\mathfrak{L}}
; for other
j
we can choose (say)
C_{j+1}^{*}=(C_{j}^{*})^{100}
.

(1.31)

C_{j+1}^{*}\gg(\textrm{a suitable function of }C_{j}^{*})

all depending on
\mathfrak{L}
. We understand that (i) for any large constant
C
under the above convention, we always assume
\mathfrak{L}^{C}\ll C_{j}^{*}
for each
j
, and (ii) any large constant which we denote by
C^{*}
will always be
\gg C_{j}^{*}
for each
j
.

(3)

Other parameters
.
We fix

\Gamma=\Gamma(d)
sufficiently large but depending only on
d
, and fix a
sequence of parameters

(A_{\ell},\Lambda_{\ell})\,(0\leq\ell\leq\mathfrak{L})
by

(1.32)

A_{\mathfrak{L}}=|\log\varepsilon|;\quad\Lambda_{\ell}=A_{\ell}^{10d},\quad A_{\ell-1}=\Lambda_{\ell}^{10d}.

They will be used in the truncation of correlation functions (Section
4.3
). It is obvious that

(1.33)

A_{\ell},\Lambda_{\ell}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}},\quad\Lambda_{\ell-1}\gg A_{\ell-1}\gg\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}A_{\ell}.

We also fix
two sequences
(which will be used in the analysis of
f^{\mathcal{A}}
in Section
14
)

(1.34)

\beta_{\ell}:=\bigg(\frac{9}{10}+\frac{\mathfrak{L}-\ell}{10\mathfrak{L}}\bigg)\frac{\beta}{2};\quad\theta_{0}:=3^{-d-8},\quad\theta_{\ell}:=\bigg(\frac{9}{10}+\frac{\mathfrak{L}-\ell}{10\mathfrak{L}}\bigg)\theta_{0}

for
1\leq\ell\leq\mathfrak{L}
, and set
\theta=\theta_{0}/2
in Theorem
1
. For the proof in Sections
9
–
15
,we also fix
\upsilon:=3^{-d-1}
.

(4)

Set, vector and integral notations
. For any positive integers
a
and
b
, define

[a:b]=\{a,a+1,\cdots,b\},\quad[a]:=[1:a].

For any set
A
, as already done in Section
1.1
, we shall adopt the
vector notation

\bm{z}_{A}:=(z_{j})_{j\in A}
, and
\bm{z}_{a:b}:=\bm{z}_{[a:b]}
and
\bm{z}_{a}:=\bm{z}_{[a]}
, and
\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{A}=\prod_{j\in A}\mathrm{d}z_{j}
. The same notion applies for other variables, such as
\bm{t}_{A}=(t_{j})_{j\in A}
etc. Define also the
tensor product
by
f^{\otimes A}(\bm{z}_{A})=\prod_{j\in A}f(z_{j})
. The notion
\bm{z}
without any subscripts will be used to denote some
\bm{z}_{N}
where
N
depends on the context.

(5)

Indicator functions and linear operators
. In the proof we will need to use a lot of
indicator functions
. These functions will be denoted by
\mathbbm{1}_{(\cdots)}
, which are defined by certain properties represented by
(\cdots)
depending on the context, and take values in
\{0,1\}
. Define similarly the functions
\chi_{(\cdots)}
, except that those may take values in
\{-1,0,1\}
. Moreover, in Definition
3.22
(and several other places related to initial cumulants) we will define the notion
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
, which equals a positive power of
\varepsilon
at points where the usual indicator function would be
0
. In practice they have the same behavior as usual indicator functions, and serve to simplify notations by unifying certain cases.

We will view any function
F
(including the above indicator functions) as a
linear operator
defined by multiplication. For any linear operators
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
we define
\mathcal{S}_{1}\circ\mathcal{S}_{2}
as their composition. For any linear operator
\mathcal{S}
and function
F
, the notion
\mathcal{S}\circ F
means the composition of
\mathcal{S}
with the multiplication operator defined by
F
. This is different from the result of applying
\mathcal{S}
to the function
F
, which we denote by
\mathcal{S}(F)
.

(6)

Summation over equivalence classes
. Let
\sim
be an equivalence class on a set
X
, define
X/\sim
be the set of equivalence classes, with the equivalent class containing
x\in X
denoted by
[x]
. Let
\mathrm{Fun}
be a function on
X
; if it is constant on each equivalence class, then the sum

\sum_{[x]\in X/\sim}\mathrm{Fun}(x)

is well-defined by taking
x
as an arbitrary representative from each
[x]
. Otherwise, we define

\sum_{[x]\in X/\sim}\mathrm{Fun}(x):=\sum_{[x]\in X/\sim}\frac{1}{|[x]|}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[x]}\mathrm{Fun}(x^{\prime}),

where
|[x]|
is the cardinality of
[x]
. We remark that the case when
\mathrm{Fun}
is not constant on each equivalence class, occurs only in Proposition
5.12
and its proof.

(7)

Miscellaneous
. We use the notion
|A|
or
\#A
to denote the cardinality of finite sets
A
, and use
|A|
to denote the Lebesgue measure of Borel sets
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
for some
p
. Define
X_{+}=\max(X,0)
and
X_{-}=-\min(X,0)
. In addition to the standard
O(\cdot)
notation, we also use
O_{1}(A)
to denote quantities
X
that satisfy the exact inequality
|X|\leq A
.

2.
A detailed overview of the proof

In this section, we give a more detailed sketch of the proof of Theorem
1
. As explained in Section
1.3
, we will divide
[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
into smaller intervals
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
where
\tau\ll 1
, see (
1.30
). Recall also the notion
\bm{z}_{A}:=(z_{j})_{j\in A}
and
f^{\otimes A}
in Definition
1.6
(
4
).

2.1.
The cumulant expansion formula

As discussed in Section
1.3
, the first step of the proof is to develop the cumulant expansion formulas (
1.20
) and (
1.22
). This is inspired by the previous works
[
49
,
7
]
, but with the key new idea of multi-layer, partial time expansion (see Section
1.3.3
) and several extra twists.

2.1.1.
Statement of the formula

We start by stating the precise version of (
1.20
) and (
1.22
) as follows:

(2.1)

\begin{gathered}f(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})=\sum_{H\subseteq[s]}\left[f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau,\cdot)\right]^{\otimes([s]\setminus H)}\cdot E_{H}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H})+\mathrm{Err}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s}),\\
|E_{H}(\ell\tau)|\leq\sum_{\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F},\,r(\mathbb{M})=H}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|.\end{gathered}

Here
\mathrm{Err}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})
, omitted in (
1.20
), denotes an extremely small error term, which we will neglect in the remainder of the analysis. The sets
\mathcal{F}
,
r(\mathbb{M})
and the quantity
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
will be defined and discussed below.

As explained in Section
1.3.4
, a
molecule

\mathbb{M}
(Definition
3.1
) is a diagram, illustrated in
Figure
3
(Section
3.1
), that encodes the combinatorial structure of the collision history. It is obtained by disregarding the precise spatial and temporal details of particle trajectories, retaining only the topological pattern of interactions, in a process called
topological reduction
(Definition
3.8
). It consists of several key components listed below, which are illustrated in
Figure
3
:

•

C/O atoms
, which represent individual collisions/overlaps, are depicted as nodes like
\circ
/
\bullet
.

•

Edges
, which represent segments of free transport between collisions along a particle trajectory, are depicted as edges between nodes.

•

Particle lines
, which represent the full trajectory of each particle, are visualized in
Figure
3
as chains of edges sharing the same color. They will be identified with particles in Sections
3
–
5
. Each particle line has two
ends
, corresponding to the initial and final trajectories of the particle.

•

Layers
are the subsets
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbb{M}
in
Figure
3
between the horizontal lines representing times
\ell^{\prime}\tau
and
(\ell^{\prime}+1)\tau
. Dividing
\mathbb{M}
into layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
corresponds to assigning a layer
\ell^{\prime}
to each atom
\mathfrak{n}
which corresponds to a collision that happens in the time interval
[\ell^{\prime}\tau,(\ell^{\prime}+1)\tau]
.

•

Each particle line
{\bm{p}}
has bottom end
e
, and a corresponding
start layer

\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]=\ell_{1}[e]
; this indicates the time
(\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau
at which the particle starts interacting with the system. Similarly, it also has a top end
e^{\prime}
and a
finish
layer
\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]=\ell_{2}[e^{\prime}]
, indicating the time
\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]\cdot\tau
at which the particle stops interacting with the system.

•

Root particle lines
are the particle lines in
H
(occurring in the cumulant
E_{H}
), i.e. those penetrating the top horizontal line
\ell\tau
in
Figure
3
.

We now explain the definitions of
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
and
\mathcal{F}
in (
2.1
). The expression
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
is given by

(2.2)

|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})})\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}})\bigg(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\prod_{{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M})}\left|f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau)\right|\bigg)\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})},

where the notations are explained as follows:

•

r(\mathbb{M})
,
p(\mathbb{M})
, and
(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})
denote the sets of root particle lines, all particle lines, and non-root particle lines respectively, and
|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}\coloneqq|(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})|
. Each particle in
p(\mathbb{M})
is connected (via collisions and overlaps) to some root particle in
r(\mathbb{M})
. Moreover,
\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]
is the start layer of
{\bm{p}}
as above, i.e. the first time that
{\bm{p}}
is involved in the system.

•

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}
denotes the dynamics involving only the particles in
p(\mathbb{M})
. The indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
restricts the integration to trajectories that topologically reduce to the molecule
\mathbb{M}
. The term
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
enforces additional constraints that certain particles are initially close to each other, which comes from initial cumulants.

•

The expression in (
2.2
) expresses
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
as the renormalized probability density contributed by the collision history encoded by
\mathbb{M}
(with additional information contained in
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}
). Here
\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})}
represent the final states of non-root particles, and integration in them leaves the result being a density function of only the root variables
\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})}
.

The input factors
f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau)
come from the start time
(\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau
where particle
{\bm{p}}
enters the system; their variables represent the initial states of particles, which are related to the variables
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}
by the transformation
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}
(strictly speaking, there should be a linear transport in the variables of
f^{\mathcal{A}}
which transforms between time
0
and
(\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau
, see (
3.13
)).

The sum in (
2.1
) is taken over molecules
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}
, which is defined as follows. Recall
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbb{M}
is formed by atoms with layer
\ell^{\prime}
. Define
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
to be the set of particle lines
{\bm{p}}
that cross into layer
\ell^{\prime}
from higher layers (i.e. when the start layer
\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]\leq\ell^{\prime}
, and the finish layer
\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]>\ell^{\prime}
), which is also the the root particle set of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Then, roughly speaking, the set
\mathcal{F}
consists of molecules
\mathbb{M}
that satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

Every connected component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
must contain at least one particle line from
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
.

(2)

The number of atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is bounded above by
|\log\varepsilon|^{O(1)}
.

(3)

Each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is “almost” a forest; i.e. we have proper control on its number of cycles.

(4)

For all
1\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
, every particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
is connected either to another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, or to a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
, via edges within
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
.

In the above conditions, (
1
) states that in layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, we only track the dynamics of those particles that are connected to the root particles in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, through a chain of collisions and overlaps within
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Next, (
2
)–(
3
) come from the truncation of dynamics (described in Section
1.3.5
), which limits the number of particles and recollisions in each layer.

Finally, (
4
) states that every particle in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
is either connected (via collisions and overlaps within
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
) to another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, or connected to a particle that occurs in the cumulant
E_{H}
at time
(\ell^{\prime}-1)\tau
(i.e. crosses into layers
<\ell^{\prime}
from layer
\ell^{\prime}
and belongs to
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
). This is because we are consideing the collision histories that contribute to the cumulant
E_{H}
, and because of our partial expansion (Section
1.3.3
), where only the cumulants
E_{H}((\ell^{\prime}-1)\tau)
are further expanded into previous time layers.

The precise definitions of
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
and
\mathcal{F}
will be given in Definitions
3.23
and
3.20
, respectively. The cumulant expansion formula (
2.1
) will be formally stated in Proposition
3.25
. The conditions (
1
)–(
4
) listed above correspond, with minor modifications, to those in Definition
3.20
(
1a
)–(
1d
).

2.1.2.
Proof of the formula

In this section, we sketch the proof of the cumulant formula (
2.1
). By (
1.10
) and (
1.11
), we have

(2.3)

\begin{gathered}f(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})=\varepsilon^{(d-1)s}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dn}}W_{s+n}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s+n})\,\mathrm{d}z_{s+1}\cdots\mathrm{d}z_{s+n},\\
W_{N}(\ell\tau)=\mathcal{S}_{N}(\tau)(W_{N}((\ell-1)\tau)).\end{gathered}

Prerequisite: The truncated dynamics.
Before starting, we note that, in order that the molecule
\mathbb{M}
satisfies (
1
)–(
3
) in Section
2.1.1
(in particular,
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
being almost a forest means we need to control the number of recollisions), we need to perform a truncation on the dynamics (see Definition
4.3
) within each layer.

In practice, the truncation is done by monitoring each potential collision event. When a collision is about to occur (i.e. a pre-collisional configuration forms), we check whether it would violate the constraints in (
2
) or (
3
). If such a violation would happen, we “turn-off” the collision by allowing the particles to pass through one another without a collision; otherwise, we allow the collision to happen as in the original dynamics.

Step 1: The induction setup.
The general strategy is to verify (
2.1
) by induction. For the base case
\ell=0
, we rely on the exact factorization at
t=0
in (
1.7
), except the factor
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
. This latter factor leads to the initial cumulants, which is the reason of the factors
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
in (
2.2
). These initial cumulants can be treated in the standard way (see Proposition
5.18
), and will be ignored in this section.

Then, we need the induction step, where we derive (
2.1
) for
\ell
from the same formula for
\ell-1
. In order to identify the factorized and cumulant parts at time
\ell\tau
based on those at
(\ell-1)\tau
, we need to perform the
cluster expansion
, which is detailed in
Step 2
below. Here, the clusters associated with the trajectories on
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
(and the corresponding transport operators
\mathcal{S}_{N}(\tau)
), are defined
as the maximal subsets of particles that are connected by collisions within
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
. Denote by
\mathbb{M}
the molecule (without O-atoms) which is the result of topological reduction of the trajectory on
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
, and let
\mathbb{M}_{j}
be the connected components of
\mathbb{M}
, corresponding to each cluster.

Step 2: The cluster expansion.
Let
p(\mathbb{M})
be the set of all particles in
\mathbb{M}
etc. Let
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
be the indicator function that the trajectory of the particles in
p(\mathbb{M})
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}
, and let
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}
be the indicator function that the particles in
p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
never collide with those in
p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})
. Then we have the following cluster expansion identity:

(2.4)

1=\sum_{k\leq N}\sum_{\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}}\bigg(\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\bigg),

which gives a partition of unity over all non-overlapping cluster decompositions.

Correspondingly, we have the following cluster expansion formula for the dynamics
\mathcal{S}_{N}(\tau)
:

(2.5)

\mathcal{S}_{N}(\tau)=\sum_{k\leq N}\sum_{\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(\tau)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\bigg),

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(\tau)
denotes the dynamics restricted to each cluster. By applying (
2.5
) to
W_{N}((\ell-1)\tau)
and using the first equation in (
2.3
), this leads to an expression of
f_{s}(\ell\tau)
in terms of cluster expansions and
f_{s}((\ell-1)\tau)
, which in particular contains the indicator functions
\prod\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}
.

Step 3: Penrose argument and overlaps.
The indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}
in (
2.5
) indicates there is no overlap between the trajectories of particles in
p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
and
p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})
, which implies the further factorization
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}=\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
, where
(e,e^{\prime})
runs over pairs of edges
e\in\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
indicates that (the trajectory piece corresponding to) the edge
e
and the edge
e^{\prime}
do not overlap.

Then, using the idendity
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=1-\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
, we can expand the product
\prod\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
into a summation of terms, using the standard
Penrose argument
similar to those in
[
8
]
. Here, compared to
[
8
]
, we need to perform an extra truncation in the Penrose argument (in order to bound the number of clusters by
\Lambda=|\log\varepsilon|^{C}
), which leads to extra complications.

In the end, we can recast (
2.5
) as a summation over single layer molecules
\mathbb{M}
which satisfy (
1
)–(
3
) in Section
2.1.1
. This
\mathbb{M}
is formed by joining the clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
(which contain only C-atoms) by O-atoms, which correspond to
overlaps
between edges
e\in\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
, which come from the indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
. Then, once
\mathbb{M}
is fixed, we can insert the induction hypothesis (
2.1
) for
(\ell-1)\tau
, and factorize the resulting term, to get an expression of form (
2.1
) for
\ell\tau
, thus completing the inductive step.

Step 4: Formation of multi-layer molecules.
With
Steps 1–3
above, we can then induct on
\ell
to prove the first line in (
2.1
), as well as an expression (upper bound) of
|E_{H}(\ell\tau)|
in terms of summation over sequences
(\mathbb{M}_{\ell},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{1})
of molecules. Then, by merging the corresponding top and bottom ends for the same particle in each different
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, we can construct a multi-layer molecule
\mathbb{M}
from the sequence
(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, so the summation over this sequence is rewritten as a summation over multi-layered molecule
\mathbb{M}
, which leads to the second line in (
2.1
) and completes the proof.

The prerequisite, i.e. truncation of dynamics, will be discussed in Section
4
. Then,
Steps 1–4
will be carried out in Section
5
. The cluster expansion in
Step 2
is contained in Section
5.1
(Lemma
5.5
), and the Penrose argument in
Step 2
is contained in Section
5.2
(Proposition
5.10
). Then we complete the inductive step in Section
5.3
–
5.4
(Proposition
5.16
), and the base case (initial cumulant) in Section
5.5
(Proposition
5.18
). Finally, in Section
5.6
, we carry out
Step 4
and put everything together to complete the proof.

2.2.
Reduction to local integral expressions

Now we have proved (
1.20
) and (
1.22
). As discussed in Section
1.3.5
, in order to prove Theorem
1
, it suffices to bound
\|E_{H}\|_{L^{1}}
. In this subsection, we apply (
2.1
) to reduce the estimate for
\|E_{H}\|_{L^{1}}
to individual estimates for a certain quantity
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
given by local integral expressions, which is equivalent to
\big\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|\big\|_{L^{1}}
. This is done in two steps.

2.2.1.
Counting the number of molecules

The first step is to prove the upper bound for the number of molecules, i.e. (
1.23
), where the quantity
\rho
is defined by
\rho=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}(s_{\ell^{\prime}}+\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}})

5
5

5

This
\rho
is equivalent (up to multiplicative constants) to the number of recollisions defined in Section
1.3.5
.
. Here,
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is the number of the independent cycles (i.e., the circuit rank) in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, and
s_{\ell^{\prime}}=|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|
is the number of particle lines crossing into layer
\ell^{\prime}
from higher layers.

The proof of (
1.23
) is contained in Proposition
7.2
in Section
7.1
. By property (
2
) in Section
2.1.1
, we know that the number of atoms in each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
can be bounded by
|\log\varepsilon|^{C}
. Then (
1.23
) follows from the combinatorial fact that the number of graphs with all degrees
\leq 4
, at most
m
vertices, and at most
n
independent cycles, is bounded by
C^{m}\cdot m^{n}
for some constant
C>0
. Applying this to molecules yields:

\displaystyle\#\mathbb{M}\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell^{\prime}}\#\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\#\{\textrm{choices of $s_{\ell^{\prime}}$ particle lines going to the layer below}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell^{\prime}}C^{|\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|}\cdot|\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|^{\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{Cs_{\ell^{\prime}}}\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C\rho},

which proves (
1.23
). Here we used the fact that the number of particle lines in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is
\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{O(1)}
, and
\#\{\textrm{choices of $s_{\ell^{\prime}}$ particle lines going to the layer below}\}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{Cs_{\ell^{\prime}}}
.

2.2.2.
Local representation formula

It now suffices to prove (
1.24
). In this subsection, we reduce the quantity
\big\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|\big\|_{L^{1}}
to another quantity
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
which is given by a local integral expression of
\bm{\delta}
functions. This
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
is defined as follows.

Consider a molecule
\mathbb{M}
. We define the
associated variables

z_{e}
and
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
for each edge
e
and atom
\mathfrak{n}
, forming
\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}}=(z_{e}:e\in\mathcal{E})
and
\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}=(t_{\mathfrak{n}}:\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M})
, where
\mathcal{E}
is the edge set of
\mathbb{M}
(and
\mathbb{M}
viewed as the atom set of itself). For a C-atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
(there is a similar and simpler definition for O-atoms), let
(e_{1},e_{2})
and
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
denote its bottom and top edges respectively. We define the
associated distribution

\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\Delta}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}},z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})
by

(2.6)

\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}},z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{\delta}\big(x_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-x_{e_{1}}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-v_{e_{1}})\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{e_{2}^{\prime}}-x_{e_{2}}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{e_{2}^{\prime}}-v_{e_{2}})\big)

\displaystyle\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{2}}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\left[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega\right]_{-}

\displaystyle\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(v_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-v_{e_{1}}+[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(v_{e_{2}^{\prime}}-v_{e_{2}}-[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega]\omega\big),

where
\omega:=\varepsilon^{-1}(x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{2}}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}))
is a unit vector, and
z_{-}:=-\min(z,0)
. On the support of
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
, if
(x_{e_{1}},v_{e_{1}})
and
(x_{e_{2}},v_{e_{2}})
represent the configurations of two particles immediately before the collision (pre-collisional configurations), then
(x_{e_{1}^{\prime}},v_{e_{1}^{\prime}})
and
(x_{e_{2}^{\prime}},v_{e_{2}^{\prime}})
correspond to their configurations immediately after the collision (post-collisional configurations).

We define the
associated domain

\mathcal{D}
by

(2.7)

\mathcal{D}:=\left\{\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}=(t_{\mathfrak{n}})\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|\mathbb{M}|}\,\middle|\,(\ell^{\prime}-1)\tau<t_{\mathfrak{n}}<\ell^{\prime}\tau\text{ if }\ell[\mathfrak{n}]=\ell^{\prime},\quad t_{\mathfrak{n}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\text{ if }\mathfrak{n}^{+}\text{ is the parent of }\mathfrak{n}\right\}.

Then, we define the
associated operator

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
by

(2.8)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q):=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)(|\mathcal{E}|-2|\mathbb{M}|)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg)\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}.

The main result of this subsection is then stated as follows: for any full molecule
\mathbb{M}
with
r(\mathbb{M})=H
, we have the following equality

(2.9)

\left\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{H})\right\|_{L^{1}}=\varepsilon^{(d-1)|H|}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}}),

where
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
is a bounded function with certain support properties, whose precise form is less important here.

The proof of (
2.9
) is presented in Proposition
7.5
in Section
7.2
. The main point here is that the factors in the integral (
2.9
) are
local
i.e. each individual
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
contains only those variables at atom
\mathfrak{n}
and edges at
\mathfrak{n}
, and
do not contain arbitrarily long chains of substitutions as in
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
. This is very important, as it allows us to apply the
cutting algorithm
below.

2.3.
The cutting algorithm

Recall that we need to prove (
1.24
). Using (
2.9
), we only need to establish an upper bound for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
. This is a multiple integral; involving many integrated variables; in order to estimate it, we need to reduce it to an
iterated integral
by choosing an appropriate order of variables in which we integrate. This process is implemented through the
cutting operation
and
cutting algorithm
(as sketched in Section
1.3.6
), which we now introduce.

2.3.1.
Cutting operations, and elementary molecules

Given any molecule
\mathbb{M}
,
cutting
is the operation that divides
\mathbb{M}
into two disjoint sub-molecules
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
, where each bond connecting an atom
\mathfrak{n}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{1}
to an atom
\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
is turned into a
free end
at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and a
fixed end
at
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
. We say
\mathbb{M}_{1}
is
cut as free
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
is
cut as fixed
. See
Figure
2
for an illustration, and Definition
8.3
for the precise definition.

Figure 2.
Cutting a molecule
\mathbb{M}
into smaller molecules
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
