(where red atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{1}
are cut as free, and green atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{2}
cut as fixed). The fixed ends are marked with red
\times
symbols.

In terms of the integral in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
, cutting
\mathbb{M}_{1}
as free (and
\mathbb{M}_{2}=\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{M}_{1}
as fixed) simply means that we first integrate in the
(z_{e})
and
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})
variables associated with
\mathbb{M}_{2}
(treating the variables associated with
\mathbb{M}_{1}
as fixed) and then integrate in those variables associated with
\mathbb{M}_{1}
. Here when a bond
e
between
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
is cut into a free end
e_{1}
at
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and a fixed end
e_{2}
at
\mathbb{M}_{2}
, we always impose the condition
z_{e}=z_{e_{1}}=z_{e_{2}}
; see
Figure
2
. This is merely a diagrammatic representation of Fubini’s theorem.

With the above conventions, it is straightforward to verify that

(2.10)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}

where in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
, we are integrating in the variables of free ends and bonds of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
, treating the variables of fixed ends as frozen in the integral. In fact, (
2.10
) follows from the calculation

(2.11)

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg)\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}\\
&=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{1}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}_{1}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{1}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg)}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}}\cdot\underbrace{\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{2}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}_{2}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{2}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg)\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{2}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}\bigg)}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q)}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\\
&=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q).\end{split}

In general, in (
2.10
),
\mathbb{M}
is allowed to have fixed ends, and the definitions of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
(and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
) in (
2.8
) is modified by replacing
\mathcal{E}
with
\mathcal{E}_{*}\coloneqq\{\textrm{bonds and free ends}\}
. The proof of (
2.10
) in full generality is presented in Proposition
8.14
.

In our cutting algorithm, presented in Section
2.3.2
below, we will repeatedly cut
\mathbb{M}
into smaller molecules until all of them becomes
elementary molecules

\mathbb{M}_{j}
, such that each of them contains only one or two atoms, so the integral
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
can be calculated explicitly. We can then control
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
by
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})\leq\|Q_{\mathbb{M}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\cdot\prod_{j}\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}
.

There are four major types of elementary molecules, denoted by {2}, {3}, {4} and {33}, indicated by the
degree
(i.e number of bonds plus free ends,
not
counting fixed ends) of each atom. See
Figure
15
(Section
8.2
) for a picture of elementary molecules and Definition
8.11
for its rigorous definition. Note that {33} molecules are divided into types {33A} and {33B}, and we also allow {44} molecules which play minor roles and will not be discussed here.

The key observation is that, for each {33} molecule
\mathbb{M}_{j}
that occurs in the cutting process (see
\mathbb{M}_{2}
in
Figure
2
), we have the operator norm bound
\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
for some positive constant
\upsilon
, at least in typical cases. For this reason, we define each {33} molecule to be
good
, and each {3} and {2} molecule to be
normal
. In fact, a {3} molecule corresponds to a normal collision, a {2} molecule corresponds to a recollision, and a {33} molecule (which can be further decomposed to a {3} and a {2} molecule) corresponds to a recollision that follows a normal collision, which constitutes the basic unit of gain, as already observed in
[
20
]
in the context of wave turbulence. Finally, we define a {4} molecule to be
bad
as it “wastes” one integral and leads to losses
\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}
. See Definition
9.4
for the precise version of classification of elementary molecules.

Returning to the estimate of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
, suppose there exists a cutting sequence that decomposes a molecule
\mathbb{M}
into a collection of molecules
(\mathbb{M}_{j})
. Then, by the discussion above and the fact that
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
is bounded (say
\|Q_{\mathbb{M}}\|\leq 1
), we obtain

(2.12)

\left\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{H})\right\|_{L^{1}}=\varepsilon^{(d-1)|H|}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})\leq\prod_{j}\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+(d-1)(|H|-\#_{\mathrm{bad}})},

where we gain a factor of
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
for each good molecule and lose a factor of
\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}
for each bad molecule. By (
2.12
), it is clear that (
1.24
) would follow if we can construct a cutting sequence satisfying

(2.13)

\upsilon\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+(d-1)\cdot(|H|-\#_{\mathrm{bad}})\geq c\cdot\rho.

This inequality is stated in Proposition
9.7
, and is the main goal of the combinatorial part of this paper. We discuss the algorithm of constructing the cutting sequence, in Section
2.3.2
below.

The discussion and estimates of elementary molecules are contained in Section
9.1
, see Propositions
9.1
–
9.3
and Definition
9.4
. The proof of (
1.24
) (as part of Proposition
6.2
, see (
9.53
)) is contained in Section
9.2
, using also the auxiliary results (Propositions
9.8
–
9.9
) in Section
9.3
.

2.3.2.
The cutting algorithm

Finally we discuss the proof of (
2.13
) stated as Proposition
9.7
. This is the main technical ingredient of the proof, and will occupy Sections
11
–
13
. The idea is to maximize the number of good (i.e. {33}) molecules and minimize the number of bad (i.e. {4}) molecules, and balance between them if needed. Below we discuss the main ideas and a few important points in this construction.

Toy models.
The full algorithm, see Sections
12
–
13
, is quite complicated. In Section
11
, we will neglect a few technicalities by making the simplifying assumptions that (i)

\mathbb{M}
has no O-atoms, and (ii) each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is precisely a forest. Moreover, we define the
toy models
(I, I+, II and III, see Definitions
11.2
,
11.9
,
11.15
,
11.20
and
Figure
20
) to be specific cases of
two layer molecules

\mathbb{M}
: it has an upper layer
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and a lower layer
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, each of which is a tree. The
\rho
in (
2.13
) is comparable to the number of bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

The proof in Section
11
(under the simplifications (i)–(ii)) will consist of two parts: reducing general multi-layer molecules
\mathbb{M}
to one of the toy models (via
layer selection
), and designing a cutting algorithm for each individual toy model.

The UP algorithm.
We start with a simple algorithm called
UP
, see Definitions
11.10
. Basically, in this algorithm we select a
lowest degree 3
atom
\mathfrak{n}
, and cut the atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
(the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
) from high to low, see
Figure
28
(Section
11.3
).

We can show that all the molecules resulting from
UP
are elementary by preserving the key monotonicity property: each atom either has
no top fixed end
or
no bottom fixed end
, see Proposition
11.12
(
1
). Moreover, this algorithm
minimizes

\#_{\{4\}}
(the number of {4} molecules) by allowing only one {4} molecule per connected component of
\mathbb{M}
.

The drawback is that, the
UP
algorithm does
not necessarily maximize

\#_{\{33\}}
(the number of {33} molecules), so it is not directly useful in our proof. However, it is still useful in “finishing off” unimportant parts of the molecule where we do not expect any gain. Moreover, in some important cases, one can actually make special adaptations to
UP
to guarantee the abundance of {33} molecules; this leads to the important algorithms
2CONNUP
and
3COMPUP
below.

Toy model I.
The feature of toy model I, see Definition
11.2
, is that each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has exactly one bond with
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. In this case, the cutting algorithm is defined in Definition
11.4
. It combines a trivial algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(always cutting lowest) with a greedy algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(cutting a {33} molecule, and the related {343} molecule, whenever possible). See
Figure
22
(Section
11.2
).

In this algorithm, one can show that the number of {4} molecules is minimized, i.e.
\#_{\{4\}}\leq 1
; moreover the greedy algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
guarantees the abundance of {33} molecules, i.e.
\#_{\{33\}}\gtrsim\rho
, see Proposition
11.7
. This relies on the fact that we never get any degree 2 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(Lemma
11.8
), which crucially uses that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest.

Toy model I+.
Toy model I+ (Definition
11.9
) is similar to toy model I, but only a subset
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{U}
of atoms has one bond with
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and the others have none. Moreover, in toy model I+ we assume the number of connected components of
X
satisfies
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\ll\rho
.

In this case the algorithm (which is essentially the
MAINUD
algorithm) is defined in Definition
11.13
, and is similar to Definition
11.4
, see
Figures
30
–
31
(Section
11.3
). The only difference is that we only apply the naive “cutting lowest” algorithm in
X
, and instead apply
UP
in the rest of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, to minimize the number of {4} molecules. In fact this guarantees that
\#_{\{4\}}\lesssim\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
, which is negligible compared to the gain
\#_{\{33\}}\gtrsim\rho
, see Proposition
11.14
.

Toy model II.
In toy model II (Definition
11.15
), we have the same assumptions as toy model I, but with
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\gtrsim\rho
. In this case the algorithm is defined in Definition
11.17
. Basically we first remove
\mathbb{M}_{D}
and cut it using
DOWN
the dual of
UP
, and then cut
\mathbb{M}_{U}
using a special variant of
UP
which is called the
3COMPUP
algorithm, see
Figure
34
(Section
11.4
).

The idea here is that, after removing
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, the degree 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
are precisely those in
X
. Then,
\mathbb{M}_{U}
will satisfy the key property that the degree 3 atoms are
separated by degree 4 atoms
, i.e. the number of connected components of degree 3 atoms satisfies
\#_{\mathrm{3comp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}=\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\gtrsim\rho
. Under this assumption, one can design a special variant of
UP
(basically choosing
two
lowest deg 3 atoms
\mathfrak{n}
instead of one), namely the
3COMPUP
, to guarantee the abundance of {33} molecules, see Proposition
11.19
.

Toy model III.
In toy model III (Definition
11.20
), we assume there exists
\gtrsim\rho
atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, each having
two bonds
with
\mathbb{M}_{D}
; they are called
2-connections
. In this case, we simply apply the
DOWN
algorithm (dual of
UP
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
), see Definition
11.21
and
Figure
38
(Section
11.5
). In the process, each 2-connection atom
\mathfrak{n}
will naturally occur as a {33} molecule, which we then cut it to obtain a good component. Consequently we get
\#_{\{33\}}\gtrsim\rho
and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
, see Proposition
11.22
.

Reduction to toy models.
With the algorithm constructed for each toy model, it now remains to reduce general multi-layer molecules
\mathbb{M}
(under the assumptions (i)–(ii)) to one of the toy models. The first step is to reduce
\mathbb{M}
to a
two-layer
molecule by a process called
layer selection
.

Since we have ignored the recollisions
within each layer

\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
by assuming each layer is a forest, we know that the recollisions in
\mathbb{M}
must happen
across different layers
. These recollisions come from the condition (
4
) in Section
2.1.1
: this condition implies that the particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
are connected both
within layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}
and
within layers
\mathbb{M}_{\leq\ell^{\prime}}:=\cup_{\ell^{\prime\prime}\leq\ell^{\prime}}\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
, resulting in cycles.

The idea of layer selection is as follows. First choose a layer
\ell_{1}
such that
s_{\ell_{1}-1}=|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})|\gtrsim\rho
, and that
s_{\ell_{1}-1}\gg s_{\ell_{1}}
; this means that the particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
are connected to each other (with negligible exceptions) within layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
. Now, choose the
highest
layer
\ell_{2}<\ell_{1}
such that sufficiently many particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
are connected to each other
within
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{2}:\ell_{1}-1]}:=\cup_{\ell_{2}\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{1}-1}\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Then, by putting together layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\,(\ell_{2}<\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{1})
into
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(and removing a negligible number of exceptions), and defining
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{2}}:=\mathbb{M}_{D}
, we obtain a two layer molecule where each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a tree (or forest), and there exist
\gtrsim\rho
bonds between them. For details see Definition
11.23
and Proposition
11.24
, and
Figure
40
(Section
11.6
).

Once we are in the two layer setting, we then consider the
dichotomy
determined by the quantities
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}
(the number of 2-connections as defined above) and
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
(the number of connected components of
X
), where
X
is the set of atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
that have exactly one bone with
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. By definition, if
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\gtrsim\rho
then
\mathbb{M}
is toy model III. If
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\ll\rho
, then the 2-connections will be negligible and we may assume
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
. Then,
\mathbb{M}
will be toy model I+ or II, depending on whether
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\ll\rho
or
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\gtrsim\rho
. This completes the proof in Section
11
.

The full algorithm.
Finally we discuss the extra ingredients in the full algorithm in Sections
12
–
13
, in addition to those in Section
11
. These are summarized in Section
11.7
and include the followings:

•

The O-atoms: in the general case we need to replace the relevant notions (adjacency, connectedness etc.) by the ones associated with ov-segments, and modify the cutting algorithms accordingly. For example, the algorithms
UP
(Definition
11.10
),
2CONNUP
(Definition
11.21
),
3COMPUP
(Definition
11.17
) and
MAINUD
(Definition
11.13
), have their full versions defined in Definitions
12.1
,
13.1
,
13.6
and
13.12
respectively.

•

Layer refinement: this is discussed in Definition
12.8
(Section
12.3
), and is needed to treat the possible recollisions
within each layer

\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. We further sub-divide each
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
into finitely many subsets, such that each of them is precisely a forest, which recovers assumption (ii). This also leads to an extra step in the layer selection process, see Definition
12.12
.

•

The strong an weak resonances: these are exceptional cases where {33} molecules do not gain powers, see Definitions
12.4
and
12.16
. In reality, if this effect becomes significant, we shall perform alternative cutting algorithms that exploit such degeneracies to gain powers of
\varepsilon
in a different way, see Propositions
12.5
and
12.17
.

•

The pre-processing step: this is discussed in Definition
13.10
(Section
13.3
). This additional step is needed to deal with possible fixed ends in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
for the two-layer molecule
\mathbb{M}
, which may cause trouble to the greedy algorithm in the toy model I+.

For a summary of the ingredients of the proofs in Sections
12
–
13
and their logical relations, see
Figure
41
(Section
11.7
).

2.4.
The Boltzmann approximation and truncation error

In Sections
2.1
–
2.3
above, we have focused on the proof of the
E_{H}
estimates in (
1.21
) (see Proposition
6.2
). Now it remains to prove the approximation of
f^{\mathcal{A}}
by the Boltzmann solution, see Proposition
6.1
.

By discussions in Section
2.1
, we know that
f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau)
is given by an explicit series expansion of iterated time integrals that
involve only
f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)
, therefore this part follows from essentially the same arguments in the short-time theory. We identify those leading terms that do not contain recollision, and those sub-leading terms that contain recollision. We then match each leading term with the corresponding term that occurs in the Duhamel expansion of
f(\ell\tau)
in terms of
f((\ell-1)\tau)
; note that the cluster expansion (see (
3.16
)) actually leads to extra terms in addition to those occurring in Boltzmann Duhamel expansions, but these extra terms turn out to cancel each other, as shown in Proposition
14.2
. As for the sub-leading terms, they can be proved to be vanishing by applying a very simple special case of the cutting algorithm in Sections
11
–
13
(see Section
14.3
,
Point 5
), since we only have
one single layer
and the number of recollisions in each cluster is bounded by
\Gamma
. All these are contained in Section
14
.

Finally we discuss the estimate for the error term
f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}
caused by the truncated dynamics. This estimate is stated in Proposition
6.3
and is proved in Section
15
. Here again we reduce the estimate to the construction of a cutting algorithm. However, as we now have many recollisions, one new difficulty arises due to the possibility that a small group of particles within distance
O(\varepsilon)
of each other producing many recollisions within time
O(\varepsilon)
. To treat this, we rely on
a new ingredient
, namely the absolute upper bound on the number of collisions given the number of particles, which is proved in Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko
[
15
]
. Basically, if
\Gamma
is sufficiently large, then these
\Gamma
recollisions must involve sufficiently many particles, with each new particle providing a {33} molecule in a suitable algorithm.

The algorithm in Section
15
is similar to the one in Sections
11
–
13
, and is based on a two-layer molecule formed by
A^{+}
and
A
where
A
is (almost) a tree and
A^{+}
is arbitrary; see Proposition
15.4
(which actually involves three layers
A^{+}
,
A
and
A^{-}
, but in reality we only exploit two of them). The main algorithm, called
MAINTRUP
, is similar to the
MAINUD
algorithm in Section
13.4
, where each time we cut a lowest atom in
A^{+}
, and keep cutting any {33} or {343} structure in
A
that occur. This will then guarantee a lower bound for the number of {33} molecules, provided we have a lower bound for the number
\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}
of bonds connecting an atom in
A
to an atom in a specific subset
X^{+}(A)\subseteq A^{+}
. This last lower bound is then provided by (the inverse function of) the Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko upper bound in
[
15
]
, see Proposition
15.2
. For more details of the algorithm, see Definition
15.9
and Proposition
15.10
.

2.5.
Comparison between wave and particle systems

As mentioned in Section
1.2
, the proof in this paper is deeply inspired by that in the wave setting by the first two authors
[
23
]
. Here we make a brief comparison between the two proofs, in terms of their similarities and differences. In the case of wave kinetic theory, the microscopic system in
[
23
]
is given by the nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation on a torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}
of size
L
, with a nonlinearity of strength
\alpha
. The lowest-order nontrivial statistical quantity is the second moment of the Fourier modes
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(t,k)|^{2}
, where
\widehat{u}(t,k)
is the Fourier transform of the solution
u(t,x)
to the NLS equation, which is to be approximated by the solution of the (homogeneous) wave kinetic equation (
1.19
), in the limit
L\to\infty
and
\alpha\to 0
. This kinetic limit is taken under an appropriate choice of scaling law between
L
and
\alpha
, namely
\alpha=L^{-\gamma}
, where
\gamma\in(0,1)
in the homogeneous setting, see
[
23
]
.

At a conceptual level, the two proofs are very similar. The fundamental difficulty represented by the divergence of the series expansion on the long time interval
[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
is the same, and the same goes for the need to create an arrow of time where, in both cases, the microscopic system is time-reversible but the limiting kinetic equation is irreversible. The idea of performing a partial expansion, where one refrains from expanding the lowest order statistic (the
f^{\mathcal{A}}
here, and the second moments in the wave case) but expands the cumulants all the way to time
t=0
, is also the same. This keeps track of all the relevant collisions or interactions on the long time interval
[0,t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
that affect the cumulants at time
t_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

Even at a more technical level, there are uncanny similarities between the two proofs. First, both proofs start with a truncation of the dynamics in order to control the size of the diagrams appearing in the cumulant expansions. Also, in both proofs, the needed cumulant estimate is reduced to a combinatorial problem that is resolved through an elaborate algorithm. This combinatorial problem, in both cases, involves the combinatorial structure of
molecules
, which are formed by
atoms
that represent collisions or interactions. While the algorithms are vastly different in the wave case and particle case, there is an important similarity in the gain mechanism. As shown in Section
2.3
, the basic unit of gain here is the special structure of {33} molecules; similarly, in the wave case, the basic unit of gain also comes from the {33} molecules and the associated five-vector counting problem.

All this being said, the analogy between the particle and wave kinetic theories has its limitations, and despite the above similarities, each proof carries its own distinct set of challenges. In the wave case, a large box limit needs to be performed to go from the discrete Fourier space of
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}
to the continuum setting of the wave kinetic equation (
1.19
). This links the wave kinetic theory to some deep number theory problems, which seems to be absent in the particle setting. Also, the Feynman diagram expansion for the NLS equation turns out to converge
only conditionally and not absolutely
, and requires highly nontrivial cancellations between arbitrarily large diagrams, which is another distinctive feature and novelty of
[
23
]
. On the other hand, the cumulant expansion in the particle setting is technically more involved, due to the application of inclusion-exclusion principle and the resulting notion of overlaps, which does not seem to be have an analog in the wave case. Also the main algorithm in the particle case seems to be conceptually more complicated than the corresponding algorithm in the wave case, which may be due to the singular nature of the
\bm{\delta}
integrals in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
compared to the counting problems in
[
23
]
.

2.6.
The rest of this paper

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Note that, although the notion of cutting and cutting algorithm is the main novelty and central to the proof, they have to wait until Section
8
(cutting) and Section
11
(algorithm) to appear, due to the necessary preparations in Sections
3
–
7
.

•

Part I: Sections
3
–
5
are devoted the statement and proof of Proposition
3.25
.

•

In Section
3
we define all the relevant notions (molecules, topological reduction etc.) in Sections
3.1
–
3.4
, and then state Proposition
3.25
in Section
3.5
.

•

In Section
4
we define the truncated dynamics (Definition
4.3
) and its properties, which are needed in the proof of Proposition
3.25
.

•

In Section
5
we prove Proposition
3.25
.

•

Interlude I: Section
6
contains the statement of the main propositions, Propositions
6.1
–
6.3
, of this paper. We also prove that these propositions imply Theorem
1
.

•

Part II: Sections
7
–
9
are devoted to the first half of the proof of Proposition
6.2
, where we reduce it to Proposition
9.7
.

•

In Section
7
we make the necessary preparations, and reduce the the expression
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
occurring in the upper bound of
E_{H}
to the local integration
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
.

•

In Section
8
we define the notion of cutting (Definition
8.3
), study its properties, and how the integral
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
behaves under cutting (Proposition
8.14
).

•

In Section
9
we state the main result concerning cutting algorithms, i.e. Proposition
9.7
, and prove that it implies Proposition
6.2
.

•

Interlude II: Section
10
contains a few simple reductions and reiterations of properties of the molecule
\mathbb{M}
, in preparation of the proof of Proposition
9.7
.

•

Part III: Sections
11
–
13
are devoted to the second half of the proof of Proposition
6.2
, i.e. the proof of Proposition
9.7
.

•

In Section
11
we study the two-layer toy models, namely toy models I, I+, II and III (in Sections
11.2
–
11.5
respectively). In Section
11.6
we define layer selection in the toy model case, and in Section
11.7
we sketch the additional ingredients needed in the full algorithm.

•

In Section

we present the first half of the proof of Proposition
9.7
, where we reduce it to Proposition
12.18
. This section contains the
UP
algorithm (Definition
12.1
), strong and weak degeneracies (Definitions
12.4
,
12.16
), layer refinement (Section
12.3
) and layer selection (Section
12.4
).

•

In Section

we finish the proof of Proposition
12.18
. In Sections
13.1
–
13.4
we introduce the algorithms
2CONNUP
,
3COMPUP
, pre-processing and
MAINUD
respectively. Finally, in Section
13.5
we finish the proof of Proposition
12.18
by invoking the dichotomy and reducing to one of the toy model cases in Section
11
.

•

The Finale: Sections
14
–
15
are devoted to the proof of Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
.

•

Appendices: Appendix
A
–
C
contain auxiliary results and tables summarizing important notions.

3.
The cumulant expansion formula

In this section, we will state the main cumulant formula in Proposition
3.25
, which has form

(3.1)

f_{s}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})=\sum_{H\subseteq[s]}(f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau))^{\otimes([s]\backslash H)}\cdot E_{H}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H})+(\mathrm{error\ terms}),

(3.2)

|E_{H}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H})|\leq\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{H}).

Here
f^{\mathcal{A}}
is an approximate solution to the Boltzmann equation, and
\mathbb{M}
is a molecule (Definition
3.1
). Also
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
is a collection of molecules (Definition
3.20
) and
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
is an integral expression associated with the molecule
\mathbb{M}
(Definition
3.23
), which represents the densities given by trajectories that topologically reduce to
\mathbb{M}
(Definition
3.8
).

The statement of Proposition
3.25
involves many concepts that need to be defined. We define these notions in Sections
3.1
–
3.4
, which are related to molecules
\mathbb{M}
and topological reductions. Then, in Section
3.5
we define the set
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
and the associated integral
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{H})
in (
3.2
), and then state Proposition
3.25
. This will be the main goal in the first part of the paper, whose proof will occupy Sections
4
–
5
.

3.1.
Molecules

We start with the definition of molecules. As seen in
Figure
5
(Section
3.2
), molecules are topological reductions of actual particle trajectories (see Definition
3.8
for precise formulation).

Definition 3.1

(Molecules)
.

We define a (layered) molecule to be a diagram demonstrated by
Figure
3
.
More rigorously, we define a
(layered) molecule

\mathbb{M}=(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
to be a diagram as follows. For the correspondence between molecule pictures and physical particle pictures, see
Table
3
in Appendix
B
.

Figure 3.
An example of molecules.

(1)

Atoms, edges, bonds and ends.

\mathcal{M}
is a set of nodes referred to as
atoms
, and
\mathcal{E}
is a set of
edges
consisting of
bonds
and
ends
to be specified below. For convenience, below we will abuse notation and use
\mathbb{M}
instead of
\mathcal{M}
to denote the set of atoms in
\mathbb{M}
.

(a)

An atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
is either a
C-atom
(collision) or an
O-atom
(overlap), denoted respectively by
\circ
and
\bullet
as in
Figure
3
. Each atom is assigned with a unique
layer

\ell[\mathfrak{n}]
which is an integer in
[\underline{\ell}:\ell]
, where
\ell
is the highest layer and
\underline{\ell}
is the lowest layer of
\mathbb{M}
. Define
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
to be the set of atoms
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
of layer
\ell[\mathfrak{n}]=\ell^{\prime}
. The boundaries between layers are indicated by horizontal lines, as in
Figure
3
.

(b)

Each edge in
\mathcal{E}
is either a
bond
between two atoms
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, or an
end
emanating from one atom
\mathfrak{n}
, or an
empty end
that does not involve any atom. Each edge is either
top
or
bottom
when viewed at each involved atom
\mathfrak{n}
; a top (resp. bottom) edge is drawn above (resp. below) the atom, as in
Figure
3
. Moreover, each end is also either
free
or
fixed
, with fixed ends indicated by a
\times
, as shown in
Figure
3
. We understand by convention that empty ends are always free, and count as both top and bottom.

(c)

For two atoms
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, if a bond exists between
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
that is bottom at
\mathfrak{n}
and top at
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, we say
\mathfrak{n}
is
parent
of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is
child
of
\mathfrak{n}
.

(2)

Particle lines.

\mathcal{P}
is a collection of paths: each path starts from a bottom end at an atom, then keeps connecting to a parent by a bond, until reaching a top end at another atom. Each chain
{\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{P}
is called a
particle line
. We require that each edge belongs to a unique particle line; in particular, each empty end is also viewed as a particle line by convention. For a top edge and a bottom edge at an atom
\mathfrak{n}
, we say they are
serial
if they belong to the same particle line. Note that each particle line
{\bm{p}}
contains a unique bottom end and a unique top end. We will identify particle lines with physical particles, see
Table
3
.

Recall
\ell
defined in (
1a
). We may define a
start layer

\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]\in[\underline{\ell}:\ell]
and
finish layer

\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]\in[\underline{\ell}:\ell+1]
for each particle line
{\bm{p}}
. As
{\bm{p}}
contains a unique bottom (and unique top) end, we may also equivalently write
\ell_{1}[e]
(resp.
\ell_{2}[e]
) for each bottom (resp. top) end
e
instead of
\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]
and
\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]
. We refer to the interval
[\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]:\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]]
as the
lifespan
of the particle line
{\bm{p}}
. In
Figure
3
, the lifespan of
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}
are
[1:3]
and
[1:2]
, respectively. For
{\bm{p}}_{1}
, its finish layer is
\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}_{1}]=\ell+1=3
, which is above the highest layer
\ell
. To indicate this, we draw it penetrating the top horizontal line.

(3)

Initial links.
Finally,
\mathcal{L}
is a collection of pairs of bottom ends
(e,e^{\prime})
with
\ell_{1}[e]=\ell_{1}[e^{\prime}]=1
(provided
\underline{\ell}=1
). Since each particle line has a unique bottom end by (
2
), we can equivalently view
\mathcal{L}
as a collection of pairs of particle lines with start layer
1
. Each pair in
\mathcal{L}
is called an
initial link
. In
Figure
3
,
\mathcal{L}
contains a single pair
(e_{4},e_{9})
, which is indicated by a dashed line connecting them.

(4)

Ov-segments.
For later use, we also define an
ov-segment
to be a path between two atoms (or ends) such that all intermediate atoms in this path are O-atoms (except the possible endpoints), and this path is contained in a single particle line. We say an ov-segment is
maximal
if it is not contained in other ov-segments. In
Figure
3
, the path consisting of edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, and atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{3}
, is an example of a maximal ov-segment. Note that each edge in
\mathbb{M}
belongs to a unique maximal ov-segment. Also each bond in
\mathbb{M}
is a special case of ov-segment (not necessarily maximal).

(5)

The objects
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
satisfies the following requirements.

(a)

Each atom has exactly 2 top edges and exactly 2 bottom edges.

(b)

Starting from any atom
\mathfrak{n}
, it is impossible to get back to
\mathfrak{n}
by iteratively taking parent or by iteratively taking child.

(c)

If
\mathfrak{n}
is a parent of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
then we must have
\ell[\mathfrak{n}]\geq\ell[\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}]
. If
\mathfrak{n}
belongs to a particle line
{\bm{p}}
then we must have
\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]\leq\ell[\mathfrak{n}]\leq\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]
.

Definition 3.2

(Full molecules)
.

If a molecule
\mathbb{M}
has no fixed end, we say
\mathbb{M}
is a
full molecule
.

In the statement of the cumulant formula, we will need to label different particles to distinguish them. Also the particles corresponding to
\bm{z}_{s}
or
\bm{z}_{H}
in
f_{s}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})
or cumulant
E_{H}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H})
, i.e. the root particles, will play a special role.

Definition 3.3

(Label and root particle lines)
.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
, we may assign
labels
(which takes values in
\mathbb{N}
) to each particle line
{\bm{p}}
of
\mathbb{M}
. This makes
\mathbb{M}
a
labeled molecule
. Note that two labeled molecules with the same molecule structure (Definition
3.1
) but different labelings are regarded as different.

For any molecule
\mathbb{M}
, define the
set of all particle lines
in
\mathbb{M}
to be
p(\mathbb{M})
. Define a particle line
{\bm{p}}
to be a
root particle line
, if its finish layer
\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]=\ell+1
, where
\ell
is the highest layer in
\mathbb{M}
. Define the set of root particle lines to be
r(\mathbb{M})
, and the set of non-root particles to be
(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M}):=p(\mathbb{M})\backslash r(\mathbb{M})
.

For two labeled molecules
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
with
\ell
layers, we define them to be
equivalent
, denoted
\mathbb{M}\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, if they have the same molecule structure (Definition
3.1
) and the same labels for root particle lines. Define
[\mathbb{M}]
to be the equivalence class of
\mathbb{M}
, which is obtained by relabeling non-root particle lines in
\mathbb{M}
.

In
Figure
3
, we can label
{\bm{p}}_{1}
,
{\bm{p}}_{2}
and
{\bm{p}}_{3}
by
1
,
2
and
3
respectively, where
{\bm{p}}_{1}
is the root particle line.

Remark 3.4
.

Clearly an equivalence class
[\mathbb{M}]
is uniquely determined by an unlabeled molecule
\mathbb{M}
, and a fixed labeling of all root particle lines. In the proof of the cumulant expansion formula (Proposition
3.25
) in Sections
4
–
5
, it is important that we use
\mathbb{M}
to represent labeled molecules and sum over equivalence classes
[\mathbb{M}]
(see relevant notations in Definition
1.6
(
6
)). Once this proof is finished, in subsequent analysis of the
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
terms, the labeling will not play any role, so we will ignore it and use
\mathbb{M}
to denote unlabeled molecules in Sections
6
–
15
(we still need to label the root particles, which will not play a major role, see Remark
3.26
).

We introduce some additional notions associated with molecules, which will be used in the proof below.

Definition 3.5

(Ordering of atoms)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule and
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}
be atoms.

(1)

Descendants and ancestors.
If
\mathfrak{n}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
by iteratively taking parents, then we say
\mathfrak{n}
is an
ancestor
of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is a
descendant
of
\mathfrak{n}
. We use
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
(resp.
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
) to denote the set of descendants (resp. ancestors) of
\mathfrak{n}
. By convention we understand that
\mathfrak{n}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
(and
\mathfrak{n}\in Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
).

(2)

The partial ordering of atoms and edges.
We also define a
partial ordering
between all atoms, by defining
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\prec\mathfrak{n}
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\neq\mathfrak{n}
and is a descendant of
\mathfrak{n}
(equivalently
\mathfrak{n}
is ancestor of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
). In particular
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\prec\mathfrak{n}
if
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is a child of
\mathfrak{n}
(equivalently
\mathfrak{n}
is parent of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
).

(3)

Given any subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, we can define a
lowest
atom in
A
to be any atom
\mathfrak{n}\in A
that satisfies
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\not\in A
for each
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\prec\mathfrak{n}
(i.e.
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\neq\mathfrak{n}
is descendant of
\mathfrak{n}
). Define
highest
atoms in the same way. These are just minimal and maximal elements of
A
under the partial ordering
\prec
, and note that they need not be unique for general
A
.

Definition 3.6

(Various sets associated with molecules)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}=(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
be a molecule.

(1)

Sets
\mathcal{E}_{*}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}
.
Define
\mathcal{E}_{*}
to be the set of all edges that are either bonds or free ends (i.e. not counting fixed ends). Define also the sets
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}
) to be the set of all ends (resp. bottom ends, top ends). Note that empty ends belong to
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{\pm}
by convention.

Note that the sets
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}
are in bijection with the set
p(\mathbb{M})
of all particle lines, see Definition
3.1
(
2
).

(2)

Number of atoms and particle lines.
We will use
|\mathbb{M}|
,
|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}
,
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}
,
|\mathbb{M}|_{r}
and
|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}
to denote the number of atoms, O-atoms, particle lines (i.e.
|p(\mathbb{M})|
), root particle lines (i.e.
|r(\mathbb{M})|
), and non-root particle lines (i.e.
|(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})|
) of
\mathbb{M}
respectively.

3.2.
Topological reduction

In this section, we rigorously formulate the notion of topological reduction. We start with the notion of admissible trajectories, which generally occur from modifications of hard sphere dynamics where certain collisions are neglected according to certain rules (see Definition
3.15
).

Definition 3.7

(Admissible trajectories)
.

Let
\bm{z}(t)=\bm{z}_{N}(t)=(z_{j}(t))_{j}
be any particle trajectory (a priori with no equation or restriction). We say it is
admissible
(see
Figure
4
), if

Figure 4.
Admissible trajectories (Definition
3.7
), where it is allowed to have
|x_{i}-x_{j}|<\varepsilon
, but two particles reaching distance
\varepsilon
from “inside” will not collide and not change velocity, as they do not satisfy the pre-collisional condition.

(1)

Each
z_{j}(t)=(x_{j}(t),v_{j}(t))
is piecewise linear,
x_{j}(t)
is continuous and
v_{j}(t)
is left continuous, with a discrete set of times of velocity discontinuity referred to as
collisions
;

(2)

At each collision time
t
, there exist two and only two particles whose velocities are discontinuous, their positions
(x_{i},x_{j})
and velocities
(v_{i},v_{j})
satisfy (
1.2
) with
x_{i}(t)-x_{j}(t)=\varepsilon\omega\,(\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, and
(v_{i}(t)-v_{j}(t))\cdot\omega\leq 0
. We refer to this set of conditions as the
pre-collisional condition
.

(3)

Additionally, we define an
overlap
to be a time when
x_{i}(t)-x_{j}(t)=\varepsilon\omega\,(\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
and
(v_{i}(t)-v_{j}(t))\cdot\omega\leq 0
, but the velocities
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
are continuous at time
t
.

Definition 3.8

(Topological reduction)
.

Let
\bm{z}(t)
be an admissible trajectory for
t\in[0,\ell\tau]
, and
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule with
\ell
layers (Definition
3.1
). We say
\bm{z}(t)

topologically reduces
to
\mathbb{M}
, if there is a bijection between maximal ov-segments of
\mathbb{M}
(Definition
3.1
(
4
)) and maximal linear pieces of the trajectory
\bm{z}(t)
, see
Figure
5
, which satisfies the following requirements:

(1)

If two maximal ov-segments intersect at a C-atom, then the corresponding linear pieces of
\bm{z}(t)
have a collision. If they intersect at an O-atom, then the corresponding linear pieces have an overlap.

(2)

The induced correspondence between atoms and collisions/overlaps preserves order (i.e. if
\mathfrak{n}
is parent of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
then the corresponding collision/overlap happens later in time), and
is bijective on collisions
(it is injective on overlaps by time ordering, but might not be surjective).

(3)

If two maximal ov-segments belong to the same particle line in
\mathbb{M}
, then the corresponding linear pieces belong to the trajectory of the same particle.

(4)

The collision/overlap corresponding to an atom in layer
\ell
must occur in the time interval
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
.

For a summary of correspondence of concepts in molecules and physical trajectories, see
Table
3
. In
Figure
5
, the overlap in
[\tau,2\tau]
of the red and green particles corresponds to the O-atom on layer
2
, and the collision in
[0,\tau]
of the red and green particles corresponds to the C-atom on layer
1
.

Figure 5.
An illustration of topological reduction.

Remark 3.9
.

The topological reduction
\mathbb{M}
of a given trajectory
\bm{z}(t)
may not be unique, since we do not require a bijective correspondence between O-atoms and the actual set of overlaps. The O-atoms may represent only an arbitrary subset of all overlaps. However there is always a
unique
topological reduction that is C-molecule (i.e. contains only C-atoms).

3.3.
Clusters

As seen in Definition
3.8
, O-atoms correspond to overlaps that do not affect the dynamics. Ignoring all such overlaps leads to the definition of clusters.

Definition 3.10

(Create and delete an O-atom)
.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
, we define the following operations, see
Figure
6
. The particle lines and ov-segments will not be broken after these operations.

Figure 6.
Creating and deleting O-atoms as in Definition
3.10
. Note that each atom
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
can be either C- or O-atoms, or absent.

(1)

Delete an O-atom.
Let
\mathfrak{o}
be an O-atom with 4 edges
e_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq 4)
connected to other atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
, where
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
are serial and same for
e_{2}
and
e_{4}
. By
deleting
the O-atom
\mathfrak{o}
, we remove
\mathfrak{o}
and the 4 edges
e_{j}
from
\mathbb{M}
, and add two bonds
e_{1}^{\prime}
between
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{3}
, and
e_{2}^{\prime}
between
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{4}
, with the same top/bottom assignment as
(e_{1},e_{3})
and
(e_{2},e_{4})
. Here if some
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
is absent, say
e_{3}
is a free end at
\mathfrak{o}
, then the bond
(e_{1},e_{3})
should be replaced by a free end at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
, etc.

(2)

Create an O-atom.
Given two edges
e_{1}^{\prime}
between
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{3}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
between
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{4}
, we can
create
an O-atom
\mathfrak{o}
by reverting the above process: add a new O-atom
\mathfrak{o}
, remove
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
, add the bonds
e_{j}
between
\mathfrak{o}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
with the same top/bottom assignment as
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
, and make
e_{1}
serial with
e_{3}
and
e_{2}
serial with
e_{4}
. We also refer to this as
joining

e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
by
\mathfrak{o}
.

Definition 3.11

(Clusters)
.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
, we obtain a new molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
by deleting all O-atoms from
\mathbb{M}
as in Definition
3.10
, leaving only C-atoms. The connected components
(\mathbb{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{M}_{k})
of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
are called the
clusters
of
\mathbb{M}
. We say that an O-atom
\mathfrak{o}

joins

\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
if the two particle lines at
\mathfrak{o}
connects the C-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{j}
and in
\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
respectively. We also define the auxiliary
cluster graph
to be the graph with nodes being clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, and an edge is drawn between
\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
for each O-atom joining them. See
Figure
7
for an illustration.

Figure 7.
An example of clusters and O-atoms joining them as in Definition
3.11
, and the corresponding cluster graph. Here we have 3 clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, with
(\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathbb{M}_{2})
and
(\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathbb{M}_{3})
each joined by an O-atom.

Definition 3.12

(C-molecule)
.

A molecule consists of only C-atoms are referred to as
C-molecules
. Clusters are always C-molecules.

Definition 3.13

(Recollision number)
.

Given a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, we define its recollision number by
\rho(A)=\#_{\mathrm{bond}(A)}-|A|+\#_{\mathrm{comp}(A)}
, where
\#_{\mathrm{bond}(A)}
and
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(A)}
are respectively the number of bonds between atoms in
A
, and the number of connected components of
A
. We say a set
A
is
recollisional
(or equivalently
cyclic
) if
\rho(A)>0
.

Remark 3.14
.

Recollision number is an important notion and will be used frequently later. It equals the
circuit rank
which is the
number of (independent) cycles
in
A
. It is easy to see that
\rho(A)\geq 0
for any
A
.

3.4.
Molecule prescribed dynamics

In Proposition
3.25
, the quantity
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
basically represents an integral over the non-root particle variables, under the restriction that the particle trajectories under certain dynamics topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}
. In order to exclude the possible effects of other collisions, we need to introduce the molecule prescribed dynamics (Definition
3.17
), which ignores any possible collision that does not come from the molecule. This is an example of general modified dynamics, which we introduce below.

Definition 3.15

(Modified dynamics)
.

We define the
modified dynamics
, which is governed by a given property
\mathtt{Pro}
, as follows.

(1)

Governing property.

\mathtt{Pro}
is a property, which can be expressed in terms of an admissible trajectory
\bm{\gamma}
and two particles
{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
. This means that, given any admissible trajectory
\bm{\gamma}
and particles
{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, we can uniquely determine whether
\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})
equals
\mathtt{True}
or
\mathtt{False}
.

(2)

Modified dynamics.
Given initial configuration
\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}_{N}^{0}
, we define the dynamics
\bm{z}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)=\bm{z}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t,\bm{z}^{0})
governed by
\mathtt{Pro}
, as follows:

(a)

We have
\bm{z}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(0)=\bm{z}^{0}
.

(b)

Suppose
\bm{z}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t^{\prime})
is known for
t^{\prime}\in[0,t]
. Let
\bm{\gamma}
be the admissible trajectory
\bm{z}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t^{\prime})\,(t^{\prime}\in[0,t])
. If two particles satisfy the pre-collisional condition (Definition
3.7
(
2
)) at time
t
,
and also
\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})=\mathtt{True}
for the molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
and the particle lines
{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
corresponding to these two particles, then they collide as in (
1.2
).

(c)

If a particle does not satisfy (
2b
) with any other particle, then it does not have any collision and moves linearly by (
1.3
).

(3)

Flow map and flow operator.
We define the
flow map

\mathcal{H}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)=\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)
by

(3.3)

\mathcal{H}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t):\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN},\quad\mathcal{H}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})=\bm{z}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)

where
\bm{z}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)=\bm{z}_{N}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)
is defined in (
2
). The mapping
\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)
need not be injective; however, we can still define the
flow operator

\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)
by

(3.4)

\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)f(\bm{z})=\sum_{\bm{z}^{0}:\mathcal{H}^{\mathtt{Pro}}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})=\bm{z}}f(\bm{z}^{0}).

(4)

Collision and overlap.
The notions of
collisions
and
overlaps
for any trajectory of modified dynamics, are defined as in Definition
3.7
.

(5)

Label of particles.
In this definition, we assume that particles are labeled by
[N]=\{1,\cdots,N\}
(see the convention in Definition
1.6
(
4
)). For a general labeling set
\lambda
, the formulation remains the same, except that the subscript
N
in expressions like
\bm{z}_{N}(t)
,
\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)
,
\mathcal{S}_{N}(t)
etc. is replaced by
\lambda
.

Remark 3.16
.

We make a few remarks regarding Definition
3.15
. First, up to Lebesgue zero sets, the modified dynamics is well-defined just as Definition
1.1
, and produces admissible trajectories in Definition
3.7
. Also, we allow
|x_{i}-x_{j}|<\varepsilon
and do not restrict to
\mathcal{D}_{N}
, and only allow collisions under pre-collisional condition.

Second, the modified dynamics can be described as “collision
\Leftrightarrow
pre-collisional and property
\mathtt{Pro}
holds”, in particular the dynamics is completely determined by the condition
\mathtt{Pro}
. Examples of this
\mathtt{Pro}
include the molecule prescribed dynamics (Definition
3.17
), extended dynamics (Definition
4.1
) and truncated dynamics (Definition
4.3
).

Definition 3.17

(Molecule prescribed dynamics)
.

Given a C-molecule
\mathbb{M}
, the dynamics
prescribed by
\mathbb{M}
is a modified dynamics as in Definition
3.15
, with particle set being
p(\mathbb{M})
, and governing property given by

\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})=\mathtt{True}\Leftrightarrow\textrm{$\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{M}$, and ${\bm{p}}$ and ${\bm{p}}^{\prime}$ intersects at a C-atom $\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{M}^{\prime}$, and $S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{\prime}$}.

Here
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is the unique C-molecule which is topological reduction
6
6

6

In this topological reduction, the requirement in Definition
3.8
(
4
) will be adjusted accordingly: when
t\in[\ell\tau,(\ell+1)\tau]
then
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
will have
\ell
layers, and those collisions and overlaps corresponding to an atom in layer
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}
must occur in
[(\ell^{\prime}-1)\tau,\ell^{\prime}\tau]
if
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
, and in
[(\ell-1)\tau,t]
if
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
.
of
\bm{\gamma}
as in Definition
3.8
(the uniqueness is guaranteed by Remark
3.9
),
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
(Definition
3.5
), and
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
are viewed as particle lines in
\mathbb{M}
. See
Figure
8
.

If
\mathbb{M}
contains O-atoms, the the dynamics prescribed by
\mathbb{M}
is defined to be the dynamics prescribed by
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
, which is the C-molecule formed by deleting all the O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
.

Figure 8.
Left: a C-molecule
\mathbb{M}
. Middle and right: two examples of the
\mathbb{M}
-prescribed dynamics. In both cases, the pre-collisional configuration between
{\bm{p}}_{2}
and
{\bm{p}}_{3}
results in a collision
C_{2}
corresponding to the C-atom
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
. In the middle picture we have another collision
C_{1}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
; however, in the right picture, the pre-collisional configuration of
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{3}
does not allow for a collision, as it does not fit in
\mathbb{M}
(according to
\mathbb{M}
, the
{\bm{p}}_{1}
should collide with
{\bm{p}}_{2}
and not
{\bm{p}}_{3}
).

3.5.
Statement of the cumulant formula

In this section, we provide a rigorous statement of the cumulant formulas (
3.1
)–(
3.2
), see Proposition
3.25
. We start with the definition of the sets
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
and
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, of particles and crossings in each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule as in Definition
3.1
with
\ell
layers. For
\ell^{\prime}\in[\underline{\ell}:\ell]
, define the sets

(3.5)

\displaystyle p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\bm{p}}:\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]\},

(3.6)

\displaystyle r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\bm{p}}:\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]\leq\ell^{\prime}<\ell^{\prime}+1\leq\ell_{2}[{\bm{p}}]\}.

We refer to the particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
as the
root particles of layer
\ell^{\prime}
. We refer to
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
as the set of particle lines
occurring in layer
\ell^{\prime}
, and
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
as the set of particle lines
crossing into layer
\ell^{\prime}
, see
Figure
3
where
r(\mathbb{M}_{2})=\{1\}
and
r(\mathbb{M}_{1})=\{1,2,3\}
(assume that
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
are labeled by
1
,
2
,
3
). Define also
|\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|_{p}=|p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|
and
|\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|_{r}=|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|
etc. similar to Definition
3.6
.

Before proceeding, we need one more notion of two particle lines being
connected
via a set (including a layer). This is needed in the properties of molecules
\mathbb{M}
in Definition
3.20
.

Definition 3.19
.

Let
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}
be two particle lines, and
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be a set. We say:

•

{\bm{p}}_{1}

intersects

A
, if an atom exists that belongs to both
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
A
.

•

{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}
are
connected via
A
, if
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}
intersect the same component of
A
;

•

{\bm{p}}_{1}
is
connected to a cycle within
A
, if
{\bm{p}}_{1}
intersects a component of
A
that contains a cycle;

•

{\bm{p}}_{1}

forms an initial link within
A
, if there exists a particle line
{\bm{p}}_{1}^{\prime}
which either equals
{\bm{p}}_{1}
or is connected to
{\bm{p}}_{1}
via
A
, such that the bottom end in
{\bm{p}}_{1}^{\prime}
is involved in an initial link.

Now we can define the objects needed in Proposition
3.25
. First is the set
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
in (
3.2
), as well as related sets
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
occurring in the definition of
f^{\mathcal{A}}
etc.

Definition 3.20

(Set of molecules
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}
)
.

Given
\ell\leq\mathfrak{L}
,
\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}:=(\Lambda_{\ell^{\prime}})_{\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell}
and
\Gamma
, we define the following sets of molecules. Recall the notions in Definition
3.1
and the sets
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
and
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
in Definition
3.18
.

(1)

The set
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
.
Define
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
to be the
set of equivalence class

[\mathbb{M}]
of labeled molecules
\mathbb{M}
that satisfy the following properties:

(a)

\mathbb{M}
has lowest layer
\underline{\ell}=1
and highest layer
\ell
, with
|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|\leq A_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell^{\prime}\in[1:\ell]
. Each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
contains
<\Lambda_{\ell^{\prime}}
clusters (see Definition
3.11
), and for each cluster
\mathbb{M}_{j}
we have
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|_{p}\leq\Lambda_{\ell^{\prime}}
and
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
(Definitions
3.6
and
3.13
).

(b)

For each
\ell^{\prime}\in[1:\ell]
, each particle line in
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
is either connected to a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, or belongs to
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. Each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
contains a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. See
Figure
9
.

(c)

The cluster graph (Definition
3.11
) of each
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is a forest. The initial link graph, formed by initial link conditions between particle lines
{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M}_{1})
, is also a forest.

(d)

For
\ell^{\prime}\in[2:\ell]
, each particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
is either connected to another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, or connected to a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(in the sense of Definition
3.19
), or belongs to
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
. For
\ell^{\prime}=1
, the set
r(\mathbb{M}_{0})
in the above definition should be replaced by the set of particle lines that are involved in an initial link. See
Figure
9
.

(2)

The set
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
is the set of equivalence classes
[\mathbb{M}]
as in (
1
) that satisfy conditions (
1a
)–(
1d
), except that (
1d
) is assumed only for
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1
. Moreover, for
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
, instead of (
1a
) we assume that each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
contains
\leq\Lambda_{\ell}
clusters and there exists a unique component that contains
exactly

\Lambda_{\ell}
clusters.

(3)

The set
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
.
Given
\Lambda_{\ell}
(which is a single number), define
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
to be the set of equivalence class
[\mathbb{M}]
of labeled molecules
\mathbb{M}
such that it has only one layer
\ell
(i.e.
\underline{\ell}=\ell
) and no initial link, has only one root particle labeled by
1
, and satisfies (
1a
)–(
1c
).

Define also
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
to be the set of
[\mathbb{M}]
such that
\mathbb{M}
is obtained from a molecule in
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
by joining two edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
with an extra O-atom.

(4)

The set
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{trc.err}}
.
Define
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{trc.err}}
to be the set of equivalence class
[\mathbb{M}]
of labeled molecules
\mathbb{M}
such that it has
\ell
layers and satisfies (
1a
)–(
1d
) for
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1
. Moreover the layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
is connected (apart from possible empty ends which are root particle lines) and has only C-atoms, and satisfies that

(3.7)

|\mathbb{M}_{\ell}|_{p}\leq 3\Lambda_{\ell},\ \mathrm{and}\ \rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})\leq 2\Gamma;\qquad\mathrm{moreover,\ either\ }|\mathbb{M}_{\ell}|_{p}>\Lambda_{\ell}\mathrm{\ or\ }\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})>\Gamma.

Figure 9.
Examples of the
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
and
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
sets in Definition
3.18
. The red edges belong to those particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{3})
, and intuitively represent that they cross into layer
\mathbb{M}_{3}
; same for blue and green edges
(corresponding to
r(\mathbb{M}_{2})
and
r(\mathbb{M}_{1})
; note that different
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
may have common particle lines). The
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
consists of those particle lines that are present in layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(including those in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
). In this picture, Definition
3.20
(
1b
) and (
1d
), and Proposition
10.4
(
7
) and (
9
) (without showing initial links), are verified.

Remark 3.21
.

We briefly explain the ideas behind the items in Definition
3.20
. Here (
1a
) comes from the truncation of dynamics (Definition
4.3
) and the truncation in the Penrose argument, and (
1c
) also comes from the construction in the Penrose argument. Next, (
1b
) follows from the fact that we only expand the particles that are connected to root particles, and (
1d
) follows from the condition for the cumulant
E_{H}
. The other conditions (
2
)–(
4
) are similar but with modifications associated with single layer or error terms.

Next, we define the integral expression
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
in (
3.2
), and its signed variant
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
, in Definitions
3.22
–
3.23
.

Definition 3.22

(Associated transport operators)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule. We define the following objects:

(1)

Prescribed dynamics and flow operators.
We use
\bm{z}_{\mathbb{M}}(t)
to denote the
\mathbb{M}
-prescribed dynamics in Definition
3.17
, with
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{M}}(t)
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}(t)
etc. defined accordingly as in Definition
3.15
. Note that the subscript
N
is omitted, as
N=|\mathbb{M}|_{p}
can be inferred from
\mathbb{M}
.

(2)

Indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
.
We define the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}(\bm{z}^{0})
such that

(3.8)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}(\bm{z}^{0})=1\Leftrightarrow\textrm{the trajectory $\bm{z}_{\mathbb{M}}(t)$, with initial configuration $\bm{z}^{0}$, topologically reduces to $\mathbb{M}$.}

Define also the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
, associated with the initial link structure
\mathcal{L}
, by

(3.9)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}=\prod_{(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathcal{L}}\mathbbm{1}_{e_{1}\sim_{\mathrm{in}}e_{2}}^{\varepsilon}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\mathbbm{1}_{e_{1}\sim_{\mathrm{in}}e_{2}}^{\varepsilon}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1\quad&&|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{2}}|\leq\varepsilon,\\
&\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad&&|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{2}}|\geq\varepsilon,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\upsilon=3^{-d-1}
(Definition
1.6
(
3
)).

(3)

The operator
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|
.
Define the
associated transport operators

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|
by

(3.10)

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}=(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}((\ell-\underline{\ell}+1)\tau)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}\quad\text{and}\quad|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}((\ell-\underline{\ell}+1)\tau)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}},

where
\ell
is the highest layer and
\underline{\ell}
is the lowest layer of
\mathbb{M}
, and recall
|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}
is the number of O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
, see Definition
3.6
.

Definition 3.23

(Associated integrals
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule, let the lowest layer of
\mathbb{M}
be
\underline{\ell}
. Define the
associated integrals

\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
as follows:

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}}(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}\,Q\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})},

(3.12)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}}|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|\,|Q|\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})},

where
\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})}:=(\bm{z}_{\bm{p}})_{{\bm{p}}\in(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})}
, and

(3.13)

Q:=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\prod_{{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M})}f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau,x_{\bm{p}}^{\prime}+(\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-\underline{\ell})\tau\cdot v_{\bm{p}}^{\prime},v_{\bm{p}}^{\prime}).

Remark 3.24
.

We make a few remarks on Definitions
3.22
–
3.23
.

(1)

The time
(\ell-\underline{\ell}+1)\tau
is because
\mathbb{M}
has
\ell-\underline{\ell}+1
layers, and each layer corresponding to evolution of time
\tau
. Note that, in the support of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
, the
\mathbb{M}
-prescribed dynamics contains exactly the collisions in
\mathbb{M}
. Thus
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
is given by a fixed composition of explicitly defined changes of variables of form (
1.2
).

(2)

In (
3.11
)–(
3.12
) we have two sets of variables, namely
z_{\bm{p}}
and
z_{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
for each particle
{\bm{p}}
. They represent the states of particle
{\bm{p}}
at the final time
\ell\tau
and initial time
(\underline{\ell}-1)\tau
respectively, in the
\mathbb{M}
-prescribed dynamics. In particular they are related by the explicit change of variables in
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
as in (
1
) (under the support of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
contained in
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
).

(3)

The integrals
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
in (
3.11
)–(
3.12
) represent the probability density under the assumption that all collisions and overlaps in
\mathbb{M}
(and initial links) occur as expected. This is clear from the transport operator and indicator function in
\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1}
as described above. The integral in all
non-root
particles
\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})}
leaves the density as a function of the root particles
\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})}
.

Finally, the input function
Q
in (
3.13
) contains the initial link/initial cumulant factor
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
. It also contains the
f^{\mathcal{A}}
factors (approximate Boltzmann solution) for each particle
{\bm{p}}
, at time
(\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau
corresponding to the start layer
\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]
. This is the first time
{\bm{p}}
becomes relevant in the dynamics prescribed by
\mathbb{M}
. Note the linear transport
x_{\bm{p}}^{\prime}+(\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-\underline{\ell})\tau\cdot v_{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
in (
3.13
); this is due to the fact that
f^{\mathcal{A}}
is measures at time
(\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-1)\tau
, while
x_{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
represents the state of
{\bm{p}}
at time
(\underline{\ell}-1)\tau
.

Now, we are finally ready to state the main cumulant expansion formula in this section, in Proposition
3.25
below.

Proposition 3.25
.

Recall
\Lambda_{\ell}
and
A_{\ell}
defined in (
1.32
), and
t_{\mathrm{fin}}=\mathfrak{L}\tau
. Assume the initial density function
W_{0,N}(\bm{z}_{N})
is given by (
1.7
). Then we can define suitable quantities
\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})
(for all
\ell\in[\mathfrak{L}]
and
s\leq A_{\ell}
) and
f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s})
(for
s\leq A_{\mathfrak{L}}
), see (
4.17
)–(
4.18
), such that we have the decomposition

(3.14)

f_{s}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s})=\widetilde{f}_{s}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s})+f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s}),

and for each
\ell\in[0:\mathfrak{L}]
and
s\leq A_{\ell}
, we have

(3.15)

\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})=\sum_{H\subseteq[s]}(f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau))^{\otimes([s]\backslash H)}\cdot E_{H}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H})+\mathrm{Err}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s}).

In (
3.14
)–(
3.15
) we have the following estimates:

(1)

(f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau))^{\otimes([s]\backslash H)}
is a function of
\bm{z}_{[s]\backslash H}
, which is the tensor product of the single variable function
f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau,z)
. This function is defined inductively in
\ell
by
f^{\mathcal{A}}(0)=f_{0}
and

(3.16)

f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau,z)=\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}(z)+f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{err}}(\ell\tau,z),\quad|f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{err}}(\ell\tau,z)|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(z).

(2)

The cumulant
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
is bounded by (where
\ell\geq 1
, for
\ell=0
see Proposition
5.18
)

(3.17)

|E_{H}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H})|\leq\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}},r(\mathbb{M})=H}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{H}).

(3)

We have
\mathrm{Err}=\mathrm{Err}^{1}+\mathrm{Err}^{2}
(where
\ell\geq 1
, for
\ell=0
we have
\mathrm{Err}(0)=0
), and

(3.18)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}^{1}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}},r(\mathbb{M})=[s]}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{s}),

(3.19)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Err}^{2}(\ell\tau)\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-2(d-1)\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}A_{\ell}}\cdot\|\mathrm{Err}((\ell-1)\tau)\|_{L^{1}}.

(4)

The truncation error
f^{\mathrm{err}}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s})
in (
3.14
) satisfies

(3.20)

\|f^{\mathrm{err}}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s})\|_{L^{1}}\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}\bigg(\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{trc.err}},r(\mathbb{M})=[s]}\big\||\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(\bm{z}_{s})\big\|_{L^{1}}+\varepsilon^{-8(d-1)\Lambda_{\ell}}\cdot\|\mathrm{Err}((\ell-1)\tau)\|_{L^{1}}\bigg).

Proof.

The proof will occupy Section
4
–
5
, and will be finished in Section
5.6
.
∎

Remark 3.26
.

The quantity
\widetilde{f}_{s}
is defined by the truncated dynamics, see Section
4
. Note that the equation (
3.16
) defines a recurrence relation for
f^{\mathcal{A}}
, because both
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
are expressed as integrals involving products of
f^{\mathcal{A}}
. These expressions are also constant on equivalence classes
[\mathbb{M}]
.

The summation (
3.17
) (and similarly (
3.18
) and (
3.20
)) involves the condition
r(\mathbb{M})=H
. Fixing the labels of these root particles may lead to an additional
|H|!
factor, which is usually harmless (but requires some extra discussion in Section
15
). Apart from this, the equivalence classes
[\mathbb{M}]
of labeled molecules
\mathbb{M}
are then the same as unlabeled molecules, see Remark
3.4
.

4.
Truncation of dynamics

We now start the proof of Proposition
3.25
. In this section we will define the truncated dynamics and establish the decomposition (
3.14
). This is needed in order to control the number of particles and recollisions in each cluster in each time layer, that occur in the molecule expansion (for example in Definition
3.20
(
1a
), the number of particles in layer
\ell^{\prime}
is bounded by
\Lambda_{\ell^{\prime}}^{2}A_{\ell^{\prime}}
).

Recall from (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) that
f(t,\bm{z}_{s})
is given by

(4.1)

f(t,\bm{z}_{s})=\varepsilon^{(d-1)s}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int(\mathcal{S}_{s+n}(t)W_{0,s+n})(\bm{z}_{s+n})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{[s+1:s+n]},

so the correlation
f(t,\bm{z}_{s})
is determined by the collisions in the dynamics
\mathcal{S}_{s+n}(t)
, for which we will define the truncation.

4.1.
The extended and truncated dynamics

In this subsection, we define the extended and truncated dynamics as two instances of modified dynamics in Definition
3.15
.

Definition 4.1

(The extended dynamics)
.

We define the
extended dynamics
(or
E-dynamics
), denoted by
\bm{z}^{E}(t)
, to be the modified dynamics in Definition
3.15
, with the governing property
\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})\equiv\mathtt{True}
.

Remark 4.2
.

In the E-dynamics, we have a collision whenever the pre-collisional condition is satisfied. In particular, if the distance of any two particles becomes
\geq\varepsilon
, then this separation is preserved thereafter.

The E-dynamics is a natural extension of the original dynamics (Definition
1.1
) from
\mathcal{D}_{N}
to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
. If we restrict the initial data of the E-dynamics to
\mathcal{D}_{N}
, then the trajectory stays in
\mathcal{D}_{N}
forever, and the E-dynamics coincides with the O-dynamics (see Proposition
4.5
below).

Definition 4.3

(The truncated dynamics)
.

Let
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
be fixed. We define the
truncated dynamics
(or
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-, or
T-dynamics
), denoted by
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
, to be the modified dynamics in Definition
3.15
, with the governing property
\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})
given by

(4.2)

\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})=\mathtt{True}\Leftrightarrow\textrm{either ($\mathbb{M}_{i}=\mathbb{M}_{j}$ and $\rho(\mathbb{M}_{i})\leq\Gamma-1$),}\\
\textrm{or ($\mathbb{M}_{i}\neq\mathbb{M}_{j}$ and $|\mathbb{M}_{i}|+|\mathbb{M}_{j}|\leq\Lambda$ and $\rho(\mathbb{M}_{i})+\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma$)},

where
\mathbb{M}_{i}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{j}
) is the connected components of
\mathbb{M}
in which the particle line
{\bm{p}}
(resp.
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
) occurs, and
\mathbb{M}
is the unique C-molecule which is topological reduction of
\bm{\gamma}
.

Remark 4.4
.

In the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-truncated dynamics, we force the property that each cluster has size
\leq\Lambda
and recollision number
\leq\Gamma
(Proposition
4.5
(
5
)), by neglecting any collisions that may violate this property.

Proposition 4.5
.

The E- and
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics (Definitions
4.1
and
4.3
) satisfy the following properties.

(1)

Up to some Lebesgue zero set of
\bm{z}^{0}
, the E- and T- dynamics in Definitions
4.1
and
4.3
are well-defined and produces admissible trajectories in Definition
3.7
.

(2)

Define
(\mathcal{H}^{E},\mathcal{S}^{E})
and
(\mathcal{H}^{\Lambda,\Gamma},\mathcal{S}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})
as special cases of (
3.3
) and (
3.4
). Then, when the initial data
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
, the E-dynamics coincides with the O-dynamics in Definition
1.1
, i.e.
\bm{z}^{E}(t)=\bm{z}(t)
and
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})=\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})
. Moreover, we have
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)f=\mathcal{S}_{N}(t)f
if
f
is supported in
\mathcal{D}_{N}
.

(3)

The
\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N}(t)
and
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)
defined in (
2
) satisfy the following flow or semi-group property for
t,s\geq 0
:

(4.3)

\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t+s)=\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(s),\qquad\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t+s)=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(s).

In general, the
\mathcal{H}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{N}(t)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{N}(t)
might not satisfy the flow property.

(4)

Both transport operators
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{N}(t)
preserve the
L^{1}
integral:

(4.4)

\int(\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)f)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v=\int(\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)f)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v=\int f\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v.

(5)

In the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics, suppose a trajectory topologically reduces to a C-molecule
\mathbb{M}
. Then each connected component
\mathbb{M}_{j}
of
\mathbb{M}
satisfies
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|_{p}\leq\Lambda
and
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
, (recall the notions of) Moreover, for O-, E- and
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics, the number of collisions
|\mathbb{M}|
has an absolute upper bound that depends only on
d
and
N
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

4.2.
The truncated domain

In this subsection, we introduce the domain
\mathcal{D}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{N}
, which characterizes the initial data such that the truncated dynamics coincide with the extended dynamics.

Definition 4.6

(The truncated and error domain
\mathcal{D}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{N}
and
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
)
.

Given
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
, we define the
truncated domain

\mathcal{D}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{N}
as follows:

(4.5)

\mathcal{D}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{N}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{N}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{k}\}\in\mathcal{P}^{k}_{N}\\
|\lambda_{j}|\leq\Lambda\end{subarray}}\bigg\{\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}:\textrm{$p(\mathbb{M}_{j})=\lambda_{j}$ and $\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma$ for each $1\leq j\leq k$}\bigg\},

where
\mathbb{M}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq k)
are the components of the (unique) C-molecule topological reduction of the admissible trajectory
\bm{z}^{E}(t)\,(t\in[0,\tau])
with initial configuration
\bm{z}^{0}
. We also refer to these
\mathbb{M}_{j}
as
clusters
.

We also define the
error domain

\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
as

(4.6)

\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}:=\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\backslash\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}.

Their indicator functions are denoted by
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}}
, and we have
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}}=1-\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}}
.

The following proposition ensures that the E- and
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics coincide on
\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

For each
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
and
t\in[0,\tau]
we have
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})=\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})
. Moreover, if
f
is supported in
\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
, then we have
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)f=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)f
. For general
f
we have

(4.7)

\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}}\cdot f\big)=\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(t)\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}}\cdot f\big).

Proof.

Suppose
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
. By definition of
\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
, in the whole E-dynamics
\bm{z}_{N}^{E}(t)
for
t\in[0,\tau]
, the size
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|
of each cluster
\mathbb{M}_{j}
(under the E-dynamics) never exceeds
\Lambda
, and the recollision number
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})
never exceeds
\Gamma
. Therefore, whenever a collision happens in the E-dynamics, we must also have a collision in the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics. This implies that the E-dynamics
\bm{z}_{N}^{E}(t)
and
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics
\bm{z}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
have exactly the same collisions, and thus coincide because they have the same initial data.

Now assume
f
is supported in
\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
, then

(4.8)

(\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)f)(\bm{z})=\sum_{\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}:\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})=\bm{z}}f(\bm{z}^{0})=\sum_{\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}:\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N}(t)(\bm{z}^{0})=\bm{z}}f(\bm{z}^{0})=(\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)f)(\bm{z}).

Then (
4.7
) follows because
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}}\cdot f
is supported in
\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
.
∎

4.3.
The truncated correlation functions

In this subsection we establish the decomposition (
3.14
) by applying the
(\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma)
-truncated dynamics on each time interval
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
.

Recall the
\mathfrak{L}
and
\tau
defined in Definition
1.6
(
1
). By the semi-group property of
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)
(Proposition
4.5
(
3
)), we have
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})=(\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau))^{\mathfrak{L}}
. Moreover, since
W_{0,N}
is supported in
\mathcal{D}_{N}
(see (
1.7
)), by Proposition
4.5
(
2
), we have
\mathcal{S}_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})W_{0,N}=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})W_{0,N}
. Combining the above two facts, we get

(4.9)

W_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})=\mathcal{S}_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})W_{0,N}=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})W_{0,N}=\big((\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau))^{\mathfrak{L}}\big)W_{0,N}.

Recall the sequence
(A_{\ell})
and
(\Lambda_{\ell})
defined in (
1.32
) and
\Gamma
introduced in Definition
1.6
. We then define
\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\ell}:=\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\ell}:=\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma}
. By definitions of the two indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\ell}}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\ell}}
(Definition
4.6
), we have

(4.10)

1=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\ell}}+\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\ell}}.

Define
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\ell}:=\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}:=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma}
, which are given by the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics with
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\ell}
. By Proposition
4.7
, we know that
\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\ell}}=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\ell}}
which implies that
(\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(\tau)-\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau))\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\ell}}=0
. We then have

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau)+(\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau)-\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau))\circ(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{N}^{\ell}}+\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\ell}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau)+(\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau)-\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau))\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\ell}}=\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau)+\mathcal{S}_{\ell,N}^{\mathrm{err}}(\tau),

where we define the operator
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{err}}_{\ell,N}(\tau)
as

(4.12)
