\mathcal{S}_{\ell,N}^{\mathrm{err}}(\tau):=(\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau)-\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau))\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}^{\ell}}.

Now, by plugging (
4.11
) into (
4.9
), we get

(4.13)

\begin{split}W_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})&=\big(\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(\tau)\big)^{\mathfrak{L}}W_{0,N}\\
&=\bigg(\prod_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau)\bigg)W_{0,N}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}\bigg[(\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau))^{\mathfrak{L}-\ell+1}\prod_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\tau)-(\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau))^{\mathfrak{L}-\ell}\prod_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\tau)\bigg]W_{0,N}\\
&=\bigg(\prod_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell}(\tau)\bigg)W_{0,N}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}\bigg((\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau))^{\mathfrak{L}-\ell}\circ\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{err}}_{\ell,N}(\tau)\circ\prod_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\tau)\bigg)W_{0,N}.\end{split}

Here in the products
\prod_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}
the factors with
larger
\ell
should appear on the left
. For each
\ell
, we define

(4.14)

\widetilde{W}_{N}(\ell\tau)=\bigg(\prod_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{S}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{N}(\tau)\bigg)W_{0,N},

then (
4.13
) we be written as
W_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})=\widetilde{W}_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})+W_{N}^{\mathrm{err}}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})
, where

(4.15)

\widetilde{W}_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})=\widetilde{W}_{N}(\mathfrak{L}\tau),

(4.16)

W^{\mathrm{err}}_{N}(t_{\mathrm{fin}})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mathfrak{L}}\bigg((\mathcal{S}^{E}_{N}(\tau))^{\mathfrak{L}-\ell}\circ\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{err}}_{\ell,N}(\tau)\bigg)\widetilde{W}_{N}((\ell-1)\tau).

Definition 4.8

(Truncated correlation function)
.

We define the
truncated correlation function

\widetilde{f}_{s}
, and the
error correlation function

f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}
, by

(4.17)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{s(d-1)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dn}}\widetilde{W}_{s+n}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s+n})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{[s+1:s+n]},

(4.18)

\displaystyle f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{s(d-1)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dn}}W^{\mathrm{err}}_{s+n}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s+n})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{[s+1:s+n]},

where
\widetilde{W}_{s+n}
and
W_{s+n}^{\mathrm{err}}
are defined in (
4.14
) and (
4.16
). This completes the proof of (
3.14
).

5.
Proof of the cumulant formula

We already have (
3.14
), with
\widetilde{f}_{s}
and
f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}
defined in (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) respectively. To complete Proposition
3.25
, in this section, we prove the upper bound for
f_{s}^{\mathrm{err}}(t_{\mathrm{fin}},\bm{z}_{s})
in (
3.20
), and the cumulant expansion formula (
3.15
) for
\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau)
, with bounds (
3.16
)–(
3.19
). This is done in several steps.

Recall that the definition of
\widetilde{W}_{N}
and
\widetilde{f}_{s}
involves the transport operator
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda_{\ell^{\prime}},\Gamma}(\tau)
defined by the truncated dynamics. In Sections
5.1
–
5.2
we will prove a crucial decomposition formula for this operator, namely Proposition
5.10
. Then in Sections
5.3
–
5.4
, we will use this decomposition to prove Proposition
5.16
. This is the key inductive step, which establishes the cumulant decomposition (
3.15
) at time
\ell\tau
provided the same expansion holds at time
(\ell-1)\tau
, and expresses the cumulant
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
in terms of
E_{H}((\ell-1)\tau)
and a molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
with single layer
\ell
. Then in Sections
5.5
–
5.6
, by induction in
\ell
using Proposition
5.16
, and using the initial cumulant formula (time
t=0
, see Proposition
5.18
), we complete the proof of Proposition
3.25
.

5.1.
Cluster expansion

In this section, we perform the cluster expansion for the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-truncated dynamics and prove Lemma
5.5
by introducing the C-molecules, which allow us to write the transport operator
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
as a summation of tensor products
\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t)
associated with dynamics prescribed by clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
.

Definition 5.1

(Partial dynamics)
.

Recall
\bm{z}=\bm{z}_{N}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{N})
as in Definition
1.6
(
4
). Let
\lambda\subseteq[N]
and
\bm{z}_{\lambda}=(z_{j})_{j\in\lambda}
. Given initial configuration
\bm{z}(0)=\bm{z}^{0}
, let
\bm{z}(t)
be a modified dynamics (for example
\bm{z}^{E}(t)
or
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
), then we can define the following notions.

(1)

The partial dynamics
\bm{z}_{\lambda}(t)
.
Consider
\lambda\subseteq[N]
as a set of particles. We define the
partial dynamics

\bm{z}_{\lambda}(t)
to be the dynamics following the same evolution rule but only taking into account particles in
\lambda
, and with initial configuration
\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0}=(z_{j}^{0})_{j\in\lambda}
. When
\lambda=[N]
, we refer to it as the
full dynamics
.

(2)

The partial flow maps
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(t)
and
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(t)
.
We define the
partial flow maps

\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(t)
by
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(t)(\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0})=\bm{z}_{\lambda}(t)
. It can be extended to
\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}^{0}_{N}
by
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(t)\bm{z}^{0}=\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(t)(\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0},\bm{z}^{0}_{\lambda^{c}})=(\bm{z}_{\lambda}(t),\bm{z}^{0}_{\lambda^{c}})
. We define the
partial flow operators

\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(t)
as follows,

(5.1)

\begin{gathered}(\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(t)f)(\bm{z}_{\lambda})=\sum_{\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0}:\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(t)(\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0})=\bm{z}_{\lambda}}f(\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0}),\\
(\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(t)f)(\bm{z})=(\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(t)f)(\bm{z}_{\lambda},\bm{z}_{\lambda^{c}})=\sum_{\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0}:\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(t)(\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0})=\bm{z}_{\lambda}}f(\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{0},\,\bm{z}_{\lambda^{c}})\end{gathered}

Finally, when
\lambda=[s]
, we will denote
\bm{z}_{\lambda}=\bm{z}_{s}
, and similarly
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{H}_{s}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{S}_{s}
.

(3)

Composition of partial dynamics.
Given disjoint subsets
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{k})
, the
composition
of the partial dynamics of these subsets is the dynamics specified by the flow map
\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda_{j}}(t)
.

Remark 5.2
.

Note that in general
\bm{z}_{\lambda}(t)\neq(\bm{z}_{N}(t))_{\lambda}
, where the left hand side is defined by the partial dynamics, and the right hand side is defined by selecting the particles in
\lambda
from the full dynamics. This is due to the possible collisions between particles in
\lambda
and
\lambda^{c}
.

Note also that, in the composition of partial dynamics of
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k})
, collisions occur
only
between two particles in the same subset
\lambda_{j}
; any pre-collisional configuration between two particles in different subsets
\lambda_{j}
are always ignored.

Definition 5.3

(Interaction of partial dynamics)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
be
C-molecules
with only one layer
\ell
, and particle sets
p(\mathbb{M})
and
p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
respectively. Let
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
be the truncated dynamics on
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
and
\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}|_{t=(\ell-1)\tau}
be the initial configuration (with particle set being
p(\mathbb{M})
or
p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
or
p(\mathbb{M})\cup p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
).

(1)

The indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}
.
We define the
indicator function
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}(\bm{z}^{0})
to be
1
if and only if the trajectory
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}
, and
0
otherwise. Note that this is different
7
7

7

Here
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
only requires all collisions in
\mathbb{M}
happen as expected, while
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}
additionally prohibits other pre-collisional configurations not in
\mathbb{M}
.
from
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
in Definition
3.22
(
2
), as the underlying dynamics is
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
instead of
\bm{z}_{\mathbb{M}}(t)
.

(2)

The indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}\not\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
.
We write
\mathbb{M}\not\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
if and only if in the trajectory
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})\cup p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
, there is no collisions between any atom in
p(\mathbb{M})
and any atom in
p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
. Define the
indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}\not\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(\bm{z}^{0})
to be
1
if
\mathbb{M}\not\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, and
0
otherwise.

(3)

The indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})}
.
Given two molecules
(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
and two edges
e\in\mathbb{M}
and
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, we write
(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
if any of the following two situations occur.

(a)

No overlap:
under the composition dynamics
\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
, the linear segments in the trajectory corresponding to
e
and
e^{\prime}
never enter a pre-collisional configuration at any time
t_{*}
.

(b)

Truncated overlap:

e
and
e^{\prime}
do enter a pre-collisional configuration under the dynamics
\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
at time
t_{*}
, but
\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})=\mathtt{False}
. Here
{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
are the particles containing edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
respectively,
\bm{\gamma}
is the trajectory of the
\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
dynamics up to time
t_{*}
, and
\mathtt{Pro}
is the condition in the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-truncated dynamics (Definition
4.3
) with particle set
p(\mathbb{M})\cup p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
.

We define the
indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})}(\bm{z}^{0})
to be
1
if
(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
, and
0
otherwise. Note that these objects are well-defined only when the trajectory of particles in
p(\mathbb{M})
and
p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
topologically reduce to
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
.

Remark 5.4
.

We explain the idea behind Definition
5.3
(
3
). The statement
(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
here means that, either the edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
do not overlap, or their overlap
does not cause a collision
in the
\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M})\cup p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
dynamics due to the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-truncation. We have to define it in this way (instead of just saying
e
and
e^{\prime}
do not overlap), in order to ensure that the equality (
5.5
) is true. Note that
(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
is
less restrictive
than saying
e
and
e^{\prime}
do not overlap, therefore the opposite statement
(\mathbb{M},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
is
more restrictive
than saying
e
and
e^{\prime}
do overlap. This implies an
upper bound
for the corresponding indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})}
, which is all we need in all subsequent proofs. We emphasize that below
it is always safe
to treat
(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
in the same way as the natural non-overlapping condition.

Now we state the main result of this subsection, Lemma
5.5
. The central formula here is (
5.6
), which factorizes the transport operator
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
into
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t)
. The product of indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
will be treated in Section
5.2
using the Penrose argument.

Lemma 5.5
.

We have the following decompositions.

(1)

We have

(5.2)

1=\sum_{\mathbb{M}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma},

where the summation is taken over all labeled C-molecules
\mathbb{M}
(of single layer) with
p(\mathbb{M})=[N]
. In this sum we may also assume that each component
\mathbb{M}_{j}
of
\mathbb{M}
satisfies
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|_{p}\leq\Lambda
and
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
.

(2)

If
\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}
are the connected components of
\mathbb{M}
, and
f
is supported on
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
, then we have

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\bigg),

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)f

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t)f.

(3)

In the set
(\mathrm{supp}\,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})\cap(\mathrm{supp}\,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})
, we have

(5.5)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}=\prod_{e\in\mathbb{M}_{j},\,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}

(4)

By putting together (
1
)–(
3
), we get

(5.6)

\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)=\sum_{k\leq N}\sum_{\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\prod_{e\in\mathbb{M}_{j},\,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg).

Here the summation is taken over
\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}
which are connected components of a labeled C-molecule
\mathbb{M}
as in (
1
) (equivalently this can also be taken over
\mathbb{M}
). Note that each
\mathbb{M}_{j}
is a labeled C-molecule itself, and they satisfy that
p(\mathbb{M}_{1})\cup\cdots\cup p(\mathbb{M}_{k})=[N]
and
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|_{p}\leq\Lambda
,
|\chi_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}|\leq\Gamma
.

Before proving Lemma
5.5
, we first state and prove the following Lemma
5.6
, which contains the core parts of Lemma
5.5
, i.e. (
5.3
)–(
5.4
).

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule with connected components
\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}
and
p(\mathbb{M})=[N]
. Then, up to Lebesgue zero sets, the trajectory
\{\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\}_{t\in[0,\tau]}
with initial data
\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(0)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}
, if and only if the following conditions hold:

(1)

For
1\leq j\leq k
, the partial dynamics
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})}(t)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, and

(2)

For
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k
,
\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
in the partial dynamics
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})\cup p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})}(t)
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be the set of initial configurations
\bm{z}^{0}
defined by the above conditions. If
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathfrak{S}
and
f
is supported in
\mathfrak{S}
, then for
t\in[0,\tau]
we have

(5.7)

\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\bm{z}_{N}^{0}=\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t)\bigg)\bm{z}_{N}^{0},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)f=\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t)\bigg)f.

Proof.

We divide the proof into 4 parts. Recall
\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
is the partial dynamics, and
(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda}
is obtained from selecting the particles in
\lambda
from the full dynamics
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
; in general they are not equal.

Proof part 1.
In this part we prove that, if no particle in
\lambda
collides with particle in
\lambda^{c}
in the full dynamics
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
, then we have
(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda}=\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
.

To prove this fact, define

t_{*}=\sup\big\{t:(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda}=\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\big\},

it suffices to show that
t_{*}=\tau
. If
t_{*}<\tau
, by left continuity we have
(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda}=\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
for all
t\leq t_{*}
. Let the C-molecule topological reduction of
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
and
\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
(up to time
t_{*}
) be
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\lambda}
, since no collision occurs between particles in
\lambda
and
\lambda^{c}
, we know that
\mathbb{M}_{\lambda}
is just a connected component of
\mathbb{M}
consisting particle lines in
\lambda
. Consider the possible collision in either
(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda}
or
\bm{z}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
at time
t_{*}
. By Definition
4.3
of the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics, we know that, whether this collision is allowed is determined only by properties of the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\lambda}
, in both cases. As such, the two dynamics must follow the same scattering rule at
t_{*}
. Moreover, up to a zero measure, no other collisions occur for
t>t_{*}
sufficiently close to
t_{*}
. Therefore, the two dynamics remain identical for such
t
, contradicting the maximality of
t_{*}
. This contradiction shows that we must have
t_{*}=\tau
, as desired.

Proof part 2.
In this part, we show that (
1
) and (
2
) hold under the assumption that
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}
. In fact, this assumption implies that no collision happens between any particle in
p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
and any particle in
p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})
in the full dynamics
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
. By Part 1, we know that

\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)=(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})\cup p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)=(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})\cup p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})}.

Clearly the first equation above implies (
1
). The second equation implies that there is also no collision between
p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
and
p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})
in the partial dynamics
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})\cup p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
, which is (
2
).

Proof part 3.
In this part, we show that (
1
) and (
2
) implies that
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}
. Let
\lambda_{j}=p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
and
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}=p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})
. We first show that under (
1
) and (
2
), there is no collision between any
\lambda_{j}
and
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}
in
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
. Assume the contrary, there must exist a first collision time
t_{*}
between some
\lambda_{j}
and
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}
. Let the next collision time in
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
be
t_{*}+\delta
, then in the time interval
[0,t_{*}+\delta]
, there is no collision between particles in
\lambda_{j}\cup\lambda_{j^{\prime}}
and
(\lambda_{j}\cup\lambda_{j^{\prime}})^{c}
. By Part 1, we know that
(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda_{j}\cup\lambda_{j^{\prime}}}=\bm{z}_{\lambda_{j}\cup\lambda_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
for
t\in[0,t_{*}+\delta]
, which is impossible because there is a collision between
\lambda_{j}
and
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}
in
(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda_{j}\cup\lambda_{j^{\prime}}}
while there is no collision between
\lambda_{j}
and
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}
in
\bm{z}_{\lambda_{j}\cup\lambda_{j^{\prime}}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
by (
2
).

Now assume
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, we only need to show
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}
. Since there is no collision between any
\lambda_{j}
and
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}
in
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
, by Part 1 we know that
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)=(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})}
. As
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}_{j}
by (
1
), we know that the components of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
are identical to
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, hence
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}
.

Proof part 4.
Finally we prove (
5.7
). If
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}
with components
\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}
, there is no collision between any
p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
and
p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})
, so
(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j})}=\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
by Part 1. Let
\lambda_{j}=p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
, we may identify
\bm{z}=\bm{z}_{N}
with
(\bm{z}_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,\bm{z}_{\lambda_{k}})
, so we have

(5.8)

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\bm{z}_{N}^{0}&=\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)=\left((\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,(\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t))_{\lambda_{k}}\right)=\left(\bm{z}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t),\cdots,\bm{z}_{\lambda_{k}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\right)\\
&=\left(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda_{1}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\bm{z}^{0}_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,\mathcal{H}_{\lambda_{k}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\bm{z}^{0}_{\lambda_{k}}\right)=\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)\bigg)\bm{z}_{N}^{0}=\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t)\bigg)\bm{z}_{N}^{0}.\end{split}

In the last step, we have applied the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t,\bm{z}^{0})=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(t,\bm{z}^{0})
(where
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
is the molecule prescribed dynamics in Definition
3.17
), which is because the trajectory
\bm{z}_{\lambda_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
topologically reduces to
\mathbb{M}_{j}
. Finally, the
\mathcal{S}
equality in (
5.7
) follows from the
\mathcal{H}
equality, in the same way as (
4.8
). This proves (
5.7
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

All the molecules in this proof are C-molecules. First, the decomposition (
1
) is obvious, because every trajectory topologically reduces to a unique C-molecule (Remark
3.9
). The property that
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|_{p}\leq\Lambda
and
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
follows from the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-truncation. Second, the equalities (
5.3
)–(
5.4
) in (
2
) follow directly from Lemma
5.6
in view of the various indicator functions defined in Definition
5.3
. As (
4
) follows from simply putting together (
1
)–(
3
), now we only need to prove (
3
).

To prove (
3
), it suffices to show that

(5.9)

\mathbb{M}\not\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow(\mathbb{M},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})\textrm{ for \emph{all} }e\in\mathbb{M}\textrm{ and }e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}.

In fact, suppose the
\not\sim
is replaced by
\sim
on either side of (
5.9
), then there exists a pre-collisional configuration between a particle
{\bm{p}}\in\mathbb{M}
and a particle
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, either in the composition dynamics
\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
or in the full dynamics
\mathcal{H}_{p(\mathbb{M})\cup p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
. Consider the
first time

t_{*}
when such a pre-collisional configuration occurs, then the two trajectories
must coincide before

t_{*}
, call it
\bm{\gamma}
. Now, if
\mathbb{M}\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
due to a collision in the full dynamics, then we must have
\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})=\mathtt{True}
by in Definition
4.3
, hence
(\mathbb{M},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
for some edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
in the particle lines
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
by Definition
5.3
(
3
). Conversely, if
(\mathbb{M},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}^{\prime},e^{\prime})
, then we also have
\mathtt{Pro}(\bm{\gamma},{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})=\mathtt{True}
by Definition
5.3
(
3
), hence
\mathbb{M}\sim\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
by Definition
4.3
.
∎

5.2.
Penrose argument and molecule representation

In this subsection, we start from (
5.6
) in Lemma
5.5
, and treat the product
\prod\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
using the Penrose argument which introduces
the O-atoms
, with the goal of proving (
5.17
) in Proposition
5.10
. This is motivated by similar arguments in
[
8
]
, except that we start from the product
\prod\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
, instead of
\prod\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\sim\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}
in
[
8
]
.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
be fixed. The molecules
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
in this definition have only one layer
\ell
, but may contain O-atoms. Let
\bm{z}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(t)
be the truncated dynamics on
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
(with suitable particle set), and let
|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}
be the number of O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
. We define the following objects:

(1)

The indicator function
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}
.
Define the
indicator function

\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}
, which extends the definition in Definition
5.3
to allow O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
, by

(5.10)

\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\cdot\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathbb{M}_{j},\,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}\\
\textrm{joined by O-atom}\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}.

Here
\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}
are clusters of
\mathbb{M}
obtained by deleting all O-atoms (Definition
3.11
), and
e
and
e^{\prime}
are edges in
\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
respectively (obtained from deleting O-atoms) that are joined by an O-atom in
\mathbb{M}
. The indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}
indicates that if
\mathfrak{o}\prec\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}
are O-atoms of
\mathbb{M}
that belong to the same ov-segment, then the overlap corresponding to
\mathfrak{o}
occurs earlier than that of
\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}
.

(2)

The operator
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
.
Recall the operators
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|
defined in (
3.10
) (with
(\ell-\underline{\ell}+1)\tau
replaced by
\tau
as
\mathbb{M}
has only one layer):

(5.11)

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}=(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}(\tau)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}},\quad|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}(\tau)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}.

We now define the operator
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
which is variant of
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
. The exact definition is very complicated and can be found in (
5.29
) (which is never used in the rest of this paper); in the proof we will only rely on its several natural properties, which we list as follows.

(a)

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
is an operator depending only on
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}
and satisfies

(5.12)

|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}f|\leq|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|(|f|),

(5.13)

\big|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}f-(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}f\big|\leq\sum_{e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}}\big|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}_{e,e^{\prime}}}\big|(|f|),

where
\mathbb{M}_{e,e^{\prime}}
is the molecule obtained by creating an O-atom joining
e
and
e^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{M}
.

(b)

If
\mathbb{M}
can be decomposed into disconnected parts
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathbb{M}^{k}
, then

(5.14)

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}=(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}^{1}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\circ(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}^{2}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\circ\cdots\circ(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}^{k}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}.

(c)

If
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is a obtained from relabeling non-root particles in
\mathbb{M}
while preserving
8
8

8

This means that if two particles
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
are in the same component, and
{\bm{p}}
has larger label than
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
before relabeling (recall labels are positive integers), then the same is true after relabeling.
the order of particle labels within each component of
\mathbb{M}
, then we have

(5.15)

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}f^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}f

where
f^{\prime}
is the function obtained by renaming the variables according to this relabeling.

(3)

The set of labeled molecules
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}
.
Define
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
(resp.
\mathscr{T}_{\Lambda}
if
s=1
) and
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}
to be the set of single layer, labeled molecules
\mathbb{M}
(
not
equivalence classes) satisfying the following conditions:

(a)

r(\mathbb{M})=[s]
and every component of
\mathbb{M}
contains at least one particle line
r\in r(\mathbb{M})
. Define
\mathbb{M}(r)
to be the component containing
r
; note that
\mathbb{M}(r)
may be the same for different
r
.

(b)

For
\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
, each component of
\mathbb{M}
contains
<\Lambda
clusters. For
\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}
, each component contains
\leq\Lambda
clusters, and there exists a unique component that contains exactly
\Lambda
clusters. In either case, each cluster
\mathbb{M}_{j}
satisfies
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|_{p}\leq\Lambda
and
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
.

(c)

The cluster graph (Definition
3.11
) of
\mathbb{M}
is a forest.

Remark 5.8
.

The Penrose argument we will perform in the proof of Proposition
5.10
below involves a lot of artificial choices, which makes the definition of
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
also highly artificial. As already pointed out, the exact formula is not important and we only need the properties (
2a
)–(
2c
) in Definition
5.7
(in practice
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
will be
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
times some artificial indicator function, so these properties are natural).

The sets of molecules
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}
in (
3
)
come from certain truncations we perform in the Penrose argument. Basically, if the number of clusters in each component is
<\Lambda
then we are in (
3a
) in Definition
5.7
; if not, we choose a first component with
\geq\Lambda
clusters, truncate it at exactly
\Lambda
clusters and treat the remaining clusters trivially, which leads to (
3b
) in Definition
5.7
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any molecule
\mathbb{M}
we have
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
.

Proof.

First assume
\mathbb{M}
is C-molecule (so
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}
is as in Definition
5.3
). Then
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
only requires all collisions in
\mathbb{M}
happen as expected, while
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}
additionally prohibits other pre-collisional configurations not in
\mathbb{M}
, so we have
\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
.

If
\mathbb{M}
contains O-atoms, by (
5.10
) and Definition
5.3
(
3
) (see Remark
5.4
) and the above proof, we have

(5.16)

\mathbbm{1}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{M}}\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\cdot\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathbb{M}_{j},\,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}\\
\textrm{joined by O-atom}\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{e\textrm{ overlaps with }e^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\prec},

and the right hand side exactly equals
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
(the
\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}
specifies the time order of O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
which is not included in other indicator functions). This completes the proof.
∎

Now we state the main conclusion of this section, namely Proposition
5.10
below.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
and
N
and
s
be fixed. We have

(5.17)

\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}O_{1}\bigg(|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|\circ\mathcal{S}_{[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg).

where
|O_{1}(A)|\leq A
(see Definition
1.6
(
7
)).

Proof.

Start from (
5.6
). We have

(5.18)

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(\tau)=\sum_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(\tau)\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\prod_{e\in\mathbb{M}_{j},\,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg),\\
\textrm{where }\overline{\mathbb{M}}\textrm{ is C-molecule, and }\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k}\}\textrm{ are connected components of }\overline{\mathbb{M}}.\end{gathered}

We divide the proof into 4 parts.

Proof part 1: preparations.
Let the labeled C-molecule
\overline{\mathbb{M}}
, and the clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, be fixed for now. Note that
p(\overline{\mathbb{M}})=[N]
, and also fix the root particles set to be
[s]
. A cluster
\mathbb{M}_{j}
is called a root cluster if it contains a root particle.

The
\mathbb{M}
on the right hand side of (
5.17
) will be formed by selecting certain clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
and joining them by certain O-atoms. In particular this
\mathbb{M}
is uniquely determined by (i) the set
p(\mathbb{M})
and the O-atoms joining edges
e\in\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
, and (ii) the parent-child orderings between O-atoms in the same ov-segment.

In the proof below, the ordering in (ii) plays no role in the main arguments below: once (i) is fixed, we can arbitrarily set the ordering
\prec
between O-atoms in the same ov-segment, and each choice of ordering leads to a unique molecule
\mathbb{M}
in (
5.17
). Correspondingly, we decompose
1=\sum_{\prec}\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}
in (
5.10
), which occurs in
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
in (
5.17
).

We also introduce the following notations, which will be used in the proof below:

•

Let
E
be the set of edge pairs
(e,e^{\prime})
such that
e\in\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j\neq j^{\prime}
. Denote elements of
E
by
\mathfrak{o}
(corresponding to possible overlaps), and denote the event
(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})
by
A_{\mathfrak{o}}
.

•

For any
G\subseteq E
, define
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}
to be the result of creating an O-atom at each
\mathfrak{o}=(e,e^{\prime})\in G
(Definition
3.10
) starting from
\overline{\mathbb{M}}
, which is in one-to-one correspondence with
G
. Define also
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}
to be the cluster graph (Definition
3.11
) of
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}
.

•

Let
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
be collections of subsets
F\subseteq E
satisfying the following properties:

–

Each component of the graph
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}
that contains an O-atom must contain a root cluster.

–

For
F\in\mathscr{F}
, each component of
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}
contains
<\Lambda
clusters. For
F\in\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
, each component of
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}
contains
\leq\Lambda
clusters, and there exists a unique component that contains exactly
\Lambda
clusters.

–

The cluster graph
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}
is a forest.

•

For any
F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
, define
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{F}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}
which is formed by the components of
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}
that contain at least one root cluster, and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{F}^{\prime}:=\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}\backslash\mathbb{M}
(which is a C-atom with each cluster being a component, due to the above properties of
F
).

Proof part 2: Penrose argument.
To treat the product
\prod\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
in (
5.18
), we apply the Penrose argument in the spirit of
[
8
]
. By writing
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=1-\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
and expanding, we get

(5.19)

\prod\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=\sum_{G\in 2^{E}}(-1)^{|G|}\mathbbm{1}_{G},\quad\mathbbm{1}_{G}=\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in G}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}.

Recall also that
(e,e^{\prime})=\mathfrak{o}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}}
.

The main issue with (
5.19
) is that the number of terms
\mathbbm{1}_{G}
is too large. We will suitably group these terms and exploit the cancellation coming from the signs
(-1)^{|G|}
, to reduce the sum
\sum_{G\in 2^{E}}
in (
5.19
) to a much smaller sum
\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}
, by the following argument:

(1)

We construct a suitable mapping
\pi:2^{E}\to\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
.

(2)

We show that, for each
F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
that belongs to the range of
\pi
, one can define a
forbidden set

FB(F)
, such that for any
G\in 2^{E}
we have

(5.20)

\pi(G)=F\Leftrightarrow F\subseteq G\textrm{ and }FB(F)\cap G=\varnothing.

(3)

Define also the
allowed set

AL(F):=E\backslash(F\cup FB(F))
. Let
(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{M}^{\prime})=(\mathbb{M}_{F},\mathbb{M}_{F}^{\prime})
be defined as above, we also show that

(5.21)

AL(F)=\{(e,e^{\prime}):e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\}\cup AL^{\prime}(F);

if
F\in\mathscr{F}
, we also show that

(5.22)

AL^{\prime}(F)\subseteq\{(e,e^{\prime}):e,e^{\prime}\textrm{ in the same component of }\mathbb{M}\}.

Finally, for
F\in\mathscr{F}
, the set
\mathrm{AL}(F)
is invariant if we relabel the non-root particles in
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{F}
while preserving the order of particle labels within each component of
\mathbb{M}
.

The mapping
\pi
will be constructed (with properties (
2
)–(
3
) proved) in Part 4 of the proof below. In this and the next part, we use
only these properties
to finish the proof of (
5.17
). The point is that we rewrite the sum in (
5.19
) as

\sum_{G\in 2^{E}}(\cdots)=\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{G:\pi(G)=F}(\cdots)+\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}\sum_{G:\pi(G)=F}(\cdots)

(where we also restrict
F
to the range of
\pi
) and apply (
5.20
) to factorize the inner sum in
G
. More precisely:

(5.23)

\displaystyle\prod\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=\sum_{G\in 2^{E}}(-1)^{|G|}\mathbbm{1}_{G}

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}\sum_{G:\pi(G)=F}\prod_{\mathfrak{o}=(e,e^{\prime})\in G}(-\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}\sum_{G=F\cup H,\,H\in 2^{AL(F)}}\prod_{\mathfrak{o}\in F\cup H}(-\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{o}\in F}(-\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}})\cdot\sum_{H\in 2^{AL(F)}}(-1)^{|H|}\prod_{\mathfrak{o}\in H}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{o}\in F}(-\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}})\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{o}\in AL(F)}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}(-1)^{|F|}\prod_{\mathfrak{o}\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{o}\in AL(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\mathfrak{o}}^{c}}.

Expanding the abbreviations, we get from (
5.23
) that

(5.24)

\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\prod_{e\in\mathbb{M}_{j},\,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}}(-1)^{|F|}\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}.

Now, by inserting (
5.24
) into (
5.18
), and decomposing
1=\sum_{\prec}\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}
as described in Part 1 (with
\prec
corresponding to the ordering between O-atoms that constitutes part of the molecule), we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq k}\prod_{e\in\mathbb{M}_{j},\,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\overline{\mathbb{M}},F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}},\prec)}(-1)^{|F|}\,\underbrace{\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}\bigg)}_{=\mathcal{S}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\textrm{ by \eqref{eq.1_notsim}}}\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)

(5.25)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}\,(F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}})}(-1)^{|F|}\,(\mathcal{S}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg).

Here in the last line, we have used that the triple
(\overline{\mathbb{M}},F,\prec)
uniquely corresponds to the molecule
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}
, and that
\mathcal{S}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
(since different clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
are connected by overlaps).

Moreover, if we define
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{F}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{F}^{\prime}
in the notion of Part 1, then it is easy to see that
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}
is in one-to-one correspondence with the pair
(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
; in addition, we have
F\in\mathscr{F}
(resp.
F\in\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
) if and only if
\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}
) due to Definition
5.7
(
3
) and the definitions in Part 1. Note also that the O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
are in bijection with elements in
F
(so
|F|=|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}
), we can then rewrite (
5.25
) as

(5.26)

\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\ell,\Lambda}\cup\mathscr{F}_{\ell,\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})=[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot(\mathcal{S}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathrm{AL}(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg).

Note also that
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is a C-molecule as shown in Part 1.

Proof part 3: the proof of (
5.17
).
Now, starting from (
5.26
), we further split the product over
AL(F)
. By (
5.21
) we know

(5.27)

\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL^{\prime}(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\cdot\prod_{e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}.

Note that we will use the product
\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
in (
5.27
) only when
F\in\mathscr{F}
; when
F\in\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
we will trivially bound it by
O_{1}(1)
. Also by (
5.10
), we have

(5.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\bigg(\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{M}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\mathbbm{1}_{\prec}\bigg)}_{=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\textrm{ by \eqref{eq.1_notsim}}}\circ\bigg(\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\bigg(\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg).

Now we define the operator
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
by

(5.29)

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}=(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL^{\prime}(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg).

Then, by inserting (
5.27
)–(
5.29
) into (
5.26
), we get that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\ell,\Lambda}\cup\mathscr{F}_{\ell,\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})=[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot(\mathcal{S}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{F}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})=[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}\underbrace{(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL^{\prime}(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)}_{=(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}},\textrm{ by \eqref{eq.proof_S_N_decomposition_25_1}}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\circ\bigg(\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})=[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ O_{1}(1)\circ\bigg(\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\circ\bigg[\underbrace{\sum_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})=[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}\bigg(\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)}_{=\mathcal{S}_{[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\textrm{ by (\ref{eq.dynamics_factorization})}}\bigg]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ O_{1}(1)\circ\bigg[\underbrace{\sum_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:p(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})=[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}\bigg(\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg)}_{=\mathcal{S}_{[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\textrm{ by (\ref{eq.dynamics_factorization})}}\bigg]

(5.30)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{err}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[N],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}O_{1}\bigg(|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|\circ\mathcal{S}_{[N]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\bigg),

where the time parameter in all
\mathcal{S}
operators are
\tau
. This proves (
5.17
).

It remains to show that the
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}
constructed in (
5.29
) satisfies the properties in Definition
5.7
(
2a
)-(
2c
). In fact, by (
5.29
), we know that

(5.31)

(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}=(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\circ\bigg(\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL^{\prime}(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\bigg).

To prove Definition
5.7
(
2a
), we need to verify (
5.12
) and (
5.13
). Here (
5.12
) is a simple corollary of the fact that
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
(by Lemma
5.9
) and
\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}\leq 1
. Moreover (
5.13
) is a corollary of the fact that

\bigg|\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}-1\bigg|\leq\sum_{(e,e^{\prime})\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})};\qquad A:=AL^{\prime}(F).

Now we prove Definition
5.7
(
2b
). Assume
\mathbb{M}
is the disconnected union
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathbb{M}^{k}
, it is easy to check that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}^{1}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}^{1}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})\circ\cdots\circ(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{M}^{k}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}^{k}}^{\Lambda,\Gamma})
. For the product over
AL^{\prime}(F)
, a similar decomposition also holds true:

\prod_{(e,e^{\prime})\in AL^{\prime}(F)}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(e,e^{\prime})\in AL^{\prime}(F)\\
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{i}\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbb{M}_{j},e)\not\sim(\mathbb{M}_{j^{\prime}},e^{\prime})},

where we have used the fact that if
(e,e^{\prime})\in AL^{\prime}(F)
then they must be in the same component of
\mathbb{M}
(hence the same
\mathbb{M}^{i}
), due to (
5.22
). The property in Definition
5.7
(
2b
) then follows. Finally Definition
5.7
(
2c
) follows from the property of
AL(F)
under relabeling, see property (
3
) in Part 2.

This completes the proof of Proposition
5.10
, pending the construction of the mapping
\pi
. We will construct this
\pi
in Part 4 below.

Proof part 4: construction of
\pi
.
In this part, we define the map
\pi
and prove properties (
2
)–(
3
) in Part 2. The idea is similar to Proposition 2.3.3 in
[
8
]
, but the arguments are more complicated due to truncation.

Before defining
\pi
, we first make some preparations. Recall
E
is the set of edge pairs
(e,e^{\prime})
; given a labeling of particle lines, we can define a linear ordering on the set
E
as follows: suppose
\mathfrak{o}=(e,e^{\prime})\in E
, where
e
and
e^{\prime}
belong to particle lines
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
respectively, and
{\bm{p}}<{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
(in terms of labels). Then the linear ordering is defined by first comparing
{\bm{p}}
(in terms of labels), then comparing
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, and then comparing the order in the same particle line (descendant
<
ancestor). Clearly this linear ordering is invariant if we relabel the non-root particles, but keeping the order of particle labels within each component of
\mathbb{M}
.

Now let
G\in 2^{E}
be fixed. In the definition below, we will consider the graph
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}
defined in Part 1 (by creating an O-atom at each
\mathfrak{o}=(e,e^{\prime})\in G
) and its cluster graph
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}
. For each root particle line
r\in[s]
, let
\mathbb{Q}_{r}
be the (root) cluster containing
r
, and
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}(r)
be the component of
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}
containing
r
(and
\mathbb{Q}_{r}
), and
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
be the corresponding component of
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}
. For each cluster
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
, consider the graph theoretic distance between
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{r}
(i.e. the length of shortest path connecting them), and define
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q}(r)
to be the set of
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
with the above distance being
q\geq 0
.

Algorithm defining
\pi
.
For each root particle
r\in[s]
, let
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}(r)
and
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
be defined above. We distinguish two cases:

Case 1: if
|\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)|<\Lambda
for each
r
.
We perform the following steps:

(1)

Start from the first root particle
r=1
, and work in the graph
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(1)
(note that this graph may contain multiple edges, but this does not affect the arguments below).

(2)

Consider all the elements
\mathfrak{o}=(e,e^{\prime})\in G
(i.e. O-atoms in
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}
) that join a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q}(1)
and a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q+1}(1)
for some
q\geq 0
. We order them first in the increasing order in
q
, and then in the increasing order in
\mathfrak{o}
(following the linear order defined above).

(3)

Start with the set
S:=\{\mathbb{Q}_{1}\}
. Each time, select the smallest
\mathfrak{o}=(e,e^{\prime})
in (
2
) that still remains in
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}(1)
. Let
\mathbb{K}
be the cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q+1}(1)
that is joined by
\mathfrak{o}
to a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q}(1)
, we add
\{\mathbb{K}\}
to the set
S
, and delete all other elements of
G
(i.e. O-atoms) that join
\mathbb{K}
to another cluster in
S
. Repeat until
S
exhausts all clusters in
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(1)
, then stop working in this component.

(4)

Then, select the next smallest root particle
r
that does not belong to any previously operated component, and repeat (
1
)–(
3
) until all root clusters have been accounted for. Finally, delete all O-atoms that do not belong to any
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}(r)
. Define the result to be
F=\pi(G)
.

Case 2: if
|\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)|\geq\Lambda
for some
r
.
We perform the same steps as in
Case 1
, except at the first time when
|\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)|\geq\Lambda
. At this time we perform (
1
)–(
3
) as in
Case 1
, but terminate early when
|S|=\Lambda
(without adding the next atom to
S
). Then we delete all the
\mathfrak{o}
that still remains in
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{G}(r)
and do not join two clusters in
S
. After this, stop exploring (even if there are still root clusters) and delete all O-atoms that do not belong to any previously operated component. Define the result to be
F=\pi(G)
.

It is easy to verify the following properties of
\pi(G)=F
; in particular, they imply that
F\in\mathscr{F}\cup\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{err}}
by definitions in Part 1.

•

The cluster graph
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}
is a forest. This is because in our construction (
3
), each new cluster in
S
has only one overlap (after deletion) joining it to earlier clusters in
S
.

•

If
|\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)|<\Lambda
, then
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
and
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r)=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q}(r)
. This is because in our construction (
3
), the set
S
remains connected in the cluster graph, and each cluster in
S\cap\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q}(r)
is always connected to
\mathbb{Q}_{r}
by a path of length
q
even after the deletions.

•

In
Case 2
,
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}
has a unique component
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r_{0})
that contains
\Lambda
clusters; each component
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
that is operated before
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r_{0})
contains
<\Lambda
clusters, and each component other than
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r_{0})
and
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
contains only one cluster. This is clear from the construction in
Case 2
.

Definition of
FB(F)
.
Now we define the set
FB(F)
as follows.

Case 1: when
|\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)|<\Lambda
for each
r
.
We define
\mathfrak{o}\in FB(F)
if and only if

(1)

\mathfrak{o}
joins two clusters in two different components
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)
;

(2)

\mathfrak{o}
joins a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r)
and a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q^{\prime}}(r)
with
|q-q^{\prime}|\geq 2
; or

(3)

\mathfrak{o}\not\in F
joins a cluster
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r)
and a cluster
\mathbb{K}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q+1}(r)
, and the unique
\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}\in F
joining
\mathbb{K}
to a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r)
satisfies
\mathfrak{o}<\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}
in the linear ordering; or

(4)

\mathfrak{o}
joins a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)
and a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}
not in any
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
.

Case 2: when
|\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r_{0})|=\Lambda
.
We define the components
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r_{0})
and previously operated components
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
as above, and let their union be
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(\leq r_{0})
. Consider also the last cluster
\mathbb{K}
reaching
|S|=\Lambda
in
Case 2
of the algorithm; assume it belongs to
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q_{0}}(r_{0})
and the associated smallest
\mathfrak{o}
is
\mathfrak{o}_{0}
. Then, we define
\mathfrak{o}\in FB(F)
if and only if

(5)

\mathfrak{o}
satisfies one of (
1
)–(
3
) holds within
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(\leq r_{0})
; or

(6)

\mathfrak{o}
joins one cluster
\mathbb{K}
outside
\mathrm{Cl}_{\leq r_{0}}
and another cluster
\mathbb{Q}
, which either belongs to
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
for some
r^{\prime}
as above, or belongs to
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r_{0})
for some
q<q_{0}-1
, or belongs to
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q_{0}-1}(r_{0})
and
\mathfrak{o}<\mathfrak{o}_{0}
.

Proof of (
2
)–(
3
) in Part 2.
Now, with the mapping
\pi
and the set
FB(F)
defined as above, we can prove the properties (
2
)–(
3
) in Part 2.

The main point is to show (
5.20
). Recall the properties (
1
)–(
6
) in the definition of
FB(F)
above. We first show that if
\pi(G)=F
then
FB(F)\cap G=\varnothing
(as
F\subseteq G
is obvious). Assume
\mathfrak{o}\in FB(F)\cap G
for contradiction, and consider each of the cases (
1
)–(
6
).

(
1
) If
\mathfrak{o}
joins a clusters in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)
and a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})\neq\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)
, then we have
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r^{\prime})
because of
\mathfrak{o}
, but this is contradiction because
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
and
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r^{\prime})
.

(
2
) If
\mathfrak{o}
joins a clusters in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r)
and a cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q^{\prime}}(r)
with
q^{\prime}\geq q+2
, then this latter cluster belongs to
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q+1}(r)
or lower because of
\mathfrak{o}
, but this is contradiction because
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q+1}(r)=\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q+1}(r)
is disjoint with
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q^{\prime}}(r)
.

(
3
) If
\mathfrak{o}
joins
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r)
and
\mathbb{K}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q+1}(r)
, and satisfies
\mathfrak{o}<\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}
where
\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}\in F
, then consider the time when we add
\{\mathbb{K}\}
into
S
. By the construction in (
3
) of the algorithm, we would select
\mathfrak{o}
and delete
\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}
, so the result will not be
F
, contradiction.

(
4
) If
\mathfrak{o}
joins one cluster in
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)
to a cluster
\mathbb{K}
not in any
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
, then
\mathbb{K}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
because of
\mathfrak{o}
, but this implies
\mathbb{K}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)
, contradiction.

(
5
) This case is basically the same as (
1
)–(
3
) but restricted to
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r_{0})
and
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
.

(
6
) Assume
\mathfrak{o}
joins
\mathbb{K}\not\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(\leq r_{0})
and
\mathbb{Q}
as specified. If
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
for some
r^{\prime}
in the definition of
FB(F)
above, then we have
\mathbb{K}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r^{\prime})=\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})
due to
\mathfrak{o}
, which is a contradiction. If
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r_{0})
for
q<q_{0}-1
, then similarly
\mathbb{K}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q+1}(r_{0})\subseteq\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r_{0})
(the subset relation is because
q+1<q_{0}
, so all clusters in
\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q+1}(r_{0})
must have been exhausted in the construction in (
3
) of the algorithm before reaching
|S|=\Lambda
), which is also contradiction. Finally if
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q_{0}-1}(r_{0})
and
\mathfrak{o}<\mathfrak{o}_{0}
, then again the in construction we would select
\mathfrak{o}
at some point before selecting
\mathfrak{o}_{0}
, so the result will not be
F
, contradiction.

We next show that if
F\subseteq G
and
FB(F)\cap G=\varnothing
, then we must have
\pi(G)=F
. This is basically done by reverting the proof in cases (
1
)–(
6
) above, which we carry out below.

Assume first that
|\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)|<\Lambda
for each
r
. Then, using the absence of elements in (
1
) and (
4
), we deduce that
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r)=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r)
for each
r
; using the absence of elements in (
2
), we can inductively show that
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r)=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q}(r)
for each
r
and
q
. Finally, using the assumptions for (
3
), we see that the choice of edges in the construction of
\pi(G)
exactly leads to those edges already in
F
.

The case when some
|F_{r_{0}}|=\Lambda
is similar. The same arguments above implies that
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(r^{\prime})=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}(r^{\prime})
for those
r^{\prime}
in the definition of
FB(F)
above, and
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{q}(r_{0})=\mathrm{Cl}_{G}^{q}(r_{0})
for
q<q_{0}
, and the result up to this point coincides with
F
. Then, when we execute (
3
) of the algorithm and each time choosing a smallest remaining
\mathfrak{o}
, the absence of elements in (
6
) guarantees that we cannot add anything outside
\mathrm{Cl}_{F}(\leq r_{0})
into
S
. So the result coincides with
F
, as desired.

Finally, we prove the properties of
AL(F)
in (
3
) in Part 2. In fact, the first two properties (
5.21
)–(
5.22
) follow immediately from the definition of
FB(F)
(and thus
AL(F)
) above. The last property concerning relabeling is also obvious, because the set
AL(F)
is constructed using the linear ordering for
\mathfrak{o}
, which is invariant under the said relabelings.
∎

5.3.
Recurrence formula for
\widetilde{f}_{s}

In this subsection we apply the decomposition of
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
, i.e. (
5.17
) in Proposition
5.10
, to obtain a representation formula of
\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau)
at time
\ell\tau
, in terms of single-layer molecules
\mathbb{M}
and the same quantity
\widetilde{f}_{s}
at time
(\ell-1)\tau
. See Proposition
5.12
.

Definition 5.11

(Sets
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
)
.

Given
\Lambda_{\ell}
, define
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
) to be the set of
equivalence class

[\mathbb{M}]
of labeled molecules
\mathbb{M}
of single layer
\ell
, such that
\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
) as in Definition
5.7
(
3
). Note that the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
in Definition
3.20
(
3
) is a special case of this
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
with only one root particle (the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
is unrelated to
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
, though.)

Proposition 5.12

(Recurrence formula for
\widetilde{f}_{s}
)
.

Recall
\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})
defined in (
4.14
) and (
4.17
). For
\mathbb{M}
with one layer
\ell
, define

(5.32)

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})})=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}\int(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})^{\mathrm{Pen}}_{\mathbb{M}}\big(\widetilde{f}_{|\mathbb{M}|_{p}}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\prime})\big)d\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})},\\
\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M},\mathrm{upp}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})})=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}\int|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|\big(|\widetilde{f}_{|\mathbb{M}|_{p}}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\prime})|\big)d\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})}.\end{gathered}

Then we have the following formula

(5.33)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}},\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]}\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})+\mathrm{Err}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s}),

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}},\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]}\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M},\mathrm{upp}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s}).

Remark 5.13
.

The expressions in (
5.32
) are just like those in (
3.11
)–(
3.12
) in Definition
3.23
, with the transport operator
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}
(and its variant) associated with the molecule
\mathbb{M}
, and integration in the non-root particles. Here the molecule
\mathbb{M}
has single layer
\ell
, as we evolve from time
(\ell-1)\tau
to time
\ell\tau
. The input function is the density
\widetilde{f}_{|\mathbb{M}|_{p}}
at time
(\ell-1)\tau
, with
\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\prime}
being the states of the particles at time
(\ell-1)\tau
. Note also that
\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]-\underline{\ell}=0
in (
3.11
)–(
3.12
) because
\mathbb{M}
has only one layer
\ell
.

We remark that the
\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}}
in (
5.32
) is
not
constant on each equivalence class
[\mathbb{M}]
due to the
(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})^{\mathrm{Pen}}_{\mathbb{M}}
factor (in fact this is the
only
instance of non-constant cases throughout this paper). By Definition
1.6
(
6
), we should interpret the sum as

(5.34)

\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}},\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]}(\cdots):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})=[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}],r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s)!}(\cdots).

Here note that (i) we may always restrict
p(\mathbb{M})=[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}]
in each equivalence class
[\mathbb{M}]
, without affecting the expression
\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}}
or the average in
[\mathbb{M}]
(thanks to Definition
5.7
(
2c
)), and (ii) once
p(\mathbb{M})=[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}]
is fixed, the size of the equivalence class equals
|[\mathbb{M}]|=(|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s)!
due to permuting the
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s
non-root labels.

Proof.

In the summation below we only consider the sum over
\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
and
[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
, as the the remaining sum over
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
is similar (in fact easier as
\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M},\mathrm{upp}}
is invariant under equivalence relation).

By (
4.14
) and (
4.17
), we have

(5.35)

\widetilde{f}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})=\varepsilon^{s(d-1)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dn}}\mathcal{S}^{\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma}_{s+n}(\tau)\widetilde{W}_{s+n}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{s+n}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{[s+1:s+n]}.

Here, for distinction, we used a different set of variables
\bm{z}_{s+n}^{\prime}
for the states at
(\ell-1)\tau
. Inserting (
5.17
), we get the decomposition

(5.36)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{s(d-1)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})\subseteq[s+n],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dn}}(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{[s+n]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}\widetilde{W}_{s+n}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{s+n}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{[s+1:s+n]}

\displaystyle+(\mathrm{Err}),

where
(\mathrm{Err})
represents the sum over
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
. By Definition
5.7
(
2c
), in the summation (
5.36
), we may always restrict
p(\mathbb{M})=[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}]
, with an additional factor
\binom{n}{|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s}
(this fixes the
set of labels
of non-root particles, but not the label of each
individual
particles). This leads to the expression

(5.37)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{s(d-1)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})=[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s)!(n-|\mathbb{M}|_{p}+s)!}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dn}}(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{[s+n]\backslash p(\mathbb{M})}^{\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma}\widetilde{W}_{s+n}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{s+n}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{[s+1:s+n]}+(\mathrm{Err}).

Upon switching the order of summation, we get

(5.38)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})=\varepsilon^{s(d-1)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})=[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s)!}\int(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{n=|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n-|\mathbb{M}|_{p}+s)!}\int\mathcal{S}_{[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}+1:s+n]}^{\Lambda_{\ell},\Gamma}\widetilde{W}_{s+n}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{s+n}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}+1:s+n]}\bigg)\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})}+(\mathrm{Err}).

Consider the parenthesis in the second line in (
5.38
). Using the
L^{1}
invariance of
\mathcal{S}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
(Proposition
4.5
(
4
)) and (
1.11
), we conclude that this parenthesis equals
\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p}}\widetilde{f}_{|\mathbb{M}|_{p}}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\prime})
. This implies that

(5.39)

\widetilde{f}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{s})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}\\
p(\mathbb{M})=[|\mathbb{M}|_{p}],\,r(\mathbb{M})=[s]\end{subarray}}\frac{\varepsilon^{-(d-1)(|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s)}}{(|\mathbb{M}|_{p}-s)!}\int(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\widetilde{f}_{|\mathbb{M}|_{p}}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M})}+(\mathrm{Err}).

Now, using (
5.34
) and note that
s=|\mathbb{M}|_{r}
, we see that the main term in (
5.39
) exactly matches that in (
5.33
). Since the error term
(\mathrm{Err})
can be treated in the same way, this completes the proof of Proposition
5.12
.
∎

5.4.
Recurrence formula for cumulants

In this subsection, starting from (
5.33
) in Proposition
5.12
, we shall insert the ansatz (
3.15
) for
\widetilde{f}_{s}((\ell-1)\tau)
and subsequently derive the same expansion for
\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau)
. This also includes a recurrence formula for both
f^{\mathcal{A}}
and
E_{H}
in (
3.15
), see Proposition
5.16
. This is the main step in the proof of Proposition
3.25
; with it, we simply proceed by induction in
\ell
, and use the initial cumulant expansion for
\ell=0
(Proposition
5.18
in Section
5.5
), which is done in Section
5.6
.

Definition 5.14
.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
and
H\subseteq p(\mathbb{M})
, we define
r(\mathbb{M})\searrow H
, if each particle line
r\in r(\mathbb{M})
belongs to a component of
\mathbb{M}
, which contains either another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M})
, or a particle line in
H
.

Remark 5.15
.

Using Definition
3.19
, the condition
r(\mathbb{M})\searrow H
can also be expressed as “each particle line in
r(\mathbb{M})
is connected to either another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M})
via
\mathbb{M}
, or connected to a particle line in
H
via
\mathbb{M}
, or belongs to
H
”, in the same manner as Definition
3.20
(
1d
).

In reality,
r(\mathbb{M})=r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
will be the root particle set of a certain layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, and
H=r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
will be the root particle set of the previous layer. The symbol
r(\mathbb{M})\searrow H
means that
r(\mathbb{M})
is either connected “horizontally” to itself, or “vertically” to
H
in the previous layer.

Proposition 5.16
.

Fix
\ell
and
(A_{\ell},\Lambda_{\ell})
defined in (
1.32
). Assume for each set
Q_{\ell-1}
with
s^{+}=|Q_{\ell-1}|\leq\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}A_{\ell}
, we have cumulant expansion at
t=(\ell-1)\tau
,

(5.40)

\widetilde{f}_{s^{+}}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell-1}})=\sum_{H_{\ell-1}\subseteq Q_{\ell-1}}(f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau))^{\otimes(Q_{\ell-1}\backslash H_{\ell-1})}\cdot E_{H_{\ell-1}}((\ell-1)\tau)+\mathrm{Err}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell-1}}),

where
E_{H_{\ell-1}}((\ell-1)\tau)
is a function of
\bm{z}_{H_{\ell-1}}
(with
E_{\varnothing}=1
by convention) and is symmetric in its variables,
f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)
is a single variable function of
z=(x,v)
, and the tensor product
(f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau))^{\otimes(Q_{\ell-1}\backslash H_{\ell-1})}
is a function of
\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell-1}\backslash H_{\ell-1}}
.

Then for each
Q_{\ell}
with
s=|Q_{\ell}|\leq A_{\ell}
, we have the cumulant expansion at
t=\ell\tau
:

(5.41)

\widetilde{f}_{s}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})=\sum_{H_{\ell}\subseteq Q_{\ell}}(f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau))^{\otimes(Q_{\ell}\backslash H_{\ell})}\cdot E_{H_{\ell}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H_{\ell}})+\mathrm{Err}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}}).

Here, if we define

(5.42)

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M},H}^{\mathrm{Pen}}(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})})\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}}(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{Pen}}\left[\left(f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)\right)^{\otimes(p(\mathbb{M})\backslash H)}\cdot E_{H}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{H}^{\prime})\right]\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})},\\
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M},H}|(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})})\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathbb{M}|_{p\backslash r}}}|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}}|\left[\left|f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)\right|^{\otimes(p(\mathbb{M})\backslash H)}\cdot|E_{H}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{H}^{\prime})|\right]\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M})},\end{gathered}

then
f^{\mathcal{A}}
,
E_{H_{\ell}}
and
\mathrm{Err}
in (
5.41
) are explicitly given by the following formulas:

(1)

(f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau))^{\otimes(Q_{\ell}\backslash H_{\ell})}
is a function of
\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}\backslash H_{\ell}}
which is a tensor product of the single variable function
f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau)
given by

(5.43)

f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau,z)=\sum_{[\mathbb{M}]\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M},\varnothing}^{\mathrm{Pen}}(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M})}).

Note that the right hand side of (
5.43
) is also a function of a single variable
z
as
|r(\mathbb{M})|=1
.

(2)

The cumulant
E_{H_{\ell}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H_{\ell}})
satisfies

(5.44)

|E_{H_{\ell}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H_{\ell}})|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbb{M}_{\ell}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}},\,r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=H_{\ell}\\
H_{\ell-1}\subseteq p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell}),\,r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})\searrow H_{\ell-1}\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell},H_{\ell-1}}|(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})}).

(3)

The error term
\mathrm{Err}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})
satisfies
|\mathrm{Err}|\leq|\mathrm{Err}^{1}|+|\mathrm{Err}^{2}|
, where
\mathrm{Err}^{1}
and
\mathrm{Err}^{2}
satisfy that

(5.45)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}^{1}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{[\mathbb{M}_{\ell}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}},\,r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=Q_{\ell},\,H_{\ell-1}\subseteq p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})}|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell},H_{\ell-1}}|(\bm{z}_{r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})}),

(5.46)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Err}^{2}(\ell\tau)\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-2(d-1)\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}A_{\ell}}\cdot\|\mathrm{Err}((\ell-1)\tau)\|_{L^{1}}.

Remark 5.17
.

We make a few remarks regarding Proposition
5.16
. First, the function
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
depends only on
|H|
and is symmetric in its variables, and the functions
\mathrm{Err}
,
\mathrm{Err}^{1}
and
\mathrm{Err}^{2}
are symmetric in all their variables. These will be guaranteed for the rest of this paper.

Second, the key condition
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})\searrow H_{\ell-1}
in (
5.44
) comes from the requirement that no root particle in
E_{H_{\ell}}
should be completely independent from the rest (and thus be factorized out). This is equivalent to the condition in Definition
3.20
(
1d
), and is crucial in the subsequent algorithms.

Proof.

Start with (
5.32
)–(
5.33
), where we replace the variable
\bm{z}_{s}
by
\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}}
. We have

(5.47)

\widetilde{f}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})=\textrm{Main}^{0}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})+\mathrm{Err}^{0}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}}),

where

(5.48)

\displaystyle\textrm{Main}^{0}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathbb{M}_{\ell}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}},\,r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=Q_{\ell}}\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}_{\ell}|_{p\backslash r}}\int(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})^{\mathrm{Pen}}_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell}}\widetilde{f}_{|\mathbb{M}_{\ell}|_{p}}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})},

and

(5.49)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Err}^{0}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{[\mathbb{M}_{\ell}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}},\,r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=Q_{\ell}}\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell},\mathrm{upp}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell},\mathrm{upp}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{Q_{\ell}})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|\mathbb{M}_{\ell}|_{p\backslash r}}\int|(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathbbm{1})_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell}}||\widetilde{f}_{|\mathbb{M}_{\ell}|_{p}}((\ell-1)\tau,\bm{z}_{p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})}^{\prime})|\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{(p\backslash r)(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})}.

In the proof below, we will show that the
\textrm{Err}^{0}
term in (
5.47
) contributes part of
\mathrm{Err}^{1}
and part of
\mathrm{Err}^{2}
, which satisfy (
3
). The
\textrm{Main}^{0}
term in (
5.47
) contributes part of
\mathrm{Err}^{2}
, as well as the main expression

(5.50)

\sum_{H_{\ell}\subseteq Q_{\ell}}(f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau))^{\otimes(Q_{\ell}\backslash H_{\ell})}\cdot E_{H_{\ell}}(\ell\tau,\bm{z}_{H_{\ell}})

in (
5.41
), with
f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau)
and
E_{H}(\ell\tau)
satisfying (
1
)–(
2
). We divide the proof into 3 parts.

Proof part 1.
We first construct the
\mathrm{Err}^{1}
and
\mathrm{Err}^{2}
terms and prove (
3
). Start with
\mathrm{Err}^{0}
in (
