\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}\backslash\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}|}\times\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{tot}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\bm{\Delta}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}},z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}\backslash\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M},\prec,t_{\mathrm{min}}}\big(\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\big),\quad a.e.

If we can prove (
7.13
) for each fixed
\prec
, then (
7.10
) follows by setting
\ell_{\mathrm{min}}=1
,
t_{\mathrm{min}}=0
and summing over all different choices of the total ordering
\prec
. In the rest of this proof, we focus on proving (
7.13
).

Proof part 3.
In this part, we prove (
7.13
) by induction on the number of collisions and overlaps.

If
\mathbb{M}
has no collision or overlap, then
\mathbb{M}=\varnothing
and both sides of (
7.13
) are vacuously
1
. Now suppose (
7.13
) is true for molecules with fewer collisions and overlaps than
\mathbb{M}
. For
\mathbb{M}
, we choose the smallest atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
under the total ordering
\prec
. We will assume
\mathfrak{n}
is a collision happening between two particles
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
; the overlap case is similar and much easier (where the incoming velocity coincide with the outgoing ones).

Consider the C-atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
. It has no child as it is the smallest atom, which means it has two bottom ends
(e_{1},e_{2})
. Let the corresponding serial top edges at
\mathfrak{n}
be
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
, and let
{\bm{p}}_{j}
be the particle line that
e_{j}
and
e_{j}^{\prime}
belong to. We will assume
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
are both bonds; the case when one or both of them are ends only requires trivial modifications. Now, if we remove the atom
\mathfrak{n}
and turn
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
to two bottom ends at the other atoms they connect to, we will reduce
\mathbb{M}
to another molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
(with the extra total ordering inherited from
\mathbb{M}
).

On the left hand side of (
7.13
), we may first fix
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
(x_{e_{1}^{\prime}},v_{e_{1}^{\prime}},x_{e_{2}^{\prime}},v_{e_{2}^{\prime}})
, and integrate in the remaining variables. By induction hypothesis for
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, the integral in the remaining variables equals

(7.14)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime},\prec^{\prime},t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\big(x_{e_{1}^{\prime}},v_{e_{1}^{\prime}},x_{e_{2}^{\prime}},v_{e_{2}^{\prime}},(z_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}\backslash\{e_{1},e_{2}\}}\big),

where the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime},\prec^{\prime},t_{\mathfrak{n}}}
is defined for the molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
with the total ordering
\prec^{\prime}
inherited from
\prec
, and
t_{\mathrm{min}}
replaced by
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
. We then integrate in the variables
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
(x_{e_{1}^{\prime}},v_{e_{1}^{\prime}},x_{e_{2}^{\prime}},v_{e_{2}^{\prime}})
; regarding this integral, we claim (for any function
Q\geq 0
) that

(7.15)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4d}\times I}\bm{\Delta}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}},z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\cdot Q(z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\,\mathrm{d}z_{e_{1}^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}z_{e_{2}^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\\
=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}})\cdot Q\big(x_{\mathrm{out}}^{1}-t_{\mathrm{col}}\cdot v_{\mathrm{out}}^{1},v_{\mathrm{out}}^{1},x_{\mathrm{out}}^{2}-t_{\mathrm{col}}\cdot v_{\mathrm{out}}^{2},v_{\mathrm{out}}^{2},t_{\mathrm{col}}\big).

Here
I=[(\ell[\mathfrak{n}]-1)\tau,\ell[\mathfrak{n}]\tau]\cap[t_{\mathrm{min}},+\infty)
,
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}
is the indicator function that a collision happens between particles
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
with initial position and velocity
(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}})
, at collision time
t_{\mathrm{col}}\in I
, and
z_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}=(x_{\mathrm{out}}^{j},v_{\mathrm{out}}^{j})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
are the position and velocity of the particles
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
right after the collision; note that
z_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
and
t_{\mathrm{col}}
are both functions of
(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}})
.

The proof of (
7.15
) follows from first integrating in
x_{e_{1}^{\prime}}
and
x_{e_{2}^{\prime}}
, then in
v_{e_{1}^{\prime}}
and
v_{e_{2}^{\prime}}
, and then in
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Note that:

(1)

The Dirac
\bm{\delta}
functions imply that a collision happens between particles
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
;

(2)

The factor
[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega]_{-}
supported in
(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega\leq 0
implies that the particles form a pre-collisional configuration at collision time;

(3)

The collision time
t_{\mathrm{col}}
is the unique time
t_{\mathrm{col}}
satisfying
|(x_{e_{1}}+t_{\mathrm{col}}v_{e_{1}})-(x_{e_{2}}+t_{\mathrm{col}}v_{e_{2}})|=\varepsilon
and the pre-collisional condition;

(4)

The value of
z_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}=(x_{\mathrm{out}}^{j},v_{\mathrm{out}}^{j})=(x_{e_{j}^{\prime}}+t_{\mathrm{col}}\,v_{e_{j}^{\prime}},v_{e_{j}^{\prime}})
equals the outgoing position and velocity of particles
p_{j}
immediately after the collision, which is uniquely determined by
(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}})
;

(5)

Once
t_{\mathrm{col}}
and
v_{e_{j}^{\prime}}
are fixed, the integral in
x_{e_{j}^{\prime}}
contains a single
\bm{\delta}
factor, and once
t_{\mathrm{col}}
is fixed, the integral in
v_{e_{j}^{\prime}}
also contains a single
\delta
factor. Finally it is easy to check that

|(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega|\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{2}}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})|-\varepsilon\big)=\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathrm{col}}),

so integrating in the order of
x_{e_{j}^{\prime}}
then
v_{e_{j}^{\prime}}
and
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
then proves (
7.15
).

Now, by (
7.15
) and induction hypothesis, we know that the left hand side of (
7.13
) equals

(7.16)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime},\prec^{\prime},t_{\mathrm{col}}}\big(x_{\mathrm{out}}^{1}-t_{\mathrm{col}}\cdot v_{\mathrm{out}}^{1},v_{\mathrm{out}}^{1},x_{\mathrm{out}}^{2}-t_{\mathrm{col}}\cdot v_{\mathrm{out}}^{2},v_{\mathrm{out}}^{2},(z_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}\backslash\{e_{1},e_{2}\}}\big).

The indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}
is equivalent to the requirement that the particles
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
collide in the layer
[(\ell[\mathfrak{n}]-1)\tau,\ell[\mathfrak{n}]\tau]
and after time
t_{\mathrm{min}}
. The indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime},\prec^{\prime},t_{\mathrm{col}}}
is equivalent to requiring that, if particle
p_{j}\,(j\in\{1,2\})
had initial position
x_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}-t_{\mathrm{col}}\cdot v_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
and initial velocity
v_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
, and other particles had the same initial position and velocity given by the corresponding
z_{e}

(e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}\backslash\{e_{1},e_{2}\})
, then the exact collisions and overlaps in the
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
-prescribed dynamics (with total ordering
\prec^{\prime}
) would happen, and they would all happen (in the corresponding time layer and) after time
t_{\mathrm{col}}
.

Note that the position and velocity of
(p_{1},p_{2})
at time
t_{\mathrm{col}}
, with the above virtual initial configuration, is exactly
(z_{\mathrm{out}}^{1},z_{\mathrm{out}}^{2})
, which equals the same outgoing position and velocity of
(p_{1},p_{2})
at time
t_{\mathrm{col}}
right after the first collision
\mathfrak{n}
, with the actual initial configuration
z_{e}

(e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-})
. Since we are restricting all collisions and overlaps other than
\mathfrak{n}
to happen after
t_{\mathrm{col}}
, we know that
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime},\prec^{\prime},t_{\mathrm{col}}}
is equivalent to requiring that the exact collisions and overlaps in the
\mathbb{M}
-prescribed dynamics (other than
\mathfrak{n}
, with total ordering
\prec
) all happen (in the corresponding time layer and) after the collision time
t_{\mathrm{col}}
for
\mathfrak{n}
. Therefore, the product indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime},\prec^{\prime},t_{\mathrm{col}}}
equals
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
in (
7.13
). This proves (
7.13
) and hence Proposition
7.5
.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

By (
7.7
) and (
7.9
), we know that changing the value of
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
on Lebesgue zero sets does not affect the value of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
. Below we will exploit this to make sure that the functions
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
integrated in (
7.6
) are everywhere defined Borel functions, and their support satisfy that no two collisions happen at the same time, and no collision happens at any
\ell\tau
.

Remark 7.7
.

Note the following difference between C-atoms and O-atoms. In (
7.3
), the distribution
\bm{\Delta}
is symmetric under permutation
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(e_{2},e_{1},e_{2}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime})
, and also
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},e_{1},e_{2})
except for the
[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega]_{-}
factor. In other words, for any C-atom we must distinguish top and bottom edges, and also distinguish serial edges, in order for (
7.3
) to be well-defined; however there is also symmetry between “top” and “bottom” in the case when the
[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega]_{-}
factor does not play a role.

On the other hand, in (
7.4
), the distribution
\bm{\Delta}
is symmetric under permutation exchanging
e_{1}
with
e_{1}^{\prime}
, or exchanging
e_{2}
with
e_{2}^{\prime}
, or exchanging
(e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime})
with
(e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime})
. Therefore, for any O-atom we do not need to distinguish top and bottom edges, as long as the serial edges (or particle lines) are properly distinguished.

8.
Operations on molecules

From this section until Section
9
, we will prove Proposition
6.2
(pending Proposition
9.7
which will be proved in Sections
10
–
13
). Thanks to the preparations in Section
7
, we only need to estimate the quantity
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
in (
7.7
)–(
7.8
) for a fixed molecule
\mathbb{M}
.

In Section
8.1
, we define several operations on the molecule
\mathbb{M}
, which reduce it to smaller and simpler
elementary molecules
(see Definition
8.11
). In Section
8.2
we study the behavior of the quantity
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
under these operations, see Propositions
8.13
–
8.14
. With these, it then suffices to estimate the
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
integrals for elementary molecules, which are done in Section
9
.

8.1.
Cutting, deleting and splitting

The operations we will define include cutting (see Definition
8.3
; this is the main operation that breaks
\mathbb{M}
into two smaller molecules, which corresponds to rewriting the integral
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
in (
7.6
) as an iterated integral), deleting (see Definition
8.1
; this forgets the overlap condition at an O-atom), and splitting (see Definition
8.9
; this restricts to different sub-domains in the integral, or equivalently the support of
Q
in (
7.6
)).

The notion of deleting an O-atom is already defined as part of Definition
3.10
. We repeat here for the reader’s convenience, together with a few more notions for molecules.

Definition 8.1

(Deleting and various other notions)
.

We define the following notions for a molecule
\mathbb{M}
.

(1)

Delete an O-atom.
Let
\mathfrak{o}
be an O-atom with 4 edges
e_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq 4)
connected to other atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
, where
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
are serial and same for
e_{2}
and
e_{4}
. By
deleting
the O-atom
\mathfrak{o}
, we remove
\mathfrak{o}
and the 4 edges
e_{j}
from
\mathbb{M}
, and add two bonds
e_{1}^{\prime}
between
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{3}
, and
e_{2}^{\prime}
between
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{4}
, with the same top/bottom assignment as
(e_{1},e_{3})
and
(e_{2},e_{4})
. Here if some
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
is absent, say
e_{3}
is a free end at
\mathfrak{o}
, then the bond
(e_{1},e_{3})
should be replaced by a free end at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
, etc.

(2)

Degree.
Define the
degree
of
\mathfrak{n}
(abbreviated
deg
) to be the number of edges at
\mathfrak{n}
that are not fixed ends (counting only bonds and free ends). Note that any atom has deg at most 4, and every atom has deg 4 if and only
\mathbb{M}
is full.

(3)

Sub-molecules.
Let
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be a subset of atoms. We will regard
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
as a molecule (referred to as a
sub-molecule
of
\mathbb{M}
), by viewing each bond between an atom in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
and an atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
as a free end at the atom in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
.

Next we define the important notion of regular molecules, which is an important property that governs our treatment of O-atoms and definition of cutting (Definition
8.3
) below.

Definition 8.2
.

We say
\mathbb{M}
is
regular
, if for any O-atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
, any fixed end at
\mathfrak{n}
must be serial with a free end at
\mathfrak{n}
(we say they form a
simple pair
), see
Figure
12
. This property will be assumed for all the molecules studied below, and will be preserved throughout the reduction process.

Figure 12.
Structures allowed and not allowed at O-atoms in regular molecules. The simple pair is formed by a fixed end and serial free end at an O-atom as shown on the left.

Now we introduce the operation of cutting. For some intuitions see Remark
8.6
.

Definition 8.3

(Cutting)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a regular molecule and
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be a set of atoms. The operation of
cutting

A

as free
(resp.
\mathbb{M}\backslash A

as fixed
) turns
\mathbb{M}
into a new molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
which is the disjoint union of two molecules, i.e.
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{1}\sqcup\mathbb{M}_{2}
. For convenience, we may also say that
\mathbb{M}
is “cut into two smaller molecules
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
”. We understand that all empty ends belong to
\mathbb{M}_{1}
; as for the atoms and other edges, we describe the procedure as follows, see
Figure
13
. For some intuitions see also Remark
8.6
.

(1)

The atom sets of
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
are
A
and
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
respectively. If
\mathbb{M}
contains only C-atoms (so each maximal ov-segment is just a bond), then we simply break each bond
e
between an atom in
A
and an atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
, by removing it and drawing a free end
e_{1}
(resp. fixed end
e_{2}
) at the corresponding atom in
A
(resp.
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
). We form
\mathbb{M}_{1}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{2}
) by atoms in
A
(resp.
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
) and these edges.

(2)

Now, suppose
\mathbb{M}
contains O-atoms. For each maximal ov-segment
\sigma
that contains an atom in
A
and an atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
(other maximal ov-segments are not affected), let the highest and lowest atoms of
\sigma
be
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{-}
, and let
(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{p}_{s})
be the atoms in
A\cap\sigma
(ordered high to low). Then, we take the following steps, which extend the steps in (
1
) from bonds to ov-segments, see
Figure
13
:

(a)

First remove every bond in
\sigma
. Then, for
1\leq j\leq s-1
, draw a bond between
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}
. Then, if
\mathfrak{p}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{n}^{+}
, draw a top free end at
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
; if
\mathfrak{p}_{s}\neq\mathfrak{n}^{-}
, draw a bottom free end at
\mathfrak{p}_{s}
. Let
\mathbb{M}_{1}
be formed by atoms in
A
and these edges (constructed from different
\sigma
).

(b)

Next, if
\mathfrak{n}^{+}\in\sigma\backslash A
, draw a bottom fixed end at
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
; if
\mathfrak{n}^{-}\in\sigma\backslash A
, draw a top fixed end at
\mathfrak{n}^{-}
. Then, for any
\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}\neq\mathfrak{n}\in\sigma\backslash A
, draw a simple pair at
\mathfrak{n}
consisting of a free and and a (serial) fixed end. Let
\mathbb{M}_{2}
be formed by atoms in
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
and these edges (constructed from different
\sigma
).

Figure 13.
Example of cutting (Definition
8.3
). Left: simple case of cutting without O-atoms. Right: cutting in the presence of O-atoms (showing only one maximal ov-segment
\sigma
). Here green atoms belong to
A
and become
\mathbb{M}_{1}
after cutting, while blue atoms belong to
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
and become
\mathbb{M}_{2}
after cutting. The
\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
are as indicated.

Note that, deleting O-atoms does not affect the correspondence between molecular and physical pictures under topological reduction. However, this correspondence will break down after cutting. Therefore, we will only talk in the molecule language after performing any cutting. For example, notions like
{\bm{p}}
will only be referring to particle lines as in Definition
3.1
(
2
), instead of physical particles.

In connection with the notion of cutting, we define the notion of ov-adjacency and a few more naturally related notions, see
Figure
14
:

Definition 8.4

(Ov- notions)
.

Recall molecules
\mathbb{M}
and ov-segments in Definition
3.1
.

(1)

Ov-adjacency.
We define two atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
to be
ov-adjacent
, if they are connected by an ov-segment; if
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
is an ancestor (resp. descendant) of
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
along this ov-segment, we say it is an
ov-parent
(resp.
ov-child
) of
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
.

(2)

Ov-connectedness.
For a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, we say it is
ov-connected
if for any two atoms
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in A
there exist atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\in\mathbb{M}\,(0\leq j\leq q)
such that
(\mathfrak{n}_{0},\mathfrak{n}_{q})=(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
is ov-adjacent with
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
. The maximal ov-connected subsets of
A
are called
ov-components
of
A
.

(3)

Ov-distance.
Finally, define the
ov-distance
of two subsets
B,B^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{M}
to be the minimal
q
such that there exist atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\in A\,(0\leq j\leq q)
such that
(\mathfrak{n}_{0},\mathfrak{n}_{q})\in B\times B^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
is ov-adjacent with
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
. Note that the ov-distance is
0
if
B\cap B^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, and is
\infty
if they are contained in different connected components
10
10

10

The ov-components of the
whole molecule

\mathbb{M}
are the same as the components. However the ov-components of
subsets

A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
might not be the same as its components.
of
\mathbb{M}
.

Figure 14.
The ov- related notions in Definition
8.4
. Here
A
is the set of colored atoms, which has 3 ov-components
A_{j}
represented by 3 different colors (as opposed to 7 connected components). The ov-distance between
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
is
2
, while the ov-distance between
A_{1}
and
A_{3}
is
\infty
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Regularity is preserved after deleting an O-atom or cutting a subset
A
as fixed or free.

Proof.

Simply note that, the forbidden configuration, where a fixed end at an O-atom is serial with another fixed end or a bond, cannot be constructed in the deleting or cutting process. This can be directly verified for each step in Definitions
8.1
and
8.3
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

We make a few remarks concerning Definitions
8.3
–
8.4
.

(1)

The way to understand cutting is the following: suppose we cut
A
as free (and
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
as fixed) and consider a maximal ov-segment
\sigma
as in Definition
8.3
(
2
), see
Figure
13
. Then, in
\mathbb{M}_{2}
(which has atom set
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
),
every possible bond
in
\sigma
is broken, and each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{2}
has a new fixed end. We may simply say that “the ov-segment
\sigma
is broken in
\mathbb{M}_{2}
”. On the other hand, in
\mathbb{M}_{1}
(which has atom set
A
), the ov-segments are preserved and the atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{1}\cap\sigma
are still ov-adjacent along
\sigma
(with the
\mathbb{M}_{2}
atoms between them neglected) and they do not have any new fixed end.

(2)

In particular, if we cut
A
as free, then this cutting generates (one or more) fixed end at every atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A
that is
ov-adjacent to an atom in
A
, and not at any other atom. When
\mathfrak{n}
is C-atom, this fixed end is
bottom
(resp.
top
) if
\mathfrak{n}
is
ov-parent
(resp.
ov-child
) of an atom in
A
(as for O-atoms, the bottom/top distinction is less important, but it is important that
\mathbb{M}
is regular, see Remark
8.7
).

Remark 8.7
.

Recall Definition
8.2
or regular molecules. This regularity will be retained in all algorithms: any simple pair formed by fixed end at O-atom and the serial free end is completely unrelated to the rest of the molecule (and this O-atom gets detached from both its neighbors). Some simple consequences of this will be used frequently below: (i) any O-atom of deg 2 must not be adjacent to any other atom, and cutting it does not affect the rest of the molecule; (ii) for any O-atom
\mathfrak{n}
of deg 3, it can be connected to other atoms along one ov-segment. All ov-parents and ov-children of
\mathfrak{n}
(if any) must belong to the same (maximal) ov-segment and are ov-adjacent to each other.

Remark 8.8
.

Note that, deleting O-atoms (Definition
8.1
) does not affect the correspondence between molecular and physical pictures under topological reduction. However, after cutting, this correspondence will break down, and we will only talk in the molecule language afterwards (for example notions like
{\bm{p}}
will only be referring to particle lines as in Definition
3.1
(
2
)), instead of physical particles.

We now discuss the operation of splitting into sub-cases. This is not an operation on the molecule
\mathbb{M}
but rather an operation on the domain of integration (or equivalently on the support of
Q
) in the integral (
7.6
). In reality, it is used to classify the various degenerate and non-degenerate cases witch will require different strategy in the algorithm (cf. Remark
9.5
).

Definition 8.9
.

In
splitting
, we do not change
\mathbb{M}
but split the support of
Q
in (
7.6
) as follows: decompose
1
into finitely many indicator functions
1=\sum_{j}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{j}}
, and decompose
Q=\sum_{j}Q\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{j}}
. Each of these terms corresponds to a
sub-case
where we make extra
restrictions
on the support of
Q
provided by the conditions defining
\mathcal{S}_{j}
. Examples of such defining conditions include
|x_{e}-x_{e^{\prime}}|\geq\lambda
(or oppositely
|x_{e}-x_{e^{\prime}}|\leq\lambda
),
|v_{e}-v_{e^{\prime}}|\geq\lambda
or
|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|\geq\lambda
(or opposite) etc., where
(e,e^{\prime})
are any two given edges,
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
are any two given atoms, and
\lambda
is any given quantity, cf. the definition of good molecules in Definition
9.4
.

Now we define the notion of operation sequences, which are sequences consisting of operations of deleting, cutting and splitting:

Definition 8.10

(Operation sequence)
.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
and
Q
, define an
operation sequence
to be a sequence of operations acting on
\mathbb{M}
or
Q
, such that each one is either
cutting
a subset of
\mathbb{M}
as in Definition
8.3
, or
deleting
an O-atom in
\mathbb{M}
as in Definition
8.1
, or
splitting
into finitely many sub-cases (by decomposing
1
into indicator functions) as in Definition
8.9
. We require that all the deletings must occur before all the cuttings (splittings can occur anywhere in the sequence). If an operation sequence does not contain splitting, then we call it a
cutting sequence
.

Finally, we define the elementary molecules, which are the result of the cutting sequences we will construct in the algorithms below, and the main object of study in Section
9
:

Definition 8.11

(Elementary molecules)
.

We define a molecule
\mathbb{M}
to be elementary, if it satisfies one of the properties, see
Figure
15
:

(1)

{2} molecules
:
\mathbb{M}
contains one atom of deg 2, with both fixed ends being top (or both being bottom) in the case of C-atom, and with the two fixed ends not serial in the case of O-atom.

(2)

{3} molecules
:
\mathbb{M}
contains one atom of deg 3.

(3)

{4} molecules
:
\mathbb{M}
contains one atom of deg 4.

(4)

{33} molecules
:
\mathbb{M}
contains two atoms connected by a bond, which are both deg 3. We say
\mathbb{M}
is
{33A} molecule
if we can cut one atom as free such that the other atom becomes a {2} molecule as defined above. Otherwise we say
\mathbb{M}
is
{33B} molecule
.

(5)

{44} molecules
:
\mathbb{M}
contains two atoms connected by a bond, which are both deg 4.

Figure 15.
The elementary molecules as in Definition
8.11
. Note the good/normal/bad property of this molecule depends on the extra restrictions on the variables due to splitting, see Definition
9.4
; the edges
e_{j}
and atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
are marked as will be used in the proof of Propositions
9.1
–
9.3
.

8.2.
Operations on associated operators

In this subsection, we study the behavior of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
in (
7.6
) under the cutting, deleting and splitting operations. Namely, we will prove
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\leq\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q)
after deleting (Propositions
8.13
) and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q)
after cutting (Proposition
8.14
).

Recall the integral (
7.6
), where
Q
is a function of
\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}}
. If we perform deleting or cutting to
\mathbb{M}
and turn it into
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
or
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{1}\sqcup\mathbb{M}_{2}
, then we will get a new set of vector variables
z_{e}
associated with the new molecule (the time variables remain the same as atoms are not affected by cutting). In order to define
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q)
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q)
for the same
Q
, we need to match the vector variables for
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
with those for
\mathbb{M}
. More precisely, we need to define the following two mappings:

•

A mapping from the set of
bonds plus free ends
of
\mathbb{M}
to that of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
; this decides how we transform the function
Q
in (
7.6
) between the integral variables of
\mathbb{M}
and those of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
.

•

A mapping from the set of new
fixed ends
of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
to the set of new
bonds plus free ends
of
\mathbb{M}_{1}
; this decides, while evaluating
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q)
, the value that
z_{f}
is
fixed to be
, for each fixed end
f
of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
.

In Definition
8.12
below, we will construct these mappings under deleting and cutting operations, which provides the correspondences between vector variables
z_{e}
.

Definition 8.12

(Transformation of associated variables under cutting and deleting)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is formed from
\mathbb{M}
by deleting or cutting. Define the following mappings:

(1)

The case of deleting
. Let
e_{j}
and
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
be as in Definition
8.1
(
1
), we define the mapping
e_{1},e_{3}\mapsto e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2},e_{4}\mapsto e_{2}^{\prime}
. This means, we identify the variables
z_{e_{1}}
and
z_{e_{3}}
before deleting, with
z_{e_{1}^{\prime}}
after deleting, and same for
(e_{2},e_{4})
.

(2)

The case of cutting: no O-atoms
. As in Definition
8.3
(
1
), for each bond
e
between
A
and
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
that is turned into a free end
e_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{1}
and a fixed end
e_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
, we define the mapping
e\mapsto e_{1}
and
e_{2}\mapsto e_{1}
. This means, we identify
z_{e}
before cutting with
z_{e_{1}}
after cutting, and also fix the fixed end variable
z_{e_{2}}
to be
z_{e_{1}}
.

(3)

The case of cutting: general
. Let
\sigma
and related notions be as in Definition
8.3
(
2
), we define the mapping as follows. For each bond
e\in\sigma
, consider the two atoms of
e
, and the one that is closer to
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
; we then map
e
to the free end
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
at this atom. For each fixed end
e\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
, we map it to the same bottom edge
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{1}\cap\sigma
at
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
(it could be a free end or a bond). For example, in
Figure
16
, we have
e_{j}\mapsto e_{j}^{\prime}
and
f_{j}\mapsto e_{1}^{\prime}
. We also identify and fix the relevant variables as in (
1
)–(
2
).

Figure 16.
The transformation of associated variables under cutting (Definition
8.3
), see Definition
8.12
. This picture shows the case of the example shown in
Figure
13
, where
e_{j}\mapsto e_{j}^{\prime}
and
f_{j}\mapsto e_{1}^{\prime}
in the notation of the mapping in Definition
8.12
. Note that all the
z_{e}
variables for edges
e
coming from this ov-segment are all equal to each other, see the proof of Proposition
8.14
.

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
be formed from
\mathbb{M}
by deleting one O-atom (Definition
8.1
). Then we have

(8.1)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\leq\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q),\qquad\forall\,Q\geq 0.

Proof.

Note that the power
|\mathcal{E}_{*}|-2|\mathbb{M}|
in (
7.6
) is the same for
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, as we have
1
less atom and
2
less bonds/free ends when going from
\mathbb{M}
to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
. Now let
e_{j}
and
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
be as in Definition
8.1
(
1
), then we have
z_{e_{1}^{\prime}}=z_{e_{1}}=z_{e_{3}}
by the mapping in Definition
8.12
(
1
) and the Dirac factors in (
7.4
), and similarly
z_{e_{2}^{\prime}}=z_{e_{2}}=z_{e_{4}}
. Concerning the integral in (
7.6
), using the identification of variables in Definition
8.12
, we see that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
is the same as
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q)
but with the extra integral

\int\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{3}}-z_{e_{1}})\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{4}}-z_{e_{2}})\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{2}}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega]_{-}\,\mathrm{d}z_{e_{3}}\mathrm{d}z_{e_{4}}\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}.

By (
7.15
) we know this integral equals an indicator function, which is
\leq 1
, hence
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\leq\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q)
.
∎

Proposition 8.14
.

Assume that a molecule
\mathbb{M}
is cut into
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
(Definition
8.3
), where
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
are cut as free and fixed respectively. Then we have

(8.2)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q).

Proof.

Recall the formula (
7.6
). Let
\mathcal{E}_{*,j}
and
\mathcal{E}_{*}^{\prime}
be the
\mathcal{E}_{*}
sets for
\mathbb{M}_{j}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
respectively, then it is easy to see that
|\mathbb{M}|=|\mathbb{M}_{1}|+|\mathbb{M}_{2}|
and
|\mathcal{E}_{*}|=|\mathcal{E}_{*,1}|+|\mathcal{E}_{*,2}|=|\mathcal{E}_{*}^{\prime}|
, so the powers on both sides of (
8.2
) match. Below we will neglect these powers of
\varepsilon
for simplicity. Now by (
7.6
), we have

(8.3)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg)\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}.

On the other hand, by Fubini we have

(8.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{1,*}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}_{1}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{1}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{1,*}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{2,*}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}_{2}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{2}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}\bigg)\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{2,*}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*}^{\prime}|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg)\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}.

Here in the second line in (
8.4
), we are identifying the variables
\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}^{\prime}}
with the variables
\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}}
, and fixing each fixed end variable
z_{e}
in
\mathbb{M}_{2}
to be the corresponding free end variable
z_{e^{\prime}}
in
\mathbb{M}_{1}
, by the mapping in Definition
8.12
. it then suffices to show that (
8.3
) equals (
8.4
) under the above identification and assignments.

No O-atom case.
Assume
\mathbb{M}
has no O-atom, then we are in the simpler scenario in Definition
8.3
(
1
). In this case, each new free end
e_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{1}
is paired with a new fixed end
e_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
and they come from breaking a bond
e\in\mathbb{M}
; by Definition
8.12
(
2
) we see that the integration in (
8.3
) and (
8.4
) are the same. Moreover, the
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
Q
factors are also the same under the above identification and assignments, so trivially (
8.3
) equals (
8.4
).

General case.
In the general case with O-atoms, the identification and assignments of variables and more complicated. We only need to consider a fixed maximal ov-segment
\sigma
(along with the relevant notions
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
) as in Definition
8.3
(
2
). Let the bonds in
\sigma
be
e_{j},\,(1\leq j\leq q)
from top to bottom, then in view of the Dirac factors in (
7.4
), we know the integral (
8.3
) involves the Dirac factors

(8.5)

\prod_{j=1}^{q-1}\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{j+1}}-z_{e_{j}}).

On the other hand, for each
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\in A\cap\sigma
, consider the bond connecting
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
to its child in
\sigma
, and let it be
e_{j_{i}}
. Then, by the identification and assignments as in Definition
8.12
(
3
), we know that the integral (
8.4
) involves the Dirac factors

(8.6)

\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}\bm{\delta}\big(z_{e_{j_{i+1}}}-z_{e_{j_{i}}}\big)\cdot\prod_{e\not\in\{e_{j_{i}}\}}\bm{\delta}\big(z_{e}-z_{e_{j_{1}}}\big)

It is easy to see that (
8.5
)=(
8.6
), as both sides are exactly the Dirac mass supported on the linear subspace
\{z_{e_{1}}=\cdots=z_{e_{q}}\}
. For example, in the context of
Figure
16
, where
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})=(1,4,5)
, we have

\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{1}}-z_{e_{2}})\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{2}}-z_{e_{3}})\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{3}}-z_{e_{4}})\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{4}}-z_{e_{5}})=\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{1}}-z_{e_{4}})\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{4}}-z_{e_{5}})\cdot\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{2}}-z_{e_{1}})\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{3}}-z_{e_{1}}).

Finally, note that all the Dirac functions in (
8.5
) and (
8.6
) imply that all the variables
z_{e_{j}}
are equal, so by inserting them into the rest of the
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
Q
factors in (
8.3
) and (
8.4
), we conclude that these remaining factors are also the same for (
8.3
) and (
8.4
). This then shows that (
8.3
) equals (
8.4
), and completes the proof of Proposition
8.14
.
∎

Remark 8.15
.

In general, (
8.2
) holds with both sides being interpreted in the sense of distributions. However, in this paper, we will only encounter the case where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
is a function whenever
Q
is, in which case (
8.2
) holds classically. The condition that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
is a function is really a condition on the molecule
\mathbb{M}
, which restricts the number and places of its fixed ends, and will always be satisfies throughout the algorithm. That being said, one can still interpret (
8.2
) in bigger generality in the sense of distributions as follows: If one replaces all the delta functions appearing in the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
by approximations of identity, then the analog of formula (
8.2
) holds with no ambiguity. The same result then holds by taking the limits of those approximations of identity (in the appropriate distributional sense).

Note that the operators
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
in Proposition
8.14
in general do not commute (unless
\mathbb{M}
has no fixed end). Therefore, it is important to order the resulting components after cutting, such that
\mathbb{M}_{1}
which is cut as free always occurs to the left of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
which is cut as fixed (i.e. the operator
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
is applied first, and the corresponding variables are integrated first in the iterated integral). This ordering is defined in the following definition.

Definition 8.16

(Ordering components after cutting)
.

Suppose a molecule
\mathbb{M}
becomes a new molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
after a sequence of cutting operations; we refer to the connected components of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
as final components. We define a total ordering
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}
between these final components inductively, as follows (see
Figure
46
for an example):

(1)

In the first cutting operation, suppose
\mathbb{M}
is cut into
\mathbb{M}_{1}
(as free) and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
(as fixed). Then for any final component
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, we require that
\mathcal{X}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathcal{Y}
if
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{2}
.

(2)

For the final components that are contained in
\mathbb{M}_{1}
(resp. in
\mathbb{M}_{2}
), order them inductively using
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}
defined for the subsequent sequence of cutting operations applied to
\mathbb{M}_{1}
(resp. to
\mathbb{M}_{2}
).

If
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is the result of
\mathbb{M}
after (deleting and) cutting, and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is the disjoint union of its final components, i.e.
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathbb{M}_{k}
with
\mathbb{M}_{1}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\cdots\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathbb{M}_{k}
, then we define the
associated integral operator
by

(8.7)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{k}}.

Remark 8.17
.

As explained above, if
\mathbb{M}_{i}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathbb{M}_{j}
, then the operator
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
is applied first, and the
\mathbb{M}_{j}
variables are integrated first. In many cases of the algorithm below, each time we will cut an elementary component as free, and work on the remaining molecule (cf. Remark
10.7
below); in this case
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}
is just the order of being cut, i.e.
\mathcal{Y}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathcal{X}
if and only if
\mathcal{Y}
is cut before
\mathcal{X}
. This ordering will be useful in identifying a good molecule after splitting, see Definition
9.4
(
3a
), (
3e
).

Suppose we have a cutting sequence, and one of the cuttings breaks a maximal ov-segment
\sigma
as in Definition
8.3
and creates free and fixed ends. Then for any such fixed end
e
, there always exists a free end or bond
e^{\prime}
such that
z_{e}=z_{e^{\prime}}
, and we have
\mathcal{Y}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathcal{X}
where
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are the final components containing
e
and
e^{\prime}
respectively. In fact, we can choose
e^{\prime}
to be the image of
e
under the mapping in Definition
8.12
(
3
), then
\mathcal{Y}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathcal{X}
follows from Definition
8.16
(
1
). This will also be helpful in getting good molecules below.

By putting together Definition
8.9
, Proposition
8.13
and Proposition
8.14
, we arrive at the main conclusion of this section, namely

Proposition 8.18
.

Start with a full molecule
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
as in (
7.7
), where
Q=Q_{\mathbb{M}}
as in (
7.8
). If we apply any cutting sequence (Definition
8.10
) to
\mathbb{M}
and turn it to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, with the associated variables transformed as in Definition
8.12
, then we always have

(8.8)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\leq\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q),

where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
is defined as in (
8.7
) with
\mathbb{M}_{1}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\cdots\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathbb{M}_{k}
being the final components of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
.

If we apply any operation sequence (including also splitting which operates on the support of
Q
) to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
and get a contribution
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
for each sub-case, then we have

(8.9)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\leq\sum_{\textrm{sub-cases}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}),

where the summation is taken over all sub-cases. Note that
Q^{\prime}=Q\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}
for some
\mathcal{P}
representing the restriction in this sub-case (see Definition
8.9
).

Proof.

We only need to prove (
8.8
). In fact, for any splitting
Q=\sum_{j}Q\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{j}}
as in Definition
8.9
, we trivially have
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\sum_{j}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{j}})
, so (
8.9
) follows from (
8.8
).

Now to prove (
8.8
), using Proposition
8.13
, we can treat the deleting operation and only need to consider cuttings, in which case we will prove the equality in (
8.8
). We proceed by induction: the case of one cutting follows from Proposition
8.14
. Now for any
\mathbb{M}
, say the first cutting cuts
\mathbb{M}_{1}
as free and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
as fixed. In subsequent cuttings, assume
\mathbb{M}_{1}
is cut into
\mathbb{M}_{1}^{\prime}=\sqcup_{j}\mathbb{M}_{1j}
(in increasing
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}
order) and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
is cut into
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\prime}=\sqcup_{j}\mathbb{M}_{2j}
(in increasing
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}
order), then the final result of the cutting sequence is
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=(\sqcup_{j}\mathbb{M}_{1j})\sqcup(\sqcup_{j}\mathbb{M}_{2j})
in increasing
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}
order, due to Definition
8.16
. Then by induction hypothesis we have

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}(Q)=\bigg(\prod_{j}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1j}}\bigg)\circ\bigg(\prod_{j}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2j}}\bigg)(Q)=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q),

which completes the proof.
∎

9.
Estimating the integral
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})

In this section, we complete the proof of Proposition
6.2
, pending the proof of the pure combinatorial Proposition
9.7
(which is in Sections
10
–
13
). Starting from Proposition
3.25
, using Propositions
7.2
,
7.5
and
8.18
, we can reduce Proposition
6.2
to the estimate of quantities of form
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
, where
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
(and
Q^{\prime}
) are possible results of an operation sequence applied to
\mathbb{M}
(and
Q=Q_{\mathbb{M}}
), corresponding to possible sub-cases.

The operation sequence is constructed in Proposition
9.7
using the algorithms in Sections
10
–
13
. In Proposition
9.7
we can guarantee that:

•

The number of sub-cases is acceptable (i.e.
\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
in Proposition
9.7
);

•

\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is formed by elementary molecules (Definition
8.11
);

•

We have adequate control of these elementary molecules (and associated support information of
Q^{\prime}
) in terms of good and bad molecules (Definition
9.4
and (
9.51
)).

The goal of this section is to use the above information to prove an estimate for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
, namely (
9.53
). Note that this
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
is a composition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
where
\mathbb{M}_{j}
are components of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
which are elementary molecules. In Section
9.1
we will estimate each
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
, and classify them as good, normal, and bad in Definition
9.4
. Then in Section
9.2
, we put together these local estimates and apply Proposition
9.7
, to prove (
9.53
) and thus finish the proof of Proposition
6.2
. These arguments rely on some technical estimates concerning volumes of
(v_{e})
and cross-sections in (
7.6
), which we leave to Section
9.3
.

9.1.
Integrals for elementary molecules

In this subsection, we prove upper bounds for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
for an elementary molecule
\mathbb{M}
, in Propositions
9.1
–
9.3
. We will use
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
e_{j}
to denote atoms and edges in
\mathbb{M}
, and abbreviate
t_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}:=t_{j}
and
z_{e_{j}}:=z_{j}
. For future reference, let us explicitly write out the support of
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}},z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})=\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},t_{1})
, which is defined in (
7.3
) and (
7.4
), as follows:

(9.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} x_{2}&=x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})-\varepsilon\omega,\\
x_{3}&=x_{1}+t_{1}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega,\\
v_{3}&=v_{1}-\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega,\\
x_{4}&=x_{2}-t_{1}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega,\\
v_{4}&=v_{2}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega,\end{aligned}\right.\ \textrm{if $\mathfrak{n}$ is C-atom;}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} x_{2}&=x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})-\varepsilon\omega,\\
x_{3}&=x_{1},\\
v_{3}&=v_{1},\\
x_{4}&=x_{2}=x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})-\varepsilon\omega,\\
v_{4}&=v_{2}\end{aligned}\right.\ \textrm{if $\mathfrak{n}$ is O-atom.}

Proposition 9.1

(Upper bounds for one-atom
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
be defined as in (
7.6
), where
\mathbb{M}
contains only one atom
\mathfrak{n}
with 4 edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{4}
, see
Figure
15
. We assume that
e_{1}
(resp.
e_{2}
) is serial with
e_{3}
(resp.
e_{4}
). Let
Q
be a function supported in
|v_{j}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
and
|x_{j}-x_{j}^{0}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, where
(x_{j},v_{j})=z_{e_{j}}
and
x_{j}^{0}
are fixed vectors, and also
\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathcal{D}
with
\mathcal{D}
as in (
7.5
).

(1)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {2} molecule as in Definition
8.11
, i.e. the two fixed ends are either both bottom or both top, by symmetry we may assume
(e_{1},e_{2})
are fixed and
(e_{3},e_{4})
are free. Then

(9.2)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot Q.

Here in (
9.2
), the indicator
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}
is defined to be
1
if and only if there exists a unique
t_{1}
such that

(9.3)

|x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})|=\varepsilon,\quad(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\leq 0,

where
\omega:=\varepsilon^{-1}(x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2}))
is a unit vector. We also have the following estimate

(9.4)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}

Moreover, in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
, the function
Q
depends on
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},t_{1})
. On the right-hand side of (
9.2
),
Q
becomes a function of
(z_{1},z_{2})
, obtained by a change of variables applied to the original
Q
. This change of variables is determined as follows:
t_{1}
is expressed as a function of
(z_{1},z_{2})
via (
9.3
), and
(z_{3},z_{4})
is defined as a function of
(z_{1},z_{2})
, either by the last four equations in the left half of (
9.1
) if
\mathbb{M}
is a C-atom, or by
(z_{3},z_{4})=(z_{1},z_{2})
if
\mathbb{M}
is an O-atom.

(2)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {3} molecule as in Definition
8.11
, by symmetry we may assume
e_{1}
is fixed and
(e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
are free. Then

(9.5)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{2}.

Here in (
9.5
),
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},t_{1})
in the input variables of
Q
are functions of the integral variables
(t,\omega,v_{2})
given by (
9.1
), with
(x_{1},v_{1})
fixed. In the integral (
9.5
) the domain of integration can be restricted to
t_{1}\in[(\ell[\mathfrak{n}]-1)\tau,\ell[\mathfrak{n}]\tau]
since
\mathrm{supp}\,(Q)\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.

(3)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {4} molecule as in Definition
8.11
. Then

(9.6)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}v_{1}\mathrm{d}v_{2}.

Here in (
9.6
),
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},t_{1})
in the input variables of
Q
are functions of the integral variables
(t,\omega,x_{1},v_{2},v_{2})
given by (
9.1
). In the integral (
9.6
) the domain of integration can be restricted to
t_{1}\in[(\ell[\mathfrak{n}]-1)\tau,\ell[\mathfrak{n}]\tau]
since
\mathrm{supp}\,Q\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.
We also have the following estimate

(9.7)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(4)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is an empty end. Then

(9.8)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q):=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}Q\,\mathrm{d}z_{e}.

We also have the following estimate

(9.9)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Proof.

We may assume
\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}=1
. We divide the proof into several parts.

Proof of (
1
).
We start with (
9.2
) and consider the C-atom case (the O-atom case is similar and much easier). Note that here we are fixing
(x_{1},v_{1},x_{2},v_{2})
and integrating in
(x_{3},v_{3},x_{4},v_{4})
and
t_{1}
, which is the same as in (
7.15
) in the proof of Proposition
7.5
. The equality (
9.2
) then follows from the same proof, where we first integrate in
(x_{3},x_{4})
, then in
(v_{3},v_{4})
, and then in
t_{1}
. Then (
9.4
) follows directly from (
9.2
).

Proof of (
2
).
Now consider (
9.5
); again consider the C-atom case, and note that here we are fixing
(x_{1},v_{1})
and integrating in
(t_{1},x_{2},v_{2},x_{3},v_{3},x_{4},v_{4})
, i.e.

(9.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6d}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{4}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{4}-v_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(v_{3}-v_{1}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{4}-v_{2}-[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\cdot Q(x_{1},v_{1},\cdots,x_{4},v_{4},t_{1})\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\prod_{j=2}^{4}\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}v_{j},

where
\omega:=\varepsilon^{-1}(x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2}))
is a unit vector. In (
9.10
), once we fix
(x_{2},v_{2})
, then the integral in
(x_{3},x_{4},v_{3},v_{4})
is given by a change of variables in the function
Q
, so we have

(9.11)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\cdot Q(x_{1},v_{1},\cdots,x_{4},v_{4},t_{1})\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\mathrm{d}v_{2},

where
\omega
is as above, and

(9.12)

\left\{\begin{aligned} x_{3}&=x_{1}+t_{1}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega,\\
v_{3}&=v_{1}-\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega,\\
x_{4}&=x_{2}-t_{1}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega,\\
v_{4}&=v_{2}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]\omega.\end{aligned}\right.

Now in (
9.11
), we may fix
v_{2}
and make the change of variables
x_{2}\leftrightarrow\omega
, which leads to

(9.13)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{\delta}(|\omega|-1)\cdot\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\cdot Q(x_{1},v_{1},\cdots,x_{4},v_{4},t_{1})\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{2},

where
x_{2}=x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})-\varepsilon\omega
, which matches (
9.1
) together with (
9.12
). The integral in
\omega
in (
9.13
) is done on the unit sphere
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, which then gives (
9.5
).

Proof of (
3
).
By integrating in the variables
(x_{1},v_{1})
in (
9.5
), we also obtain (
9.6
). Then (
9.7
) immediately follows from (
9.6
) and the fact that
\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
on the support of
Q
(which is due to our support assumptions for
v_{e}
).

Proof of (
4
).
This immediately follows from (
7.6
) and the support assumptions for
x_{e}
and
v_{e}
.
∎

Proposition 9.2

(Better upper bounds for one-atom
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
)
.

Consider the same setting (with
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
for an one-atom molecule
\mathbb{M}
etc.) as in Proposition
9.1
above, but with extra restrictions on the support of
Q
, which are specified below.

(1)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {3} molecule (by symmetry we may assume
e_{1}
is fixed and
(e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
are free), and we further restrict the support of
Q
to be a subset of

(9.14)

|t_{1}-t^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|v_{i}-v_{j}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|x_{j}-x^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}

for some
0<\upsilon<1/2
. Here
(x^{*},v^{*},t^{*})
are some external parameters (it is possible that
x^{*}
or
v^{*}
coincide with
x_{1}
or
v_{1}
), and the support of
Q
may depend on these parameters. Moreover, in the second and fourth cases in (
9.14
), we require
i\neq j
, and also
\{i,j\}\neq\{1,3\},\{2,4\}
when
\mathbb{M}
is an O-atom. In the third case we require
j\neq 1
, and also
j\neq 3
when
\mathbb{M}
is an O-atom.

Then we have the following estimate (uniform in all parameters):

(9.15)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(2)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {4} molecule, and we further restrict the support of
Q
to be a subset of

(9.16)

|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|x_{i}-x^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|v_{i}-v_{j}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}

for some
0<\upsilon<1/2
. Here
x^{*}
is an external parameter as in (
1
). Moreover we require
i\neq j
, and also
\{i,j\}\neq\{1,3\},\{2,4\}
when
\mathbb{M}
is an O-atom. Then we have the following estimate (uniform in all parameters):

(9.17)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)+\upsilon}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}

(3)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is an empty end with an edge
e_{1}
and associated variable
z_{1}=(x_{1},v_{1})
, and we replace the
Q
in (
7.6
) by
\mathbbm{1}_{*}^{\varepsilon}\cdot Q
, where

(9.18)

\mathbbm{1}_{*}^{\varepsilon}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1\quad&&|x_{1}-x^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon},\\
&\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad&&|x_{1}-x^{*}|\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon},\end{aligned}\right.

for some
0<\upsilon<1/2
. Here
x^{*}
is an external parameter as in (
1
). Then we have the following estimate (uniform in
x^{*}
)

(9.19)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\mathbbm{1}_{*}^{\varepsilon}\cdot Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{-(d-1)+\upsilon}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(4)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is {3} or {4} molecules, and we
do not
make any restriction on the support of
Q
as in (
1
)–(
2
), but
replace

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
with
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\mathbbm{1}_{*}^{\varepsilon}Q)
where

(9.20)

\mathbbm{1}_{*}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1\quad&&|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon},\\
&\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad&&|x-x^{\prime}|\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\end{aligned}\right.

for some
0<\upsilon<1/2
. Here
(x,x^{\prime})
is either
(x_{i},x^{*})
or
(x_{i},x_{j})
,
x^{*}
is an external parameter, and the indices
i
and
j
also satisfy the requirements in (
1
) and (
2
) respectively. Then the estimates (
9.15
) and (
9.17
) hold true.

Proof.

We may assume
\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}=1
. We divide the proof into several parts.

Proof of (
1
) and (
2
).
Recall that the expression of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
is given by (
9.5
) and (
9.6
), where the variables
(x_{i},v_{i})
are given by (
9.1
). Consider the conditions (
9.14
) and (
9.16
). The case
|t_{1}-t^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
in (
9.14
) is trivial as we gain
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
in the integral in
t_{1}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
, which proves (
9.15
). Next assume
|v_{i}-v_{j}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
in (
9.14
) and
\mathbb{M}
is a C-atom (the O-atom case is the same as long as
\{i,j\}\neq\{1,3\},\{2,4\}
); let
v_{1}-v_{2}:=u
and
u-(u\cdot\omega)\omega:=u^{\perp}
, then we have

(9.21)

either
|u|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
, or
|u\cdot\omega|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
, or
|u^{\perp}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
, or
|u-2(u\cdot\omega)\omega|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
.

Note that
|u-2(u\cdot\omega)\omega|=|u|
, and
|u|^{2}=|u\cdot\omega|^{2}+|u^{\perp}|^{2}
, we may reduce to one of the cases
|u\cdot\omega|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
, or
|u^{\perp}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
. With fixed
\omega
(say
\omega=(1,0,\cdots,0)
by symmetry), in either case, at least one coordinate of
u
is bounded by
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
, and all the other coordinates (as well as the weight
((v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega)_{-}
) are bounded by
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, which implies (
9.15
) by this volume gain.

Next, assume
|x_{j}-x^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
(or similarly
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
) in (
9.14
), then since the distance between any two
x_{j}+t_{1}v_{j}
is at most
\varepsilon
, we must have
|(x_{1}-x^{*})+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{j})|\leq 2\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
; by dyadically decomposing
|t_{1}|
and gaining from both the support of
t_{1}
and the restriction on
v_{1}-v_{j}
as above, we can obtain a volume gain which is
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
, and this is enough to prove (
9.15
). The cases of (
9.16
) involving
x_{i}-x_{j}
and
v_{i}-v_{j}
follow similarly, and the case involving
x_{i}-x^{*}
is trivial as we gain from integrating in
x_{1}
.

Proof of (
3
) and (
4
).
The bound in (
3
) is immediate (we gain from the
x_{1}
integral in (
9.8
) if
|x_{1}-x^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
). For (
4
), the case
|x-x^{\prime}|\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
is also immediate. For the case
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
, the estimate follows by the same argument as in the
|x_{j}-x^{*}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
case from the proofs of (
1
) and (
2
).
∎

Proposition 9.3

(Better upper bounds for two-atom
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
be defined as in (
7.6
), where
\mathbb{M}
is a regular molecule containing only two atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}
with 7 edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{7}
, see
Figure
15
.
We also denote by
e
the edge between
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
, which equals some
e_{j}
. Let
Q
be a function supported in
|v_{j}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
and
|x_{j}-x_{j}^{0}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, and also
\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathcal{D}
, as in Proposition
9.1
. Consider the following cases:

(1)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {33A} molecule with atoms
(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})
. Let
e
be the bond connecting them and
(e_{1},e_{7})
be the two fixed ends at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
respectively. Assume moreover that either (i)
\ell[\mathfrak{n}_{1}]\neq\ell[\mathfrak{n}_{2}]
for the two atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
, or (ii) the support of
Q
is a subset of

(9.22)

\max(|x_{1}-x_{7}|,|v_{1}-v_{7}|)\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|t_{1}-t_{2}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}

for some
0<\upsilon<1/3
.

(2)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {33B} molecule with two C-atoms. Assume the support of
Q
is a subset of

(9.23)

|t_{1}-t_{2}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad|v_{i}-v_{j}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\textrm{for\ any\ edge\ }(e_{i},e_{j})\textrm{\ at\ the\ same\ atom},

for some
0<\upsilon<1/(6d)
.

(3)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a {44} molecule with atoms
(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})
and
(e_{1},e_{7})
being two free ends at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
respectively, such that
\mathbb{M}
becomes a {33A} molecule after turning these two free ends into fixed ends. Moreover assume the support if
Q
is a subset of

(9.24)

\max(|x_{1}-x_{7}|,|v_{1}-v_{7}|)\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\textrm{and}\quad|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}.

for some
0<\upsilon<1/(4d)
.

Then, in any of the above cases (
1
)–(
3
), we have

(9.25)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Proof.

We may assume
\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}=1
. We divide the proof into several parts.

Proof of (
1
).
Since
\mathbb{M}
is a {33A} molecule (Definition
8.11
), by symmetry, we may assume that
\{\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
becomes a {2} molecule after cutting
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
as free. First assume (ii) is true. To calculate
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
, we cut
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
as free to obtain
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\mathcal{I}_{\{\mathfrak{n}_{1}\}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\{\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}}(Q)
. By assumption, we know that
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
becomes a deg 2 atom with two top fixed ends. The inner integral
\mathcal{I}_{\{\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}}
can be computed using (
9.2
) in Proposition
9.1
.

Let the bond between
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
be
e
, denote the edges at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
by
(e_{1}.\cdots,e_{4})
as in Proposition
9.1
, then
e=e_{j}
for some
j\in\{2,3,4\}
and
j\neq 3
if
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
is an O-atom (because
\mathbb{M}
is regular, a fixed end cannot be serial with a bond at O-atom). After plugging in the above formula for the inner integral
\mathcal{I}_{\{\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}}
, we can reduce
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
to an integral of form
\mathcal{I}_{\{\mathfrak{n}_{1}\}}
as described in (
2
) of Proposition
9.1
, namely

(9.26)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(z_{j},z_{7})\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{2},

where the input variables of
Q
are explicit functions of
(t_{1},\omega,v_{2})
and
(z_{1},z_{7})
using Proposition
9.1
and the fact that
z_{j}
satisfies (
9.1
). To prove (
9.25
) for (
1
), it suffices to control the volume of the support of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}
(which is a set depending on
z_{1}
and
z_{7}
).

By definition (
9.3
) of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}
, we know this restriction implies that

(9.27)

|(x_{j}-x_{7})\wedge(v_{j}-v_{7})|\leq\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}

for some
2\leq j\leq 4
, where
j\neq 3
if
\mathfrak{n}
is O-atom. Let
v_{1}-v_{2}=u
and
u-(u\cdot\omega)\omega=u^{\perp}
. Plugging in the formula (
9.1
), and noting that
|x_{j}-(x_{1}+t_{1}u_{j})|\leq\varepsilon
where
u_{j}=v_{1}-v_{j}
and
(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})=(u,(u\cdot\omega)\omega,u^{\perp})
if
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
is a C-atom and
(u_{2},u_{4})=(u,u)
if
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
is an O-atom, we obtain that

(9.28)

\big|\big(x_{1}-x_{7}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{7})\big)\wedge u_{j}-\bm{g}\big|\leq\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}

for some constant 2-form
\bm{g}
depending only on
(x_{1},v_{1},x_{7},v_{7})
.

In (
9.28
), by dyadic decomposition, we may assume
|x_{1}-x_{7}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{7})|\sim\varepsilon^{\beta}
(say with
0<\beta<1
, the endpoint cases are treated in the standard way), then with fixed
(x_{1},v_{1},x_{7},v_{7})
and
t_{1}
, the vector
u_{j}
must belong to a one-dimensional tube in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which has length
\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
and width
\varepsilon^{1-\beta}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
. Note that
u_{j}\in\{u,(u\cdot\omega)\omega,u-(u\cdot\omega)\omega\}
, by elementary calculations, we see that this restriction on
u_{j}
implies that
(u,\omega)
must belong to a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with volume
\leq\varepsilon^{1-\beta}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
. This already implies (
9.25
), if
\beta\leq 1-\upsilon
.

Now suppose

|x_{1}-x_{7}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{7})|\sim\varepsilon^{\beta},\quad\beta>1-\upsilon,

we shall exploit the
t_{1}
integral in (
9.26
). If
|v_{1}-v_{7}|\gtrsim\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
, then
t_{1}
belongs to an interval of length
\leq\varepsilon^{\beta-1+\upsilon}
, which then implies (
9.25
) together with the above volume bound for
(u,\omega)
. If
|v_{1}-v_{7}|\ll\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
, then we must also have
|x_{1}-x_{7}|\ll\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
, while by the support of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(z_{j},z_{7})
we also get

(9.29)

|x_{j}-x_{7}+t_{2}(v_{j}-v_{7})|=\varepsilon,\quad|x_{j}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{j}-v_{1})|\in\{0,\varepsilon\},

which implies
|t_{2}-t_{1}|\cdot|v_{j}-v_{1}|\lesssim_{*}\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
given that
(x_{7},v_{7})
is close to
(x_{1},v_{1})
. Since now we must have
|t_{2}-t_{1}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
by (
9.22
), we conclude that
|v_{j}-v_{1}|\lesssim_{*}\varepsilon^{1-2\upsilon}
which again implies (
9.25
) as in the proof of Proposition
9.1
(
2
).

Finally, assume (i) is true but (ii) is not, by the above proof, we may assume that
Q
is supported in the set where
|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
. But since
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
must belong to disjoint time intervals in view of
\ell[\mathfrak{n}_{1}]\neq\ell[\mathfrak{n}_{2}]
, we conclude that
t_{1}
itself must belong to a fixed interval of length at most
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
. Then (
9.25
) follows from exploiting the volume of support of
t_{1}
. This completes the proof of (
1
).

Proof of (
2
).
Now consider the case of {33B} molecules. Recall that in this case both atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
are
C
atoms, the higher atom
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
has one top fixed end, and the lower atom
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
has a bottom fixed end. Concerning the serial relations of edges and up to symmetry, there are 3 cases for the molecule
\mathbb{M}
(see
Figure
15
), which we list below.

Case 1.
This is the case when both fixed ends are serial with the common edge
e
. Here

(9.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{10d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{4}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{4}-v_{2})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{3}-v_{1}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(v_{4}-v_{2}-[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{6}-x_{5}+t_{2}(v_{6}-v_{5})\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(x_{7}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{3})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{6}-v_{5}+[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{7}-v_{3}-[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{5}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{5}-v_{3})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta\big]_{+}\cdot Q(x_{1},v_{1},\cdots,x_{7},v_{7},t_{1},t_{2})\prod_{j=2}^{6}\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}v_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j},

where
\omega:=\varepsilon^{-1}(x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2}))
and
\eta:=\varepsilon^{-1}(x_{5}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{5}-v_{3}))
are two unit vectors. Here we first integrate in
(x_{4},v_{4},x_{6},v_{6})
, then make the change of variables
x_{5}\leftrightarrow\eta
to reduce to

(9.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{3}-v_{1}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})|-\varepsilon\big)\bm{\delta}\big(x_{7}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{3})\big)\cdot\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\prod_{j=2}^{3}\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}v_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{\delta}\big(v_{7}-v_{3}-[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)\cdot\big[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta\big]_{+}\cdot Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}v_{5},

where the exact expressions of the input variables of
Q
are omitted for simplicity. Note that in (
9.31
), the
\bm{\delta}
functions imply that
\eta=(v_{7}-v_{3})/|v_{7}-v_{3}|
and
(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta=(v_{7}-v_{3})\cdot\eta
, we may decompose
v_{5}-v_{3}
into
[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta
, and a vector
w\in\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}
which is orthogonal to
\eta
. This leads to

(9.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{3}-v_{1}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})|-\varepsilon\big)

\displaystyle\times\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{7}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{3})\big)|v_{7}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}\prod_{j=2}^{3}\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}v_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\cdot\int_{\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}}Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{,}

where
\eta=(v_{7}-v_{3})/|v_{7}-v_{3}|
as above,
\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal complement of
\eta
, and the input variables of
Q
are again omitted. The factor
|v_{7}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}
comes from the factor
\big[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta\big]_{+}
, together with the Jacobian coming from viewing
[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta
as the polar coordinates of
v_{7}-v_{3}
. Now for fixed
(x_{3},v_{3})
, we make the change of variables
x_{2}\leftrightarrow\omega
, and argue similarly as above (namely decomposing
v_{1}-v_{2}
into
[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega
and a vector
\theta\in\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}
orthogonal to
\omega
), to get

(9.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(x_{7}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{3})\big)

\displaystyle\times|v_{7}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}\,\mathrm{d}x_{3}\mathrm{d}v_{3}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\cdot\int_{\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}\times\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}}Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
\omega=-(v_{3}-v_{1})/|v_{3}-v_{1}|
and
\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal complement of
\omega
. Finally, by integrating the
\bm{\delta}
functions in (
9.33
), first in
x_{3}
and then in
v_{3}
and then in
(t_{1},t_{2})
, we get

(9.34)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-d}|v_{7}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\int_{\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}\times\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}}Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\theta,

where each of
(v_{3},\eta,\omega)
is a function of
(t_{1},t_{2})
and the fixed parameters. The weight
|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-d}|v_{7}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
is bounded above by
\varepsilon^{-1/2}
due to (
9.23
) and
0<\upsilon<1/(6d)
, and can be absorbed by
\varepsilon^{d-1}
, and hence (
9.25
) follows.

Case 2.
This is the case when the top fixed edge is serial with
e
, but the bottom fixed edge is not. Here

(9.35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{10d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{4}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{4}-v_{2})\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(v_{3}-v_{1}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(v_{4}-v_{2}-[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{7}-x_{5}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{5})\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(x_{6}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{6}-v_{3})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{7}-v_{5}+[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{6}-v_{3}-[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{5}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{5}-v_{3})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta\big]_{+}\cdot Q(x_{1},v_{1},\cdots,x_{7},v_{7},t_{1},t_{2})\prod_{j=2}^{6}\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}v_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j},

where
\omega
and
\eta
are as above. Here we first integrate in
(x_{4},v_{4})
, make the change of variables
x_{2}\leftrightarrow\omega
and argue as in Case 1 above (i.e. decomposing
v_{1}-v_{2}
into
[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega
and a vector
\theta\in\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}
orthogonal to
\omega
), to reduce

(9.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{7}-x_{5}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{5})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(x_{6}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{6}-v_{3})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{7}-v_{5}+[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(v_{6}-v_{3}-[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)\bm{\delta}\big(|x_{5}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{5}-v_{3})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta\big]_{+}

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\cdot|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}\prod_{j\in\{3,5,6\}}\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}v_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\cdot\int_{\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}}Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
\omega=-(v_{3}-v_{1})/|v_{3}-v_{1}|
and
\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal complement of
\omega
. Now in (
9.36
), we argue as in (
2
) of Proposition
9.1
, namely make the change of variables
x_{6}\leftrightarrow\eta
, noting that

\varepsilon\eta=x_{5}-x_{3}+t_{2}(v_{5}-v_{3})=x_{7}-x_{6}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{6})

as well as
(v_{5}-v_{3})\cdot\eta=(v_{6}-v_{7})\cdot\eta
, to reduce it to

(9.37)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big[(v_{6}-v_{7})\cdot\eta\big]_{+}\,\mathrm{d}v_{6}\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\cdot|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}\cdot\int_{\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}}Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
(x_{3},v_{3})
is determined by
(v_{6},\eta,t_{1},t_{2})
and the fixed parameters. Note that with
fixed

(t_{1},t_{2},\eta)
, we have

x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})=\gamma+(t_{1}-t_{2})\sigma

where
\gamma
depends only on
(t_{1},t_{2},\eta)
and the fixed parameters, and
\sigma
is the projection of
v_{6}-v_{7}
to the orthogonal complement
\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}
of
\eta
. Then the
\bm{\delta}
function in (
9.37
) can be written as
\bm{\delta}_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma\cdot\eta)\bm{\delta}_{\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}}(\gamma+(t_{1}-t_{2})\sigma)
where
\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal complement of
\eta
. The latter
\bm{\delta}_{\Pi_{\eta}^{\perp}}
yields
|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-(d-1)}
upon integrating in
\sigma
, and leaves us with the remaining component
\alpha:=(v_{6}-v_{7})\cdot\eta
from the integral in
v_{6}
. Note also that

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot\eta

\displaystyle=\eta\cdot\big[(x_{3}+t_{1}v_{3})-(x_{1}+t_{1}v_{1})\big]=\eta\cdot\big[(x_{3}+t_{2}v_{3})-(x_{1}+t_{1}v_{1})+(t_{1}-t_{2})v_{3}\big]

\displaystyle=\eta\cdot\big[(x_{7}+t_{2}v_{7})-\varepsilon\eta-(x_{1}+t_{1}v_{1})+(t_{1}-t_{2})v_{3}\big]=\eta\cdot\zeta-\varepsilon,

where
\zeta:=(x_{7}+t_{1}v_{7})-(x_{1}+t_{1}v_{1})
, and we have used that
v_{3}\cdot\eta=v_{7}\cdot\eta
. We also have
|\zeta|\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2\upsilon}
since

(9.38)

(x_{7}+t_{1}v_{7})-(x_{1}+t_{1}v_{1})=(x_{7}+t_{2}v_{7})-(x_{1}+t_{1}v_{1})+(t_{1}-t_{2})v_{7}\\
=(x_{3}+t_{2}v_{3})+\varepsilon\eta-(x_{3}+t_{1}v_{3})+(t_{1}-t_{2})v_{7}=(t_{1}-t_{2})(v_{7}-v_{3})+\varepsilon\eta

due to (
9.23
). As a result, we now have that

(9.39)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\max(\alpha,0)\cdot|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-(d-1)}|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}\bm{\delta}(\eta\cdot\zeta-\varepsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\cdot\int_{\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}}Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
v_{3}
and
\omega
are determined by
(\alpha,\eta,t_{1},t_{2})
and the fixed parameters. The integral in
\eta
can be performed by decomposing
\eta
into a vector
\eta^{\circ}
parallel to
\zeta
and a vector
\eta^{\perp}
orthogonal to
\zeta
. The part of integrand involving
\eta^{\circ}
is
|\xi|^{-1}\bm{\delta}_{\mathbb{R}}(\eta^{\circ}-\frac{\varepsilon\zeta}{|\zeta|^{2}})
, and the integral in
\eta^{\perp}
can be reparametrized into an integral on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
in the orthogonal complement of
\zeta
up to a multiplicative factor of
(1-\varepsilon|\zeta|^{-1})^{d-2}\sim 1
. Thus

(9.40)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\max(\alpha,0)|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-(d-1)}|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}\frac{|\zeta|^{-1}}{(1-\varepsilon|\zeta|^{-1})^{d-2}}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\eta^{\perp}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\int_{\Pi_{\omega}^{\perp}}Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
\eta
is determined by
(t_{1},t_{2},\eta^{\perp})
and the fixed parameters, and
v_{3}
and
\omega
are determined by
(\alpha,\eta,t_{1},t_{2})
and the fixed parameters. The weight
|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-(d-1)}|v_{1}-v_{3}|^{-(d-2)}|\zeta|^{-1}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
are bounded by
\varepsilon^{-2/3}
due to (
9.23
) and can be absorbed by
\varepsilon^{d-1}
, and hence (
9.25
) follows.

Case 3.
This is the case when neither fixed end is serial with
e
. Here, we define the unit vectors
\omega
and
\eta
similar to the above, but instead of substituting, we shall keep both
(\omega,\eta)
and the original
(x_{j},v_{j})
variables in the same integral, but with additional
\bm{\delta}
factors. This leads to the integral (note that the
\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}
is turned into
\varepsilon^{d-1}
due to introducing the two
\omega
and
\eta
variables)

(9.41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{10d}\times(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}-x_{1}+t_{1}(v_{3}-v_{1})\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{4}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{4}-v_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(v_{3}-v_{1}+[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{4}-v_{2}-[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{6}-x_{4}+t_{2}(v_{6}-v_{4})\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(x_{7}-x_{5}+t_{2}(v_{7}-v_{5})\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{6}-v_{4}+[(v_{4}-v_{5})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)\bm{\delta}\big(v_{7}-v_{5}-[(v_{4}-v_{5})\cdot\eta]\eta\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(x_{1}-x_{2}+t_{1}(v_{1}-v_{2})-\varepsilon\omega\big)\bm{\delta}\big(x_{4}-x_{5}+t_{2}(v_{4}-v_{5})-\varepsilon\eta\big)

\displaystyle\times\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\big[(v_{4}-v_{5})\cdot\eta\big]_{+}\cdot Q(x_{1},v_{1},\cdots,x_{7},v_{7},t_{1},t_{2})\prod_{j=2}^{6}\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}v_{j}\cdot\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}\eta\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j}.

Now let
(t_{1},t_{2})
be fixed, then
y^{*}:=x_{1}+t_{1}v_{1}
and
z^{*}:=x_{7}+t_{2}v_{7}
are also fixed, and the
\bm{\delta}
functions occurring in (
9.41
) that involve
x_{j}
(there are six of them) can be rearranged as

(9.42)

\displaystyle\bm{\delta}\big(x_{2}+t_{1}v_{2}-(y^{*}-\varepsilon\omega)\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{3}+t_{1}v_{3}-y^{*}\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x_{4}+t_{1}v_{4}-(y^{*}-\varepsilon\omega)\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(x_{4}+t_{2}v_{4}-(z^{*}+\varepsilon\eta)\big)\bm{\delta}\big(x_{5}+t_{2}v_{5}-z^{*}\big)\bm{\delta}\big(x_{6}+t_{2}v_{6}-(z^{*}+\varepsilon\eta)\big).

Therefore, once
\omega,\eta
and
v_{j}
are fixed for some
j\in\{2,3,4,5,6\}
, the corresponding
\bm{\delta}
factor will take care of the integration in
x_{j}
, except that the two
\bm{\delta}
factors involving
x_{4}
in (
9.42
) will lead to the new
\bm{\delta}
factor

\bm{\delta}((t_{2}-t_{1})v_{4}+y^{*}-z^{*}-\varepsilon(\omega+\eta)).

Now, suppose
(\omega,\eta,v_{4})
are fixed, we shall integrate the remaining four
\bm{\delta}
functions in (
9.41
) involving only
v_{j}
, in the variables
(v_{2},v_{3},v_{5},v_{6})
, as follows. The integration in
(v_{3},v_{6})
result in substitution, and for the integration in
(v_{2},v_{5})
we note that

(9.43)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{\delta}\big(v_{4}-v_{2}-[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\cdot\widetilde{Q}(v_{2})\,\mathrm{d}v_{2}=\bm{\delta}\big((v_{4}-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big)\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{Q}(v_{4}+\alpha\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\alpha

for any function
\widetilde{Q}
, which is easily proved by assuming
\omega
is a coordinate vector. The same holds for the integration in
v_{5}
, and putting them together, we can reduce

(9.44)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\max(\alpha_{1},0)\max(\alpha_{2},0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{\delta}((t_{2}-t_{1})v_{4}+y^{*}-z^{*}-\varepsilon(\omega+\eta))\\
\times\bm{\delta}\big((v_{4}-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big)\bm{\delta}\big((v_{4}-v_{7})\cdot\eta\big)\cdot Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}v_{4}\cdot\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}\eta\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{j}.

Next, with
v_{4}
fixed, we integrate in
\eta
and
\omega
using the formula

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bm{\delta}(X\cdot\omega)f(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega=|X|^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-2}}f(R_{X}\omega^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime},\qquad X\in\{v_{4}-v_{1},v_{4}-v_{7}\}

where
