\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
is the unit sphere in the orthogonal complement of a fixed coordinate vector
e
, and
R_{X}
is the rotation such that
R(e)=\frac{X}{|X|}
. This follows from applying the spherical coordinates. Therefore, we have

(9.45)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-2})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-d}|v_{4}-v_{1}|^{-1}|v_{4}-v_{7}|^{-1}\max(\alpha_{1},0)\max(\alpha_{2},0)\\
\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{\delta}\bigg(v_{4}+\frac{y^{*}-z^{*}-\varepsilon(R_{v_{4}-v_{1}}\omega^{\prime}+R_{v_{4}-v_{7}}\eta^{\prime})}{t_{2}-t_{1}}\bigg)\cdot Q(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}\mathrm{d}v_{4}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{j}.

Now, with
(t_{1},t_{2},\omega^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
fixed, we evaluate the integral in
v_{4}
by first changing variables into
v_{4}\to\xi
where
\xi
is the argument of the
\bm{\delta}
. Since
\varepsilon^{1/2}\ll|t_{2}-t_{1}|
by assumption, the Jacobian of this change of variables is close to
1
, so (
9.25
) follows, using the lower bounds in (
9.23
).

Proof of (
3
).
This is easy and follows from (
1
): simply note that
(x_{1},v_{1},x_{7},v_{7})
belongs to a set of volume
\varepsilon^{2d(1-\upsilon)}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
with
0<\upsilon<1/(4d)
. Once
(x_{1},v_{1},x_{7},v_{7})
is fixed, we can perform the the integration in the remaining variables just as in the {33A} molecule case in (
1
). This leads to the desired bound (
9.25
).
∎

Now we define the normal, good, and bad components, based on Propositions
9.1
–
9.3
. The cutting algorithm aims to produce good components while avoiding bad ones. These properties depend not only on the molecule
\mathbb{M}
, but also on the restriction on the support of
Q
, which comes from splitting (Definition
8.9
).

Definition 9.4

(Normal, bad and good molecules)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is formed from a full molecule
\mathbb{M}
by an operation sequence (Definition
8.10
) and picking one sub-case at each splitting, and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
contains only elementary molecules. The initial link structure
\mathcal{L}
between the bottom ends of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
are inherited from that of
\mathbb{M}
(as the operations do not affect any bottom end). Note also that in the splitting steps, by selecting a sub-case each time, we may have made certain extra restrictions to the support of
Q
in (
7.6
).

Based on all this information, we now define the notions of good, normal and bad molecules. Below, for any one-atom molecule
\{\mathfrak{n}_{1}\}
, we denote the 4 edges by
e_{1},\dots,e_{4}
, where
e_{1}
is serial with
e_{3}
, and
e_{2}
is serial with
e_{4}
. For two-atom molecule
\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
, we denote the 7 edges by
e_{1},\cdots,e_{7}
with
e_{1}
and
e_{7}
being the fixed ends in the case of {33} molecules, see
Figure
15
. Recall also the constant
\upsilon:=3^{-d-1}
.

(1)

The following molecules are
normal
:

(a)

Any {2} molecule.

(b)

Any {3} molecule, except those described in (
3a
) below.

(c)

Any {33A} molecule, except those described in (
3b
) below.

(2)

The following molecules are
bad
:

(a)

Any {4} molecule.

(b)

By convention, we also view any empty end as bad.

(3)

The following molecules are
good
:

(a)

Any {3} molecule
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathfrak{n}\}
with fixed end
e_{1}
, where either the extra restriction includes one of the following conditions:

(9.46)

|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|v_{e_{i}}-v_{e_{j}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|x_{e}-x_{e^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|x_{e_{i}}-x_{e_{j}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}

or
(e_{i},e_{j})\in\mathcal{L}
, or
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}
(i.e. the relevant pair of ends are initial linked as in Definition
3.1
; see also the
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
factor in Definition
7.3
).

Here in (
9.46
) we assume that (i)
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is an atom in a molecule
\mathcal{Y}
with
\mathcal{Y}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathcal{X}
(Definition
8.16
), and
e^{\prime}
is a free end or bond at
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
; (ii)
e
is a free end at
\mathfrak{n}
and
e_{i}\neq e_{j}
are two ends at
\mathfrak{n}
; (iii) if
\mathfrak{n}
is O-atom, then
e^{\prime}
is not serial with
e_{1}
, and
e_{i}
not serial with
e_{j}
. In this case,
t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
and
x_{e^{\prime}}
are fixed variables, so the extra restrictions (or initial link) makes
\mathcal{X}
good.

(b)

Any {33A} molecule
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
with fixed ends
(e_{1},e_{7})
, where either the extra restriction includes one of the following conditions:

(9.47)

|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{7}}|\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{7}}|\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|t_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon},

or
\ell[\mathfrak{n}_{1}]\neq\ell[\mathfrak{n}_{2}]
. In this case
\mathcal{X}
is good as a {33A} molecule without strong degeneracy.

(c)

Any {33B} molecule
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
of two C-atoms with fixed ends
(e_{1},e_{7})
, where the extra restriction includes both of the following conditions:

(9.48)

|v_{e_{i}}-v_{e_{j}}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\textrm{and}\quad|t_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}.

Here
(e_{i},e_{j})
are two different ends at the same atom (
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
or
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
). In this case
\mathcal{X}
is good as a {33B} molecule without weak degeneracy.

(d)

Any {44} molecule
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
, where the extra restriction includes both of the following conditions:

(9.49)

|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{7}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{7}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}.

Here
(e_{1},e_{7})
are two free ends at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
respectively, such that
\mathbb{M}
becomes a {33A} molecule after turning these two free ends into fixed ends. In this case the strong extra restrictions (
9.49
) makes
\mathcal{X}
good.

(e)

Any {4} molecule
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathfrak{n}\}
, where either the extra restriction includes one of the following conditions:

(9.50)

|v_{e_{i}}-v_{e_{j}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|x_{e}-x_{e^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|x_{e_{i}}-x_{e_{j}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon},

or
(e_{i},e_{j})\in\mathcal{L}
, or
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}
(i.e. the relevant pair of ends are initial linked as in Definition
3.1
; see also the
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
factor in Definition
7.3
).

Here in (
9.50
) we assume that (i)
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is an atom in a molecule
\mathcal{Y}
with
\mathcal{Y}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathcal{X}
(Definition
8.16
), and
e^{\prime}
is a free end or bond at
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
; (ii)
e
is a free end at
\mathfrak{n}
; (iii)
(e_{i},e_{j})
are two different ends at
\mathfrak{n}
, and are non-serial when
\mathfrak{n}
is O-atom. In this case,
x_{e^{\prime}}
is a fixed variable, so the extra restrictions (or initial link) makes
\mathcal{X}
good.

(f)

Finally, by convention, we also view an empty end
e
as good, if
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}
for some
e^{\prime}
, i.e.
e
is involved in some initial link. In this case the initial link condition (see the
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
factor in Definition
7.3
) will ensure the gain and thus we view
e
as good.

Remark 9.5
.

We explain the classification of elementary molecules in Definition
9.4
as follows:

(1)

Note that Definition
9.4
allows for some {4} molecules and empty ends to be both good and bad; this will not cause any confusion, because in practice the property of good/non-good and bad/non-bad are always exploited independently. The rule of thumb is that, each good molecule leads to a power
\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
gain in the estimate of the integral
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
in (
7.6
), and each bad molecule leads to a power
\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}
loss in this integral (and each normal molecule leads to no gain or loss). These gain and loss estimates easily follow from Definition
9.4
and Propositions
9.1
–
9.3
.

(2)

Note that the definition of good molecules depends on various extra restrictions imposed in Definition
9.4
. In fact, these extra restrictions (corresponding to degeneracy and non-degeneracy conditions) are what motivates the
splitting
steps in the operation sequence. Basically, we may split into degenerate and non-degenerate sub-cases, and exploit different good molecules based on the extra restrictions in each different sub-case. We may also
design different cutting strategies
based on different sub-cases, which will be clear in the algorithm below (cf. the strongly and weakly degeneracies in Propositions
12.5
and
12.17
, where we have a different cutting strategy from the main case).

Remark 9.6
.

Most of the elementary molecules that will occur in the algorithm (Sections
11
–
13
) are {2}, {3}, {4} and {33A} molecules. In fact, {33B} molecules will occur
only
in the
MAINUD
algorithm (Definition
13.12
), and the {44} molecule will occur
only
in
Choice 1
in the proof of Proposition
12.5
. Our construction will guarantee that the {33B} and {44} molecules are
always good
every time they occur.

9.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.2

In this subsection we finish the proof of Proposition
6.2
, assuming Proposition
9.7
below, which states that there exists an operation sequence that satisfies the nice property (
9.51
). This condition is natural in view of the gains and losses associated with good and bad molecules (see Remark
9.5
; the choice of
\upsilon/2
instead of
\upsilon
is due to the need to cover certain log losses).

The construction of the operation sequence in Proposition
9.7
will be left to Sections
10
–
13
. The proof in this subsection relies on Propositions
3.25
,
7.2
,
7.5
,
8.18
and
9.1
–
9.3
. We also rely on two estimates for
(v_{e})
volume and cross sections in (
7.6
), namely Propositions
9.8
and
9.9
. The proof of these estimates are technical, and we leave it to Section
9.3
.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
\mathbb{M}=(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
be a full (unlabeled) molecule as in Definition
3.20
, which belongs to either
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
or
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
. Let
r(\mathbb{M})=H
and
Q=Q_{\mathbb{M}}
as in (
7.8
). Let also
\rho
be defined as in (
7.1
) in Definition
7.1
.

Then, there exist an operation sequence (Definition
8.10
) which produces at most
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
total sub-cases, such that in each sub-case the
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
contains only good, normal and bad elementary molecules (Definition
9.4
). Moreover, if
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
, we have the following estimate (recall
\upsilon=3^{-d-1}
):

(9.51)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+(d-1)(|H|-\#_{\mathrm{bad}})\geq(C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho,

where
\#_{\mathrm{good}}
and
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}
are the number of good and bad molecules respectively. If
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
, then (
9.51
) holds with an extra term
-C^{*}|H|
on the right hand side.

Proof.

The proof will occupy Sections
10
–
13
, and will be finished in Section
13.5
(after reducing to Proposition
12.18
at the end of Section
12
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2
assuming Proposition
9.7
.

We first make some reductions. By Proposition
3.25
, we can estimate
|E_{H}|
and
|\mathrm{Err}^{1}|
by sum of
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
over
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
and
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
respectively. We can bound the number of choices of
\mathbb{M}
by Proposition
7.2
, and reduce the
L^{1}
norms of
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
by By Proposition
7.5
. We then apply the operation sequence to
\mathbb{M}
as in Proposition
9.7
(which includes the upper bound on the number of sub-cases produced), and apply Proposition
8.18
to reduce to estimating
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
for each sub-case. In summary, we get

(9.52)

\|E_{H}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\sum_{m;\rho\geq|H|}C^{m}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}\cdot\varepsilon^{(d-1)|H|}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}:r(\mathbb{M})=H\\
|\mathbb{M}|=m,\,\rho\mathrm{\,fixed}\end{subarray}}\sup_{\textrm{sub-cases}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}),

and the same for
\|\mathrm{Err^{1}}\|_{L^{1}}
but with
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
replaced by
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
. Here note that
|H|\leq\rho
because
|H|=|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})|=s_{\ell}
; moreover, for
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
we must have
|\mathbb{M}|\geq\Lambda_{\ell}
(as there exists a component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
that has exactly
\Lambda_{\ell}
clusters, see Definition
3.20
(
2
)).

Now, using (
9.52
) and the above discussions, it is clear that Proposition
6.2
would follow if we can prove the following estimate for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
(note that
3^{-d-2}
on the right hand side of (
6.3
) equals
\upsilon/3
):

(9.53)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|/9}\cdot\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|H|+(2\upsilon/5)+(5C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}

for any
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
and any sub-case in the operation sequence that leads to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
. Note that for
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
the right hand side of (
9.53
) should include an extra factor of
\varepsilon^{-C^{*}|H|}
, and we will leave it to Part 6 of the proof below. In practice this factor will not matter because
|H|\leq A_{\ell}
and
\Lambda_{\ell}\geq A_{\ell}^{4}\geq|\log\varepsilon|^{4}
by (
1.32
), so
\varepsilon^{-C^{*}|H|}
is easily absorbed by
\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|/2}
provided
|\mathbb{M}|\geq\Lambda_{\ell}
.

The rest part of this proof is devoted to the proof of (
9.53
). We divide into 6 parts.

Proof part 1: properties of final components.
In this part, as a preparation, we prove a simple fact for the (elementary) final components in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
after the operation sequence. More precisely, we will show that

(9.54)

\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}\leq C(\#_{\mathrm{good}}+\rho),

where
\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
is the number of final components in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
that are either nor normal or not {3} molecules, and
\#_{\mathrm{good}}
and
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}
are the number of good and bad final components in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
respectively.

To prove (
9.54
), we will consider the quantity
3|\mathbb{M}|-|\mathcal{E}_{*}|
under the operation sequence. It is easy to see that this quantity decreases by
1
under deleting, and does not change under cutting. The latter is obvious when there is no O-atom (since a bond is cut into a free and a fixed end, and fixed ends do not count in
|\mathcal{E}_{*}|
); in general it can be directly verified using Definition
8.3
(
2
)).

Next consider the initial value of
3|\mathbb{M}|-|\mathcal{E}_{*}|
for
\mathbb{M}
(for which
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{*}
as there is no fixed end). In Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
7.5
, we have proved that
4|\mathbb{M}|=2|\mathcal{E}|-|\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}|-|\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}|
. Note that each edge in
\mathcal{E}
is classified as either bond, or top end in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}
, or bottom end in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}
, and each empty end counts as both top end end bottom end, we deduce that

(9.55)

3|\mathbb{M}|-|\mathcal{E}|=|\mathcal{E}|-|\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}|-|\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}|-|\mathbb{M}|=\#_{\mathrm{bond}}-|\mathbb{M}|-\#_{\mathrm{ee}},

where
\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
and
\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
are the number of bonds and empty ends in
\mathbb{M}
respectively. Note that
\#_{\mathrm{ee}}\geq 0
, and the number of bonds between different layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is bounded by
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|
(as each such bond corresponds to a particle line crossing two different layer which will be in some
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
), we see that the initial value

(9.56)

3|\mathbb{M}|-|\mathcal{E}|\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}(\#_{\mathrm{bond}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})}-|\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|)\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})\leq\rho

using also Definition
3.13
.

Now we prove (
9.54
). By the above discussions, we see that
3|\mathbb{M}^{\prime}|-|\mathcal{E}_{*}^{\prime}|\leq\rho
for the final result
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
(and corresponding
\mathcal{E}_{*}^{\prime}
). Note that this value equals 0 for {3} molecules, equals
-1
for {2} and {33} molecules, and equals
1
for {4} and {44} molecules and empty ends, so we conclude that

(9.57)

\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33\}}\leq\rho+\#_{\mathrm{ee}}+\#_{\{4\}}+\#_{\{44\}}\leq 4(\#_{\mathrm{good}}+\#_{\mathrm{bad}}+\rho)

but now for
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, where
\#_{\{2\}}
and
\#_{\{33\}}
etc. are the number of corresponding molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
. Here the last inequality is because each {4} and {44} molecule and empty end is either good or bad by Definition
9.4
. Finally, since there are only elementary components in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, by Definition
8.11
, we have

\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}=\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33\}}+\#_{\mathrm{ee}}+\#_{\{4\}}+\#_{\{44\}}+\#(\textrm{good \lx@text@lbrace 3\lx@text@rbrace molecules}),

thus
\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}\leq 9(\#_{\mathrm{good}}+\#_{\mathrm{bad}}+\rho)
thanks to (
9.57
). Since we also have
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}\leq C(\#_{\mathrm{good}}+|H|)
by (
9.51
) and
|H|\leq\rho
, we conclude that
\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}\leq C(\#_{\mathrm{good}}+\rho)
. This proves (
9.54
).

Proof part 2: decomposition of the support of
Q
.
Recall
Q^{\prime}
corresponds to a sub-case in the operation sequence obtained by splittings; as in Proposition
8.18
, we have
Q^{\prime}=Q_{\mathbb{M}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}
where
\mathcal{P}
represents the restriction in this sub-case. Recall also the
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
in (
7.8
). In this part, we will decompose
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
as

(9.58)

Q_{\mathbb{M}}\leq\sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}Q_{\alpha},

where
\alpha
is the summation index,
w_{\alpha}\geq 0
is a scalar, and
Q_{\alpha}
are nonnegative functions that have localized support in the
x_{e}
and
v_{e}
variables, specified as below:

(1)

Each
Q_{\alpha}
has the following form for some
(x_{e}^{*})
with
x_{e}^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
:

(9.59)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\big(e^{-\beta|v_{e}|^{2}/3}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{e}-x_{e}^{*}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}},\,|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}}\big).

(2)

The
w_{\alpha}
satisfies

(9.60)

\sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}.

(3)

In the support of
Q_{\alpha}
and
\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
(see (
7.6
)), for each edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we have
|x_{e}-x_{e^{\prime}}^{*}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
and
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, where
e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}
is the bottom end of the particle line containing
e
.

Now we proceed to prove (
9.58
) and (
1
)–(
3
). Recall that
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
is given by (
7.8
),

(9.61)

Q_{\mathbb{M}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}})\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\big|f^{\mathcal{A}}\big((\ell_{1}[e]-1)\tau,x_{e}-(\ell_{1}[e]-1)\tau\cdot v_{e},v_{e}\big)\big|.

We begin by decomposing
f^{\mathcal{A}}
and then inserting into (
9.61
).

Decomposition of
f^{\mathcal{A}}
.
By (
6.1
), (
6.2
), (
1.29
) and (
1.34
), we know that
\|f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{2\beta/5}}\leq C
. The definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{2\beta/5}}
(
1.15
) implies that
f^{\mathcal{A}}
decay exponentially in
v
, so we may restrict
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
for each bottom end
e
, otherwise the exponential decay will overwhelm the powers
\varepsilon^{-(d-1)(|\mathcal{E}_{*}|-2|\mathbb{M}|)}
in (
7.6
) and the desired bound becomes trivial.

Next, with the assumption that
f^{\mathcal{A}}
is supported in
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, we will decompose

(9.62)

\begin{gathered}|f^{\mathcal{A}}(x,v)|\leq\sum_{x^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}w_{x^{*}}\cdot g_{x^{*}}(x,v),\\
g_{x^{*}}(x,v)\leq e^{-(2\beta/5)|v|^{2}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|x-x^{*}|\leq C,\,|v|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}},\quad\sum_{x^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}w_{x^{*}}\leq C,\end{gathered}

In fact, this can be proved by choosing
C=\sqrt{d}
and

w_{x^{*}}:=\sup_{|x-x^{*}|\leq C,\,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\beta|v|^{2}}|f^{\mathcal{A}}(x,v)|,\quad g_{x^{*}}(x,v)=(w_{x^{*}})^{-1}\cdot g(x,v)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|x-x^{*}|\leq C},

and (
9.62
) follows from the definition of
\|g\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{2\beta/5}}
.

Inserting the decomposition of
f^{\mathcal{A}}
.
Now, by inserting the decomposition (
9.62
) into (
9.61
), we get

(9.63)

Q_{\mathbb{M}}\leq\sum_{\alpha}\underbrace{\bigg(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}w_{x_{e}^{*}}\bigg)}_{w_{\alpha}}\underbrace{\bigg(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}g_{x_{e}^{*}}((\ell_{1}[e]-1)\tau,x_{e}-(\ell_{1}[e]-1)\tau\cdot v_{e},v_{e})\bigg)}_{Q_{\alpha}}=\sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}Q_{\alpha},

where
\alpha=(x_{e}^{*})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}
, and
g_{x_{e}^{*}}
is as in (
9.62
) but with
x_{e}^{*}
instead of
x^{*}
. This already establishes (
9.58
), and (
1
)–(
2
) also follow from (
9.62
) where we note that
|(\ell_{1}[e]-1)\tau\cdot v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
.

Finally we prove (
3
). The bound for
v_{e}
follows from (
9.62
) and energy conservation implied by the support of
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
:
|v_{e}|^{2}\leq\sum_{e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e^{\prime}}|^{2}
(and that the size
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
). As for
x_{e}
, let
e
be any edge and
e^{\prime}
be the bottom end of the particle line containing
e
. Then we have
|x_{e^{\prime}}-x_{e^{\prime}}^{*}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
by (
9.62
); moreover, since
e
is equal to or connected to
e^{\prime}
by a sequence of C- and O-atoms, we can recursively apply the relation between incoming and outgoing positions in (
9.1
) to show that
|x_{e}-x_{e^{\prime}}|\leq\sum_{j}\mathfrak{L}\tau\cdot|v_{e_{j}}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
where
e_{j}
are edges on the path from
e
to
e^{\prime}
. This proves (
3
).

Now that we have the decomposition (
9.62
), using that
Q^{\prime}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot Q_{\mathbb{M}}
, we can get the associated decomposition of
Q^{\prime}
by defining
Q_{\alpha}^{\prime}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot Q_{\alpha}
, so that
Q^{\prime}\leq\sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}Q_{\alpha}^{\prime}
.

Proof part 3: estimating the local integral.
In this part, we reduce the main goal (
9.53
) to estimates of the local integrals for the elementary molecules
(\mathbb{M}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{M}_{k})
forming
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
. By (
9.58
) and (
9.60
), we know that (
9.53
) would follow once we can prove (uniformly in
\alpha
)

(9.64)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|/8}\cdot\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|H|+(2\upsilon/5)+(5C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}.

Below we will prove (
9.64
). Before continuing, let us discuss some useful heuristics. By (
8.7
) we have
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
, and by Propositions
9.1
–
9.3
we have upper bounds for each
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
. Basically
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}_{j}}=O(\tau)
for normal molecules,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}_{j}}=O(\varepsilon^{\upsilon})
for good molecules, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}_{j}}=O(\varepsilon^{-(d-1)})
for bad molecules. We would like to insert these bounds into (
9.64
), and derive an estimate of the form
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})\leq\tau^{\mathrm{power}}\varepsilon^{\mathrm{power}}
with exponents matching those in (
9.64
).

However, there is one subtlety regarding normal {3} molecules (the other molecules are fine as their number is bounded by (
9.54
), so logarithmic loss coming from them will be negligible in view of the gain from good molecules and the gain on the right hand side of (
9.64
)). For a normal {3} molecule
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, by Proposition
9.1
(
2
) we know
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{2}
, and
t_{1}\in[(\ell[\mathfrak{n}]-1)\tau,\ell[\mathfrak{n}]\tau]
in this integration. If
Q
is supported in
|v_{e}|\lesssim 1
, this leads to
\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\tau\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}=O(\tau)
from the integral over
t
. Nevertheless, in the support of
Q^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, we only have the weaker bound
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, which leads to logarithmic losses due to both the volume of
v_{2}
and the cross section
\big[(v_{1}-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}
. If estimated naively, we would have losses of
O\left(|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}|\mathbb{M}|}\right)
, which cannot be controlled by (
9.64
).

We will address these issues in Part 4 below, using Propositions
9.8
–
9.9
whose proof will be postponed to Section
9.3
. For now, let us introduce some preliminary reductions on (
9.64
).

Recall
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})
. For a final component
\mathbb{M}_{j}
that is a normal {3} molecule with one atom
\mathfrak{n}
, we have
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}(Q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big[(v_{e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}-v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})\cdot\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\big]_{-}\cdot Q\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}
(by (
9.5
)), where
e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
,
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
are the two bottom (or top) edges at
\mathfrak{n}
. We introduce the following notions:

(9.65)

\displaystyle\int(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}

\displaystyle:=\int(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})},

\displaystyle\int(\cdots)\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}

\displaystyle=\int(\cdots)\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathbb{M}_{j}},

\displaystyle B_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}

\displaystyle:=\big[(v_{e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}-v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})\cdot\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\big]_{-},

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}}B_{\mathbb{M}_{j}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}

\displaystyle:=\{\textrm{normal \lx@text@lbrace 3\lx@text@rbrace molecules}\}.

Then
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})
can be rewritten as

(9.66)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})=\int\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\Big(B_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}\cdot Q^{\prime}_{\alpha}\Big)\ \mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}.

Here, in order to obtain (
9.66
), we have chosen to integrate the
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
variables (which are independent variables) in the end, and treat these variables as fixed when applying the remaining operators
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
and integrating in the remaining variables. Schematically, we start with a composition operator
\int_{(1)}f_{1}\int_{(2)}f_{2}\cdots(Q)
and rewrite it as
\int_{(1)}f_{1}\int_{(2)}f_{2}\cdots(Q)=\int_{(1)}\int_{(2)}\cdots(f_{1}f_{2}\cdots Q)
, then change the order of integration to move a part of the variables to the (left) end of the integral.

Proof part 4: weight and volume bounds.
In this part, we derive the upper bounds of the weights
B_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
and the integral over
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
. We will rely on the estimates in Propositions
9.8
and
9.9
; for their statements and proof see Section
9.3
.

Application of the upper bound of weights.
We first derive an upper bound of the cross sections
B_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
. The goal is to prove (
9.71
). Thanks to Proposition
9.8
, we have

(9.67)

B_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}=\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}}B_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\leq\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}}|v_{e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}-v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}|\leq\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}(1+|v_{e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}-v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}|)\leq\mathfrak{B}\cdot e^{(\beta/6)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}},

where
e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
,
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
are the two bottom (or top) edges at
\mathfrak{n}
, and in the last step we have used Proposition
9.8
with
\gamma=\beta/6
. The quantity
\mathfrak{B}
is as in Proposition
9.8
and will be discussed only in Part 5 below.

Now, combining (
9.67
) with the definition of
Q_{\alpha}
in (
9.59
) (with
Q_{\alpha}^{\prime}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot Q_{\alpha}
), we obtain

(9.68)

\begin{split}B_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}\cdot Q^{\prime}_{\alpha}&\leq\mathfrak{B}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\prod_{e}\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{e}-x_{e}^{*}|\leq C,\,|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}}\cdot e^{-(\beta/6)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}}\\
&\leq\mathfrak{B}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\prod_{e}\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{e}-x_{e}^{*}|\leq C,\,|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}}\\
&\times\sum_{(X_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}:\,X_{e}\in[1,|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}]\cap 2^{\mathbb{Z}}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}e^{-(\beta/48)|X_{e}|^{2}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|v_{e}|\leq X_{e}}.\end{split}

Here in (
9.68
), we have used energy conservation to replace
(\beta/6)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}
by
(\beta/12)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|v_{e}|^{2}
(with
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}
being the set of all ends of
\mathbb{M}
), and performed a dyadic decomposition in
|v_{e}|
for each
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}
(i.e. we have
e^{-(\beta/12)|v|^{2}}\leq\sum_{X}e^{-(\beta/48)|X|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|v|\leq X}
; note that we may assume
\beta\leq 1
).

Inserting (
9.68
) into (
9.66
), we get

(9.69)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})\leq\sum_{(X_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}}e^{-(\beta/96)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|X_{e}|^{2}}\int\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathfrak{B}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\cdot e^{-(\beta/96)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|X_{e}|^{2}}\right)\ \mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}},\end{split}

where

(9.70)

\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{e}-x_{e}^{*}|\leq C,\,|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}},\,\forall e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|v_{e}|\leq X_{e},\,\forall e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}.

Clearly the sum
\sum_{(X_{e})}e^{-(\beta/96)\sum_{e}|X_{e}|^{2}}
only leads to a factor
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}
. Therefore we derive that

(9.71)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\sup_{(X_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}}\,\mathfrak{B}\cdot e^{-(\beta/96)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|X_{e}|^{2}}\cdot\int\mathcal{I}^{\neg\,\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)\ \mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}},

where
X_{e}\in[1,|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}]\cap 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
in the supremum.

Treating the integral in
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
.
In this section, we apply Proposition
9.9
to bound the volume of the set which the variables in
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
belong to, thereby controlling the integral in these variables by the corresponding supremum. The goal is to prove (
9.75
).

Recall the set
\mathfrak{S}
of normal {3} molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, which also occurs in Proposition
9.9
. Let the associated notations be as in Proposition
9.9
:
\mathfrak{n}
is the unique atom in
\mathbb{M}_{j}
,
e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
is the unique fixed end at
\mathfrak{n}
, and
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
is the other top (bottom) end at
\mathfrak{n}
if
e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
is a top (bottom) end. Recall that
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}:=\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
and
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}:=\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}
, see (
9.65
). We also denote by
e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})
and
e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})
the two remaining (free) ends at
\mathfrak{n}
. Then, using the support of
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
, we always have

(9.72)

v_{e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})}=v_{e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}-[(v_{e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}-v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})\cdot\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}]\omega_{\mathfrak{n}},\quad v_{e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})}=v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}+[(v_{e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}-v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})\cdot\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}]\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}

(this is for C-atoms; for O-atoms we simply have
v_{e_{j}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})}=v_{e_{j}(\mathfrak{n})}
).

The idea in this part is to fix
(\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
(which belongs to a compact set) and control the volume of the sets of
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})
and
(v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})
for the normal {3} molecule variables. For
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})
this is simple: let
A
be the set of atoms
\mathfrak{n}

not
in one of the normal {3} molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, then
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
is associated with the normal {3} molecules. Since
Q
is supported in
\mathcal{D}
in (
7.5
), we know that
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
is the projection of the set
\mathcal{D}
onto the variables
\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}\backslash A}
. Note that the volume
|\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}|=\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}1\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}\backslash A}
is precisely the quantity in (
9.85
) that controls the quantity
\mathfrak{B}
in Proposition
9.8
.

As for
(v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})
, we will use Proposition
9.9
. Recall that
X_{e}\in[1,|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}]\cap 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
has been fixed for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}
as in (
9.71
). We may also fix
(\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
. Then, by Proposition
9.9
, there exists a set
Y=Y(\omega)\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{|\mathcal{F}|}
(where
\mathcal{F}
is the set of all
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
), which depends on
(\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
and
(X_{e})
, and on
(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
and the cutting sequence, but not on the concrete choices of
(v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})
, such that
(v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})
always takes value in
Y
, and

(9.73)

|Y|\leq\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}X_{e}^{d}\cdot C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}}.

Putting together the above arguments, we get that

(9.74)

\begin{split}\int\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\mathrm{N}\{3\}}&=\int\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A}\mathrm{d}\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A}\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\int_{Y}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A}\mathrm{d}v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}\cdot\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)\\
&\leq|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{|\mathbb{M}\backslash A|}\cdot|\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot|Y|\cdot\sup_{(\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}):|\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}|=1}\sup_{(t_{\mathfrak{n}})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\sup_{(v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})\in Y(\omega)}\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right).\end{split}

Combining this with (
9.71
) and (
9.73
), we get

(9.75)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot e^{-(\beta/96)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|X_{e}|^{2}}\cdot\mathfrak{B}\cdot|\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}X_{e}^{d}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}}\cdot\sup\left[\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)\right],

where
(X_{e})
is fixed,
|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{|\mathbb{M}\backslash A|}
is merged into
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}
, the supremum is taken over
(\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
,
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})
and
(v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})})
, and
\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}
is given by

(9.76)

\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{e}-x_{e}^{*}|\leq C,\,|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}},\,\forall e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|v_{e}|\leq X_{e},\,\forall e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}.

Proof part 5: Finishing the proof for
E_{H}
.
In this step, we finish the proof of (
9.64
) by applying the upper bounds of local integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}
. First we have

(9.77)

\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)=\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)\leq\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\in\mathfrak{S}}\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}.

Then, for each
\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\in\mathfrak{S}
, depending on whether it is good, normal or bad, we can apply Propositions
9.1
–
9.3
to bound
\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}
. Here we note that (i) if a good molecule
\mathcal{X}
in Definition
9.4
involves another molecule
\mathcal{Y}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\mathcal{X}
, then the corresponding
z_{e^{\prime}}
(or
t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
) variables in
\mathcal{Y}
has been fixed when integrating in the
\mathcal{X}
variables, so they play the role of the external variables in Proposition
9.2
; (ii) in the initial link case corresponding to Proposition
9.2
(
3
)–(
4
), we should extract the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{e_{1}\sim_{\mathrm{in}}e_{2}}
from
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
and bound
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\circ\mathbbm{1}_{e_{1}\sim_{\mathrm{in}}e_{2}}^{\varepsilon}
instead. In the end we get

(9.78)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varepsilon^{\upsilon},&&\mathbb{M}_{j}\mathrm{\ is\ good};\\
&1,&&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.\times\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varepsilon^{-(d-1)},&&\mathbb{M}_{j}\mathrm{\ is\ bad};\\
&1,&&\mathrm{otherwise,}\end{aligned}\right.

where recall
\upsilon=3^{-d-1}
. This leads to

(9.79)

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)&\leq\prod_{\mathbb{M}_{j}\not\in\mathfrak{S}}\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}}\cdot\varepsilon^{\upsilon\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}-(d-1)\#_{\mathrm{bad}}}.\end{split}

Combining with (
9.75
), we get

(9.80)

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})&\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot e^{-(\beta/96)\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|X_{e}|^{2}}\cdot\mathfrak{B}\cdot|\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}X_{e}^{d}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}}\cdot\sup\left[\mathcal{I}^{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\heartsuit)}\right)\right]\\
&\leq(\mathfrak{B}\cdot|\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}|)\cdot\bigg(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}X_{e}^{d}\cdot e^{-(\beta/96)|X_{e}|^{2}}\bigg)\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}}\cdot\varepsilon^{\upsilon\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}-(d-1)\#_{\mathrm{bad}}}.\end{split}

By Proposition
9.8
we know that
|\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot\mathfrak{B}\leq(C\tau)^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}(\rho+|A|)}
, and it is also obvious that
\prod_{e}(X_{e}^{d}\cdot e^{-(\beta/96)|X_{e}|^{2}})\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}
. Recall
\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
is the number of molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
that are not normal {3} molecules, and we have
|A|\leq 2\cdot\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
(as an elementary component has at most two atoms). Using also (
9.54
), we know that
|A|+\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}\leq C(\#_{\mathrm{good}}+\rho)
. Inserting all the above into (
9.80
), we obtain

(9.81)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}(\rho+\#_{\mathrm{good}})}\cdot\varepsilon^{\upsilon\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}-(d-1)\#_{\mathrm{bad}}}\cdot(C\tau)^{|\mathbb{M}|}.

Now consider (
9.51
) in Proposition
9.7
. Since
\rho>0
, we know the left hand side of (
9.51
) is at least
\upsilon/2
; by interpolation we get that this is also
\geq(2\upsilon/5)+(5C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho
. Using this and (
9.81
), we conclude that

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}_{\alpha})\leq(C\tau)^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot\big(|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\varepsilon^{\upsilon/2}\big)^{\#_{\mathrm{good}}}\cdot\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|H|}\cdot\varepsilon^{(2\upsilon/5)+(5C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}.

Since
C\tau\leq\tau^{1/8}
and
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\varepsilon^{\upsilon/2}\leq 1
, this proves (
9.64
) and thus completes the proof of Proposition
6.2
for
E_{H}
.

Proof part 6: the case of
\mathrm{Err}^{1}
.
Finally, to prove Proposition
6.2
for
\mathrm{Err}^{1}
, we only need to prove (
9.53
) with an extra factor of
\varepsilon^{-C^{*}|H|}
on the right hand side, i.e.

(9.82)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|/9}\cdot\varepsilon^{-C^{*}|H|}\cdot\varepsilon^{-(d-1)|H|+(2\upsilon/5)+(5C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}.

However, this follows the same arguments as in Parts 1–5 above; the only difference is that we now have an extra term
-C^{*}|H|
on the right hand side of (
9.51
), which leads to the loss
\varepsilon^{-C^{*}|H|}
. This proves Proposition
6.2
for
\mathrm{Err}^{1}
, and completes the proof of Proposition
6.2
.
∎

9.3.
Auxiliary results in the proof of Proposition
6.2

In this subsection we state and prove the auxiliary results (Propositions
9.8
and
9.9
) that are used in the proof of Proposition
6.2
in Section
9.2
.

Proposition 9.8
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule as in Definition
3.20
, which satisfies Definition
3.20
(
1a
) and (
1c
). Let
v_{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the associated variables defined for each edge
e
of
\mathbb{M}
, satisfying

(9.83)

|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}},\quad|v_{e_{1}}|^{2}+|v_{e_{2}}|^{2}=|v_{e_{1}^{\prime}}|^{2}+|v_{e_{2}^{\prime}}|^{2};\quad v_{e_{j}}=v_{e_{j}^{\prime}}\ \textrm{if }\mathfrak{n}\ \textrm{is O-atom}

for each atom
\mathfrak{n}
, where
(e_{1},e_{2})
are the two bottom edges and
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
are the two top edges at
\mathfrak{n}
, and
e_{1}
is serial with
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}
is serial with
e_{2}^{\prime}
as in Definition
7.3
(
2
). Fix also a constant
\gamma
with
|\log\varepsilon|^{-1}\leq\gamma\leq 1
. Then there exists a scalar quantity
\mathfrak{B}\geq 1
depending only on
\mathbb{M}
, such that

(9.84)

\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)\leq\mathfrak{B}\cdot\exp\bigg(\gamma\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg),

where the product on the left hand side of (
9.84
) is taken over all atoms
\mathfrak{n}
and
(v_{e_{1}},v_{e_{2}})
is as above. Moreover, this quantity
\mathfrak{B}
satisfies the following estimates. For any atom set
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, we have

(9.85)

\mathfrak{B}\cdot\bigg(\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}1\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}\backslash A}\bigg)\leq\big(C\gamma^{-1/2}\tau\big)^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}(\rho+|A|)},

where the integral is taken over all variables
\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}\backslash A}=(t_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
is the projection of the set
\mathcal{D}
defined in (
7.5
) onto the variables
\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}\backslash A}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into 4 parts.

Proof part 1: the binary tree case.
In this part, we consider the case when
\mathbb{M}=T
has only one layer
\ell^{\prime}
and is a
binary tree
, where
T
has a unique highest atom
\mathfrak{m}
, and each atom in
T\backslash\{\mathfrak{m}\}
has a unique parent in
T
.

Proof of (
9.84
) for binary trees.
By considering
\gamma^{1/2}\cdot v_{e}
instead of
v_{e}
, we may reduce to the case
\gamma=1
. For the domain
\mathcal{D}_{T}
of time variables
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}
defined in the same way as in (
7.5
), we know
t_{\mathfrak{n}}\in[(\ell^{\prime}-1)\tau,\ell^{\prime}\tau]
because
T
only has one layer. It is easy to prove by induction that

(9.86)

\int_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}1\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{T}=\tau^{|T|}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{T}^{-1},\quad\mathfrak{C}_{T}:=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}\sigma(\mathfrak{n}),

where
\sigma(\mathfrak{n})
is the number of descendant atoms of
\mathfrak{n}
in
T
(Definition
3.5
; this includs
\mathfrak{n}
itself).

For any atom
\mathfrak{n}\in T
, by iteratively applying (
9.83
) for the atom
\mathfrak{n}
and its descendants in
T
, we get that

(9.87)

(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)^{2}\leq 2+2(|v_{e_{1}}|^{2}+|v_{e_{2}}|^{2})\leq 2+2\sum_{(\mathfrak{l},e)}|v_{e}|^{2},

where the sum is taken over all lowest atoms
\mathfrak{l}
of
T
that are descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
, and all bottom ends
e
at
\mathfrak{l}
. Note that for each fixed
e
there is at most one
\mathfrak{l}
, and for each fixed
\mathfrak{l}
there are at most two
e
in the summation. From (
9.87
) we have

(9.88)

\displaystyle\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{5/3}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}\bigg(\frac{2}{(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{5/3}}+\frac{2}{(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{5/3}}\sum_{(\mathfrak{l},e)}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{C}_{T}^{5/3}2^{|T|}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}\bigg(1+(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{-5/3}\bigg(\sum_{(\mathfrak{l},e)}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg)^{3/2}\bigg).

where
\mathfrak{C}_{T}
is as in (
9.86
), and in the last line we used
\sum x_{j}^{3/2}\leq(\sum x_{j})^{3/2}
for
x_{j}\geq 0
. By elementary inequality, for any
Z>0
we have
1+Z^{3/2}\leq Ce^{Z}
; applying this to
Z:=(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{-10/9}\sum_{(\mathfrak{l},e)}|v_{e}|^{4/3}
, we get from (
9.88
) that

(9.89)

\displaystyle\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{C}_{T}^{5/3}(2C)^{|T|}\exp\bigg(\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{-10/9}\sum_{(\mathfrak{l},e)}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{C}_{T}^{5/3}(2C)^{|T|}\exp\bigg(\sum_{(\mathfrak{l},e)}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}}(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{-10/9}\bigg).

Here, for fixed
(\mathfrak{l},e)
, the summation is taken over all
\mathfrak{n}\in T
that are ancestors of
\mathfrak{l}
. Note that since
T
is a binary tree, each atom in
T
has at most one parent atom in
T
, so all such
\mathfrak{n}
(for fixed
\mathfrak{l}
) form a monotonic sequence on which the value of
\sigma(\mathfrak{n})
is strictly increasing. This implies that
\sum_{\mathfrak{n}}(\sigma(\mathfrak{n}))^{-10/9}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-10/9}\leq C
uniformly in
(\mathfrak{l},e)
, and hence

(9.90)

\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in T}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)\leq\mathfrak{C}_{T}^{5/6}C^{|T|}\cdot\exp\bigg(C\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg),

where
\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-}
is the set of all bottom ends of atoms in
T
.

Analog of (
9.85
) for binary trees.
In the same setting as above (where
\mathbb{M}=T
is a single layer binary tree), now let
A\subseteq T
be a fixed atom set. Next we shall prove that

(9.91)

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{T}}1\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{T\backslash A}\leq\tau^{|T|-|A|}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{T}^{-1}\cdot\frac{(|T|)!}{(|T|-|A|)!},

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{T}
is projection of
\mathcal{D}_{T}
(as defined above) onto the variables
\bm{t}_{T\backslash A}
. We will prove (
9.91
) by induction; the base case is trivial.

Suppose (
9.91
) is true for
|T|<m
, now consider some
T
with
|T|=m
. By time translation and dilation we may assume
\tau=1
and
\ell^{\prime}=0
so
t_{\mathfrak{n}}\in[0,1]
for each
\mathfrak{n}
. Let the unique highest atom of
T
be
\mathfrak{m}
, we will assume
\mathfrak{m}
has 2 children atoms; the case when
\mathfrak{m}
has only one child atom is similar with slightly adjusted proof. Let the two children atoms of
\mathfrak{m}
be
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
, and let
T_{j}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
with
|T_{j}|=m_{j}
, then
m=m_{1}+m_{2}+1
. Let
A_{j}=A\cap T_{j}
and
|A_{j}|=k_{j}
, we consider two cases, namely when
\mathfrak{m}\in A
or
\mathfrak{m}\not\in A
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{m}\not\in A
, then
0\leq k_{j}\leq m_{j}
and
|A|:=k=k_{1}+k_{2}
. For any
\bm{t}_{T\backslash A}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{T}
, we must have
\bm{t}_{T_{j}\backslash A_{j}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{T_{j}}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, and
t_{\mathfrak{n}}\in[0,t_{\mathfrak{m}}]
for all
\mathfrak{n}\in T_{j}\backslash A_{j}
. By applying induction hypothesis to
(T_{j},A_{j})
and then integrating in
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
, we get that

(9.92)

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{T}}1\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{T\backslash A}\leq\mathfrak{C}_{T_{1}}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{T_{2}}^{-1}\frac{m_{1}!m_{2}!}{(m_{1}-k_{1})!(m_{2}-k_{2})!}\int_{0}^{1}t_{\mathfrak{m}}^{m_{1}+m_{2}-k_{1}-k_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{m}}\\
\leq\mathfrak{C}_{T_{1}}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{T_{2}}^{-1}\frac{m_{1}!m_{2}!}{(m_{1}-k_{1})!(m_{2}-k_{2})!}\frac{1}{m_{1}+m_{2}-k_{1}-k_{2}+1}.

Note also that

(9.93)

\mathfrak{C}_{T}^{-1}=\mathfrak{C}_{T_{1}}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{T_{2}}^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{m_{1}+m_{2}+1},

we see that (
9.91
) will follows from (
9.92
) and (
9.93
), provided we can show that

(9.94)

\frac{m_{1}!m_{2}!}{(m_{1}-k_{1})!(m_{2}-k_{2})!}\leq\frac{(m_{1}+m_{2})!}{(m_{1}+m_{2}-k_{1}-k_{2})!}.

Now to prove (
9.94
), simply note that
(x+y)!/x!
is increasing in
x\geq 0
for fixed
y\geq 0
; by applying it to
y=m_{2}-k_{2}
and
x=m_{1}-k_{1}
(and using symmetry), we see that the ratio between the left hand side and right hand side of (
9.94
) is maximized when
x
and
y
are both maximized, i.e.
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
. In this extremal case (
9.94
) is trivially true, so it is true in general.

Suppose
\mathfrak{m}\in A
, then
0\leq k_{j}\leq m_{j}
and
|A|:=k=k_{1}+k_{2}+1
. By similar argument as above (except that now we do not integrate in
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
as
\mathfrak{m}\in A
), we get

(9.95)

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{T}}1\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{T\backslash A}\leq\mathfrak{C}_{T_{1}}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{T_{2}}^{-1}\frac{m_{1}!m_{2}!}{(m_{1}-k_{1})!(m_{2}-k_{2})!}.

Using also (
9.93
) and (
9.94
), this again implies (
9.91
).

Proof part 2: the forest case.
Now consider the case when
\mathbb{M}
has only one layer
\ell^{\prime}
, and is a forest. By restricting to components, we may assume
\mathbb{M}
is a tree (which need not be a binary tree). We shall divide the atom set of
\mathbb{M}
into finitely many binary trees and upside down binary trees (where the notions of parent and child etc. are the opposite from binary trees), as follows, see
Figure
17
.

First choose any atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
, and let
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
. This is a binary tree, because each atom has at most one parent in
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
(as
\mathbb{M}
has no cycle). Moreover, all bottom ends of
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
are also bottom ends of
\mathbb{M}
. Consider all the atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\not\in T_{\mathfrak{n}}
that are connected to
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
by a bond. Since
\mathbb{M}
is a tree, if we remove
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
from
\mathbb{M}
(and turn any bond between
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
to a free end at
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
), then
\mathbb{M}
becomes the disjoint union of trees
\mathbb{M}^{j}
, where
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\in\mathbb{M}^{j}
for each
j
. Note also that each
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
has only one bond connected to
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
, which is turned to a bottom free end in
\mathbb{M}^{j}
.

Now for each
j
, let
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}^{*}
be the set of ancestors of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
in
\mathbb{M}^{j}
, which is now an upside down binary tree. Clearly all top ends of
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}^{*}
are also top ends of
\mathbb{M}
, and all the discussions in Step 1 above also work for upside down binary trees due to time reversal symmetry. Consider all the atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{jk}\in\mathbb{M}^{j}\backslash T_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}^{*}
that are connected to
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}^{*}
by a bond, then after removing
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}^{*}
from
\mathbb{M}^{j}
, the
\mathbb{M}^{j}
will become the disjoint union of trees
\mathbb{M}^{jk}
where
\mathfrak{n}_{jk}\in\mathbb{M}^{jk}
for each
k
. Also each
\mathfrak{n}_{jk}
has only one bond connected to
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}^{*}
, which is turned to a top free end in
\mathbb{M}^{jk}
. Therefore, we can choose
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{jk}}
to be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}_{jk}
in
\mathbb{M}^{jk}
, which is a binary tree, so that all bottom ends of
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{jk}}
are also bottom ends of
\mathbb{M}
, and so on.

Figure 17.
The decomposition of a general tree
\mathbb{M}
into binary trees and upside down binary trees, as described in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
9.8
. Here
\mathfrak{n}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{jk}
etc. and
T_{\mathfrak{n}}
,
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}^{*}
,
T_{\mathfrak{n}_{jk}}
etc., are defined as in Part 2. Different colors represent different binary trees and upside down binary trees.

By repeating the above, we have divided the atom set of
\mathbb{M}
into the disjoint union of finitely many
T^{(j)}
, where each
T^{(j)}
is either a binary tree or an upside down binary tree. If we apply (
9.90
) and (
9.91
) to each
T^{(j)}
, we then obtain that

(9.96)

\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)\leq\prod_{j}\mathfrak{C}_{T^{(j)}}^{5/6}\cdot C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot\exp\bigg(C\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg),

(9.97)

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}1\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathbb{M}\backslash A}\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|-|A|}\prod_{j}\mathfrak{C}_{T^{(j)}}^{-1}\cdot\prod_{j}\frac{(|T^{(j)}|)!}{(|T^{(j)}|-|A\cap T^{(j)}|)!}\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|}\prod_{j}\mathfrak{C}_{T^{(j)}}^{-1}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}|A|},

in view of the upper bound
|\mathbb{M}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
(see Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
7.2
) and the trivial bound
\tau^{-1}\leq|\log\varepsilon|
. Here
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}
in (
9.96
) is the set of all ends of
\mathbb{M}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
in (
9.97
) is the same as in (
9.85
).

Proof part 3: single layer case.
Now consider a molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
with a single layer
\ell^{\prime}
satisfying the relevant conditions in Definition
3.20
(
1a
) and (
1c
).

Consider the clusters
\mathbb{M}_{j}
of
\mathbb{M}
(Definition
3.11
). For each
j
, we may choose at most
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
C-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, such that if we remove these C-atoms, and turn any bond between these atoms and other atoms
\mathfrak{n}
into a free end at
\mathfrak{n}
, then the recollision number
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})
will be reduced to
0
. Therefore, in the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, if we remove all such C-atoms (their total number is
\leq\sum_{j}\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})=\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, see Definition
7.1
), then the resulting molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
will be a forest. By applying (
9.96
) and (
9.97
) to this forest, and using (
9.83
) for each of the removed atoms, we get that

(9.98)

\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)\leq\mathfrak{C}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{5/6}\cdot C^{|\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}}\cdot\exp\bigg(C\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg)\cdot\exp\bigg(C\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*}}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg),

(9.99)

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}1\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\backslash A}\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}|A\cap\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}|}.

Here
\mathfrak{C}_{\ell^{\prime}}:=\prod_{j}\mathfrak{C}_{T^{(j)}}
as in (
9.96
) and (
9.97
) (where the decomposition is made for the resulting forest after removing these C-atoms, following Part 2), and the other notions are defined as above but associated with
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Moreover in (
9.98
),
\mathcal{E}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*}
is the set of all bonds at all the C-atoms removed above, so that each such bond becomes a free end in the reduced molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(which is a forest) after removing these C-atoms.

Consider each
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*}
, which is at some C-atom
\mathfrak{n}
removed above. Assume that this C-atom
\mathfrak{n}
belongs to the cluster
\mathbb{M}_{j}
. By iterating (
9.83
) and noticing that all collisions happen between particles in
p(\mathbb{M}_{j})
, we see that

(9.100)

\sum_{e}|v_{e}|^{2}\leq 2\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{j})}|v_{e}|^{2},\quad\mathrm{and\ hence}

(9.101)

\sum_{e}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\leq C\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{j})}|v_{e}|^{4/3},

where the left hand side of (
9.100
) and (
9.101
) is the sum over (at most 4) bonds
e
at some fixed atom
\mathfrak{n}
as above. Notice that the each end in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{j})
naturally corresponds to an end in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
(identify
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{j})
with the bottom end of the particle line in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
that
e
belongs to), and each
\mathbb{M}_{j}
has at most
\Gamma\leq C
different atoms
\mathfrak{n}
removed. We obtain, upon summing over
\mathfrak{n}
and then over
j
, that

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*}}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\leq C\sum_{j}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{j})}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\leq C\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})}|v_{e}|^{4/3},

so the last exponential factor on the right hand side of (
9.98
) can be absorbed by the one before it.

Proof part 4: general multi-layer case.
Now we put together all layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
and consider the general multi-layer molecule
\mathbb{M}
. By applying (
9.98
)–(
9.99
) for each
\ell^{\prime}
we get that

(9.102)

\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)\leq\mathfrak{C}^{5/6}\cdot C^{|\mathbb{M}|}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}\cdot\exp\bigg(C\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg),

(9.103)

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}1\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathbb{M}\backslash A}\leq\tau^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot\mathfrak{C}^{-1}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}|A|},

where
\mathfrak{C}:=\prod_{\ell^{\prime}}\mathfrak{C}_{\ell^{\prime}}
and the other notions are defined above for
\mathbb{M}
.

Now for each
(e,\ell^{\prime})
such that
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, this
e
is either an end in the molecule
\mathbb{M}
(which we call case 1), or a bond connecting layer
\ell^{\prime}
and some other layer
<\ell^{\prime}
(which we call case 2). For case 1, recall
\gamma=1
, we simply apply
\exp(C|v|^{4/3})\leq C\cdot\exp(|v^{2}|/20)
to get

(9.104)

\exp\bigg(C\sum_{\textrm{case\ 1}\textrm{ (for all }\ell^{\prime})}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg)\leq C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{20}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg)=C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{10}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg),

where
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}|v_{e}|^{2}=2\cdot\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}
by energy conservation. For case 2, any such bond
e
belongs to a particle line
p\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
. Note also that the number of all such particles
p
is bounded by

(9.105)

\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}s_{\ell^{\prime}}\leq\rho,

following Definition
7.1
. Now we apply the inequality
\exp(C|v|^{4/3})\leq C^{*}\cdot\exp(|v|^{2}/20d\mathfrak{L})
(recall our convention of
C
and
C^{*}
in Definition
1.6
(
2
)), and use also (
9.105
), to obtain that

(9.106)

\exp\bigg(C\sum_{\textrm{case\ 2}\textrm{ (fix }\ell^{\prime})}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg)\leq(C^{*})^{C^{*}\rho}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{20d\mathfrak{L}}\sum_{\textrm{case\ 2}\textrm{ (fix }\ell^{\prime})}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg).

By energy conservation, we have

(9.107)

\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{20d\mathfrak{L}}\sum_{\textrm{case\ 2}\textrm{ (fix }\ell^{\prime})}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg)\leq\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{20d\mathfrak{L}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg)

Multiplying all possible
\ell^{\prime}
yields

(9.108)

\exp\bigg(C\sum_{\textrm{case\ 2}\textrm{ (for all }\ell^{\prime})}|v_{e}|^{4/3}\bigg)\leq(C^{*})^{C^{*}\rho}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{10d}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg).

Now, we may put together (
9.102
),(
9.104
) and (
9.108
), and choose
\mathfrak{B}
to be the factor
\mathfrak{C}^{5/6}\cdot C^{|\mathbb{M}|}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
in (
9.102
), multiplied by the
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}
and
(C^{*})^{C^{*}\rho}
factors in (
9.104
), (
9.106
) and (
9.108
). It is then clear that
\mathfrak{B}\geq\mathfrak{C}^{6/5}\geq 1
, and (
9.84
) and (
9.85
) are true with these choices. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 9.9
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule, and fix a cutting sequence that reduces
\mathbb{M}
to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, which contains only empty ends and elementary molecules. Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a subset of the set of {3} molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
. For each atom
\mathfrak{n}
with
\{\mathfrak{n}\}\in\mathfrak{S}
, it has a unique fixed end
e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
, and a unique free end
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
that is either both top or both bottom
e_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
. Let the collection of all such
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
be
\mathcal{F}
.

Now suppose for each atom
\mathfrak{n}
with
\{\mathfrak{n}\}\in\mathfrak{S}
we have fixed
\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Assume also
v_{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the associated variable for each edge
e\in\mathbb{M}
, respectively. Moreover, assume
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
for each edge
e
of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, and for each atom
\mathfrak{n}
with
\{\mathfrak{n}\}\in\mathfrak{S}
and edges
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
as in Definition
3.23
, assume also that

(9.109)

v_{e_{1}^{\prime}}=v_{e_{1}}-[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}]\omega_{\mathfrak{n}},\quad v_{e_{2}^{\prime}}=v_{e_{2}}+[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}]\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}

(this is for C-atoms; for O-atoms we simply have
v_{e_{j}^{\prime}}=v_{e_{j}}
). For each
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}(\mathbb{M})
, fix

X_{e}\in[1,|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}]\cap 2^{\mathbb{Z}},

and assume that
|v_{e}|\leq X_{e}
. Finally let
K
be the number of molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
that do not belong to
\mathfrak{S}
(in reality
\mathfrak{S}
will be the set of normal {3} molecules, so
K=\#_{\neg\mathrm{N}\{3\}}
using the notion in (
9.54
)).

Then, there exists a set
Y\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{|\mathcal{F}|}
, which depends on
(\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
and
(X_{e})
, and on
(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
and the cutting sequence, but not on the concrete choices of
(v_{e})
, such that

(9.110)

(v_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{F}}\in Y,\quad|Y|\leq\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}X_{e}^{d}\cdot C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}K}.

Proof.

For each atom
\mathfrak{n}
with
\{\mathfrak{n}\}\in\mathfrak{S}
, let
(e_{1}(\mathfrak{n}),e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
and
(e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n}),e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})
be defined as in Proposition
9.9
and (
9.109
). Recall
\mathcal{F}
is the set of all
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
for
\{\mathfrak{n}\}\in\mathfrak{S}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be the set of all edges of molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
that are not in
\mathfrak{S}
, plus the ends of
\mathbb{M}
(which are preserved in cutting, so they are also free ends of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
). Note that
|\mathcal{F}|,|\mathcal{G}|\leq 10|\mathbb{M}|
. The idea is to show that, with
(\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
fixed and using (
9.109
), each vector
v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}
can be written as a constant coefficient linear combination of vectors
(v_{e^{\prime}})_{e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}
, and

(9.111)

\sum_{\mathfrak{n}}|v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}|^{2}\leq\sum_{e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}|v_{e^{\prime}}|^{2}.

We first prove the linear relation and (
9.111
) as claimed above. Start from any
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
. If
\mathfrak{n}
is O-atom then let
e=e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})
; if
\mathfrak{n}
is C-atom then we apply (
9.109
) to get a linear combination of
v_{e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})}
and
v_{e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})}
(and the energy inequality
|v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n})}|^{2}\leq|v_{e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})}|^{2}+|v_{e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})}|^{2}
), and then let
e
be either
e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})
or
e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n})
. If
e\in\mathcal{G}
then this is already what we need; if
e\not\in\mathcal{G}
we need to further explore from
e
to reach
\mathcal{G}
, as follows.

Note that
e
is not an end in
\mathbb{M}
(as
e\not\in\mathcal{G}
), so it must occur during a cutting operation, say while operating on a maximal ov-segment
\sigma
(with associated notions
\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
) as in Definition
8.3
. It is easy to verify that
e
does not belong to a simple pair (Definition
8.2
), so by Definition
8.3
(
2
), we see that the only way this cutting creates a new free end
e
is that
\mathfrak{p}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{n}^{+}
or
\mathfrak{p}_{s}\neq\mathfrak{n}^{-}
(for example in
Figure
16
, we get a new free end
e_{5}^{\prime}
due to
\mathfrak{p}_{3}\neq\mathfrak{n}^{-}
). Assume the former by symmetry, then
\mathfrak{n}^{+}\in\sigma\backslash A
, so the same cutting creates a fixed end
e_{1}
at
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
, with
v_{e}=v_{e_{1}}
. If
\{\mathfrak{n}^{+}\}\not\in\mathfrak{S}
, then we have
e_{1}\in\mathcal{G}
by definition, which is what we need; thus we will assume below that
\{\mathfrak{n}^{+}\}\in\mathfrak{S}
.

if
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
is O-atom, then
e_{1}
must be serial with a free end
e^{\prime}
, so
v_{e}=v_{e^{\prime}}
. If
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
is C-atom, then
e_{1}=e_{1}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})
, so we can add in
e_{2}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})\in\mathcal{F}
: both
v_{e}
and
v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})}
are linear combinations of
v_{e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})}
and
v_{e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})}
, and
|v_{e}|^{2}+|v_{e_{2}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})}|^{2}=|v_{e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})}|^{2}+|v_{e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})}|^{2}
, then we can choose
e^{\prime}
be either
e_{1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})
or
e_{2}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})
. In either case, if
e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
then this is what we need; if not then we can explore from
e^{\prime}
as above. In the end, every exploration will stop at some
e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
, which proves the desired linear relation and (
9.111
).

Now, by (
9.111
), we have
(v_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}\big((v_{e^{\prime}})_{e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\big)
for some constant matrix
\mathcal{P}
which matrix norm
\|\mathcal{P}\|\leq 1
.
Note that
|v_{e^{\prime}}|\leq X_{e^{\prime}}
is
e^{\prime}
is an end in
\mathbb{M}
, and
|v_{e^{\prime}}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
if
e^{\prime}
belongs to a molecule in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
that is not in
\mathfrak{S}
, and the number of the latter case does not exceed
10K
. It follows that the vector
(v_{e^{\prime}})_{e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}
belongs to the union of at most

A:=C^{|\mathcal{G}|}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}K}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}}X_{e}^{d}

fixed unit boxes in
\mathbb{R}^{d|\mathcal{G}|}
. However, the image of any unit box in
\mathbb{R}^{d|\mathcal{G}|}
under
\mathcal{P}
is contained in a ball of radius
(d|\mathcal{G}|)^{1/2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d|\mathcal{F}|}
, which is covered by at most

C^{F}\cdot(F+G)^{F/2}\cdot\mathrm{Vol}\,(\textrm{unit\ ball\ in\ }\mathbb{R}^{F})=C^{F}\cdot(F+G)^{F/2}\cdot\pi^{F/2}\cdot\big(\Gamma(F/2+1)\big)^{-1}\leq C^{F+G}

unit boxes, where
G:=d|\mathcal{G}|
and
F:=d|\mathcal{F}|
. It then follows that
(v_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{F}}
belongs to the union of at most
C^{d(|\mathcal{F}|+|\mathcal{G}|)}\cdot A
fixed unit boxes in
\mathbb{R}^{d|\mathcal{F}|}
, so (
9.110
) follows.
∎

10.
Preparations for the algorithm

From this section until Section
13
, we will prove Proposition
9.7
, the main goal of the third part of the paper. In this section, we make some preparations for the proof by restating the properties of the molecule
\mathbb{M}
in Proposition
9.7
(originally stated in Definition
3.20
) in a more convenient way for the algorithm.

Start from the initial links
\mathcal{L}
; we shall replace it by some new initial link set
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
where each particle line belongs to
at most two
new initial links in
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
. This is because, see the proof of Proposition
12.17
, for any initial link, we will gain a power of
\varepsilon
from the particle line in it that is cut
after
the other; then the condition for
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
ensures that the number of gains from this is comparable with the number of initial links.

Proposition 10.1
.

Recall the set
\mathcal{L}
of initial links (see Proposition
5.18
), let the set of particle lines involved in
\mathcal{L}
be
H_{0}
. Then we have

(10.1)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}\leq\sum_{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{\varepsilon+}.

Here
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}
is defined as in (
5.72
),
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
is a set of pairs
(p_{1},p_{2})
where
p_{1},p_{2}\in H_{0}
satisfying that each particle line
p\in H_{0}
belongs to
one or two
pairs in
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
, and
\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{\varepsilon+}
is defined as

(10.2)

\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{\varepsilon+}=\prod_{(p_{1},p_{2})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}\mathbbm{1}_{e_{1}\sim_{\mathrm{in}}p_{2}}^{\varepsilon+}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\mathbbm{1}_{e_{1}\sim_{\mathrm{in}}p_{2}}^{\varepsilon+}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1\quad&&|x_{p_{1}}-x_{p_{2}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon},\\
&\varepsilon^{\upsilon}\quad&&|x_{p_{1}}-x_{p_{2}}|\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon},\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

For fixed values of
(x_{p})_{p\in H_{0}}
, we only need to prove that there exists
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(x_{p})\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{\varepsilon+}(x_{p})
. Define a graph
\mathcal{L}_{0}
which is a subgraph of
\mathcal{L}
, such that
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
if and only if
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
and
|x_{p_{1}}-x_{p_{2}}|\leq\varepsilon
. Clearly we may restrict to a single component of the forest
\mathcal{L}
(which is a tree), then in this component,
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is obtained from
\mathcal{L}
by deleting those edges
(p_{1},p_{2})
with
|x_{p_{1}}-x_{p_{2}}|\geq\varepsilon
. Let the number of deleted edges be
q
, then
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(x_{p})=\varepsilon^{\upsilon q}
by definition. On the other hand,
\mathcal{L}_{0}
has exactly
q+1
connected components (as it is formed by deleting
q
edges from a tree), and for any
(p_{1},p_{2})
that belong to the same component, we must have
|x_{p_{1}}-x_{p_{2}}|\leq\varepsilon|H_{0}|\ll\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
by triangle inequality. Now we simply list all the particle lines in
H_{0}
by enumerating each component of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
one after another, and construct
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
by forming an edge between any two adjacent particle lines in the above list, then it is easy to see that
|x_{p_{1}}-x_{p_{2}}|\geq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
occurs at most
q
times (when entering new components), which implies
\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{\varepsilon+}(x_{p})\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon q}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(x_{p})
, as desired.
∎

Remark 10.2
.

In view of Proposition
10.1
, below we will replace
\mathcal{L}
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
in all instances, and refer to
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
as the (new)
initial links
, and change Definition
9.4
etc. using these new initial links. Note that (i) the number of choices of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
(i.e. the number of terms in the summation (
10.2
)) is bounded by
(C|H_{0}|)!
. Since
|H_{0}|\leq\rho\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, this resulting combinatorial factor is bounded by
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
which can be absorbed by Proposition
7.2
. (ii) The proof of Proposition
6.2
in Section
9
is not affected by these new initial links. For example, in Proposition
9.2
(
3
), we are only assuming the weaker assumption
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
consistent with
\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{\varepsilon+}
, so any good molecule with respect to the new initial links still carries the same power gain as in (
9.78
).

Definition 10.3
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
be the layers of
\mathbb{M}
. Define the
layer intervals

\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{1}:\ell_{2}]}:=\bigcup_{\ell_{1}\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{2}}\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}},\quad\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1}:\ell_{2})}:=\bigcup_{\ell_{1}<\ell^{\prime}<\ell_{2}}\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\quad\mathbb{M}_{<\ell_{1}}=\bigcup_{\ell^{\prime}<\ell_{1}}\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}},\quad\textrm{etc.}

After defining the refined layers
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta}^{T}
(Definition
12.8
) below. we will use the same notations
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{1}:\zeta_{2}]}^{T}
etc. for the intervals of refined layers.

Now we can state the main result of this section, namely Proposition
10.4
below.

Proposition 10.4
.

Let
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
as in Definition
3.20
(
1
) and
r(\mathbb{M})=H
. Then we have the following:

(1)

The total number of atoms and particle lines in
\mathbb{M}
is bounded by
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
.

(2)

No two atoms can be connected by two different ov-segments.
11
11

11

This property is specific to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and needs to be appropriately modified for
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Properties (
3
)–(
4
) below correspond to the clusters (Definition
3.11
) in each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\,(1\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell)
.

(3)

Any cycle in a single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
must be formed by ov-segments connecting C-atoms in the same cluster. In particular, for any cycle in a single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
and any O-atom in this cycle, the two bonds of the cycle at this O-atom must be serial. see
Figure
18
.

(4)

For any cluster
\mathbb{Q}
in each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, we have that
\rho(\mathbb{Q})\leq\Gamma
(Definition
3.13
). Recall that a cluster
\mathbb{Q}
or component is cyclic (or recollisional) if
\rho(\mathbb{Q})>0
, i.e. if it contains a cycle.

Properties (
5
)–(
7
) below correspond to the particle line sets
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
and
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
(Definition
3.18
).

(5)

Each particle line
{\bm{p}}
must belong to some
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
for some
\ell^{\prime}
. If
{\bm{p}}
intersects
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(Definition
3.19
), then
{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. If
{\bm{p}}
contains an atom in both
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{2}}
, then
{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
for all
\ell^{\prime}\in[\ell_{1}:\ell_{2}]
.

(6)

We have
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=H
, and
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})=p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})\cap p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1})
for
1\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1
. Define also
r(\mathbb{M}_{0})=H_{0}
.

(7)

For
1\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
, each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
must intersect a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. Each particle line in
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
is either connected to a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, or belongs to
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. See
Figure
9
.

Property (
8
) below corresponds to the (new) initial links, see Proposition
10.1
.

(8)

The bottom end of each particle line in
H_{0}
is involved in 1 or 2 initial links. Note that initial links can be understood as either between particle lines or between bottom ends (which are in bijection).

Property (
9
) below ensures the connectedness of particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. Recall Definition
3.19
and Definition
10.3
.

(9)

For each
1\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
, and any particle line
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, this
{\bm{p}}
is either connected to another particle line
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
via
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
, or connected to a cycle within
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
, or forms an initial link within
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
. See
Figure
9
.

The
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
case.
Finally, suppose
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
and
r(\mathbb{M})=H
. Then
\mathbb{M}
satisfies all of the above properties (
1
)–(
9
), except that (
9
) is true only for
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1
.

Figure 18.
An example of Proposition
10.4
(
3
); here
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
are clusters in a single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Since the cluster graph is a forest, it follows that the only cycles in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
are formed by ov-segments between C-atoms in the same cluster (the triangle in
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
formed by ov-segments), so one cannot transit to another ov-segment (i.e. non-serial) at any O-atom (
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
or
\mathfrak{p}_{3}
).

Proof.

We first prove (
1
)–(
8
), then prove the harder (
9
). The proof in the
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
case is the same (since Definition
3.20
(
1a
)–(
1d
) are the same except that (
1d
) now requires
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1
).

Proof of (
1
)–(
8
).
For (
1
): this is already proved in Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
7.2
.

For (
2
): this follows from the correspondence between molecules and physical trajectories in Definition
3.8
, and the fact that two (physical) particles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
cannot collide/overlap and immediately collide/overlap again without other any other collision in between.

For (
3
): recall Definition
3.11
. We know
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
can be constructed, starting from the C-molecule with only the clusters, by adding the O-atoms corresponding to the overlaps joining different clusters, according to cluster graph of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Since this cluster graph is a forest (Definition
3.20
(
1c
)), we know that the process of adding O-atoms will
not
generate new cycles in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, and will only insert O-atoms into edges of existing cycles (turning these edges into ov-segments). Therefore the first statement in (
3
) is true. The second statement follows from the first, as the O-atom must be an interior atom of an ov-segment connecting two C-atoms.

For (
4
): this is just Definition
3.20
(
1a
).

For (
5
): this follows directly from Definition
3.18
.

For (
6
