): this follows directly from Definition
3.18
(see also Definition
3.3
) that
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=r(\mathbb{M})=H
, and
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})=p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})\cap p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1})
.

For (
7
): this is just Definition
3.20
(
1b
).

For (
8
): this directly follows from Proposition
10.1
.

Proof of (
9
).
Fix any
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. If
{\bm{p}}
is connected to another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
then we are done. If not, by Definition
3.20
(
1d
), there exists
{\bm{p}}_{1}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
such that
{\bm{p}}
is either equal to or connected to
{\bm{p}}_{1}
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. We then replace
{\bm{p}}
by
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
\ell^{\prime}
by
\ell^{\prime}-1
and repeat the above discussion: either
{\bm{p}}_{1}
is connected to
{\bm{p}}_{1}\neq{\bm{p}}_{1}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-1}
, or we can find
{\bm{p}}_{2}
, and so on.

In the end, either we reach
{\bm{p}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{0})=H_{0}
, or we get
{\bm{p}}_{i}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-i})
for some
0\leq i<\ell^{\prime}
, which is connected to
{\bm{p}}_{i}\neq{\bm{p}}_{i}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-i})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-i}
. In the former case, either
{\bm{p}}={\bm{p}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
or
{\bm{p}}
is connected to
{\bm{p}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
via
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
; by Proposition
10.4
(
8
), this
{\bm{p}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is involved in an initial link, so
{\bm{p}}
forms an initial link within
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
by definition.

Now assume we get
{\bm{p}}_{i}
with
0\leq i<\ell^{\prime}
as said above. By (
7
) we just proved (and note that
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-i})\subseteq p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-i+1})
), there exists
{\bm{p}}_{i-1}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-i+1})
such that
{\bm{p}}_{i}^{\prime}
either equals or is connected to
{\bm{p}}_{i-1}^{\prime}
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-i+1}
. We then replace
{\bm{p}}_{i}^{\prime}
by
{\bm{p}}_{i-1}^{\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime}-i
by
\ell^{\prime}-i+1
and repeat this process, and subsequently find
{\bm{p}}_{i-2}^{\prime},\cdots,{\bm{p}}_{1}^{\prime},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, see
Figure
19
. Note that
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
.

Now, if
{\bm{p}}\neq{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, then we can transit from
{\bm{p}}
to
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and then
{\bm{p}}_{2},\cdots,{\bm{p}}_{i}
in this order, and then transit to
{\bm{p}}_{i}
, and then transit to
{\bm{p}}_{i-1}^{\prime},\cdots,{\bm{p}}_{1}^{\prime},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
in this order. In each transit step, the two particle lines involved are either equal or connected via bonds within a subset of
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
; putting together, we get that
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{p}}\neq{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
are connected via
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
.

If instead
{\bm{p}}={\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, note that
{\bm{p}}_{i}\neq{\bm{p}}_{i}^{\prime}
, so we choose the smallest
1\leq n\leq i
such that
{\bm{p}}_{n}\neq{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime}
. In this case, we know that
{\bm{p}}_{n}
and
{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime}
are both connected to
{\bm{p}}_{n-1}
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-n+1}
, so
{\bm{p}}_{n}
is also connected to
{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime}
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-n+1}
(see Remark
10.5
below). Moreover
{\bm{p}}_{n}
is also connected to
{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime}
via
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}-n]}
, by transiting from
{\bm{p}}_{n}
to
({\bm{p}}_{n+1},\cdots,{\bm{p}}_{i})
in this order and then to
({\bm{p}}_{i}^{\prime},{\bm{p}}_{i-1}^{\prime},\cdots,{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime})
in this order similar to the above paragraph. Then, by putting together a chain of bonds in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}-n+1}
connecting
{\bm{p}}_{n}
to
{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime}
, another chain of bonds in
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}-n]}
connecting
{\bm{p}}_{n}
to
{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime}
, and possibly a chain of bonds within the particle line
{\bm{p}}_{n}
and another chain of bonds within the particle line
{\bm{p}}_{n}^{\prime}
, we obtain a cycle in
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}-n+1]}\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
. Since
{\bm{p}}
is connected to
{\bm{p}}_{n}
(and thus to this cycle) via
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
by transiting from
{\bm{p}}
to
{\bm{p}}_{1},\cdots{\bm{p}}_{n}
in this order similar to the above paragraph, we conclude that
{\bm{p}}
is connected to cycle within
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Figure 19.
Left: a set
A
as in Remark
10.5
with particle lines
{\bm{p}}
,
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}
all connected via
A
; note that the part of
{\bm{p}}
between two atoms in
A
is again
\subseteq A
. Right: the procedure of finding
({\bm{p}}_{1},\cdots,{\bm{p}}_{i})
and then
({\bm{p}}_{i},\cdots,{\bm{p}}^{\prime})
in the proof of Proposition
10.4
(
9
).

To end this section, we record some intuitive facts about molecules, particle lines and cuttings, which will be used frequently in the algorithm below.

Remark 10.5
.

Let
A
be a layer interval
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{1}:\ell_{2}]}
(Definition
10.3
), or any union of connected components of a layer interval, or the same for thin layers (defined in Definition
12.8
below). It is easy to see that: for any atoms
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in A
that belong to the same particle line, and any atom
\mathfrak{m}
in the same particle line between
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, we must have
\mathfrak{m}\in A
. This implies that (i) any particle line
{\bm{p}}
can intersect at most one component of
A
, and (ii) if two particle lines
{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2}
are both connected to a particle line
{\bm{p}}
via
A
(Definition
3.19
), then
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}
are also connected via
A
. This is used in the proof of Proposition
10.4
(
9
) above; see
Figure
19
.

Remark 10.6
.

The following fact will be convenient to note: suppose we cut
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}

as free
, then this may divide the remaining set
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
into connected components; however, any
full
component of
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
must also be a full component of
\mathbb{M}
before cutting
A
as free (in other words, any
newly created
connected component must have at least one fixed end). This is because, when any bond or ov-segment is broken in cutting
A
as free, we get a fixed end at
every atom
in this ov-segment that belongs to
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
, see Remark
8.6
.

Remark 10.7
.

In the algorithm below, we usually adopt the following convention: once we cut out any elementary molecule
S
, or cut out any subset
S
(as free or as fixed) and subsequently cut it into elementary molecules, we will ignore this
S
for the rest of the cutting sequence and consider the remaining molecule
\mathbb{M}\backslash S
only. For example, when we say “keep cutting deg 2 atoms as free until there is no more deg 2 atom”, we always mean there is no more deg 2 atom in the remaining molecule.

11.
Main thread of the algorithm

We now start the proof of Proposition
9.7
. Most of the proof below will focus on the case
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}
; the case
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
is left to the end of Section
13.5
, and will only take two paragraphs. In this section, we first discuss a simplified setting which contains most of the main ideas.

11.1.
Toy models

To prove Proposition
9.7
, we need to construct an operation sequence (with cutting, deleting and splitting). This will be done using a number of
cutting algorithms
, which will be presented in Sections
12
–
13
.

The full cutting algorithms are quite complicated; however, there is a much simpler
main thread
, and many of the complications actually come from the additional structures and conditions (“accessories”) that have to be added to this main thread. In this section, we present the proof in a simplified scenario, which basically isolates this main thread. The accessories needed in full generality will be discussed in Section
11.7
.

Throughout this section, we will make the following two simplifications (and more later):

(1)

\mathbb{M}
has no O-atoms, all {33} molecules are good, and
H_{0}=\varnothing
(see Proposition
10.4
(
6
));

(2)

Each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
of
\mathbb{M}
is a forest.

Here, regarding (
1
), recall that {33} molecules are good provided certain
non-degeneracy
conditions are met (which is different for {33A} and {33B} molecules, see (
9.47
)–(
9.48
) in Definition
9.4
). As such, simplification (
1
) allows us to ignore any O-atoms, degeneracies and initial (time
t=0
) cumulants, and (
2
) allows us to ignore any cycles (or “recollisions”) happening within each layer.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
satisfying Proposition
10.4
and (
1
)–(
2
), the goal of this section is to construct a cutting sequence that cuts
\mathbb{M}
into elementary molecules, such that (recall
\upsilon=3^{-d-1}
)

(11.1)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\{33\}}-d\cdot\#_{\{4\}}\geq c\cdot\rho,

where
\#_{\{33\}}
is the number of {33} molecules etc.,
c
is a positive constant and
\rho
is in Definition
7.1
(the splitting and deletion of O-atoms is not needed in this simplified model; also note that there cannot be any empty end if
H_{0}=\varnothing
, which easily follows from Proposition
10.4
(
9
)).

In Sections
11.2
–
11.5
, we will consider a further simplified case, when
\mathbb{M}
contains only
two layers
(for treatment of multi-layer case, see Section
11.6
). We make the following definition:

Definition 11.1
.

We say
\mathbb{M}
is a
2-layer model
, if it satisfies (
1
)–(
2
), and that

(3)

\mathbb{M}
can be written as
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
, such that no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is parent of an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
;

(4)

Each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a tree, has no fixed end, and the number of bonds connecting
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is at least
\rho
.

Note that in practice, the
\rho
in (
4
) may be replaced by some different quantity, but they will always be comparable, so this will not affect the proof.

Figure 20.
Examples of toy models. Upper left: toy model I (
X=\mathbb{M}_{U}
). Upper right: toy model I plus (where
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}=2
). Lower left: toy model II (where
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}=|X|
). Lower right: toy model III (where
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\sim|X|/2
). The blue bonds connect
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. The orange and pink atoms indicate those in
X
and 2-connections respectively. The pink dots represent patterns that can be repeated.

In our proof, the 2-layer model in Definition
11.1
will be further divided into 3 cases, corresponding to
toy models I–III
defined below. This classification is based on the following two considerations:

(1)

Consider the set of atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
that are adjacent to
two
atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
by two bonds. Is this set big or small relative to
\rho
?

(2)

Consider all the atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
that are adjacent to
at least one
atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
by at least one bond. Are they
all connected
to each other, or are they
separated
by other atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
?

The reason for considering these two questions will be clear later. For convenience, in this section we will refer to the atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
that are adjacent to
two
atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as
2-connections
, and denote their number by
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}
. We also define the set of atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
that are adjacent to
at least one
atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
by
X
(note that
|X|\gtrsim\rho
), and define the number of connected components of
X
(with
X
viewed by itself, without passing through atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash X
) by
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
. We now define a 2-layer molecule to be:

•

Toy model I
: if
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
and
X=\mathbb{M}_{U}
. This case involves the core arguments in our algorithm in its simplest form, and will be proved in Section
11.2
.

•

Toy model I plus
: if
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
and
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\ll|X|
(no 2-connections,
X
has few components). This is an extension of the toy model I, and can be treated in similar manners, in Section
11.3
. Here we need to introduce the new idea of
UP
algorithm, which will play an important role in the later proofs.

•

Toy model II
: if
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
and
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\gtrsim|X|
(no 2-connections,
X
has many components). This requires a different argument, which involves making a clever choice in the
UP
algorithm introduced above, see Section
11.4
.

•

Toy model III
: if
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\gtrsim|X|
(many 2-connections). This relies on a simpler argument, which again involves making suitable choices in the
UP
algorithm, see Section
11.5
.

See
Figure
20
for examples of toy models I–III (we distinguish toy models I and I plus only for convenience of exposition). Also the case when
0<\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\ll|X|
, which is missing in the toy models, can be treated similarly to toy models I–II but with an extra step of removing 2-connections, see Section
11.7.4
.

Once all the toy models (and hence the 2-layer model) have been addressed in Sections
11.2
–
11.5
, we can then treat the multi-layer case in Section
11.6
. This relies on the process of
layer selection
, which effectively reduces any multi-layer
\mathbb{M}
to (basically) a 2-layer model, for which the proofs in Sections
11.2
–
11.5
apply.

Finally, in Section
11.7
we discuss the simplifications made in (
1
)–(
4
) above compared to the full generality case, as well as the corresponding accessories (additions, extra conditions and proofs) that are needed to treat the full generality setting.

Before proceeding, we make one remark about the presentation in this section and in Sections
12
–
13
: in this section, the cases are arranged for
exposition purposes
, for example we present the 2-layer model before layer selection, and present toy models I and I plus before the other toy models, in order to showcase the core ideas of our proof as early as possible. However, in Sections
12
–
13
we need to present the materials in
logical order
, so we need to introduce layer selection (Section
12.4
) before the toy models (Section
13
), and toy model III (Definition
13.1
–Proposition
13.2
) before toy model I plus (Definition
13.12
–Proposition
13.13
), etc.

To make the proof easier to read, each time we discuss any argument in Sections
12
–
13
that has a counterpart in this section, we will explicitly point out which argument in this section does it correspond to. See
Table
1
for a summary of the toy models and layer selection, and where each of them occurs in Sections
12
–
13
. Also, in
Table
2
we list the simplifications made in Definition
11.1
, the corresponding accessories needed in the general setting, and where each of them occurs in Sections
12
–
13
.

11.2.
Toy model I

In this subsection we consider the following (first) toy model:

Definition 11.2
.

We say
\mathbb{M}
is a
toy model I
, if it is a 2-layer model as in Definition
11.1
, and that each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has
exactly
one bond connecting to an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(i.e.
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
and
X=\mathbb{M}_{U}
).

Definition 11.3
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{D}
be part of the 2-layer model as in Definition
11.1
(or any resulting molecule after an operation sequence). We define
\mathbb{M}_{D}
to be
proper
, if it does not contain deg 1 or 2 atoms, does not contain two adjacent deg 3 atoms, and does not contain two deg 3 atoms which are both adjacent to the same deg 4 atom, see
Figure
21
.

Figure 21.
Examples of {33} and {343} molecules (left), and proper molecules (right, see Definition
11.3
). Note these notions are applied to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, in which the top/bottom distinction is less important, hence we draw an equivalent simplified picture below, with no distinction of top/bottom, and with the deg (i.e. degree) of each atom indicated. Similar simplified illustrations will occur a few times below.

If
\mathbb{M}
is a toy model I, we define the following algorithm applied to it:

Definition 11.4

(algorithm for toy model I)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a toy model I. We define the following cutting sequence. For an example see
Figure
22
:

(1)

If not all atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
have been cut, then choose a lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
that has not been cut (i.e. either
\mathfrak{n}
has not child in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
or all its children in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
have been cut).

(2)

If
\mathfrak{n}
is adjacent to an atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
which has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m}\}
as free and cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free from
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m}\}
if
\mathfrak{n}
has deg 4; otherwise just cut
\mathfrak{n}
as free.

(3)

If
\mathbb{M}_{D}
contains two adjacent deg 3 atoms (say
\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
), then cut
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}\}
as free; repeat until
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no adjacent deg 3 atoms.

(4)

If
\mathbb{M}_{D}
contains two degree 3 atoms (say
\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}
) both adjacent to the same deg 4 atom (say
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
), then cut
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
as free and cut
\mathfrak{r}
as free from
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
; then go to
3
–
4
and repeat, until
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes proper (Definition
11.3
).

(5)

Go to
1
and choose the next lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
that has not been cut, and so on.

(6)

Suppose all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
have been cut. If
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is proper, then cut any deg 3 atom; if not, then go to
3
–
4
and repeat to remove them, and next cut any deg 3 atom, and so on.

Figure 22.
Algorithm for toy model I (Definition
11.4
), applied to the example in
Figure
20
. Each time we cut the atom(s) in the red box, creating fixed ends. The type of the elementary molecules to be cut is listed at the upper right corner.

Remark 11.5
.

In Definition
11.4
, we are essentially applying a “naive” algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
where we simply cut a lowest atom each time, and a “greedy” algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
where we exploit a {33} molecule (or what we call a {343} molecule
12
12

12

The reason to include this {343} molecule is technical: removing only {33} cannot exclude all forbidden scenarios, i.e. Lemma
11.8
would not be true if we did this.
where two deg 3 atoms are adjacent to the same deg 4 atom). As mentioned above, this is the core idea of our proof, in its bare simplest form. It is a simple special case of the
MAINUD
algorithm, which corresponds to the toy model I plus in Section
11.3
.

Before stating the main result for the algorithm in Definition
11.2
, we first prove a useful lemma that will be applied frequently below, see
Figure
23
.

Lemma 11.6
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a forest and we cut a
connected subset

S\subseteq\mathbb{M}
as free (for example, when
S
contains a single atom, or 2 atoms connected by a bond, or 3 atoms connected by 2 bonds, etc.). Then, for each atom
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash S
that is connected to an atom in
S
by a (pre-cutting) bond
e
, the post-cutting molecule
\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
has exactly one component
X_{\mathfrak{q}}\ni\mathfrak{q}
, which is in bijection with
\mathfrak{q}
(and
\mathfrak{q}
is the unique atom in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
that is adjacent to
S
pre-cutting, via the bond
e
). When viewed in
X_{q}
, the bond
e
is viewed as a free end at
\mathfrak{q}
before cutting, and is turned into a fixed end after cutting.

Figure 23.
An illustration of Lemma
11.6
. Here
S
is a connected subset, and there are bijections between
\mathfrak{q}
and
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, where
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
is connected to
S
by a bond
e
, and
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
is the component containing
\mathfrak{q}
after cutting
S
as free. When viewed in
X_{q}
, the operation of cutting
S
as free creates exactly one fixed end in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
. All these crucially depend on the fact that
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
have no cycles.

Proof.

This follows from the property of forests. In fact, any
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{2}
(with corresponding bonds
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
) must belong to different components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
after cutting
S
; if not, then (pre-cutting) they can be connected by a path involving atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
, but they can also be connected by a path with all intermediate atoms being in
S
(using the bonds
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
and the connectivity of
S
by itself), which produces a cycle and contradicts the forest assumption. Thus, for each
\mathfrak{q}
we get a unique post-cutting component
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, such that
e
is the only pre-cutting bond between
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
and
S
. Clearly, when viewed in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, the effect of cutting
S
as free is equivalent to turning one and only one free end at
\mathfrak{q}
(the one corresponding to
e
) into a fixed end.
∎

Proposition 11.7
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a toy model I, and we apply the algorithm defined in Definition
11.4
. Then in this process we only obtain elementary molecules, and we have

(11.2)

\#_{\{4\}}=1,\qquad\#_{\{33\}}\geq(|\mathbb{M}_{U}|-1)/5.

Since
|\mathbb{M}_{U}|=|X|\gtrsim\rho\gg 1
, this clearly implies (
11.1
).

Proof.

Recall Definition
11.4

1
–
6
. We divide the proof into 4 parts.

Proof part 1.
We first prove that each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is contained in an elementary molecule (after cutting). By the choices we make in
1
and
5
in Definition
11.4
, we know that for each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
, at the time it is cut, it must be a
lowest
atom that still remains in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
; thus no
child
of it still remains in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, so cutting
\mathfrak{n}
will not introduce any
top fixed end
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(and neither does cutting anything in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
). This means that
\mathbb{M}_{U}
will not contain any top fixed end throughout the process. Since each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
is either cut by itself (when we cut
\{\mathfrak{n}\}
as free in Definition
11.4

2
) or belongs to a {33} molecule (when we cut
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m}\}
as free in Definition
11.4

2
), it is clear by definition of elementary molecules that
\mathfrak{n}
always belongs to an elementary molecule.

Proof part 2.
We next prove that each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is contained in an elementary molecule. Note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
initially is a tree, and will remain a forest in the whole cutting process (cutting an atom will not create any cycle, but may cause a tree to become a disconnected forest). Now consider each step in Definition
11.4

2
involving
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
, at which time
\mathbb{M}_{D}
must be proper by the construction of our algorithm. Consider the possible effect of this step on
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(we may assume that
\mathfrak{n}
is connected to one atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
by a bond, otherwise cutting
\mathfrak{n}
will have no effect on
\mathbb{M}_{D}
), see
Figure
24
:

(1)

If we cut
\mathfrak{n}
as free (which means
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 4), then when viewed in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, this step simply turns one and only one free end at
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
into a fixed end, turning
\mathfrak{m}
from deg 4 to deg 3.

(2)

If we cut
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m}\}
as free (which means
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3), then when viewed in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, this step simply cuts
\mathfrak{m}
, which has deg 3, as free from
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Here in
1
, we note that
\mathfrak{m}
must have deg 4 before cutting
\mathfrak{n}
(otherwise
\mathfrak{m}
must have deg 3 because
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is proper, in which case we will cut
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m}\}
by Definition
11.4

2
); also when viewed in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, the bond between
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}
is initially viewed as a free end at
\mathfrak{m}
, and is then turned into a fixed end after cutting
\mathfrak{n}
as free, turning
\mathfrak{m}
from deg 4 to deg 3. Likewise
\mathfrak{m}
must have deg 3 in
2
, and when viewed in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, cutting
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m}\}
as free is equivalent to cutting
\{\mathfrak{m}\}
as free because
\mathfrak{n}
has no other bonds connecting to other atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Figure 24.
Operations (a) and (b) defined in the proof of Proposition
11.7
. In either case an atom
\mathfrak{m}_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
is connected to an atom
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
by a blue bond. In case (a)
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
has deg 4, and we cut
\{\mathfrak{n}_{1}\}
as free, creating a fixed end at
\mathfrak{m}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
and turning it into deg 3; in case (b)
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
has deg 3, and we cut
\{\mathfrak{n}_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2}\}
as free, which when viewed in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
just corresponds to cutting
\{\mathfrak{m}_{2}\}
as free, creating fixed ends at its adjacent atoms.

Now, regarding the effects described in
1
–
2
, we have the following lemma:

Figure 25.
The greedy algorithm in Definition
11.4
. Starting from upper left graph (which is proper), we apply operation (a), which creates a {343} molecule (in red box). We cut it, which then creates a {33} molecule. We cut it, and the graph becomes proper again.

Lemma 11.8
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is proper at some point of the algorithm, and becomes non-proper after either
1
or
2
. Then,
\mathbb{M}_{D}
will once more become proper after applying Definition
11.4

3
–
4
, via cuttings of {33} and {343} molecules only (with each {343} molecule further cut into a {3} and a {33} molecule), and
without seeing any deg 2 atom
, see
Figure
25
for an illustration.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is always a forest, so we can apply Lemma
11.6
. Note that right before performing
2
at a deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{m}
, any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
adjacent to
\mathfrak{m}
must have deg 4 (because
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is proper). Then by Lemma
11.6
,
\mathbb{M}_{D}
will break into at most 2 components after performing
2
, and the effect of
2

when viewed in each component
is equivalent to
1
. Therefore, we only need to prove Lemma
11.8
for
1
.

Assume now
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes non-proper, say a {33} molecule forms, after
1
. Let this molecule be
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}\}
, where
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
has deg 3 before
1
, while
\mathfrak{r}
has deg 4 before
1
and is turned into deg 3 after
1
. Then,
before
1
, any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
adjacent to
\mathfrak{r}
or
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}

must have deg 4
(see
Figure
26
) because otherwise we either have two adjacent deg 3 atoms before
1
, or have two deg 3 atoms adjacent to the same deg 4 atom before
1
, contradicting properness. Therefore, by Lemma
11.6
, we see that cutting the {33} molecule breaks
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into at most 4 components, and the effect of this cutting
when viewed in each component
is again equivalent to
1
. We then induct on the size of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
and apply induction hypothesis to each of these components, to conclude the proof.

Finally, assume
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes non-proper, but with no {33} molecule forming, after
1
. In this case there must be a {343} molecule forming, say
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
, where
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}
respectively have deg 4 and 3 before
1
, and
\mathfrak{r}
has deg 4 before
1
and is turned into deg 3 after
1
. Then,
before
1
, any atom adjacent to
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
or
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}

must have deg 4
(see
Figure
26
) for the same reason as above thanks to properness. Moreover, before
1
, any atom adjacent to
\mathfrak{r}
must also have deg 4, because otherwise we would get a {33} molecule after
1
, contradicting our assumption. Therefore, by Lemma
11.6
, we see that cutting the {343} molecule breaks
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into at most 6 components, and the effect of this cutting
when viewed in each component
is again equivalent to
1
. We then conclude the proof by the same induction as above. This proves Lemma
11.8
.
∎

Figure 26.
Proof of Lemma
11.8
. After applying (a) to a proper
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, a deg 4 atom is turned into deg 3, thus creating a {33} (top) or {343} molecule (bottom). Crucially, all the atoms with “?” must be deg 4, because
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is proper before applying (a), and because applying (a) does not create {33} molecule in the bottom case. Then, after cutting the {33} or {343} molecule, when viewed in each resulting component (which contains one “?” atom), we create only one fixed end and turn the “?” atom from deg 4 to deg 3, and so on.

Now, with Lemma
11.8
, we can finish Part 2 of the proof, i.e. each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is contained in an elementary molecule. In fact, those cut in Definition
11.4

2
clearly belong to {33} molecule. Those cut in Definition
11.4

3
–
4
also belong to {33} or {343} (and subsequently {3} or {33}) molecules by Lemma
11.8
. Finally, those cut in Definition
11.4

6
either belong to {3} molecule, or are cut in the process of applying Definition
11.4

3
–
4
and thus belong to {3} or {33} molecules by Lemma
11.8
.

Proof part 3.
With Parts 1 and 2 of the proof, we now know that every resulting molecule is elementary; it suffices to calculate
\#_{\{4\}}
and
\#_{\{33\}}
. We first show that
\#_{\{4\}}=1
. Since the first atom cut in Definition
11.4

2
must have deg 4, it suffices to show that
\#_{\{4\}}\leq 1
. In fact, for each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
, at the time
\mathfrak{n}
is cut, it must be the
lowest remaining atom
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, which means any possible child of
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
must have been cut before, creating a fixed end at
\mathfrak{n}
. Therefore,
if
\mathfrak{n}
has any child in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
at all
before any cutting, then it cannot have deg 4 when it is cut.

On the other hand, before any cutting,
\mathbb{M}_{U}

can have at most one atom without child in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(see
Figure
27
); if not, say there exist two such atoms
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, then consider a path
(\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
between
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
within
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(since
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is a tree). Then
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
must be parent of
\mathfrak{n}
as
\mathfrak{n}
has no child in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and then
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
must be parent of
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
as
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
has a child
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
and another child in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
by Definition
11.2
, and so on. In the end
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{n}_{j}
must be parent of
\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
, which contradicts the assumption on
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
. This proves
\#_{\{4\}}=1
.

Figure 27.
Illustration of each component of
X
(or
\mathbb{M}_{U}
if
X=\mathbb{M}_{U}
in toy model I), in the proof of Propositions
11.7
and
11.14
. It has a unique lowest atom (in red). The other atoms can only be constructed from the red one by taking
parents
, as they must have a bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(in blue) and cannot have a third child.

Proof part 4.
Finally we show that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq(|\mathbb{M}_{U}|-1)/5
. Consider the bahevior of the quantity

\lambda:=\#_{\mathrm{deg}3}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}},

where
\#_{\mathrm{deg}3}
and
\#_{\mathrm{comp}}
represent the number of (remaining) deg 3 atoms and components in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
respectively, so that initially
\lambda=-1
and finally
\lambda=0
. Note that when viewed from
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, each step in Definition
11.4

1
–
6
is one of the followings:

•

Operation
1
or
2
defined above (which occur in Definition
11.4

2
);

•

Cutting {33} or {343} molecule as free (which occur in
3
–
4
and
6
in Definition
11.4
);

•

Cutting {3} molecule as free (which occur in Definition
11.4

6
).

Define the number of these operations to be
\#_{(a)}
,
\#_{(b)}
and
\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{33\}}
etc., then we have

(11.3)

\#_{\{33\}}=\#_{(b)}+\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{33\}}+\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{343\}}.

Moreover, using Lemma
11.6
in the proof of Lemma
11.8
(and the fact that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is proper right before each
1
or
2
or cutting of {3}), it is easy to see that

•

Operation
1
increased
\lambda
by 1, and operation
2
does not change
\lambda
;

•

Cutting {33} or {343} decreases
\lambda
by
1
, and cutting {3} does not change
\lambda
.

Using the initial and final values of
\lambda
we deduce that

(11.4)

\#_{(a)}-(\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{33\}}+\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{343\}})=1.

Finally, consider the bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(there are
|\mathbb{M}_{U}|
of them). Each such bond is broken either in Definition
11.4

2
(which corresponds to exactly one operation
1
or
2
), or in cutting one of {33} or {343} molecules; moreover each
1
, each
2
, each cutting of {33} and each cutting of {343} can break at most 1, 2, 3 and 4 such bonds respectively. This implies that
\#_{(a)}+2\#_{(b)}+3\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{33\}}+4\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{343\}}\geq|\mathbb{M}_{U}|
. Combining this with (
11.3
) and (
11.4
), we deduce that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq(|\mathbb{M}_{U}|-1)/5
, as desired.
∎

11.3.
Toy model I plus

In this subsection we extend the toy model I to allow more general connections between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Definition 11.9
.

We say
\mathbb{M}
is a
toy model I plus
, if it is a 2-layer model as in Definition
11.1
, and each atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
has
at most
one bond connecting to an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(i.e.
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
), and the number of connected components of
X
satisfies

(11.5)

\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\ll|X|,

where
X
is defined at the beginning of this note. Here, if two atoms in
X
can only be connected via atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash X
, then they belong to different components of
X
.

Let us explain the intuition behind Definition
11.9
, in particular the role of (
11.5
). In the case of toy model I, we have
X=\mathbb{M}_{U}
which is connected, hence
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}=1
. Moreover, as in Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
11.7
, we see that
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has at most one lowest atom (i.e. atom without child in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
; in fact it is not hard to see that
\mathbb{M}_{U}
must have an “upside down binary tree” shape). This latter fact allows us to apply a “naive” strategy in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
where we
always
cut the lowest remaining atom (and avoid any top fixed ends),
without producing too many
{
4
}
atoms
.

In the current case,
\mathbb{M}_{U}
may have many lowest atoms, so we cannot always choose and cut a lowest remaining atom. Instead, we improve the strategy by choosing a
lowest remaining deg 3
atom
\mathfrak{n}
(here we do not consider deg 2 atoms, as they will always be lowest in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and always have to be cut at top priority). Now cutting
\mathfrak{n}
may create top fixed ends, but only at descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
(which all have deg 4 when
\mathfrak{n}
is chosen). As such, we need to subsequently cut all descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
after cutting
\mathfrak{n}
. This leads to the idea of the
UP
algorithm, see Definition
11.10
below, which is another basic ingredient in our proof.

Note, however, that this
UP
algorithm cannot be simply combined with the “greedy” algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as in Definition
11.4
, because
\mathfrak{n}
may have (deg 4) descendants in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and this naive attempt may break the key monotonicity property (see Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
11.14
) and lead to non-elementary molecules. As such, we should only apply
UP

at places in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
which will not affect
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, i.e. in
\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash X
. Within
X
we still apply the naive strategy, which may lead to {4} molecules due to
X
.

We can then see the role of (
11.5
) and the
dichotomy
concerning
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
; in fact, same as in Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
11.7
,
each component
of
X
can have
at most one lowest atom
. In the naive strategy, this leads to {4} molecules whose number is comparable to
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
. Under the assumption (
11.5
), this is negeligible compared to the number of good {33} molecules, which will be comparable to
|X|
(i.e. the number of bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
).

With the above intuition, we start by defining the
UP
algorithm in the toy model case. For the full version in the real problem, see Definition
12.1
.

Definition 11.10

(The
UP
algorithm, toy model case)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be any molecule which
does not have top fixed end
. Define the following cutting sequence. For an example see
Figure
28
:

Figure 28.
The
UP
algorithm (Definition
11.10
). As in the above figures, the red box indicates the atom(s) to be cut next, the green box indicates the atom(s) in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
(see Definition
11.10

2
), which will be cut after cutting
\mathfrak{n}
. Note also when
\mathfrak{n}
and its parent
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
(or child
\mathfrak{n}^{-}
) form a {33} molecule, we should cut them together; see Definition
11.10

3
.

(1)

If
\mathbb{M}
has a deg 2 atom, then cut it as free. Repeat until
\mathbb{M}
does not have deg 2 atoms.

(2)

Choose a lowest deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{n}
that has not bee cut (or a lowest atom if
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 3 atoms). Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
. This
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is fixed until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut.

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, each time choose a highest atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
that has not been cut. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3 and has a parent
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
or child
\mathfrak{m}^{-}
that also has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}\}
as free; otherwise cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. Repeat until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut, then go to
1
–
2
and choose the next
\mathfrak{n}
, and so on.

Remark 11.11
.

The
UP
algorithm is a combination of a “global low-to-high” (where each time we select the
lowest
remaining deg 3 atom in Definition
11.10

2
) and “local high-to-low” procedure (where each time we cut the
highest
remaining atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
in Definition
11.10

3
). This leads to a key
monotonicity
property, see Proposition

11.12
.

The step
3
in Definition
11.10
may seem complicated, we explain it as follows: let
\mathfrak{m}
be the highest remaining atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
, the simple option is to just cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. However, in the case described in Definition
11.10

3
, the
\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}
will become deg 2 once
\mathfrak{m}
is cut as free; moreover
\mathfrak{m}^{-}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
will be a highest remaining atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
, and
\mathfrak{m}^{+}\not\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
will occur as a deg 2 atom in Definition
11.10

1
once all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut. As such, this deg 2 atom
\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}
have to be cut at top priority, so instead of cutting
\{\mathfrak{m}\}
we can combine this with the cutting of
\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}
, which also forms a good {33} molecule
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}\}
. This then leads to the step
3
in Definition
11.10
.

We next prove a key monotonicity property of the
UP
algorithm and a sufficient condition for it to produce at least one {33} molecule (in fact {33A} molecule). See Proposition
12.2
for the full version.

Proposition 11.12
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be as in Definition
11.10
and connected (otherwise consider each component of
\mathbb{M}
). Then after applying algorithm
UP
to
\mathbb{M}
(and same for
DOWN
):

(1)

We have the following
monotonicity
property, which we refer to as (MONO-toy): at any time, any atom in
(\mathbb{M}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
has no
top fixed end
, and any atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
has no
bottom fixed end
. In particular, applying
UP
only produces elementary molecules.

(2)

We have
\#_{\{4\}}\leq 1
, and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
if and only if
\mathbb{M}
is full.

In (
3
) below we assume
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 2 atoms.

(3)

If
\mathbb{M}
contains a cycle or contains at least two deg
3
atoms, then
\#_{\{33\}}\geq 1
. If not (i.e.
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and contains at most one deg 3 atom), then all the elementary molecules are {3} molecules with at most one exception of {4} molecule.

Proof.

Proof of (
1
).
We first prove (MONO-toy), which implies all components are elementary (since each atom is either cut by itself or belongs to a {33} molecule, due to
1
–
3
in Definition
11.10
). Initially there is no
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
\mathbb{M}
has no top fixed end by assumption, so it suffices to prove that (MONO-toy) is perserved. First, any deg 2 atom
\mathfrak{n}
cut in Definition
11.10

1
must have two
bottom
fixed ends by (MONO-toy), so it has no child remaining when it is cut, thus cutting it will not create any
top fixed end
.

Now let
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be as in Definition
11.10

2
, and
\mathfrak{m}
be a highest remaining atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
as in Definition
11.10

3
. When
\mathfrak{n}
is chosen in Definition
11.10

2
, all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}

must have deg 4
by construction, and thus have no bottom fixed end. Consider the operation of cutting
\mathfrak{m}
as free in Definition
11.10

3
. We then know that that this cutting does not create any
bottom fixed end
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
(as
\mathfrak{m}
is highest), and does not create any
top fixed end in
(\mathbb{M}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
(as any child of
\mathfrak{m}
must belong to
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
, see
Figure
29
). The same holds for the operation of cutting
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}\}
, because this is equivalent to cutting
\{\mathfrak{m}\}
and then cutting a deg 2 atom (either
\mathfrak{m}^{+}\not\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
with two bottom fixed ends or
\mathfrak{m}^{-}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
with two top fixed ends). This proves that (MONO-toy) is perserved, as desired.

Figure 29.
Illustration of the (MONO-toy) property of the
UP
algorithm (Proposition
11.12
). Left: when we choose
\mathfrak{n}
in Definition
11.10

2
, there is no top fixed end outside
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
and no fixed end at all in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
(i.e. all deg 4). Right: when we cut a highest remaining atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
, we only get top fixed end in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
and bottom fixed end outside
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
.

Proof of (
2
).
Note that after the first cutting,
\mathbb{M}
will
always
contain an atom that is not deg 4 (cf. Remark
10.6
). By construction, this means that the
\mathfrak{n}
chosen in Definition
11.10

2
will always be deg 3; moreover, any
\mathfrak{m}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
that is cut in Definition
11.10

3
also cannot be deg 4, because
\mathfrak{m}
has a parent in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
which must have been cut before
\mathfrak{m}
thanks to the highest assumption of
\mathfrak{m}
. Thus
only
the first atom can produce a possible {4} molecule, so
\#_{\{4\}}\leq 1
; moreover
\#_{\{4\}}=1
if and only if the first atom cut has deg 4, i.e. when
\mathbb{M}
is full.

Proof of (
3
).
Suppose
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 2 atom, we first prove that either
\#_{\{33\}}\geq 1
, or all resulting molecules are {4} and {3} (with
\#_{\{4\}}\leq 1
). If not, then we must have a {2} molecule. Consider the first atom
\mathfrak{m}
that has deg 2 when we cut it, and consider the cutting operation that turns
\mathfrak{m}
into deg 2, which must be cutting some deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{p}
as free (this
\mathfrak{p}
cannot have deg 4, because a degree 4 atom is cut only when no degree 3 atoms are present, and in this case cutting a degree 4 atom cannot produce a degree 2 atom). This cutting creates a fixed end at
\mathfrak{m}
, so this
\mathfrak{p}
must be a parent or child of
\mathfrak{m}
; moreover this
\mathfrak{m}
and this
\mathfrak{p}
must both be deg 3 at the time of cutting
\mathfrak{p}
, but this violates Definition
11.10

3
.

Finally we prove
\#_{\{33\}}=0
if and only if
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and has at most one deg 3 atom. For this, consider the increment of the quantity
\sigma:=\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}-3\#_{\mathrm{atom}}
, where
\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}
is the number of bonds plus the number of free ends (not counting fixed ends) and
\#_{\mathrm{atom}}
is the number of remaining atoms. It is easy to see that cutting each {2}, {3}, {33} and {4} molecule increases
\sigma
by 1, 0, 1 and
-1
respectively. Since finally
\sigma=0
, we see that
\#_{\{4\}}-(\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33\}})=\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}
(initial value of
\sigma
).

Note that initially
\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}=\#_{\mathrm{deg}}-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
where
\#_{\mathrm{deg}}
is the total degree of all atoms (not counting fixed ends) and
\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
is the number of bonds (because each bond contributes twice to the degrees), so
\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}=(|\mathbb{M}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}})-|\mathbb{M}_{3}|
where
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|
is the number of (initially) deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
. Moreover by connectedness we have
\#_{\mathrm{bond}}\geq|\mathbb{M}|-1
with equality if and only if
\mathbb{M}
is a tree, and note also
\#_{\{4\}}\in\{0,1\}
(and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
if and only if
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|=0
by the same proof in Part 2), it is then easy to see that
\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33\}}=0
if and only if
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|\leq 1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Now we can define the algorithm for toy model I plus. As described in the above heuristics, this features a naive algorithm (always cutting the lowest) in
X
, an
UP
algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash X
, and a greedy algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Up to some simplifications, this is just the
MAINUD
algorithm, which is the main algorithm in Section
13
, see Definition
13.12
. Note also that the part of Definition
11.13
involving
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is exactly the same as Definition
11.4
; the only difference occurs in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
.

Definition 11.13

(Algorithm for toy model I plus)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a toy model I plus with
X
be defined in the beginning of this note (this
X
is fixed throughout the algorithm). We define the following cutting sequence. For an example see
Figures
30
–
31
:

Figure 30.
Algorithm for toy model I plus (first half) in Definition
11.13
, applied to the example in
Figure
20
. In addition to
Figure
22
, we use green box to indicate atom(s) in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
(see Definition
11.13

2
) which will be cut after cutting
\mathfrak{n}
.

(1)

If
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has a deg 2 atom, then cut it as free. Repeat until
\mathbb{M}_{U}
does not have deg 2 atoms.

(2)

Consider the set of all (remaining) atoms that either belong to
X
, or has deg 3; choose
\mathfrak{n}
to be a lowest atom in this set (if this set is empty then choose
\mathfrak{n}
to be any lowest atom). Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and fix
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
until the end of (
6
).

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, each time choose a highest atom
\mathfrak{p}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
that has not been cut. If
\mathfrak{p}
is adjacent to an atom
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
which has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
as free and cut
\mathfrak{q}
as free from
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
if
\mathfrak{p}
has deg 4; otherwise just cut
\mathfrak{p}
as free.

(4)

If
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes non-proper, then perform the same steps
3
–
4
in Definition
11.4
until it becomes proper again. Then go to
3
and repeat, until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut.

(5)

When all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut, go to
1
–
2
and choose the next
\mathfrak{n}
, and so on.

(6)

When all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
have been cut, perform the same step
6
in Definition
11.4
to finish off
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Figure 31.
Algorithm for toy model I plus (second half). Here, the next atom
\mathfrak{n}
we choose is not the unique deg 3 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, but rather the
atom in
X
below it, see Definition
11.13

2
. This leads to another {4} loss, but the total loss is bounded by
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}=2
. In the end, we subsequently cut two deg 2 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and two deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, to conclude.

Proposition 11.14
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a toy model I plus, and we apply the algorithm defined in Definition
11.13
. Then in this process we only obtain elementary molecules, and we have

(11.6)

\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)},\qquad\#_{\{33\}}\geq(|X|-1)/5-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}.

Since
|X|\gtrsim\rho\gg 1
and using (
11.5
), this clearly implies (
11.1
).

Proof.

We divide the proof into 4 parts just as Proposition
11.7
.

Proof part 1.
We show that each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is contained in an elementary molecule. As in Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
11.12
, this follows from the same (MONO-toy) property but
for
\mathbb{M}_{U}
only
: any atom in
(\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
has no
top fixed end
, and any atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
has no
bottom fixed end
.

To prove the monotonicity property, we first note that, for any
\mathfrak{n}
chosen in Definition
11.13

2
and at the time it is chosen, each atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
has deg 4 and thus has no bottom fixed end. Then consider the process of cutting in Definition
11.13

3
and below, see
Figure
32
. Following exactly Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
11.12
, we see that cutting any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
in this process does not create any top fixed end in
(\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
or any bottom fixed end in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
; moreover, for the cutting of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
in this process (such as the one in Definition
11.13

4
), it
only creates bottom fixed ends
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and
only at atoms in
(\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
. This is because
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
contains no atom in
X
by construction, and thus has no bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, hence no fixed end can be created at any atom in this set when cutting anything in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. This proves (MONO-toy) for
\mathbb{M}_{U}
.

Figure 32.
Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
11.14
: the preservation of the monotonicity property (just as for
UP
) in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. This is essentially the same as the right half of
Figure
29
, except the green bonds connecting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
to atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(
those atoms must be in
X
). By our choice,
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
is
disjoint
with
X
, so none of these bonds involve any atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
(once
\mathfrak{n}
has been cut). Thus, if we do any cutting in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(as in Definition
11.13

4
), this only creates bottom ends outside
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
, hence preserving the monotonicity property in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
.

Proof part 2.
We show that each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is contained in an elementary molecule. Since the part of Definition
11.13
involving
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is exactly the same as Definition
11.4
, this part is also exactly the same as Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
11.7
.

Proof part 3.
We show that
\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
. Same as Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
11.12
, we see that any
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
in Definition
11.13

3
cannot have deg 4 when it is cut. Therefore, the only possible {4} atoms must come from those
\mathfrak{n}
chosen in Definition
11.13

2
. By our choice in Definition
11.13

2
, such
\mathfrak{n}
must belong to
X
and must be
lowest atoms
in
X
, i.e. it belongs to
X
but does not have any child in
X
before any cutting.

Now we claim that any two lowest atoms in
X

must belong to different components of
X
(see
Figure
27
). If not, say
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are connected by a path
(\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
in
X
, then arguing as Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
11.7
and noticing that each atom in
X
has a child in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, we deduce that each
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
must be parent of
\mathfrak{n}_{i-1}
which then leads to contradiction with properties of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
. We then conclude that the number of lowest atoms in
X
does not exceed
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
, hence
\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
.

Proof part 4.
Finally we show that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq(|X|-1)/5-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
. Since the part of Definition
11.13
involving
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is exactly the same as Definition
11.4
, this part is essentially the same as Part 4 of the proof of Proposition
11.7
. There is only one difference: in the current case, each step of cutting
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
as free in Definition
11.13

3
(which corresponds to the operation
2
in Part 4 of the proof of Proposition
11.7
when viewed in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
) may produce two {3} molecules instead of one {33} molecule, but this only happens when
13
13

13

This cannot happen for toy model I, because there
\mathfrak{p}
can have deg 4 only when it is the first atom cut, in which case
\mathfrak{q}
cannot have deg 3.

\mathfrak{p}
has deg 4, and the number of such exceptions is bounded by
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
following Part 3 above. Therefore, instead of (
11.3
) we have

(11.7)

\#_{\{33\}}\geq\#_{(b)}+\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{33\}}+\#_{\mathrm{cut}\{343\}}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)},

and the same proof then yields that, instead of
\#_{\{33\}}\geq(|X|-1)/5
as in (
11.2
), we now have
\#_{\{33\}}\geq(|X|-1)/5-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
. This completes the proof.
∎

11.4.
Toy model II

In this subsection we introduce the toy model II, which is opposite to toy model I plus via the dichotomy, where we assume
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\gtrsim|X|
instead of
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\ll|X|
in Definition
11.9
.

Definition 11.15
.

We say
\mathbb{M}
is a toy model II, if it satisfies all the assumptions in Definition
11.9
for toy model I plus with the same notations, except that (
11.5
) is replaced by
|X|\geq 2
and

(11.8)

\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}\gtrsim|X|.

Let us explain the strategy for toy model II, which will be completely different from toy models I and I plus. Here we will not gain from the greedy algorithm in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
; instead we first cut all of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free without exploiting any gain, and then gain from
\mathbb{M}_{U}
under the assumption (
11.8
). Note that, after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free, there will not be any deg 2 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and the deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
are
exactly those in
X
.

The intuition is that, due to the lower bound of the number of components if
X
given by (
11.8
), the deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
are now
separated by deg 4 atoms
. Essentially, we expect that for such molecules, we can design a cutting algorithm with minimal loss caused {4} molecules, such that
each connected component of the set of deg 3 atoms
provides a good {33} molecule. In fact, this can be achieved by a clever choice in the
UP
algorithm.

We next introduce the main algorithm in this case (together with an auxiliary lemma needed in this algorithm). Up to simplifications, this is just the
3COMPUP
algorithm in Definition
13.6
.

Lemma 11.16
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a molecule that is a tree, contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms and has no top fixed end. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
be
\mathbb{M}_{3}
with
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|\geq 2
. Define a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
to be
no-bottom
if any child of any atom in
A
must also be in
A
(see
Figure
33
). Then, there always exists a connected, no-bottom subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
with
|A\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}|=2
.

Figure 33.
An example of connected, no-bottom sets in Lemma
11.16
; this one is a union of two intersecting descendant sets
S_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
. In general each such set is a union of descendant sets
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
with
\mathfrak{n}
forming a connected set; see the proof of Lemma
11.16
.

Proof.

For any
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
, let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
(including
\mathfrak{n}
itself), then it is always connected and no-bottom. If there exists
\mathfrak{n}
such that
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}|\geq 2
, then choose a lowest such
\mathfrak{n}
. Since
\mathfrak{n}
has no top fixed end, we know that either (i)
\mathfrak{n}
has one child
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
, or (ii)
\mathfrak{n}
has deg 4 and has two children
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}
. In either case, by assumption we have
|S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}|\leq 1
for each
j
, which then implies
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}|\leq 2
because
S_{\mathfrak{n}}=(\cup_{j}S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
. This implies
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}|=2
, as desired.

Now assume
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}|\leq 1
for each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
. If
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}|=1
, there is a unique
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n})\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}
; now consider all atom pairs
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
such that
\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n})
and
\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
both exist and
are different
(which exist because
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|\geq 2
), and one such pair
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
that are connected by
a shortest path
in
\mathbb{M}
. Let this path be
(\mathfrak{n}_{0},\mathfrak{n},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
, then for each
0<i<j
we must have either
S_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}=\varnothing
or
\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}_{i})=\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n})
(otherwise the pair
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}_{i})
corresponds to a shorter path, contradiction). Now let
A=\cup_{i=0}^{j}S_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}}
, it is clear that
A
is connected, no-bottom and
A\cap\mathbb{M}_{3}=\{\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}),\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})\}
, as desired.
∎

Definition 11.17

(The
3COMPUP
algorithm, toy model case)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule that is a tree, contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms and has no top fixed end. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
be
\mathbb{M}_{3}
with
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|\geq 2
. Define the following cutting sequence. For an example see
Figure
34
:

Figure 34.
Algorithm for toy model II, applied to the example in
Figure
20
. Here we first cut all of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free. Then, each time we choose a connected, no-bottom subset
A
that contains exactly two deg 3 atoms, see Definition
11.17
(
1
). We then apply
UP
to cut all atoms in
A
(cf.
Figure
28
for the application of
UP
) and repeat. Note that each application of
UP
guarantees at least one {33} molecule.

(1)

Apply Lemma
11.16
to
\mathbb{M}
and select a connected no-bottom subset
A
. This set is fixed until the end of
5
.

(2)

Choose a lowest deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{n}
in
A
that has not been cut. Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
. This
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
are fixed until the end of
3
.

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, each time choose a highest atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
that has not been cut. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3 and has a parent
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
or child
\mathfrak{m}^{-}
that also has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}\}
as free; otherwise cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. Repeat until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut.

(4)

If
\mathbb{M}
has deg 2 atom, then cut it; repeat until
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 2 atoms. Then go to
2
and choose the next
\mathfrak{n}
, and so on.

(5)

When all atoms in
A
have been cut, we subsequently cut all deg 2 atoms as in
4
, and then cut all
components of
\mathbb{M}
with only one deg 3 atom
using
UP
.

(6)

Go to
1
and select the next
A
from one of the remaining components of
\mathbb{M}
, and so on.

Remark 11.18
.

Let us explain the ideas in Lemma
11.16
and Definition
11.17
. Note that steps
2
–
4
in Definition
11.17
are exactly the same as
UP
but applied to a particular set
A
, so we can view
3COMPUP
as a clever way of carrying out
UP
. The role of the set
A
is explained as follows: first it is connected (otherwise we can cut each of its components separately); it should also be no-bottom, because when cutting any atom we should also cut its descendants to guarantee the monotonicity property. Finally we need it to contain two deg 3 atoms so that this sequence of cutting will produce at least one {33} molecule, and we need
exactly two
deg 3 atoms so that we do not waste too many components of the deg-3-atom set (which are the key objects leading to the gain).

Proposition 11.19
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a toy model II. We first cut
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free and cut it into elementary molecules by
DOWN
(i.e. the dual of
UP
with all directions reversed), then apply
3COMPUP
(Definition
11.17
) to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. Then all resulting components are elementary, and we have

(11.9)

\#_{\{4\}}=1,\qquad\#_{\{33\}}\geq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}/10.

Since
|X|\gtrsim\rho\gg 1
and using (
11.8
), this clearly implies (
11.1
).

Proof.

Note that steps
2
–
4
in Definition
11.17
are exactly the same as
UP
, so the proof that all molecules are elementary (as well as
\#_{\{4\}}=1
which comes only from
\mathbb{M}_{D}
) follows in the same way as Proposition
11.12
. We now focus on the proof that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}/10
.

For any molecule
\mathbb{M}
, define
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}
to be the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
, and
\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}
to be the number of components of the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
, and

(11.10)

\lambda(\mathbb{M}):=\frac{1}{5}\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}+\frac{1}{10}\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}.

In Definition
11.17
(applied to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
), the whole algorithm consists of finitely many loops where one loop is formed by steps
1
–
5
in Definition
11.17
. Consider each such loop which reduces
\mathbb{M}
to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, we shall prove the following:

(
\clubsuit
)

If
\mathbb{M}
is a tree, has no top fixed end and contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms with at least two deg 3 atoms, then each component of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
satisfies the same property. Moreover for this loop we have

(11.11)

\#_{\{33\}}\geq\lambda(\mathbb{M})-\lambda(\mathbb{M}^{\prime}).

Note that adding up (
11.11
) leads to the desired lower bound of
\#_{\{33\}}
(initially
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has no deg 2 atoms nor top fixed end, and
\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}=\#_{\mathrm{comp}(X)}
), thus it suffices to prove (
\clubsuit
) for a given loop. We divide the proof into 4 parts.

Proof part 1.
We prove that each component of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
satisfies the desired properties. This is obvious, as the lack of top fixed ends follows from the same proof as Proposition
11.12
, and the lack of deg 2 atoms and components with only one deg 3 atom follows from the cuttings done in Definition
11.17

5
.

Proof part 2.
Now we need to prove (
11.11
). We start by making some preparations. Note that
A
is a connected subset of
\mathbb{M}
; by Lemma
11.6
in the proof of Lemma
11.8
, each atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A
that is connected to an atom
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}(\mathfrak{m})
in
A
by a pre-loop bond
e
corresponds to a unique component
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
after cutting the atoms in
A
as free, see
Figure
35
(the loop will in fact cut as free more atoms). Note also that
\mathfrak{m}
must be a parent of
\mathfrak{n}
due to the no-bottom property of
A
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{3}
be the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
(before executing the loop), and consider all components of
\mathbb{M}_{3}
that intersect
A
or are connected to
A
by a bond. We know
A
has exactly two atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{3}
; apart from the one or two components that contain these two atoms, each component under consideration must be a subset of a unique
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
where
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3 and
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}(\mathfrak{m})
has deg 4 (call it
Y_{\mathfrak{m}}
). Let
B
be the union of these components with
A
(
Figure
35
). We claim that after steps
1
–
4
in Definition
11.17
and cutting the deg 2 atoms in Definition
11.17

5
, exactly those atoms in
B
have been cut as free.

Figure 35.
Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
11.19
. Here
A
is the connected, no-bottom set as in Lemma
11.16
; various atoms
\mathfrak{m}
(together with the corresponding component
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
as in Lemma
11.6
are drawn, each of which connected to its child
\mathfrak{n}\in A
by a bond. Apart from 2 exceptions, this
\mathfrak{n}
will have deg 4. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 4 we do nothing; if
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3, we take all the cubsequently connected deg 3 atoms in
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
and call them
Y_{\mathfrak{m}}
(i.e. the blue circle within the green circle
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
).
The
B
is defined as the union of
A
and all blue circles.
Within one loop
1
–
5
in Definition
11.17
, we cut everything in
A
, and subsequently everything in the blue circles as deg 2 (or as part of {33} molecule). That is, we cut everything in
B
and nothing else (as cutting
B
only turns deg 4 atoms into deg 3 but does not produce further deg 2 atom).

In fact, the atoms
A
must have been cut by definition. For any atom in the
\mathbb{M}_{3}
components forming
B
, by definition it is connected to an atom in
A
by a chain of deg 3 atoms; once the atom in
A
has been cut, the atoms on this chain will successively become deg 2, so they must have been cut (either in Definition
11.17

4
–
5
, or as part of {33} molecule in Definition
11.17

3
). On the other hand, each atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash B
has at most one bond connecting to
B
(as
B
is connected and
\mathbb{M}
is a tree), and must be deg 4 if it has one such bond (otherwise it would belong to one of the
\mathbb{M}_{3}
components forming
B
), so each atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash B
will have deg 3 or 4 after cutting
B
as free, thus it will not be cut in Definition
11.17

1
–
4
or in cutting the deg 2 atoms in Definition
11.17

5
.

Proof part 3.
Next we obtain a lower bound on
\#_{\{33\}}
. Note that
A
contains two deg 3 atoms, we can show that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq 1
in the same way as in Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
11.12
. Moreover, since
A
contains
exactly
two deg 3 atoms, by arguing as in Part 4 of the proof of Proposition
11.12
and calculating the quantity
\sigma
defined there but within
A
, we can show that, apart from at most two exceptions, each deg 4 atom in
A
will have deg 3 when it is cut.

Now let
\lambda
be the number of
\mathbb{M}_{3}
components forming
B
. With at most two exceptions, each such component corresponds to a deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{m}\in X_{\mathfrak{m}}
together with its deg 4 child
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}(\mathfrak{m})\in A
as in Part 2 above. With at most two more exceptions, this
\mathfrak{n}
will have deg 3 when it is cut, at which time
\mathfrak{m}
also has deg 3 (see
Figure
36
); then by Definition
11.17

3
, we know that
\mathfrak{n}
must belong to a {33} molecule (which may or may not be
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m}\}
). Note that each {33} molecule can be so obtained by at most one
\mathfrak{n}
, and each
\mathfrak{n}
can be obtained by at most two
\mathfrak{m}
(hence at most two components), we conclude that

(11.12)

\#_{\{33\}}\geq\max\bigg(1,\frac{\lambda-2}{2}-2\bigg).

Figure 36.
Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
11.19
: here
\mathfrak{n}\in A
is connected to
\mathfrak{m}\in X_{\mathfrak{m}}
which has deg 3, as in
Figure
35
. With at most 2 more exceptions, this
\mathfrak{n}
will also have deg 3 when it is cut (as we are essentially doing
UP
within
A
, see Proposition
11.12
), which leads to a {33} molecule formed by
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof part 4.
Now we consider the components of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{M}_{3}^{\prime}
(set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
). Note that
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is formed from
\mathbb{M}
by cutting
B
as free and then removing all components with only one deg 3 atom. Since
B
is connected, by applying Lemma
11.6
in the proof of Lemma
11.8
again, we see that each component
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
corresponds to a unique atom
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash B
that is adjacent to an atom in
B
, see
Figure
37
. Let
\mu
be the number of such bonds (i.e. corresponding to those new components with at least two deg 3 atoms after cutting
B
), then we have

(11.13)

\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}=1-\mu.

Finally, consider the set of deg 3 atoms, i.e.
\mathbb{M}_{3}
and
\mathbb{M}_{3}^{\prime}
, and their components. The components of
\mathbb{M}_{3}
includes those
\lambda
components forming
B
as in Part 2 (which have been cut when forming
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
), and the other components which are contained in some
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
defined above. Note that for each new component
Y
formed after cutting
B
that contains only one deg 3 atom, it will be removed and will not occur in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
; however, all atoms in
Y
originally have deg 4 before any cutting, so initially
Y
also has no contribution to
\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}
, and thus we can ignore such
Y
.

By Lemma
11.6
, when viewed in each
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, the effect of cutting
B
exactly turns one free end into fixed end (turning one deg 4 atom into deg 3). Therefore the part of
\mathbb{M}_{3}^{\prime}
within
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
is formed by
adding one deg 3 atom
into the existing part of
\mathbb{M}_{3}
within
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
. This new deg 3 atom may
decrease

\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(X_{\mathfrak{q}})}
(i.e. the number of components of the set of deg 3 atoms in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
) by joining existing components together, but this decrease is
at most 2
because this new deg 3 atom can join at most 3 components into 1. From this, and accounting for the
\lambda
components removed when cutting
B
, we deduce that

(11.14)

\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}\leq\lambda+2\mu.

Putting together (
11.10
) and (
11.12
)–(
11.14
), we deduce that

(11.15)

\displaystyle\lambda(\mathbb{M})-\lambda(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{5}\big(\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}\big)+\frac{1}{10}\big(\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{3\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{5}+\frac{\lambda}{10}\leq\max\bigg(1,\frac{\lambda-6}{2}\bigg)\leq\#_{\{33\}}.

This proves (
11.11
) and completes the proof of Proposition
11.19
.
∎

Figure 37.
Part 4 of the proof of Proposition
11.19
. Left: suppose we cut
B
and are left with all the components as in Lemma
11.6
. Within each such component, a fixed end is created, turning a deg 4 atom into deg 3. Right: for each component, turning one deg 4 atom into deg 3 may decrease the number of
components of the set of deg 3 atoms
by 2 (by joining 3 such components into 1 with the new deg 3 atom), but
no more
. Note that the blue circles here are unrelated to the blue circles in
Figures
35
–
36
and represent different things.

11.5.
Toy model III

In this subsection we introduce the toy model III. Note that in both toy models I plus and II, we are assuming the absence of 2-connections, i.e.
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
. Now we define the toy models III, which are (essentially) opposite to toy models I plus and II via the
dichotomy
, where we assume
14
14

14

There is also the case
0<\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\ll|X|
; in this case we will first cut all such 2-connections, which generates an error which is eventually negligible but still requires some extra care; see Section
11.7.4
for more discussions.

\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\gtrsim|X|
instead of
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}=0
.

Definition 11.20
.

We say
\mathbb{M}
is a toy model III, if it is a simplified 2-layer model in Definition
11.1
, and

(11.16)

\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}\gtrsim|X|,

where
\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}
and
X
are defined at the beginning of this note.

The strategy for toy models III is simple: we work in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as if
\mathbb{M}_{U}
were absent, and apply the
DOWN
algorithm (the dual of
UP
) to it. Now consider any 2-connection
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
, which will become deg 2 when all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
have been cut. Assume
\mathfrak{n}
is adjacent to two atoms
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and assume
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
is cut before
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
in the
DOWN
algorithm applied to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Then, right before
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is cut, this
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
will have deg 3 (say) by Proposition
11.12
; also since
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
has been cut at this time, now
\mathfrak{n}
must also have deg 3, which leads to a {33} molecule, so we simply cut it and enjoy the gain. This way we guarantee that each 2-connection belongs to a {33} molecule, which is sufficient under the assumption (
11.16
). We shall refer to this as the
2CONNDN
algorithm (and the dual version
2CONNUP
), see Definition
13.1
for the full version (which is more complicated due to the various necessary additions including the O-atoms).

Definition 11.21

(Algorithm for toy model III)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be toy model III. We define the following cutting sequence. For an example see
Figure
38
:

(1)

If
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has a deg 2 atom, then cut it as free. Repeat until
\mathbb{M}_{D}
does not have deg 2 atoms.

(2)

Choose a highest deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that has not bee cut (or a highest atom if
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no deg 3 atoms). Let
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of ancestors of
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. This
\mathfrak{n}
and
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
is fixed until all atoms in
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut.

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, each time choose a lowest atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
that has not been cut. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3 and has a parent
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
that also has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{p}\}
as free; otherwise cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. Repeat until all atoms in
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut, then go to
1
–
2
and choose the next
\mathfrak{n}
, and so on.

(4)

When all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
have been cut, finish off
\mathbb{M}_{U}
by applying the
UP
algorithm.

Figure 38.
Algorithm for toy model III, applied to the example in
Figure
20
. Here we perform
DOWN
which is the dual of
UP
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, so in Definition
11.10

2
in
UP
algorithm we choose the
highest
deg 3 atom instead of the lowest, and in in Definition
11.10

3
in
UP
algorithm we choose the
lowest
remaining atom instead of the highest, etc. Here green box indicates atoms in
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
(the set of
ancestors
of
\mathfrak{n}
), and when an atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
forms a {33} molecule with an atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
we always cut it. In the end we see that each 2-connection (pink atom) belongs to one {33} molecule, as desired. As the gain is already enough, the rest can be cut into elementary molecules using
UP
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
DOWN
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Note that steps
1
–
3
in Definition
11.21
are essentially just
DOWN
(i.e. the dual of
UP
) applied to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, except we propritize the {33} molecules formed by an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
with an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
.

Proposition 11.22
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be toy model III. Then, after applying the cutting sequence defined in Definition
11.21
, all molecules we get are elementary, and we have

(11.17)

\#_{\{4\}}=1,\qquad\#_{\{33\}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}.

Proof.

Since steps
1
–
3
in Definition
11.21
are essentially the same as in
DOWN
, it is easy to prove all molecules we get are elementary and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
, in the same way as the proof of Proposition
11.12
. Now we only need to prove that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}/2
.

Let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
be any 2-connection, which is connected to two atoms
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
by two bonds. Assume
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
is cut before
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
in Definition
11.21
(they cannot be cut at the same time, as Definition
11.21

3
does not provide an option to cut a {33} molecule with both atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
). Arguing in the same way as in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
11.12
, we know that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
cannot have deg 4 when it is cut. On the other hand, note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a tree initially without deg 3 atoms; by arguing in the same way as in Part 4 of the proof of Proposition
11.12
and calculating the quantity
\sigma
defined there but within
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, we conclude that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
also cannot have deg 2 when it is cut.

Therefore,
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
must have deg 3 when it is cut; at this time
\mathfrak{n}
must also have deg 3 because
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
has been cut,
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
has not, and
\mathfrak{n}
also has two top edges (not fixed ends) that are not affected, see
Figure
39
. By Definition
11.21

3
we conclude that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
must belong to a {33} molecule, which means that
\mathfrak{n}
must be adjacent with an
\mathbb{M}_{D}
atom in
some
{33} molecule. Since this is true for each 2-connection, and each {33} molecule may correspond to at most two 2-connections in this way (i.e. given the {33} molecule there can be at most two
\mathfrak{n}
that are adjacent with the
\mathbb{M}_{D}
atom in this {33} molecule), we conclude that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq\#_{\mathrm{2conn}}/2
.
∎

Figure 39.
The proof of Proposition
11.22
. Here
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
is a 2-connection connected to
\mathfrak{m}_{1,2}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
; assume
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
is cut before
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
when applying
DOWN
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Then at the time
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is cut, this
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
has deg 3 due to properties of
DOWN
(Proposition
11.12
), and
\mathfrak{n}
also has deg 3 because
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
has been cut.

11.6.
Reduction to 2-layers: layer selection

In Sections
11.2
–
11.5
we have considered the 2-layer models in Definition
11.1
. In fact, this two-layer case already captures most of the essential difficulties in the multi-layer case, and a simple process allows us to reduce the general
\mathfrak{L}
-layer case (where
\mathfrak{L}
is any large constant) to the 2-layer case. This is refered to as
layer selection
, which we define as follows; for the precise definition see Definition
12.12
.

Definition 11.23
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
satisfy Proposition
10.4
and simplifications (
1
)–(
2
) in Section
11.1
. Recall the set
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})
of root particles at layer
\ell
(Definition
3.18
), and
s_{\ell}=|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})|
; they are the particle lines that
cross
into layer
\ell
. Assume also
|H|=s_{\ell}\ll\rho
(the case when
|H|\gtrsim\rho
is easy due to the positive term
|H|
in (
9.51
); see Proposition
12.3
). We define the
layer selection
process as follows, see
Figure
40
:

(1)

Choose
\ell_{U}
such that

(11.18)

\rho^{\prime}:=s_{\ell_{U}-1}\sim\max_{\ell^{\prime}}s_{\ell^{\prime}}\sim\rho,\qquad\rho^{\prime}\gg\max_{\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell_{U}}s_{\ell^{\prime}};

here the first condition in (
11.18
) states that the majority of crossings happen between layers
\ell_{U}
and
\ell_{U}-1
, and the second condition states that the crossings in layers above
\ell_{U}
are negligible.

(2)

Consider each of the
s_{\ell_{U}-1}
particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
. For
\ell_{D}<\ell_{U}
, define
v_{\ell_{D}}^{*}
to be the number of particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
that are connected to either a particle line in in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
or to a cycle
within
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
; in other words, we start from layer
\ell_{U}-1
and move down, keeping track of the number of crossings that are connected to other crossings or to a cycle
at each layer
. Note that
v_{1}^{*}=s_{\ell_{U}-1}
by Proposition
10.4
(
9
) (and
H_{0}=\varnothing
); now as in
1
, we choose
\ell_{D}
such that

(11.19)

\rho^{\prime\prime}:=v_{\ell_{D}}^{*}\sim s_{\ell_{U}-1}=\rho^{\prime},\qquad\rho^{\prime\prime}\gg\max_{\ell^{\prime\prime}>\ell_{D}}v_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{*}.

(3)

Now we focus on the layers
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{D}:\ell_{U}]}:=\cup_{\ell_{D}\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{U}}\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, and ignore all other layers. Consider each component
Z
of the set
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
. If
Z
