intersects at least two particle lines
in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
or
contains a cycle
, then by the choice of
\ell_{D}
in
2
, the number of
such exceptional components
is
\leq v_{\ell_{D}+1}^{*}\ll\rho^{\prime\prime}
which is negligible. We will assume this number is zero (otherwise we need an additional cutting and
pre-processing
step, see Section
11.7.4
below).

(4)

Define the result of layer selection, to be
\mathbb{M}_{UD}:=\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
, where
\mathbb{M}_{U}:=\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U}]}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}:=\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{D}}
.

Figure 40.
Layer selection (Definition
11.23
). The blue edges in layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1}
belong to particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
, whose size satisfies
s_{{\ell_{U}}-1}\gtrsim\rho
. Each of them is connected to a red edge crossing
\ell_{U}\tau
(they belong to the particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}})
, and their number
s_{\ell_{U}}\ll s_{\ell_{U}-1}
, i.e. these particle lines are essentially connected to each other (basically, this condition is used to guarantee that all the bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
do lead to cycles). in layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}}
.

On the other hand, these particle lines are connected to pink edges below, but are
not connected to each other
until reaching layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{D}}
(by yellow edges in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{D}}
). This corresponds to the case
\lambda_{{\ell_{U}};\ell_{D}+1}=0
and
\lambda_{\ell_{U};\ell_{D}}\gtrsim s_{\ell_{U}}
(the general case
0<\lambda_{{\ell_{U}};\ell_{D}+1}\ll s_{\ell_{U}}
requires a little more work). We then set
\mathbb{M}_{U}
to contain layers
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{D}+1:\ell_{U}]}
(which is a forest in our case; in general case we need to cut some exceptional components to make it a forest), and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
to contain layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{D}}
, to reduce to the 2-layer case, where each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
contains no cycle, and the number of bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
being
\gtrsim\rho
.

Proposition 11.24
.

Assume
\mathbb{M}
satisfies Proposition
10.4
and simplifications (
1
)–(
2
) in Section
11.1
, and
|H|\ll\rho
, and assume that the
number of exceptional components is zero
in Definition
11.23

3
. Then the process in Definition
11.23
is well-defined, and for
\mathbb{M}_{UD}=\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
in Definition
11.23

4
, we have that

•

Each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a
forest
with no fixed end, the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
being
\ll\rho^{\prime\prime}
, and no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a parent of an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
;

•

There exist at least
\rho^{\prime\prime}
bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and each such bond is connected to another bond between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, by a path within
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Proof.

To prove that the procedure in Definition
11.23
is well-defined, we just need to prove the existence of
\ell_{U}
in Definition
11.23

1
and
\ell_{D}
in Definition
11.23

2
. In fact, we can choose two large constants (see Definition
12.12
and Proposition
12.13
for more details)
C\gg(C^{\prime})^{\ell}
such that

(11.20)

\ell_{U}-1:=\max\big\{\ell^{\prime}:s_{\ell^{\prime}}\geq C^{-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\rho\big\},\qquad\ell_{D}:=\max\big\{\ell^{\prime\prime}<\ell_{U}:v_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\geq(C^{\prime})^{-\ell^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\big\}.

Note that
\rho\sim\max_{\ell^{\prime}}s_{\ell^{\prime}}
by (
7.1
) (as
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}=0
by assumption) and
s_{\ell}=|H|\ll C^{-\ell}\cdot\rho
by assumption, so
\ell_{U}
exists; similarly
\ell_{D}
exists because
v_{1}^{*}=\rho^{\prime}
by Proposition
10.4
(
9
).

Now, we have assumed
v_{\ell_{D}+1}^{*}=0
(i.e. no exceptional component) in Definition
11.23

3
. It is obvious that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest (by simplification (
2
) in Section
11.1
),
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no fixed end (because nothing is cut in
\mathbb{M}_{UD}=\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
), and no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is parent of an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(because the layers forming
\mathbb{M}_{U}
are above that forming
\mathbb{M}_{D}
).

We next show that
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is a forest. In fact, each component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
must intersect a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
(it must intersect a particle line in
p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
for some
\ell^{\prime}<\ell_{U}
by Proposition
10.4
(
5
); by applying Proposition
10.4
(
7
) we know that this component must also intersect a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})\subseteq p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1})
, by repeating this and using Remark
10.5
, we eventually get to a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
). As there is no exceptional component in Definition
11.23

3
, we know that each component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
is a tree by itself, and intersects a unique particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}})
. Since
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}}
is a forest by itself, we see that
\mathbb{M}_{U}=\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U}]}
is also a forest (namely, if we start with a forest, and attach a number of trees to it with each tree connected to the original forest by a unique edge, then the result is still a forest).

We next show that the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
does not exceed
s_{\ell_{U}}
, which is
\ll\rho^{\prime\prime}
because

s_{\ell_{U}}\leq C^{-1}\cdot s_{\ell_{U}-1}=C^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\leq C^{-1}\cdot(C^{\prime})^{\ell}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}\ll\rho^{\prime\prime}

by (
11.20
) and
C\gg(C^{\prime})^{\ell}
. In fact, by the same arguments for
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
as above, we see that each component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U}]}
must intersect a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}})
, so the number of these components is bounded by
|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}})|=s_{\ell_{U}}
by Remark
10.5
.

Finally, consider each of the
\rho^{\prime\prime}=v_{\ell_{D}}^{*}
particle lines that is connected to either a particle line in in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
or to a cycle within
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
, say
{\bm{p}}
. By definition there exists a path going from an atom on
{\bm{p}}
to an atom on either a cycle or another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
. This path
must enter

\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{D}}
, because the above-stated property of
{\bm{p}}
will not hold without layer
\ell_{D}
; moreover it also
must exist

\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{D}}
(including exiting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
when getting onto another particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}-1})
), because
\mathbb{M}_{D}
by itself does not contain a cycle. By considering the
first entry
and
first exit
at
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, we obtain two bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that are connected within
\mathbb{M}_{D}
; moreover the first entry bond must be connected to
{\bm{p}}
via
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
, and is thus in bijection with
{\bm{p}}
. This proves the desired result, namely the existence of at least
\rho^{\prime\prime}
bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, each of which being connected to another by a path within
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.
∎

Remark 11.25
.

We make two remarks concerning Definition
11.23
and Proposition
11.24
.

(1)

In Definition
11.23
, we focus on
\mathbb{M}_{UD}=\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}=\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{D}:\ell_{U}]}
and ignore other layers. In practice, we need to
cut
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell_{D}:\ell_{U}]}
as free
from
\mathbb{M}
and cut the other layers into elementary components (first
\mathbb{M}_{>\ell_{U}}
using
UP
, then
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell_{D}}
using
DOWN
). This may create some {4} molecules, but their number is easily bounded using Proposition
11.12
and the upper bound on the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{>\ell_{U}}
and
\mathbb{M}
(see Definition
12.14
and Proposition
12.15
).

(2)

Compared to Definition
11.1
, the
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
in Proposition
11.24
are
forests
instead of
trees
. However, the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is
\ll\rho^{\prime\prime}
by Proposition
11.24
and is thus negligible; on the other hand,
\mathbb{M}_{D}
may have many components, but this is compensated by the fact that the
\rho^{\prime\prime}
bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
in Proposition
11.24
are connected within
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Indeed, in the case when
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has many components, this (in fact a related property of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, see Proposition
12.15
(3b)) allows us to first cut
\mathbb{M}_{U}
as free and gain from subsequently cutting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, see Proposition
13.3
.

Remark 11.26
.

In practice, there are two complications to layer selection: first, if some single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
contains cycles (without simplification (
2
) in Section
11.1
), then we need an extra
layer refining
process (see Definition
12.12
), which is discussed in Section
11.7.2
. Second, if
v_{\ell_{D}+1}^{*}\neq 0
(i.e. there are exceptional components in Definition
11.23

3
, though their number is still
\ll\rho^{\prime\prime}
and negligible), we need to first cut them as free before defining
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(Definition
12.14
). This will create some {4} molecules but their number is bounded (see Propositions
12.13
and
12.15
), and will also introduce fixed ends to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
which necessitates the
pre-processing
step, see discussions in Section
11.7.4
.

11.7.
Additions in the general case

In this subsection, we discuss the additional “accessories” needed to address the general case, without the simplifications (
1
)–(
2
) in Section
11.1
. These should be viewed as branches that are attached to the main thread of proof, which are less essential but necessary. These accessories are listed in
Table
2
, and will be discussed in Sections
11.7.1
–
11.7.4
below.

11.7.1.
O-atoms and ov-segments

First, in the real problem, the molecule
\mathbb{M}
may contain O-atoms, and the cutting operation in this case is more complicated (see Definition
8.3
(
2
) compared to Definition
8.3
(
1
)).

As we already see in Definition
8.3
, the notion of ov-segments is important in the analysis of O-atoms, and should be viewed as a generalization of edges/bonds. In Definition
8.4
, we have introduced the notions of being ov-adjacent, ov-parent and ov-child, and ov-components etc., which are direct generalizations of adjacent, parent and child, and connected components. Fortunately, in almost all cases, this generalization
does not cause any essential change
to the proof; we simply translate every sentence in the proofs in Sections
11.2
–
11.5
into the language of ov-segments, with “adjacent” replaced by “ov-adjacent” and “components” replaced by “ov-components”, etc. As an example, by replacing “connected” with “ov-connected” and “bond” with “ov-segment” in Lemma
11.6
, we obtain the following statement, which is just Lemma
13.4
:

•

Suppose we cut
S\subseteq\mathbb{M}
as free, where
S
is an
ov-connected
subset of
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}
is a tree. Then, for each atom
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash S
that is connected to an atom in
S
by a (pre-cutting)
ov-segment
e
, the post-cutting molecule
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has exactly one component
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{q}
. Moreover, when viewed in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, the operation of cutting
S
turns exactly one free end at
\mathfrak{q}
into a fixed end.

11.7.2.
Reducing each layer to a tree: layer refining

In both the toy models in Sections
11.2
–
11.5
and layer selection in Section
11.6
, it is crucial that each layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
of
\mathbb{M}
is a forest. In reality, to deal with the case where some
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
contains cycles, we need to define the
layer refining
process as follows.

Recall (Proposition
10.4
(
3
)–(
4
)) that each
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is formed by joining finitely many C-molecules by O-atoms, such that each C-molecule has circuit rank
\rho(W)\leq\Gamma
, and joining these C-molecules by O-atoms does not add any cycle. We start with the following simple intuitive lemma:

•

For each molecule
\mathbb{M}
with at most
\Gamma
independent cycles, we can always divide it into at most
\Gamma+1
subsets
\mathbb{M}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq\Gamma+1)
, such that each subset is a tree, and no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{i}
can be parent of atom in
\mathbb{M}_{j}
for
i<j
.

For the proof of the above lemma, see Lemma
12.7
. Now, to complete the layer refining process for each single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, we need to delete some O-atoms to ensure each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
has
\rho
value at most
\Gamma
, and then apply this lemma to divide it into at most
\Gamma+1

thin layers
, such that each thin layer is now a forest. For details see Definition
12.8
and Proposition
12.9
. This then allows us to repeat the layer selection process in Section
11.6
; the use of thin layers instead of layers leads to one extra step in layer selection, but this is easily adjusted, see Definition
12.12
.

We remark that the above layer refining process does require the total number of independent cycles in these single-layer C-molecules to be negligible (compared to
\rho
). When this total number of cycles is large, we can perform an alternative cutting sequence that exploits these cycles, see Propositions
12.10
and
12.11
. This requires layer refining of a subset of layers and also leads to a minor auxiliary notion of
primitivity
, see Definition
12.4
and Lemma
12.6
.

11.7.3.
Strong and weak degeneracies

In Definition
11.1
we have assumed that all {33} molecules are good. In reality there may be exceptions to this when the
(x_{e},v_{e})
for
fixed ends

e
at this {33} molecule takes some very specific values, as stated in the definition of good molecules (Definition
9.4
). For example, a {33A} molecule will not be good, if

(11.21)

|x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{7}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\textrm{and}\quad|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{7}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon},

where
e_{1}
and
e_{7}
are the two fixed ends of the {33} molecule.

As such, we define a
strong degeneracy
to be an instance where (
11.21
) happens at two edges that
would become the two fixed ends if we cut the two atoms as a
{
33A
}
molecule
; see Definition
12.4
. Similarly we can define the notion of
weak degeneracy
associated with the {33B} molecules, which involves one or two atoms and some conditions opposite to the ones stated in Definition
9.4
; see Definition
12.16
. Note that these definitions
only involve
certain edges and atoms in
\mathbb{M}
, and is
not
a priori associated with any concrete cutting sequence. This provides the flexibility, so that we can classify the degeneracies and non-degeneracies
before
performing any cutting, and perform different cutting operations according to different cases, which allows us to minimize the need of backtracking.

Our strategy is that, when there are many degeneracies, we shall perform an
alternative cutting sequence
, where we do not gain from {33} molecules but instead gain from the extra degeneracy assumptions such as (
11.21
). Compared to the sophisticated algorithms exploiting {33} molecules, this alternative cutting sequence essentially follows the simple procedure where we cut every atom by itself, so that most of them are cut as {3} molecules. Usually integrating a {3} molecule does not gain any power, but if this {3} molecule contains an edge or atom that is involved in some degeneracy condition like (
11.21
), then it is clear that
this
{3} molecule integration should gain some power, which makes it a good molecule, see Definition
9.4
.

In practice, there will be two places in the overall algorithm where we need to consider degeneracies: the first time for strong degeneracies, see Proposition
12.5
and the second time for weak degeneracies, see Proposition
12.17
. If there are many (strong or weak) degeneracies, then we can apply the alternative cutting sequence to conclude; if there are few (strong and weak) degeneracies, then their effect will be negligible and we can get rid of them and proceed with the main thread of our algorithm.

11.7.4.
Additional ingredients and pre-processing

Finally, we discuss a few minor ingredients that are needed in the full case, that are not discussed in Sections
11.7.1
–
11.7.3
above.

(a) Initial cumulants.
In this section we have assumed
r(\mathbb{M}_{0})=H_{0}=\varnothing
in Proposition
10.4
; in reality this
H_{0}
may exist, which corresponds to the initial (time
t=0
) cumulants. This may affect the layer selection process, as it is now possible in Proposition
10.4
(
9
) that most particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
form initial links instead of being connected to other particle lines or cycles. In this case we simply exploit the initial link condition to obtain good {3} molecules (see Definition
9.4
); the will be quite easy, see Proposition
12.17
.

(b) Cutting exceptional components.
Recall the notion of exceptional components in Definition
11.23

3
; in Section
11.6
we have assumed there is no such exceptional components, so
\mathbb{M}_{U}=\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{U}}\cup\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{D}:\ell_{U})}
is a forest (Proposition
11.24
). In general, when such exceptional components exist (but with a negligible number), we need to first cut them as free, before defining
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
which can be forests. This cutting process is described in Definition
12.14
; it may create some {4} molecules, but their number can be bounded in Propositions
12.13
and
12.15
. It may also introduce fixed ends to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, which then requires the
pre-processing
step to treat, see (d) below.

(c) Cutting 2-connections.
Recall that in the 2-layer models, there is a case when the number of 2-connections satisfies
0<\#_{2\mathrm{conn}}\ll|X|
(see Section
11.5
), which is not covered in any toy model. In this case we first cut all these 2-connections as free, so no more 2-connections remain in the resulting molecule; this corresponds to
Stage 1
in the proof of Proposition
12.18
in Section
13.5
. After this, there will be fixed ends in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
similar to (b) above, so we should apply the extra pre-processing step (see (d) below).

(d) Pre-processing.
In Definition
11.1
, we have assumed that the 2-layer molecule
\mathbb{M}
has no fixed ends; however, in reality, such fixed ends may exist as shown in (b)–(c) above, which also leads to fixed ends and deg 3 atoms after we subsequently cut all deg 2 atoms. In fact those fixed ends in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
will not matter as there will not be any top fixed ends; however the fixed ends in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
may affect the properness of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(which is needed in the
MAINUD
algorithm) and needs to be addressed.

In this case, depending on the number of components of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(similar to the dichotomy in Sections
11.3
–
11.4
), we either apply
3COMPDN
(i.e. the dual of
3COMPUP
) in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
similar to Section
11.4
with enough gain (this corresponds to
Stage 2
in Section
13.5
, or apply an extra
pre-processing
step to turn
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into a proper molecule (this corresponds to
Stage 3
) . The pre-processing depends on a specific process
SELECT
, see Definition
13.10
and Proposition
13.11
.

After pre-processing, we are then ready to apply the dichotomy in Sections
11.3
–
11.4
, to reduce to either toy model II (corresponding to
Stage 5
), or toy model I plus (corresponding to
Stage 6
). Finally, note that the pre-processing step may also cause some complications, as the set
X
involved in the dichotomy needs to be replaced by some
natural modification
X_{1}
, which will be explained in details in the
MAINUD
, see the definition of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
in Definition
13.12
, and
Stage 4
.

11.7.5.
Strategy flowchart

We present a flowchart summarizing the proof of Proposition
9.7
, see
Figure
41
.

Figure 41.
Yes/No flow chart for the proof of Proposition
9.7
. Here each green box indicates end of a proof path; each “Do” describes the algorithm, process or steps we take, and “Define” indicates the new notions defined. The notions of strongly degenerate, primitive and weakly degenerate (abbreviated str. deg., prm. and wea. deg.) pairs are defined in Definitions
12.4
and
12.16
; the other notions are defined at the place indicated. The “many/large/small” etc. are all relative to
\rho
; for the precise threshold of these statements, see the corresponding propositions.

12.
Cutting algorithm I: reduction to UD molecules

In this section and Section
13
we will construct the cutting algorithm in Proposition
9.7
. As already mentioned in the beginning of Section
11
, these algorithms and arguments will be presented in a different order from Section
11
, to be consistent with the logical flow of the proof. The goal of this section is to prove several cases of Proposition
9.7
, namely Propositions
12.3
,
12.5
,
12.11
and
12.17
, and reduce Proposition
9.7
to Proposition
12.18
, by getting rid of degeneracies (Propositions
12.5
and
12.17
), introducing layer refinement (Section
12.3
) and layer selection (Section
12.4
).

12.1.
The
UP
algorithm and the large
|H|
case

In this subsection, we prove the first case of Proposition
9.7
, namely Proposition
12.3
, where we assume
|H|
is large. We start by introducing the full version of algorithm
UP
, which is a direct extension of its toy model version in Definition
11.10
, by replacing parent/child with ov-parent/ov-child (cf. Section
11.7.1
). This is a general purpose or “default” algorithm that minimizes the number of {4} molecules, which also guarantees sufficiently many good molecules
only in some cases
. However, this algorithm alone is enough to treat the simple scenarios studied in this section. Moreover, even in later cases where other algorithms are needed to achieve gain in a key subset of the molecule, it is usually helpful to use
UP
(or its dual
DOWN
) to finish the decomposition of the remaining parts of the molecule without loss.

Definition 12.1

(The algorithm
UP
)
.

Define the following cutting algorithm
UP
. It takes as input any (regular) molecule
\mathbb{M}
, which has
no top fixed end at any C-atom
(we henceforth abbreviate it as
C-top fixed ends
; same for
C-bottom fixed ends
). For any such
\mathbb{M}
, we define the cutting sequence as follows:

(1)

If
\mathbb{M}
contains any deg
2
atom
\mathfrak{n}
, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg
2
atom left.

(2)

Consider the set of all remaining deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
(i.e. those deg 3 that have not been cut), and choose a lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}
in this set. If
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 3 atoms, then choose a lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}
. Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
(including
\mathfrak{n}
); this
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
will be fixed until the end of (
3
).

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, each time choose a
highest
remaining atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3, and either an ov-parent
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
or an ov-child
\mathfrak{m}^{-}
of
\mathfrak{m}
also has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\}
or
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{-}\}
as free; otherwise cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. Repeat until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut. Then go to (
1
).

We also define the dual algorithm
DOWN
, by reversing the notions of parent/child, lowest/highest, top/bottom etc. It applies to any molecule that has no C-bottom fixed end.

The next proposition is the full version of Proposition
11.12
.

Proposition 12.2
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be any molecule as in Definition
12.1
. We may assume
\mathbb{M}
is connected (otherwise consider each component of
\mathbb{M}
). Then after applying algorithm
UP
to
\mathbb{M}
(and same for
DOWN
):

(1)

We have the following monotonicity property (MONO): throughout the process of
UP
, the set
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
will not have any
C-bottom
fixed end, and
(\mathbb{M}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
will not have any
C-top
fixed end (in particular, when all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut and before the next
\mathfrak{n}
is chosen in Definition
12.1
(
2
), there is no C-top fixed end in whole
\mathbb{M}
). In particular, the molecule
\mathbb{M}
is cut into elementary molecules, and
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
.

(2)

We have
\#_{\{4\}}\leq 1
, and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
if and only if
\mathbb{M}
is full.

In (
3
)–(
4
) below we assume
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 2 atoms.

(3)

If
\mathbb{M}
contains a cycle or contains at least two deg
3
atoms, then
\#_{\{33A\}}\geq 1
. If not (i.e.
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and contains at most one deg 3 atom), then all the elementary molecules are {3} molecules with at most one exception which might be a {4} molecule.

(4)

If
\mathbb{M}
contains at most one deg 3 atom
and
contains a cycle, then we can split into
2
sub-cases such that for each sub-case we have
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq 1
.

Proof.

We first make some comments on the result and the proof. Here, compared to (MONO-toy) in Proposition
11.12
, the (MONO) property changes the absence of fixed ends to the absence of C-fixed ends, because of the presence of O-atoms (we do not need to worry about fixed ends at O-atoms because they cannot affect any other atom; see Remark
8.7
).

The main new ingredient compared to Proposition
11.12
is (
4
), which guarantees a
good
molecule instead of just a {33A} molecule, and is thus useful in strongly degenerate cases (Definition
12.4
, Proposition
12.5
) where {33A} molecules might not be good. The proof of (
4
) also provides an example how the splitting into sub-cases in Proposition
9.7
works, by decomposing 1 into suitable indicator functions with prescribed supports. Apart from these, the proof is essentially the same as Proposition
11.12
.

Proof of (
1
).
We first prove (MONO). Note that this statement is true initially, as
\mathbb{M}
has no C-top fixed end. Moreover at each time of choosing
\mathfrak{n}
in Definition
12.1
(
2
), there is also no C-bottom fixed end in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
, because all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
must have deg 4 (since
\mathfrak{n}
is the lowest deg 3 atom). Therefore, it suffices to show that (MONO) is preserved. For this, first note that if
\mathfrak{n}
has deg 2 as in Definition
12.1
(
1
), and has no C-top fixed end, then cutting
\mathfrak{n}
as free will only generate bottom fixed ends at its ov-parents (cf. Remark
8.7
), so the absence of C-top fixed end in the whole molecule is preserved.

Next, suppose
\mathfrak{n}
is chosen as in Definition
12.1
(
2
), and
\mathfrak{m}
is a highest remaining atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
as in Definition
12.1
(
3
). If we cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free, this does not create any C-bottom fixed end in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
(because
\mathfrak{m}
is a highest remaining atom, so any ov-parent of
\mathfrak{m}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
has already been cut), and does not create any C-top fixed end in
(\mathbb{M}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
(because any ov-child atom of
\mathfrak{m}
must again be in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
), so (MONO) is preserved.

The same can be proved for the operation of cutting
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\}
or
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{-}\}
; let us consider
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\}
as the other case is similar. Here, cutting
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\}
may create bottom fixed ends at ov-parents of
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
, but none of them belong to
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
(so (MONO) is not affected), because
\mathfrak{m}
is a highest remaining atom (so no ov-parent of
\mathfrak{m}
still remains in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
, in particular
\mathfrak{m}^{+}\not\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
and no ov-parent of
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
can be in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
). Moreover, cutting
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\}
may create top fixed ends at ov-children of
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
, but they all have to be in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
(so again (MONO) is not affected). This last statement is obvious for
\mathfrak{m}
as
\mathfrak{m}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
. As for
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
, since it has deg 3, has no C-top fixed end, and has one ov-child being
\mathfrak{m}
, we know it must have a bottom fixed end (or is O-atom, cf. Remark
8.7
). Since
\mathfrak{m}
is ov-child of
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
, we know that any other ov-child of
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
must belong to the maximal ov-segment containing
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{m}
. Using the structure of ov-segments, we know that any other ov-child of
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
that is C-atom must occur at the bottom of this ov-segment, thus it must be an ov-child of
\mathfrak{m}
and belong to
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
. This shows (MONO) is preserved in the whole process, thus it is true for all time.

Now, note that by construction, any atom in
\mathbb{M}
is either cut by itself, or belongs to a {33} molecule. By (MONO), all the single-atom molecules we cut must have deg at least 2, and those of deg 2 must have two fixed ends either both being top or both being bottom (or non-serial for O-atoms, due to regularity). Similarly, all the {33} molecules
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\}
and
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{-}\}
cut in Definition
12.1
(
3
) must be {33A}, because at the time it is cut, (MONO) implies that the
\mathfrak{m}^{+}\not\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
must be an ov-parent of
\mathfrak{m}
with no C-top fixed end, and
\mathfrak{m}^{-}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
must be an ov-child of
\mathfrak{m}
with no C-bottom fixed end (in particular, if we cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free from
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}\}
, then
\{\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}\}
will become an elementary {2} molecule). This proves that all molecules we cut are elementary, and
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
.

Proof of (
2
).
If
\mathbb{M}
has only deg 4 atoms, then the first cutting in
\mathbb{M}
must create a {4} molecule; at any time thereafter,
\mathbb{M}
will contain at least one non-deg-4 atom (cf. Remark
10.6
), and by Definition
12.1
(
2
)–(
3
), any subsequent cutting will
not
create any {4} molecule (the
\mathfrak{n}
chosen in Definition
12.1
(
2
) must have deg 3; also any
\mathfrak{m}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
will have deg at most 3 when it is cut in Definition
12.1
(
3
), because
\mathfrak{m}
has an ov-parent in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
that has already been cut before). The same holds true for any
\mathbb{M}
that contains non-deg-4 atoms initially, in which case we do not get any {4} molecule. This proves (
2
).

Proof of (
3
).
Here and in the proof of (
4
) below we assume
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 2 atoms. We claim that if
\#_{\{33A\}}=0
, then necessarily also
\#_{\{2\}}=0
. Assume the contrary, consider the first atom
\mathfrak{m}
that has deg 2 when it is cut, and consider the cutting operation that turns
\mathfrak{m}
into deg 2, which must be cutting a single atom
\mathfrak{p}
(otherwise
\mathfrak{p}
would belong to a {33A} molecule). Note that
\mathfrak{m}
must have deg 3 at the time
\mathfrak{p}
is cut (because cutting
\mathfrak{p}
only generates one fixed end at
\mathfrak{m}
, see Proposition
10.4
(
2
)), and
\mathfrak{p}
also has deg 3 or 4 when it is cut (it cannot have deg 2 because
\mathfrak{m}
is the first). If
\mathfrak{p}
has deg 4 when it is cut (so
\{\mathfrak{p}\}
is cut as a {4} molecule), then by the proof in (
2
) we know that all atoms must have deg 4 initially, and
\mathfrak{p}
must the first atom cut; but that would imply that
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 4 at the time
\mathfrak{p}
is cut, contradiction.

We now know that
\{\mathfrak{p}\}
is cut as a {3} molecule, and both
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{m}
have deg 3 at the time
\mathfrak{p}
is cut. As
\mathfrak{p}
must be an ov-parent or ov-child of
\mathfrak{m}
at this time (because cutting
\mathfrak{p}
creates a fixed end at
\mathfrak{m}
), by Definition
12.1
(
3
), we know that we should have cut
\mathfrak{p}
together with another atom instead of cutting it alone as a {3} molecule, contradiction.

Next, define
\sigma:=\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}-3|\mathbb{M}|
and consider its increment under cutting operations, where
\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}
is the number of bonds plus the number of free ends (not counting fixed ends), and
|\mathbb{M}|
is the number of (remaining) atoms in
\mathbb{M}
. It is straightforward to check that cutting a {2}, {33}, {3} and {4} molecule increases the value of
\sigma
by
1
,
1
,
0
and
-1
. For example, cutting a {4} molecule turns
4
bonds/free ends into fixed ends when there is no O-atom (the case of O-atoms is similar and the result is the same) which decreases
\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}
by
4
, and also decreases
|\mathbb{M}|
by
1
, leading to
\Delta\sigma=-1
. In view of the initial and final values of
\sigma
, we then get

(12.1)

\#_{\{4\}}-(\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33A\}})=\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}=|\mathbb{M}|-|\mathbb{M}_{3}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}},

where
\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}
is the initial value of
\sigma
(with final value
\sigma_{\mathrm{fin}}=0
),
\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
is the number of bonds (initially) in
\mathbb{M}
and
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|
is the number of deg 3 atoms; here the last equality is because each atom has deg
3
or
4
, and each bond is exactly counted twice, so
\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}=(\textrm{total\ deg\ of\ all\ atoms})-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}=4|\mathbb{M}|-|\mathbb{M}_{3}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
.

Since
\mathbb{M}
is connected, we know
|\mathbb{M}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}\leq 1
, with equality if and only if
\mathbb{M}
is a tree. Now, if
\#_{\{33A\}}=0
(thus
\#_{\{2\}}=0
), then we have
\#_{\{4\}}+|\mathbb{M}_{3}|=|\mathbb{M}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
by (
12.1
), which is only possible when
|\mathbb{M}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}=1
(so
\mathbb{M}
is a tree) and
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|\leq 1
; conversely, if
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and has at most one deg 3 atom, then
|\mathbb{M}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}=1
and
|\mathbb{M}_{3}|+\#_{\{4\}}=1
by (
2
), so we get
\#_{\{2\}}=\#_{\{33A\}}=0
. This proves (
3
).

Proof of (
4
).
Finally we prove (
4
). Following
UP
, we first cut
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
as free, which is either the only deg 3 atom, or some deg 4 atom in
\mathbb{M}
; note that this does not create any deg 2 atom (because it creates at most one fixed end at any atom, see Proposition
10.4
(
2
)). In the subsequent cuttings, consider the first {33A} molecule (which exists due to (
2
)) and the two fixed ends
(e_{1},e_{2})
at it, which are created when cutting previous atoms
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
as free, and neither
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
nor
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
has deg 2 when it is cut, by the same proof in (
3
). If
\mathfrak{p}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{p}_{2}
, say
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
is cut before
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
, then there exist two free ends
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
in the elementary molecules
\{\mathfrak{p}_{1}\}
and
\{\mathfrak{p}_{2}\}
respectively (i.e. with
e_{1}^{\prime}
belonging to the same ov-segment as
e_{1}
in the original molecule, and same for
e_{2}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}
), such that
{z}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}={z}_{e_{1}}
and
{z}_{e_{2}^{\prime}}={z}_{e_{2}}
(cf. Remark
8.17
; see also
Figure
42
).

Figure 42.
The proof of Proposition
12.2
(
4
). Here both
\mathfrak{p}_{1}=\mathfrak{p}_{2}
is cut as free before the {33A} molecule
\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
(and thus
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
as in Definition
8.16
), so there exist free ends
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
in
\{\mathfrak{p}_{1}\}
such that we have
z_{e_{1}}=z_{e_{1}^{\prime}}
and
z_{e_{2}}=z_{e_{2}^{\prime}}
for the fixed ends
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
in
\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\}
(cf. Remark
8.17
), which leads to the splitting.

We perform the
splitting
by considering the dichotomy:

\mathrm{either}\quad|{x}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-{x}_{e_{2}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad|{x}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-{x}_{e_{2}^{\prime}}|>\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}.

In the former case, we may insert the corresponding indicator function, and this extra restriction implies that
\{\mathfrak{p}_{2}\}
is cut as a
good
{3} molecule due to Definition
9.4
(note that
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
is cut before
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
). In the latter case we have
|{x}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-{x}_{e_{2}^{\prime}}|=|{x}_{e_{1}}-{x}_{e_{2}}|>\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
, and this extra restriction implies that the {33A} molecule is good due to Definition
9.4
.

Similarly, if
\mathfrak{p}_{1}=\mathfrak{p}_{2}
, then both
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
are free ends at
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
. Moreover, if
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
is an O-atom, then
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
cannot be serial (otherwise the two atoms in the {33A} molecule are connected by an ov-segment containing both
(e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime})
and
(e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime})
and goes through
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
, so they are connected by two different ov-segments, violating Proposition
10.4
(
2
)). We then perform the same splitting; if
|{x}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-{x}_{e_{2}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
, then
\{\mathfrak{p}_{1}\}
is cut as a good {3} or {4} molecule due to Definition
9.4
; if
|{x}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}-{x}_{e_{2}^{\prime}}|=|{x}_{e_{1}}-{x}_{e_{2}}|>\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
, then again the {33A} molecule is good due to Definition
9.4
. This completes the proof.
∎

Now, with the
UP
algorithm, we can already prove the first simple case of Proposition
9.7
, namely when
|H|\geq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho
, see Proposition
12.3
. In this case the proof is easy due to the positive term
|H|
in (
9.51
).

Proposition 12.3
.

Proposition
9.7
is true in the case when
|H|\geq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho
.

Proof.

Under the assumption
|H|\geq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
, we simply cut the whole molecule
\mathbb{M}
using
UP
. By Definition
9.4
we know that
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}=\#_{\{4\}}+\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
, where
\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
is the number of empty ends. Recall (
9.51
) in Proposition
9.7
. We shall prove that
\nu:=|H|-\#_{\{4\}}-\#_{\mathrm{ee}}\geq 0
, and that (note that
d-1\geq 1
and
C_{13}^{*}\gg C_{12}^{*}
, so (
12.2
) is stronger than (
9.51
))

(12.2)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+\nu\geq(\upsilon/8)\cdot(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho.

In the proof below, in Part 1 we will show
\nu\geq 0
by using Proposition
12.2
and bounding the number of
connected components
of
\mathbb{M}
by
|H|
(the empty ends will be trivial), which is done by repeatedly iterating Proposition
10.4
(
5
)–(
7
) and showing that every component of
\mathbb{M}
must intersect a particle line in
H
, a process we call the (PL) argument below. In Parts 2–3, by using Proposition
10.4
(
9
), we will show that either
\nu\gtrsim|H|
(i.e. the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
is strictly less than
|H|
), or a constant percentage of these components contain cycles or initial links. In the former case we already gain enough powers, while in the latter case we can obtain sufficiently many good molecules either by Proposition
12.2
(
4
) or by exploiting the initial links.

Proof part 1.
We first prove
\nu\geq 0
. By Proposition
12.2
(
2
) we have
\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{comp}}
, where
\#_{\mathrm{comp}}
is the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
, so it suffices to show
\#_{\mathrm{comp}}+\#_{\mathrm{ee}}\leq|H|
. Here we present an argument which will be used many times later in different variants, which we refer to as the
(PL) argument
below (see
Figure
43
). Start from any component
W
of
\mathbb{M}
; by Proposition
10.4
(
5
), this
W
must intersect a particle line
{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
for some
\ell^{\prime}
. By Proposition
10.4
(
7
), this
{\bm{p}}
is equal to or connected to some
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1})
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}
; in either case
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
also intersects
W
(note that each particle line can intersect at most one component of
\mathbb{M}
, see Remark
10.5
). We then replace
{\bm{p}}
by
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
and repeat the same argument to get
{\bm{p}}^{\prime\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+2})
and so on, in the end we see that
W
must intersect a particle line
\bm{q}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=H
(in particular this
\bm{q}\in H
is not an empty end). Since each
\bm{q}
intersects at most one component of
\mathbb{M}
, this shows that
\#_{\mathrm{comp}}
is bounded by the number of particle lines in
H
that are not empty ends.

Figure 43.
The (PL) argument in the proof of Proposition
12.3
: start from the blue particle line
{\bm{p}}
intersecting a component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(green dotted circle, green edges) that is
\subseteq W
. This
{\bm{p}}
is connected to the red particle line
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
via
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}
, leading to a larger component of
\mathbb{M}_{[\ell^{\prime}:\ell^{\prime}+1]}
(pink dotted circle, pink edges) that is also
\subseteq W
and now intersects
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
; then connect
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
to another particle line
{\bm{p}}^{\prime\prime}
, and so on. This argument will be used in a few variants below (e.g. Proposition
12.13
relying on Proposition
12.9
).

On the other hand, if
{\bm{p}}
is any particle line which is an empty end, then
{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
for some
\ell^{\prime}
, and contains no atom; by repeating Proposition
10.4
(
7
) we know that
{\bm{p}}
must also belong to
H
. This means that
\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
is equal to the number of particle lines in
H
that are empty ends, so putting together we obtain that
\#_{\mathrm{comp}}+\#_{\mathrm{ee}}\leq|H|
, as desired.

Proof part 2.
Now we have
\nu\geq|H|-\#_{\mathrm{comp}}-\#_{\mathrm{ee}}\geq 0
, it suffices to prove (
12.2
). Define

•

X
to be the number of particle lines in
H
that are connected to another particle line in
H
via
\mathbb{M}
,

•

Y
to be the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
that contains a cycle,

•

and
Z
to be the number of initial links involving two non-empty-end particle lines, such that at least one of them belongs to a component that does not contain a cycle.

We claim that

(12.3)

X+Y+2Z\geq|H|-4\cdot\#_{\mathrm{ee}}.

In fact, by Proposition
10.4
(
9
), each particle line
{\bm{p}}
in
H
either (i) belongs to the
X
particle lines listed above, or (ii) is not connected to other particle lines in
H
but is connected to a cycle, or (iii) does not belong to (i) or (ii) but forms an initial link. Here in (ii) the
{\bm{p}}
must intersect one of the
Y
components with cycle, and each component is counted at most once (if two particle lines correspond to the same component then they would be connected and thus would be in (i)), leading to at most
Y
particle lines. In (iii), suppose
{\bm{p}}
forms an initial link that involves
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
as in Definition
3.19
, then each
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
corresponds to a unique
{\bm{p}}
(otherwise they are again in (i)), and the number of
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
is at most
2Z+2\cdot\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
(those
Z
initial links not involving empty ends contribute at most
2Z
choices of
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, and those initial links involving empty ends contribute at most
4\cdot\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
choices of
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
because each empty end belongs to at most two initial links). This proves (
12.3
).

Consider all components of
\mathbb{M}
(indexed by some
\alpha
), and the number of particle lines in
H
intersecting each component (denoted by
m_{\alpha}
); by the above proof we have
m_{\alpha}\geq 1
. Note that the
X
defined above satisfies
X=\sum_{m_{\alpha}\geq 2}m_{\alpha}
, and the total number of non-empty-end particle lines is equal to
\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}
, we conclude that

(12.4)

\nu\geq|H|-\#_{\mathrm{ee}}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}}=\sum_{\alpha}(m_{\alpha}-1)=\sum_{m_{\alpha}\geq 2}(m_{\alpha}-1)\geq X/2.

Proof part 3.
Next we prove that

(12.5)

\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq Y+Z/2+\#_{\mathrm{ee}}.

In fact, each empty end must belong to a link by Proposition
10.4
(
9
), so it must be good. In the process of
UP
we may choose to first cut the components with cycles; by Proposition
12.2
(
4
), each of these components contributes at least one good molecule under
UP
(possibly after splitting into 2 sub-cases, but the total number of sub-cases caused by this is bounded by
2^{|\mathbb{M}|}
and is acceptable in Proposition
9.7
), so we get at least
Y
good molecules.

After this, we are left with a forest without fixed end, so each of the subsequent elementary molecules we cut will be {3} or {4} molecules by Proposition
12.2
(
3
). Moreover, for each of the
Z
initial links, at least one of the involved particle lines (i.e. the one belonging to a no-cyclic component) has still not been cut; we call the corresponding bottom free end
e_{1}
and call the one initial linked to it
e_{2}
, note that
e_{2}
may or may not have been cut.

In the subsequent cuttings of the remaining forest, let
\sigma_{j}
be the maximal ov-segment containing
e_{1}
, and assume
\sigma_{2}
is broken (i.e. has any atom on it cut) before
\sigma_{1}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the first atom in
\sigma_{1}
that is cut, then
\mathfrak{p}
must be cut as a {3} or {4} molecule and has no fixed end along
\sigma_{1}
(as it is the first atom cut in
\sigma_{1}
), so the initial link condition guarantees that
\mathfrak{p}
is a good ({3} or {4}) molecule by Definition
9.4
, see
Figure
44
. Similarly, if
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are broken at the same time, say when cutting an atom
\mathfrak{p}
, then the initial link condition again guarantees that
\{\mathfrak{p}\}
is a good {3} or {4} molecule.

Figure 44.
Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
12.3
. Here
\sigma_{2}
is broken before
\sigma_{1}
, and
\mathfrak{p}
is the first atom cut in
\sigma_{1}
(which is a {3} molecule in the picture). In the picture,
z_{e_{2}}
has been fixed, and an initial link exists between
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, and
z_{e_{3}}=z_{e_{1}}
(where
e_{3}
is the edge at
\mathfrak{p}
), so
\{\mathfrak{p}\}
is a good {3} molecule, thanks to the initial link condition and Definition
9.4
.

In summary, each of the
Z
initial links provides one good molecule in the process of cutting forests, leading to
Z/2
good molecules (each good molecule is counted at most twice as each particle line is involved in at most 2 initial links). This produces
Z/2
extra good molecules and thus proves (
12.5
). Finally, putting together (
12.3
)–(
12.5
), we get

(12.6)

\#_{\mathrm{good}}+\nu\geq\frac{1}{2}(X+2Y+Z+2\cdot\#_{\mathrm{ee}})\geq\frac{1}{4}|H|\geq(4C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho.

As the left hand side of (
12.6
) is strictly positive and is trivially
\geq 1
, this proves (
12.2
).
∎

12.2.
Strong degeneracies

From now on we only need to consider the case
|H|<(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho
in Proposition
9.7
(we will make this assumption until the end of Section
13
), and the goal is to prove (
9.51
). Note also that
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}=\#_{\{4\}}+\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
and
|H|\geq\#_{\mathrm{ee}}
by the same proof as in Proposition
12.3
, so it suffices to prove

(12.7)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}-d\cdot\#_{\{4\}}\geq(C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho.

In this subsection we treat the case of sufficiently many strong degeneracies (Definition
12.4
below), see Proposition
12.5
(also cf. Section
11.7.3
). For technical reasons (explained in the proof of Proposition
12.5
below) we also need the extra notion of
primitivity
in Definition
12.4
below.

Definition 12.4
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule as in Proposition
10.4
. Define a pair of atoms
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
in
\mathbb{M}
to be
strongly degenerate
if

(1)

They are ov-adjacent and in the same layer (
\ell[\mathfrak{n}]=\ell[\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}]
).

(2)

There exist edges
(e,e^{\prime})
at
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
respectively, such that cutting
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
as free in
\mathbb{M}
and turning
(e,e^{\prime})
into fixed ends results in a {33A} molecule, as in Definition
8.11
;

(3)

We have the restriction that
|x_{e}-x_{e^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
and
|v_{e}-v_{e^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
.

We also define an atom pair
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
in
\mathbb{M}
to be
primitive
, if they are in the same layer (
\ell[\mathfrak{n}]=\ell[\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}]
),
\mathfrak{n}
is ov-parent of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, and there
do not exist
C-atoms
\mathfrak{q}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
with
r\geq 1
, such that
\mathfrak{q}_{j+1}
is ov-child of
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
for each
j
with
\mathfrak{q}_{0}=\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{r+1}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 12.5
.

Recall Definition
12.4
. Suppose we make the further restriction that
\mathbb{M}
contains at least
(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
disjoint strongly degenerate primitive pairs (by inserting the corresponding indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{str.deg.prm}}
), then Proposition
9.7
is true.

Proof.

Recall
|H|<(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho
and the goal is to prove (
12.7
). The idea is to restrict sufficiently many strong degeneracies to a single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(by definition, the two atoms in any strongly degenerate pair must be in the same layer), and cut most of the atoms involved in these degeneracies as {3} molecules which will be good due to the extra restrictions given by strong degeneracy, see
Choice 3
below. If this is not possible, then there must exist sufficiently many cycles in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(i.e.
Y\gtrsim\rho
in our notions below), so we can try to gain from the cyclic clusters using Proposition
12.2
(
4
), see
Choice 2
. If this is also not possible, then
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
must have many components (i.e.
X\gtrsim\rho
in our notions below); in this case we can cut each pair of strongly degenerate atoms as a good {44} molecule, see
Choice 1
. However, we need to make sure that cutting this {44} molecule does not violate the (MONO) property needed to execute the
UP
algorithm, which then naturally leads to the notion of primitive atom pairs in Definition
12.4
.

First assume there exists at least
\mathfrak{A}:=(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
disjoint strongly degenerate primitive pairs. By splitting into at most
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\mathfrak{A}}
sub-cases (i.e. decomposing
1
into the corresponding indicator functions), we may fix one choice of these pairs. By using pigeonhole principle and replacing
\mathfrak{A}
with
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{A}
, we may restrict all these atom pairs
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
to a single layer, say
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Define
\mathbb{Q}
to be the union of all connected omponents of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
that contain at least one strongly degenerate primitive pair
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
. We then

(1)

Cut
\mathbb{Q}
as free, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{>\ell^{\prime}}
as free from
\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{Q}
and cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
;

(2)

Then cut
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell^{\prime}}\cup(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\backslash\mathbb{Q})
into elementary molecules using
DOWN
.

It is easy to see that this can always be done, and the contribution of these steps (i.e. coming from atoms outside
\mathbb{Q}
) to
\#_{\{4\}}
is at most
2|H|
. This because the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{>\ell^{\prime}}
is
\leq|H|
by repeating the (PL) argument in the proof of Proposition
12.3
(i.e. starting with a particle line intersecting one component and iteratively applying Proposition
10.4
(
7
)), and moreover each
full
component of
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell^{\prime}}\cup(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\backslash\mathbb{Q})

after the rest has been cut
must also be a full component in the original molecule
\mathbb{M}
(see Remark
10.6
) and there are at most
|H|
of them, so the upper bound on
\#_{\{4\}}
follows from Proposition
12.2
(
2
).

Now we only need to cut
\mathbb{Q}
into elementary molecules. For each connected component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{Q}
, consider the clusters in
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
as defined in Proposition
10.4
(
4
) (i.e. ov-components of the set of C-atoms in
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
); denote these clusters by
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
, then
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
is formed by joining these
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
with O-atoms. Denote by
X
the number of components of
\mathbb{Q}
, and by
Y
the total number of
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
that are cyclic (i.e.
\rho(\mathbb{Q}_{j})>0
or
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
contains a cycle). Depending on the sizes of
X
and
Y
, we may then choose to perform one of the following three cutting sequences.

Choice 1
: in this choice we will gain from
X
. For each component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{Q}
, choose one pair
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
in it, then cut
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
as free (
Figure
45
), which is a good {44} molecule due to the restrictions
|x_{e_{j}}-x_{e_{j}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
and
|v_{e_{j}}-v_{e_{j}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
. Recall that
S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
is the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
. We next prove that
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
has no ov-parent
\mathfrak{q}\neq\mathfrak{n}_{j}
that is a C-atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
. In fact, if such a
\mathfrak{q}
exists, then
\mathfrak{q}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
, so there exist a path from
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
to
\mathfrak{q}
by iteratively taking children. This path forms a cycle together with the ov-segments from
\mathfrak{q}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
and from
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
. As the cycle belongs to a single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, by Proposition
10.4
(
3
) it must be formed by ov-segments connecting C-atoms; in particular, by considering the path between
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{q}
, we see that the
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
must exist as in Definition
12.4
, so
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
is not primitive, contradicting our assumption that
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
is a strongly degenerate primitive pair.

Now, we have proved that the above
\mathfrak{q}
does not exist; this implies that, after cutting
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
as free, there is no C-top fixed end in
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
(recall that
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
), and no C-bottom fixed end in
S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
, in particular the monotonicity property (MONO) in Proposition
12.2
is true. Since this property is preserved under
UP
, we can then repeat the steps in Definition
12.1
(
3
) and subsequently apply
UP
to the rest of
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
to cut it into elementary molecules. By applying this to each component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{Q}
, we obtain
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq X
and
\#_{\{4\}}=0
.

Figure 45.
Choice 1
in the proof of Proposition
12.5
, cutting out a {44} molecule
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
which is good due to the strong degeneracy assumption. Note that cutting this {44} molecule does not violate the (MONO) property (i.e. creates no C-bottom fixed end in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
) if and only if this pair is primitive.

Choice 2
: in this choice we will gain from
Y
. For each component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{Q}
, let
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
be the clusters in
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
defined as above, which are joined by O-atoms to form the full component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
. For convenience, we allow some
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
to be empty ends (where joining an edge with an empty end simply means creating an O-atom in the interior of this edge, and drawing two serial free ends at this O-atom). We may reorder them as follows: first choose
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
arbitrarily, and each time choose one remaining cluster
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
that is joined with some
\mathbb{Q}_{j^{\prime}}\,(j^{\prime}<j)
by some O-atom (such
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
must exist because
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
is connected).

In this case, suppose (for the sake of contradiction) that
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
is joined with the clusters
\mathbb{Q}_{j^{\prime}}\,(j^{\prime}<j)
by
two
different O-atoms
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
, then we get a cycle by collecting (i) the two non-serial ov-segments connecting
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
to C-atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}_{j_{1}}\,(j_{1}<j)
, and the same for
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
(with
\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}_{j_{2}}\,(j_{2}<j)
), (ii) the ov-segments connecting
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
via O-atoms and C-atoms in
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
, and (iii) the ov-segments
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
to
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
via O-atoms and C-atoms in
\mathbb{Q}_{j^{\prime}}\,(j^{\prime}<j)
(note that
\mathbb{Q}_{j_{1}}
is indirectly joined to
\mathbb{Q}_{j_{2}}
by a sequence of O-atoms due to our construction). In this way we get a cycle in
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
with an O-atom
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
such that the two bonds of this cycle at
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
are
not serial
, contradicting Proposition
10.4
(
3
).

By the above arguments, we have reordered the
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
such that each
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
is joined with
exactly one

\mathbb{Q}_{j^{\prime}}\,(j^{\prime}<j)
by
exactly one
O-atom, say
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
. We then cut
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
as free, which creates the
unique
deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
in an ov-segment connecting two C-atoms in
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
(as
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
is the only atom joining
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
with
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
); then we cut
\mathbb{Q}_{2}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}_{2}\}
as free, after which we have the unique deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{p}_{3}
in an ov-segment connecting two C-atoms in
\mathbb{Q}_{3}
(as
\mathfrak{p}_{3}
is the only atom joining
\mathbb{Q}_{3}
with
\mathbb{Q}_{1}\cup\mathbb{Q}_{2}
), and so on, see
Figure
46
. Note that when we cut each
\mathbb{Q}_{j}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}_{j}\}
, it always contains
at most one
deg 3 atom (i.e.
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
if
j\geq 2
and none if
j=1
), so by Proposition
12.2
(
4
), we can subsequently cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
or
DOWN
depending on whether the unique fixed end is bottom or top, and get a good molecule for each
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
that is
cyclic
.

By applying the above process to each component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{Q}
, we obtain
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq Y
(number of cyclic
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
) and
\#_{\{4\}}\leq X
(total number of components). We note that strongly degenerate and primitive is not used in this part.

Figure 46.
Choice 2
in the proof of Proposition
12.5
, each time cutting
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
together with
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
; note that each
\mathbb{Q}_{j}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}_{j}\}
contains a unique deg 3 atom which is
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
. This is also an example of the cutting order
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}
in Definition
8.16
, i.e. we have
\mathbb{Q}_{1}\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}(\mathbb{Q}_{2}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}_{2}\})\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}(\mathbb{Q}_{3}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}_{3}\})
.

Choice 3
: in this choice we will gain from
\mathfrak{A}
. For each component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{Q}
, we cut it as free using
UP
. To analyze this choice, for each
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
, we define the quantity
\sigma=\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}-3|\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}|
as in the proof of Proposition
12.2
(
3
). By the same arguments there, we get

(12.8)

\#_{\{4\}}-(\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33A\}})=\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}=|\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}

(note that initially
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
has no deg 3 atom), where
\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
is the number of bonds in
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
. To calculate
\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}
, assume
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
is formed by joining
q
C-molecules with
q-1
O-atoms as discussed in
Choice 2
, then we have

(12.9)

\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}=|\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}=\sum_{j=1}^{q}(1-\rho(\mathbb{Q}_{j}))-(q-1)=1-\sum_{j=1}^{q}\rho(\mathbb{Q}_{j}),

where
\rho(\mathbb{Q}_{j})
is the circuit rank of the C-molecule
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
(to see this, just note that joining two C-molecules by an O-atom decreases the value of
|\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
by 1 as it increases
|\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}|
by 1 and increases
-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
by 2, while value of
|\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}|-\#_{\mathrm{bond}}
before introducing any O-atom equals
\sum_{j=1}^{q}(1-\rho(\mathbb{Q}_{j}))
by definition).

Now by adding up (
12.8
) and (
12.9
) for each component
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
, and note that
0\leq\rho(\mathbb{Q}_{j})\leq\Gamma
with
\rho(\mathbb{Q}_{j})>0
if and only if
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
is cyclic, we conclude that

\#_{\{4\}}-(\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33A\}})\geq X-\Gamma\cdot Y

(recall
X
is the number of components
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
, and
Y
is the number of cyclic
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
); since also
\#_{\{4\}}\leq X
, we then have

(\textrm{number of molecules that are not \lx@text@lbrace 3\lx@text@rbrace})=\#_{\{4\}}+(\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33A\}})\leq X+\Gamma\cdot Y.

In particular, by excluding this many exceptions, we see that the number of pairs
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
such that
both
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
are cut as
{
3
}
molecules
is at least
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{A}-2(X+\Gamma Y)
.

For each such pair
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
, if
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
is cut before
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
, since
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
is cut as a {3} molecule, we know that
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
must be good due to the restriction
|x_{e_{j}}-x_{e^{\prime}_{j}}|\leq\varepsilon^{1-\upsilon}
and Definition
9.4
. Here when
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
is O-atom, the edge
e_{j}^{\prime}
cannot be serial with the bonds between
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
, otherwise
\{\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}\}
will not be regular (and thus not a {33A} molecule) after turning
e_{j}^{\prime}
into a fixed end. In this way we get
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{A}-2(X+\Gamma Y)
and
\#_{\{4\}}\leq X
.

Summing up, by choosing one of the
Choices 1–3
, we get

(12.10)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot\#_{\{4\}}\geq\max\big((\upsilon/2)X,(\upsilon/2)Y-d\cdot X,(\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\upsilon/2)\mathfrak{A}-(d+1)(X+\Gamma Y)\big)\geq(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\mathfrak{A},

which proves (
12.7
) as
\mathfrak{A}=(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
.
∎

Note that Proposition
12.5
deals with the case where there exist many strongly degenerate primitive pairs. As for the non-primitive pairs, it turns out that they always lead to good molecules if they are cut as {33A}, as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 12.6
.

For each {33A} molecule
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
obtained
in any cutting sequence after any deletion of O-atoms
, if the atom pair
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is
not primitive in
\mathbb{M}
before any operation
, then by splitting into 2 sub-cases, we can either make this {33A} molecule good, or cut it into a good {3} and a normal {2} molecule.

Proof.

Consider any {33A} molecule
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
that is not primitive before any operation, and let
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
be as in Definition
12.4
. Note that
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
must belong to the same (original) layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(otherwise
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is good by Definition
9.4
), and so does every
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
. Consider the cycle formed by the ov-segments from
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
to
\mathfrak{q}_{j+1}
(with
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{q}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{q}_{r+1}
) and from
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
to
\mathfrak{n}
, which is contained in the single layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. By Proposition
10.4
(
3
), we see that both
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
must be C-atom. Moreover the C-atoms
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
, and the ov-adjacency relations between them, are not affected by deletion of O-atoms. Now, consider the C-atom
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
which is ov-child of
\mathfrak{n}
, and
\mathfrak{q}_{r}
which is ov-parent of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
(they could be the same atom).

Consider any cutting sequence. If
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
, or any (O-) atom
\mathfrak{r}
in the ov-segment between
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}
is cut before
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
(i.e. belongs to a molecule after cutting that is
\prec_{\mathrm{cut}}\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
), then we can make the
splitting
depending on whether (i)
|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|\geq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
or (ii)
|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
. In case (i) the
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is already good by Definition
9.4
; in case (ii), due to the ov-child relations we have

t_{\mathfrak{n}}\geq t_{\mathfrak{r}}\geq t_{\mathfrak{q}_{1}}\geq\cdots\geq t_{\mathfrak{q}_{r+1}}=t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}},

in particular
|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
implies that

|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{r}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon},\quad|t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}-t_{\mathfrak{r}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}.

Now we may cut either
\mathfrak{n}
or
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
from
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
as a {3} molecule such that the remaining atom becomes a {2} molecule (one of the two choices must be possible due to the definition of {33A} molecules), then this {3} molecule will be good by Definition
9.4
, so the lemma is proved.

In the same way, if
\mathfrak{q}_{r}
, or any (O-) atom
\mathfrak{r}
in the ov-segment between
\mathfrak{q}_{r}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is cut before
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
, then the lemma is also proved by the same arguments as above.

Finally, if none of
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{r}
and none of the above-mentioned O-atoms is cut before
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
, then at the time
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is cut, the atom
\mathfrak{n}
cannot have any bottom fixed end (because it is ov-parent of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, and the ov-segment between
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
does not lead to any fixed end at
\mathfrak{n}
as the other atoms on this ov-segment are all cut after
\mathfrak{n}
); in the same way
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
has no top fixed end at the time
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is cut. However
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is a {33} molecule, which means that
\mathfrak{n}
must have a top fixed end and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
has a bottom fixed end, and thus
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
will be a {33B} molecule instead of {33A}, contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

Reduction 1
.

With Propositions
12.3
and
12.5
and Lemma
12.6
proved, from now on, we may always assume in Proposition
9.7
that
|H|\leq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
and the number of disjoint strongly degenerate primitive pairs is at most
(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
.

Using also Lemma
12.6
, we see that each {33A} molecule must be good with at most
(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
exceptions (these exceptions are when the corresponding atom pair is both strongly degenerate and primitive; if it is not strongly degenerate then we get a good molecule by Definition
9.4
, if it is not primitive we get a good molecule by Lemma
12.6
).

As such, we see that Proposition
9.7
will follow if we can prove that

(12.11)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot(\#_{\{4\}})\geq(C_{11}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho

under the assumption that
all {33A} molecules are good
, which we will assume throughout the rest of the proof.

12.3.
Layer refinement and the large
\mathfrak{R}
case

The goal of this subsection is to introduce the layer refining process (discussed in Section
11.7.2
) and prove the next case of Proposition
9.7
when
\mathfrak{R}
is large, namely Proposition
12.11
. The following lemma is already stated in Section
11.7.2
:

Lemma 12.7
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a connected molecule with
\rho(\mathbb{M})\leq\Gamma
, see Definition
3.13
. Then we can divide
\mathbb{M}
into at most
\Gamma+1
disjoint subsets
\mathbb{M}_{(k)}
for
1\leq k\leq\gamma\leq\Gamma+1
, such that (i) each
\mathbb{M}_{(k)}
is a tree, and (ii) if
k<k^{\prime}
then no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{(k)}
can be parent of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{(k^{\prime})}
. See
Figure
47
. Note that, unlike the original layer defined by time intervals, there is
no time inequality
between atoms in different thin layers within the same original layer, other than those coming from parent-child relations.

Figure 47.
Left: example of layer refining (Lemma
12.7
, each thin layer is a forest). Right: the proof of Lemma
12.7
. Note that
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
are connected by a path above
\mathfrak{n}
(forming a cycle); also
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
consists of those atoms that can be connected to
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
without entering
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
.

Proof.

We induct on
\rho(\mathbb{M})
. If
\rho(\mathbb{M})=0
then
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and there is nothing to prove. If
\rho(\mathbb{M})>0
, then
\mathbb{M}
must contain a cycle of atoms. Let a
lowest
atom in this cycle be
\mathfrak{n}
, which is adjacent to two atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
in this cycle, then both
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
must be parents of
\mathfrak{n}
. Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
(including
\mathfrak{n}
itself), we then define

(12.12)

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}:=\big\{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}:\mathfrak{m}\textrm{\ is\ connected\ to\ }\mathfrak{n}_{1}\textrm{\ by\ a\ path\ not\ containing\ atoms\ in\ }S_{\mathfrak{n}}\big\},

and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}:=\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
, see
Figure
47
. Note in particular
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
(
\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
because any atom in the cycle other than
\mathfrak{n}
cannot belong to
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
). Clearly
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
is connected, because for any
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
and any
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
on the path connecting
\mathfrak{m}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
defined by (
12.12
), we must also have
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{n}_{1}
by definition, so each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
is connected to
\mathfrak{n}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
by a path completely within
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
.

To prove
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
is connected, choose any atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Since
\mathbb{M}
is connected, there is a path in
\mathbb{M}
connecting
\mathfrak{m}
to
\mathfrak{n}
. Let the first atom in this path (starting from
\mathfrak{m}
) that belongs to
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be
\mathfrak{p}
, and consider any atom
\mathfrak{q}\neq\mathfrak{p}
between
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{p}
on this path, then
\mathfrak{m}
is connected to
\mathfrak{q}
by a path not containing atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
, which means
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
(as
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
would imply
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
). Thus we know each
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is connected to an atom
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
by a path completely within
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
, but
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is connected (every atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is connected to
\mathfrak{n}
by a path completely within
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
), so
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
is also connected.

Moreover, if an atom
\mathfrak{m}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
is connected to an atom
\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
by a bond, then we must have
\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
(otherwise
\mathfrak{m}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
would imply
\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
). This means
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
must be a parent of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
, since otherwise
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
is a child of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
, so
\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
implies
\mathfrak{m}_{1}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
, which is impossible. Finally, since there are at least two bonds between
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
(those connecting
\mathfrak{n}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
), we see that

|\mathbb{M}|=|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}|+|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}|,\,\,\,|\mathcal{B}|\geq|\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{high}}|+|\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{low}}|+2\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}})+\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}})\leq\rho(\mathbb{M})-1\leq\Gamma-1,

where
\mathcal{B}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{high}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{low}}
) is the set of bonds in
\mathbb{M}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
). We now apply induction hypothesis to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
, and note that no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}}
is parent of an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}}
, to divide
\mathbb{M}
into at most
(\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{high}})+1)+(\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{low}})+1)\leq\Gamma+1
subsets that satisfy requirements (i) and (ii), so the proof is complete.
∎

With Lemma
12.7
we can then define the full layer refining process. The idea is to
delete
those O-atoms joining two cyclic clusters (or cyclic components), so that each component contains at most one cyclic cluster after the deletions; in particular each component will have
\rho
value at most
\Gamma
, so we can apply Lemma
12.7
to each component.

Definition 12.8

(Layer refining)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule formed by layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, as in Proposition
10.4
. We define the following operation of
layer refining
to turn
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
into a new layered molecule
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Here the atoms and edges of
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
are the same as
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
except some O-atoms being deleted as in Definition
8.1
(for convenience, we will not distinguish between
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
below), but the layering of the atoms are different: the atoms in
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
are divided into at most
\Gamma+2
disjoint subsets, each of them indicated by a refined layer number. We will call these
thin layers
. The layer refining process is defined as follows:

(1)

Recall all the clusters (i.e. C-molecules) in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. We repeatedly delete O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, as long as each deletion disconnects two
cyclic
clusters
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
(i.e. these two clusters belong to different components after deletion), until no more O-atoms can be deleted. For each connected component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
that does not contain a cyclic cluster, we assign thin layer
(\ell^{\prime},0)
to all its atoms; note that with this definition, the thin layer
(\ell^{\prime},0)
plays a special role different from those thin layers
(\ell^{\prime},k)
for
k\geq 1
.

(2)

Apply Lemma
12.7
to each component
\mathbb{Q}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
that contains a cyclic cluster (we are guaranteed to have
\rho(\mathbb{Q})\leq\Gamma
, which will be proved in Proposition
12.9
below), to divide it into
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
, where
1\leq k\leq\Gamma+1
. Then, for each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, assign it thin layer
(\ell^{\prime},k)
if it belongs to
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
for some component
\mathbb{Q}
.

The thin layers defined above are linearly ordered by lexicographic ordering (where we first compare
\ell^{\prime}
and then compare
k
); if layer
\ell^{\prime}
is not refined, we identify it with
(\ell^{\prime},0)
. Note that the original layer
\ell^{\prime}
precisely contains all the thin layers
(\ell^{\prime},k)
. For any
\zeta
and
\xi
, we define
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta}^{T}
to be the set of atoms with thin layer
\zeta
; the associated notions
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta:\xi)}^{T}
,
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta:\eta]}^{T}
etc. are defined similarly.

In Proposition
12.9
below we state and prove some properties of the molecule
\mathbb{M}
after layer refining. The important points are that (a) each thin layer after refinement is a forest, see Proposition
12.9
(
3
)–(
4
); (b) the number of O-atoms deleted is bounded by
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}:=\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
for the refined layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, see Proposition
12.9
(
1
); and (c) the original properties of
\mathbb{M}
(notably Proposition
10.4
(
5
)–(
7
) and (
9
)) still hold after layer refining, with a number of exceptions that is bounded by the number of deleted O-atoms, see Proposition
12.9
(
5
)–(
8
).

The proof of (a)–(b) above relies on the construction in Definition
12.8
, in particular the fact that we delete all O-atoms joining two cyclic clusters, so each component contains at most one cyclic cluster after layer refining; see Parts 1–2 in the proof of Proposition
12.9
below. The proof of (c) is straightforward, basically because deleting each O-atom creates at most two exceptions to the relevant properties in Proposition
12.9
(
5
)–(
8
), see Part 3 in the proof of Proposition
12.9
below.

Proposition 12.9
.

If we perform the layer refining for
\ell^{\prime}
as in Definition
12.8
, then

(1)

The number of O-atoms deleted in the process is at most
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}:=\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
.

(2)

After deleting the O-atoms, each component
\mathbb{Q}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
that contains a cyclic cluster satisfies
\rho(\mathbb{Q})\leq\Gamma
, in particular Lemma
12.7
is applicable in Definition
12.8
.

(3)

Each
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},k)}^{T}
(where
1\leq k\leq\Gamma+1
) is a forest, and has most
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
connected components.

(4)

\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},0)}^{T}
is a forest, and the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},0)}^{T}
is not more than the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
before refining.

Now consider the result of refining any set of layers in
\mathbb{M}
, which we still denote by
\mathbb{M}
for convenience. Then we have the followings

(5)

For
\zeta<\xi
, no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta}^{T}
can be parent of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\xi}^{T}
.

(6)

Each connected component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},0)}^{T}
(where
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
) must intersect some particle line
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
.

(7)

With at most
2\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
exceptions, for each particle line
{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, either
{\bm{p}}
intersects a component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},0)}^{T}
, or it intersects a component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},k)}^{T}
for some
1\leq k\leq\Gamma+1
, or
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
.

(8)

Recall
\mathfrak{R}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. For each
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
, with at most
2\mathfrak{R}
exceptions, each particle line
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
either forms an initial link within
\mathbb{M}_{<(\ell^{\prime}+1,0)}^{T}
, or is connected to a cycle or another particle line
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
via the subset
\mathbb{M}_{<(\ell^{\prime}+1,0)}^{T}
.

Proof.

Proof part 1.
First note the following facts about the refinement in Definition
12.8
:

(1)

Deleting any O-atom
\mathfrak{o}
in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
always breaks a component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
into two;

(2)

Each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
that does not contain a cyclic cluster remain the same after refining. Each of the other components after refining contains a unique cyclic cluster.

In fact, (i) is true because if
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
are the two atoms adjacent to
\mathfrak{o}
along two ov-segments in two different particle lines, then they cannot be connected after deleting
\mathfrak{o}
, otherwise we get a cycle (before deleting
\mathfrak{o}
) in view of the two bonds from
\mathfrak{o}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\,(j\in\{1,2\})
, such that the two bonds at
\mathfrak{o}
are not serial in the cycle, contradicting Proposition
10.4
(
3
). Also (ii) is obvious by our construction (each component not containing cyclic clusters is not affected, and each component after refining cannot contain two cyclic clusters, otherwise we would be able to delete one more O-atom).

Proof part 2.
