Next we prove (
1
)–(
4
). Note first that the number of cyclic clusters in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
does not exceed
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(because each cyclic cluster contributes one the to the value of
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
). Now (
1
) is easy because each deletion increases the number of cyclic components by 1 (due to (i) above), and this number cannot exceed
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
which is the total number of cyclic clusters (imagine we delete all O-atoms, then the number of cyclic components would be equal to
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
), so the number of O-atoms deleted cannot exceed
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Moreover, (
2
) follows from the fact that each component contains only one cyclic cluster (the other clusters are trees and do not add to the
\rho
value, and joining the clusters by O-atoms also does not increase the
\rho
value).

Also (
4
) is obvious because
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},0)}^{T}
has not been affected by the deletion of O-atoms, and is a forest because it does not contain any cyclic cluster (cf. Proposition
10.4
(
3
)). As for (
3
), the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},k)}^{T}\,(k\geq 1)
is at most
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
because this is an upper bound for the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
containing one cyclic cluster; also each
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},k)}^{T}\,(k\geq 1)
is a forest which directly follows from Lemma
12.7
.

Proof part 3
. Now we prove (
5
)–(
8
), where we perform layer refining to any set of layers
\ell^{\prime}
, and denote the result still by
\mathbb{M}
. To prove (
5
), due to the lexicographic ordering of thin layers, we only need to prove it for a single layer
\ell^{\prime}
, and we only need to consider those components containing one cyclic cluster
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
(otherwise all thin layers are
(\ell^{\prime},0)
). But by definition, this follows from Lemma
12.7
(for each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
) and the fact that no bond exists between different components of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
.

Next we prove (
6
)–(
7
). Note that particle lines can be defined in the same way before and after the refinement, since deleting an O-atom corresponds to removing one atom from a particle line
{\bm{p}}
and merging two consecutive edges. From Proposition
10.4
(
5
)–(
7
) we already know that
before layer refining
, any connected component in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
must intersect some particle line
{\bm{p}}
with
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. This then proves (
6
) because
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},0)}^{T}
is not affected by layer refining: there is no bond connecting it to any
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},k)}^{T}
for
k>0
, and it also does not contain any of the O-atoms deleted in the refinement process. Similarly (
7
) follows from Proposition
10.4
(
5
)–(
7
): any particle line
{\bm{p}}\in p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
must either belong to
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
, or intersects
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(which is then divided into
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},k)}^{T}
for
0\leq k\leq\Gamma+1
). The only exceptions are those containing one of the deleted O-atoms, but the number of these particle lines is at most
2\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
as each O-atom belongs to at most two particle lines.

Finally we prove (
8
). Note that before layer refining, this just corresponds to Proposition
10.4
(
9
); however, after the layer refinement, we have deleted at most
\mathfrak{R}
O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
(note that by our conventions,
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
is just
\mathbb{M}_{<(\ell^{\prime}+1,0)}^{T}
after the refinement). If we add back these O-atoms one by one, then each time we create at most one cycle in one component, or merge at most two components of
\mathbb{M}_{[1:\ell^{\prime}]}
, and does not affect any initial link. Define a particle line
{\bm{p}}
to be
single
if it does not satisfy the requirement of (
8
), then adding back one O-atom at most reduces the number of single particle lines by
2
(for example, if adding back an O-atom merges two components, then it may turn two single particle lines into non-single as they are now connected to each other; the other particle lines are unaffected, as they cannot intersect the relevant components by the single assumption). By Proposition
10.4
(
9
), it then follows that the number of single particle lines is at most
2\mathfrak{R}
, as desired.
∎

Before stating and proving Proposition
12.11
below (i.e. the case
\mathfrak{R}\gtrsim\rho
), we need one more auxiliary result concerning a special case of
UP
. The intuition will be explained in the proof of Proposition
12.11
.

Proposition 12.10
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a full molecule divided into two subsets
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, such that (i)
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is a forest, and (ii) no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is parent of atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. Define
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}
to be the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
, and define
\#_{\mathrm{cyc.comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}
to be the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contains a cycle within this component. Then, see
Figure
48
, after applying the algorithm
UP
, we have that
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
, and

(12.13)

\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\#_{\{33A\}}\geq\#_{\mathrm{cyc.comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}/4.

Figure 48.
Proposition
12.10
. Here the cyclic clusters in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
are shown, with
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
being the first atom in each component that has deg 2 when cut, and
\mathfrak{p}_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
is its ov-parent; see the proof of Proposition
12.10
below.

Proof.

The statements about
\#_{\{33B\}}
,
\#_{\{44\}}
and
\#_{\{4\}}
already follow from Proposition
12.2
. Now we prove the statement about
\#_{\{33A\}}
. First we prove the following claim: if
A
is a component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contains a cycle, and no atom in
A
belongs to a {33A} molecule, then at least one atom in
A
must be deg 2 when it is cut. To prove this claim, choose a cycle
\theta
within
A
, we may think of
\theta
as formed by ov-segments, and consider the endpoints of these ov-segments. Let
\mathfrak{n}
be the one among these endpoints that is cut
after all the other endpoints
, then
\mathfrak{n}
is ov-adjacent to other endpoints which are cut before
\mathfrak{n}
, which creates two fixed ends at
\mathfrak{n}
, so
\mathfrak{n}
must be deg 2 when it is cut.

Now, if
A
is as above, and suppose
\mathfrak{n}\in A
is the first atom in
A
that has deg 2 when it is cut, and consider the cutting operation that turns
\mathfrak{n}
into deg 2. Then this cutting operation must involve an atom
\mathfrak{p}
that is ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{n}
; note that this
\mathfrak{p}
belongs to either
A
or
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. If
\mathfrak{p}\in A
, then
\mathfrak{p}
cannot be deg 2 when it is cut (as
\mathfrak{n}
is the first); this
\mathfrak{p}
also cannot be deg 4 when it is cut (because we cut deg 4 only when every atom has deg 4, and cutting a deg 4 atom
\mathfrak{p}
cannot turn the deg 4 atom
\mathfrak{n}
into deg 2, cf. Proposition
10.4
(
2
)), so it must be deg 3 when it is cut, but
\mathfrak{n}
also must has deg 3 when
\mathfrak{p}
is cut, which violates Definition
12.1
(
3
).

Therefore we must have
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
. Since
\mathfrak{p}
is cut before
\mathfrak{n}
, it cannot be cut in Definition
12.1
(
1
) (as cutting any deg 2 C-atom in Definition
12.1
(
1
) requires that this atom has two bottom fixed ends, see also Remark
8.7
), nor can it have deg 4 when it is cut (for the same reason as in the last paragraph). Suppose
\mathfrak{p}
belongs to
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
for some
\mathfrak{q}
as in Definition
12.1
(
3
), since
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is a forest, we know that
\mathfrak{p}
has at most one parent in
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
(otherwise we have two different paths going from
\mathfrak{p}
to
\mathfrak{q}
that form a cycle in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
), so it must have deg 3 when it is cut (the top edge at
\mathfrak{p}
corresponding to the non
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
parent, as well as the two bottom edges at
\mathfrak{p}
, are not fixed when
\mathfrak{p}
is cut; see
Figure
49
), while at the same time
\mathfrak{n}
also has deg 3. By Definition
12.1
(
3
), we then know that
\mathfrak{p}
belongs to a {33A} molecule.

As a conclusion, we know that each component
A
of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contains a cycle, must either contain one atom in a {33A} molecule, or intersect a particle line of some atom in some {33A} molecule. Clearly each {33A} molecule can be so obtained by at most 4 components (as each particle line can intersect at most one component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, see Remark
10.5
), so we get
\#_{\{33A\}}\geq\#_{\mathrm{cyc.comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}/4
.
∎

Figure 49.
The proof of Proposition
12.10
showing
\mathfrak{p}
has deg 3 when it is cut; this is because
\mathfrak{p}
has two bottom free ends/bonds and one top free end/bond coming from a parent that does not belong to
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
(and is not affected after cutting atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
). This fact will be used a few times below (such as in the proof of Proposition
12.17
).

We are now ready to prove Proposition
9.7
, in the case when
\mathfrak{R}\geq(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho
(discussed in Section
11.7.2
).

Proposition 12.11
.

Proposition
9.7
is true, if the total number of cyclic clusters
\mathfrak{R}\geq(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho
.

Proof.

In this proof we may assume that
|H|\leq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
, and restrict that
\mathbb{M}
contains at most
(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
disjoint strongly degenerate primitive pairs (by inserting the indicator function
1-\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{str.deg.prm}}
opposite to the one in Proposition
12.5
). In fact, the case when
|H|\geq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
follows from Proposition
12.3
, and the case of many strongly degenerate primitive pairs follows from Proposition
12.5
. Recall our goal is to prove (
12.11
) (see Reduction
1
), which we repeat here as

(12.14)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}-d\cdot\#_{\{4\}}\geq(C_{11}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho,

where we also treat all {33A} molecules as good.

In the proof, we fix a layer
\ell_{1}
such that
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}\gtrsim\mathfrak{R}\gtrsim\rho
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}\gg\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell^{\prime}>\ell_{1}
(see (
12.15
) below), then the number of cyclic cluster in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
is at least
\geq\Gamma^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}\gtrsim\rho
as each cyclic cluster contributes at most
\Gamma
to
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. We could exploit the
\gtrsim\rho
cyclic clusters in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
using Proposition
12.2
(
4
),
except
when these cyclic clusters belong to many different components in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
. In the latter case, we start from
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
and keep going up to include more layers, until these cyclic clusters are almost connected to each other (in the sense that the number of components containing them becomes negligible). Here we need to perform layer refining for layers
>\ell_{1}
the number of deleted atoms being bounded by
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for
\ell^{\prime}>\ell_{1}
and thus negligible), so that the new thin layer we include at each time is a forest. Then, at the layer of transition, the relevant molecule will be a union
\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
, where
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is the union of thin layers before the transition, and
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is the newly included thin layer (which is a forest). We are then in the situation of Proposition
12.10
, so applying it leads to an acceptable upper bound on
\#_{\{4\}}
and lower bound on
\#_{\{33\}}
.

To start, we hoose a maximal
\ell_{1}\in[1:\ell]
(which always exists because
\mathfrak{R}\geq(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho
), such that

(12.15)

\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}>(C_{6}^{*})^{-\ell_{1}}\cdot(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho;\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}\leq(C_{6}^{*})^{-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho<(C_{6}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}},\quad\forall\ell_{1}<\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell.

Note that, since each cyclic cluster
W\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
satisfies
\rho(W)\leq\Gamma
and thus contribute at most
\Gamma
to
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}})
, and joining the clusters by O-atoms does not create new independent cycles (and thus does not increase
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}})
), we know that the number of cyclic clusters in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
is at least
\Gamma^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}
.

Consider all the components of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
(i.e. layer
\ell_{1}
) that contain at least one cyclic cluster, let them be
\mathbb{X}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq N)
where
N
is the total number of such components, and let their union be
\mathbb{X}
. If
N<(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}
(which includes when
\ell_{1}=\ell
, because in this case
N\leq|H|
by repeating the (PL) argument in the proof of Proposition
12.3
, and note also that
C_{12}^{*}\gg C_{10}^{*}
), then we

(1)

Cut
\mathbb{X}
as free, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{>\ell_{1}}
as free from
\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{X}
and cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
;

(2)

Then cut
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell_{1}}\cup(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}\backslash\mathbb{X})
into elementary molecules using
DOWN
.

It is easy to see that this can always be done, and the contribution of these steps to
\#_{\{4\}}
is at most
2|H|
by repeating the (PL) argument as above (this allows us to bound the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{>\ell_{1}}
; moreover, the number of
full
components of
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell_{1}}\cup(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}\backslash\mathbb{X})
after cutting
X
and
\mathbb{M}_{>\ell_{1}}
as free does not exceed the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
before cutting, cf. the proof of Proposition
12.5
). Then, to cut
\mathbb{X}
into elementary molecules, we argue as
Choice 2
in the proof of Proposition
12.5
; however instead of the
\mathbb{Q}_{j}
in the proof of Proposition
12.5
, we consider all the clusters forming each component of
\mathbb{X}
. The same proof of Proposition
12.5
implies that each cyclic cluster provides one good molecule, so for the contribution of the cuttings involving
\mathbb{X}
we have
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq\Gamma^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}
and
\#_{\{4\}}\leq N
, which easily implies (
12.14
), in view of
N<(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}
, and (
12.15
) and that
|H|\leq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho
.

Now assume
N\geq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}
(in particular we must have
\ell_{1}<\ell
). We then perform layer refining (Definition
12.8
) for each layer
\ell_{1}<\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
. After that, for each thin layer
\zeta\geq(\ell_{1},0)
(where we identify the unrefined layer
\ell_{1}
with
(\ell_{1},0)
), define

(12.16)

N_{\zeta}:=\textrm{number of components of $\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta]}^{T}$ that contains some $\mathbb{X}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq N)$}.

Note that for
\zeta=(\ell_{1},0)
we have
N_{\zeta}=N
, and for
\zeta
being the highest thin layer of
\mathbb{M}
, we have

(12.17)

N_{\zeta}\leq|H|+(2\Gamma+4)\sum_{\ell^{\prime}>\ell_{1}}\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}<C_{2}^{*}(C_{6}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}};

this is proved by repeating the (PL) argument in the proof of Proposition
12.3
but using Proposition
12.9
(
3
), (
6
) and (
7
). In fact, start from any component
Z
in
\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta]}^{T}
; by dividing each layer
\ell^{\prime}>\ell_{1}
into thin layers and using Proposition
12.9
(
3
), with at most
(\Gamma+1)\sum_{\ell^{\prime}>\ell_{1}}\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
exceptions, we may assume
Z
contains a component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},0)}^{T}
for some
\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell_{1}
(note that the layer
\ell_{1}
or
(\ell_{1},0)
is unrefined). Then by Proposition
12.9
(
6
) and Proposition
10.4
(
5
), this
Z
must intersect a particle line
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})\subseteq p(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1})
. By Proposition
12.9
(
7
), with at most
2\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}
exceptions, this
{\bm{p}}
must either intersect a component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime}+1),0}^{T}
(in which case this component must be contained in
Z
, as
{\bm{p}}
can intersect at most one component of
\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta]}^{T}
, see Remark
10.5
), or intersect a component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime}+1,k)}^{T}\,(k\geq 1)
(in which case this component must be contained in
Z
, leading to at most
(\Gamma+1)\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}
choices for
Z
due to Proposition
12.9
(
3
)), or belong to
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1})
. In any case, by excluding the
2\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}+(\Gamma+1)\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}
exceptions, this
Z
must intersect a particle line
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}+1})
(if
Z
contains a component of
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime}+1),0}^{T}
, then apply Proposition
12.9
(
6
) again). Repeating this process until reaching
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=H
(at which time
Z
will intersect one ofthe
|H|
particle lines, leading to at most
|H|
choices) will then lead to at most

|H|+(\Gamma+1)\sum_{\ell^{\prime}>\ell_{1}}\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell_{1}}\big(2\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}+(\Gamma+1)\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}\big)

choices for
Z
, which proves (
12.17
).

Now, using (
12.17
), we see that there exists a (maximal) thin layer
\zeta\geq(\ell_{1},0)
, which is not the highest thin layer of
\mathbb{M}
, such that

(12.18)

N_{\zeta}\geq(C_{5}^{*})^{n((\ell_{1},0))-n(\zeta)-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}};\qquad N_{\zeta^{\prime}}<(C_{5}^{*})^{n((\ell_{1},0))-n(\zeta^{\prime})-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}\leq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot N_{\zeta},\quad\forall\zeta^{\prime}>\zeta,

where
n(\zeta)
is the number of thin layers below or equal to
\zeta
; see
Figure
50
. Let
\zeta^{+}
be the lowest thin layer above
\zeta
, then define
\mathbb{Y}
to be the union of all components of
\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta^{+}]}^{T}
that contains some
\mathbb{X}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq N)
, and write it as
\mathbb{Y}=\mathbb{Y}_{U}\cup\mathbb{Y}_{D}
, where
\mathbb{Y}_{U}=\mathbb{Y}\cap\mathbb{M}_{\zeta^{+}}^{T}
and
\mathbb{Y}_{D}=\mathbb{Y}\cap\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta]}^{T}
. It is easy to verify that the assumptions of Proposition
12.10
are satisfied, and that

(12.19)

\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{Y})}\leq N_{\zeta^{+}},\qquad\#_{\mathrm{cyc.comp}(\mathbb{Y}_{D})}\geq N_{\zeta}

(note that each
\mathbb{X}_{j}
already contains a cycle, and so does any component that contains some
\mathbb{X}_{j}
; therefore each of the
N_{\zeta}
components of
\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta]}^{T}
containing some
\mathbb{X}_{j}
will contribute to
\#_{\mathrm{cyc.comp}(\mathbb{Y}_{D})}
).

Figure 50.
The proof of Proposition
12.11
, where we start from the cyclic components of the unrefined layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
, and keep going up (adding one thin layer each time) until the first thin layer after which these components are connected together (with a negligible number of resulting components if we account for all thin layers). Define
\mathbb{Y}=\mathbb{Y}_{U}\cup\mathbb{Y}_{D}
as in the figure, then we can apply Proposition
12.10
to get many {33} molecules.

Then, as above, we

(1)

Cut
\mathbb{Y}
as free, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta^{+}}^{T}
as free from
\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{Y}
and subsequently cut it using
UP
;

(2)

Then cut
\mathbb{M}_{<(\ell_{1},0)}^{T}\cup(\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta^{+}]}^{T}\backslash\mathbb{Y})
into elementary molecules using
DOWN
.

It is easy to see that this can always be done (as there is no bond between
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{M}_{[(\ell_{1},0):\zeta^{+}]}^{T}\backslash\mathbb{Y}
), and the contribution of these steps to
\#_{\{4\}}
is at most

(12.20)

2|H|+8(\Gamma+1)\sum_{\ell^{\prime}>\ell_{1}}\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}<C_{2}^{*}(C_{6}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}};

this follows from the upper bounds on the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta^{+}}^{T}
and
\mathbb{M}
, which is proved by repeating the above (PL) arguments (as in the proof of (
12.17
)) which uses Proposition
12.9
(
3
), (
6
) and (
7
).
Finally we cut
\mathbb{Y}
into elementary molecules using
UP
, and by Proposition
12.10
, for the contribution of the cuttings involving
\mathbb{Y}
, we have

(12.21)

\#_{\{33A\}}\geq N_{\zeta}/4,\qquad\#_{\{4\}}\leq N_{\zeta^{+}}.

By putting together (
12.15
), (
12.18
), (
12.20
) and (
12.21
), and recall that all {33A} molecules are treated as good (Reduction
1
), we get

(12.22)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot(\#_{\{4\}})\geq(\upsilon/8)\cdot N_{\zeta}-d\cdot(N_{\zeta^{+}}+C_{2}^{*}(C_{6}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}})\geq(\upsilon/16)\cdot N_{\zeta}\geq(C_{10}^{*})^{-2}\cdot\rho,

which proves (
12.14
). The proof is now complete.
∎

12.4.
Layer selection and weak degeneracies

From now on we will focus on proving (
12.11
) under the assumptions in Reduction
1
. The goal of this subsection is to reduce this result to Proposition
12.18
(and prove Proposition
12.17
which contains the simple cases of initial cumulant and weak degeneracies), by applying the procedure of
layer selection
(discussed in Section
11.6
).

The full layer selection process, defined in Definition
12.12
below, is essentially the same as the toy version in Definition
11.23
in Section
11
. The only difference is that we need to perform layer refining for layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\,(\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{1})
, which leads to an extra step of picking a thin layer
\zeta_{U}
within layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
, see Definition
12.12
(
2
). The rest is the same as in Definition
11.23
, except that
v_{\zeta}^{*}
in Definition
12.12
(
2
) is defined for thin layers instead of layers, and the
\zeta_{D}
we pick is also a thin layer (following the same rule as in Definition
11.23
).

Definition 12.12

(Layer selection)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule as in Proposition
10.4
. Assume that
|H|\leq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
and
\mathfrak{R}\leq(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho
as in Reduction
1
. Then we will perform the following steps to determine the values of two
thin layers

\zeta_{U}
and
\zeta_{D}
(in one case it is possible that
\zeta_{D}
is absent and we only determine
\zeta_{U}
), see
Figure
51
. Recall that
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=H
and
r(\mathbb{M}_{0})=H_{0}
, and that we denote
s_{\ell}=|r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}})|
.

Figure 51.
The full layer selection algorithm (Definition
12.12
). Compared to the toy case (Definition
11.23
,
Figure
40
), we have an extra step of layer refining and selecting
\zeta_{U}
. Here the blue particle lines are in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
, but we only care for those in
S
(i.e. those that have atoms in the thin layer
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
but not in lower thin layers within
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
).

Another difference is that there are exceptional components in
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
(the red ones, compared to the green ones in
Y
) that contain cycles, or intersect a particle line not in
S
, or intersect two particle lines in
S
. These will be cut in Definition
12.14
,
Option 1
below.

(1)

Choose
1\leq\ell_{1}\leq\ell
such that

(12.23)

s_{\ell_{1}-1}\geq(C_{7}^{*})^{-(\ell_{1}-1)}\cdot\rho;\qquad s_{\ell^{\prime}}<(C_{7}^{*})^{-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\rho\leq(C_{7}^{*})^{-1}\cdot s_{\ell_{1}-1},\quad\forall\ell_{1}\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell,

and refine layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}}
as in Definition
12.8
. Define also
s_{\ell_{1}-1}:=\rho^{\prime}
.

(2)

After refining, consider all particle lines
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
(there are
\rho^{\prime}
of them), for any
0\leq\gamma\leq\Gamma+1
, define
u_{\gamma}^{*}
to be the number of these
\rho^{\prime}
particle lines that
do not
contain any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
for any
k\leq\gamma
(denote
u_{-1}^{*}:=s_{\ell_{1}-1}
). Then choose
0\leq\gamma\leq\Gamma+1
such that

(12.24)

u_{\gamma-1}^{*}\geq(C_{6}^{*})^{-\gamma}\cdot\rho^{\prime},\quad u_{\gamma}^{*}<(C_{6}^{*})^{-\gamma-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\leq(C_{6}^{*})^{-1}\cdot u_{\gamma-1}^{*}.

Fix
the thin layer
\zeta_{U}:=(\ell_{1},\gamma)
. Define also
u_{\gamma-1}^{*}-u_{\gamma}^{*}:=\rho^{\prime\prime}
. If
\ell_{1}=1
, then we stop the process with
\zeta_{D}
absent.

(3)

If
\ell_{1}>1
, refine the layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for all
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{1}-1
, and consider all the particle lines
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
that does not contain any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
for any
k\leq\gamma-1
but does contain an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},\gamma)}^{T}
; there are
\rho^{\prime\prime}
of them, and define the set of them to be
S
. Let
\zeta_{0}
be the highest thin layer below
\zeta_{U}
. For each thin layer
\zeta\leq\zeta_{0}
, define
v_{\zeta}^{*}
to be the number of particle lines in
S
that are either
connected to a cycle
via
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta:\zeta_{0}]}^{T}
, or
are connected to another particle line

{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
via
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta:\zeta_{0}]}^{T}
(this
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
need not be in
S
). Now, if for the lowest thin layer
(1,0)
we have
v_{(1,0)}^{*}\leq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}
, then stop the process with
\zeta_{D}
absent; otherwise
choose a thin layer

\zeta=\zeta_{D}
such that

(12.25)

v_{\zeta_{D}}^{*}\geq(C_{5}^{*})^{-n(\zeta_{D})}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime},\qquad v_{\zeta}^{*}<(C_{5}^{*})^{-n(\zeta)}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}\leq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot v_{\zeta_{D}}^{*},\quad\forall\zeta_{D}<\zeta\leq\zeta_{0},

where
n(\zeta)
is the number of thin layers below or equal to
\zeta
. Define also
v_{\zeta_{D}}^{*}:=\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. In the case when
\zeta_{D}
is absent, we also define
\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}:=\rho^{\prime\prime}
.

In Proposition
12.13
below we state and prove several properties of layer selection. That this process is well-defined is proved in the same way as in Proposition
11.24
(except for the extra step in Definition
12.12
(
2
)). Additionally, this proposition contains the upper bound on the number of components of various subsets of
\mathbb{M}
, which will be used to bound
\#_{\{4\}}
for the cutting procedures defined in Definition
12.14
, see Proposition
12.15
(
1
); most important among them is the set
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}\backslash Y
, which precisely contains the
exceptional components
as in Definition
11.23
. The proof of these upper bounds follows from repeating the post-refining (PL) argument using Proposition
12.9
, similar to the proof of (
12.17
) in Proposition
12.11
.

Proposition 12.13
.

The construction process in Definition
12.12
is well-defined. Moreover, in Definition
12.12
, consider all the connected components of
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
(or
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}^{T}
if
\zeta_{D}
is absent) that (i) intersect exactly one particle line in
S
(as defined in Definition
12.12
(
3
)), and (ii) does not intersect any other particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
, and (iii) does not contain a cycle. Denote the union of these components by
Y
. Then, each of the following quantities is bounded by
10(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
:

(1)

The number of components of
\mathbb{M}
,
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{U}}^{T}
,
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{D}}^{T}
,
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
and
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
;

(2)

The number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
that are not connected to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
by a bond.

(3)

The number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}\backslash Y
;

Proof.

Recall from (
7.1
) that
\rho=\mathfrak{R}+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\geq 0}s_{\ell^{\prime}}
; since
\mathfrak{R}\leq(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho
, there exists at least one
\ell^{\prime}
(including
0
) such that
s_{\ell^{\prime}}\geq(C_{7}^{*})^{-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\rho
. Using also that
s_{\ell}=|H|\leq(C_{12}^{*})^{-1}\rho
, we must have
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
, and may then define the largest such
\ell^{\prime}
to be
\ell_{1}-1
in in Definition
12.12
(
1
), so (
12.23
) holds.

Next, consider
u_{\gamma}^{*}
defined in Definition
12.12
(
2
); by Proposition
12.9
(
7
) we know the number of particle lines in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
that do not contain any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
for any
k
, is bounded by

u_{\Gamma+1}^{*}\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{\ell_{1}}+s_{\ell_{1}}<\big(2(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}(C_{7}^{*})^{\mathfrak{L}}+(C_{7}^{*})^{-1}\big)\cdot\rho^{\prime}<(C_{6}^{*})^{-10\Gamma}\cdot\rho^{\prime}

using (
12.23
). Since also
u_{-1}^{*}=\rho^{\prime}
, we know that there exists a largest value
\gamma-1
satisfying
u_{\gamma-1}^{*}\geq(C_{6}^{*})^{-\gamma}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
in Definition
12.12
(
2
), so (
12.24
) holds.

Next, if
\ell_{1}>1
, consider
v_{\zeta}^{*}
defined in Definition
12.12
(
3
). Let
\zeta_{1}
be the highest thin layer within
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1}
. For the lowest thin layer above
\zeta_{1}
(which is
(\ell_{1},0)
) we obviously have
v_{(\ell_{1},0)}^{*}=0
(by definition of
S
, these particle lines do not intersect any thin layer between
(\ell_{1},0)
and
\zeta_{0}
); now if
v_{(1,0)}^{*}\geq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}
, we know that there exists a largest value
\zeta_{D}
satisfying
v_{\zeta_{D}}^{*}\geq(C_{5}^{*})^{-n(\zeta_{D})}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}
in Definition
12.12
(
3
), so (
12.25
) holds. This shows that the thin layers
\zeta_{U}
and
\zeta_{D}
are well defined and
\zeta_{D}\leq\zeta_{1}
.

Now we bound the number of components as stated in (
1
)–(
3
). We will consider the case where
\zeta_{D}
exists; the case where
\zeta_{D}
is absent only requires trivial modifications. In fact, by repeating the (PL) argument using Proposition
12.9
(
3
), (
6
) and (
7
) as in the proof of (
12.17
) in Proposition
12.11
, we can bound the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
,
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{U}}^{T}
and
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{D}}^{T}
by
|H|+(2\Gamma+4)\mathfrak{R}\leq 2(C_{10}^{*})^{-1}\rho
. The number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
(or of any union of thin layers within layer
\ell_{1}
) is also bounded by
s_{\ell_{1}}+(\Gamma+1)\mathfrak{R}\leq 2(C_{7}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
by Proposition
12.9
(
3
)–(
4
) and Proposition
10.4
(
7
). This establishes the first four estimates in (
1
).

In addition, consider each component
X
of
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
or
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
or
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
. If it intersects some thin layer
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
, then the number of such components is bounded by
s_{\ell_{1}}+(\Gamma+1)\mathfrak{R}\leq 2(C_{7}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
as above. If not, it must intersect some thin layer
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell^{\prime},k)}^{T}
for
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{1}-1
, which allows us to repeat the (PL) argument using Proposition
12.9
(
3
), (
6
) and (
7
). In summary we see that, with at most
4(C_{7}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
exceptions, each component
X
must intersect a particle line
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
. Next, with at most
u_{\gamma}^{*}<2(C_{6}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}
exceptions (and the total number of exceptions
\ll(C_{5}^{*})^{-2}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
), we may assume this
{\bm{p}}
intersects
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
for some
k\leq\gamma
. Now, if we are considering
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
or
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
which includes all thin layers
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}\,(k\leq\gamma)
, then we know that the component
X
must intersect some
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}\,(k\leq\gamma)
, which leads to
\leq 2(C_{7}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
choices for
X
(and
\ll(C_{5}^{*})^{-2}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
accounting for all the above exceptions, recall again Remark
10.5
that each
\rho
intersects at most one
X
). This establishes the last two estimates in (
1
). Moreover, (
2
) follows because any component of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
that is not connected to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
by a bond is also a component of
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
.

Finally consider (
3
). As above, with
\ll(C_{5}^{*})^{-2}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
exceptions, for each component
Z
of
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
, it must intersect a particle line
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
that intersects
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
for some
k\leq\gamma
. We may assume
{\bm{p}}
does not intersect
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
for
k<\gamma
(otherwise
Z
must intersect
\mathbb{M}_{(\ell_{1},k)}^{T}
, leading to
\leq 2(C_{7}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
possibilities for
Z
), so in particular
{\bm{p}}\in S
, which means that
Z
intersects some particle line
{\bm{p}}\in S
. Let
\eta
be the lowest thin layer above
\zeta_{D}
. By excluding at most
v_{\eta}^{*}\leq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
exceptions, we may assume that
{\bm{p}}
is not connected to a cycle within
\mathbb{M}_{[\eta:\zeta_{0}]}^{T}
, and is not connected to another particle line
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
via
\mathbb{M}_{[\eta:\zeta_{0}]}^{T}
, but this means that
Z\subseteq Y
by definition of
Y
. Putting all these together, we see that the number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
that are not contained in
Y
is at most
2(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, as desired.
∎

Now, once the thin layers
\zeta_{U}
and
\zeta_{D}
are chosen by Definition
12.12
, we need to cut as free all the exceptional components in
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}\backslash Y
in Definition
11.23
(discussed Section
11.7.4
(b)). Together with a few auxiliary operations treating other layers (and cutting components of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
that have no connections to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup Y
which is purely for convenience), this forms the
Option 1
in Definition
12.14
below, which is the main option to be executed in the rest of the proof. On the other hand, the
Option 2
in Definition
12.14
involves cutting
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
first, which guarantees that all relevant atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
belong to {3} or {4} molecules, and will be used in the
weakly degenerate
case (Definition
12.16
, Proposition
12.17
).

Definition 12.14
.

Recall the layer selection process in Definition
12.12
and the definition of
Y
in Proposition
12.13
. Define also the set
\mathbb{V}
to be the union of all components of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
that are connected to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup Y
by a bond, and let
G
be the number of those components of
\mathbb{V}
that are connected to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
by at least two bonds. Below we define two options of cutting sequences to be applied to
\mathbb{M}
.

•

Option 1
. We perform the following cutting operations (note that if
\zeta_{D}
is absent, then the steps here involving
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
and
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{D}}^{T}
will be skipped); see
Figure
52
.

Figure 52.
The result of
Option 1
in Definition
12.14
, starting from
Figure
51
. We cut as free all exceptional components in
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}\backslash Y
(red components in
Figure
51
), so we are only left with
Y
(green components). We also subsequently cut any atom that becomes deg 2 in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
or
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
(the ones marked by green star in
Figure
51
).

In the end
\mathbb{M}_{U}
becomes a forest, while
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is still a forest. The number of {4} molecules created is bounded using Proposition
12.13
; in addition, this will create fixed ends in both
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, but only
bottom
fixed ends in the former and only
top
fixed ends in the latter, which is all we need.

(1)

We cut
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U}]}^{T}
as free, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{U}}^{T}
as free from
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{D}}^{T}
and cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{D}}^{T}
into elementary molecules using
DOWN
.

(2)

We cut as free all the components of
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
(or
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}^{T}
if
\zeta_{D}
is absent) not in
Y
, and cut each of them into elementary molecules using
UP
. If any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
or
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
becomes deg 2 then we cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
.

(3)

We cut as free all components of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
that are not connected to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup Y
by a bond, and cut each of them into elementary molecules using
DOWN
.

After the cutting sequence in
Option 1
, define the remaining molecule to be
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
(or
\mathbb{M}_{U}
if
\zeta_{D}
is absent); it contains parts of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
,
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
and
Y
and will be studied in Section
13
.

•

Option 2
. This cutting sequence applies only to the case where
\zeta_{D}
exists, and cuts
\mathbb{M}
completely into elementary molecules. We perform the following cutting operations, see
Figure
53
.

Figure 53.
Illustration of
Option 2
(and
Option 1
) in Definition
12.14
. Among the connected components of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
, components
4\sim 5

do not belong to
\mathbb{V}
and will be ignored in either option; components
1\sim 3
belong to
\mathbb{V}
, but only
1\sim 2
count in the
G
components (i.e.
G=2
). By Definition
12.14
, in
Option 2
, components
1\sim 2
are cut as free, and
3
is cut as fixed. In
Option 1
, component
3
will be included in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and
1\sim 2
remain in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

(1)

With
\mathbb{V}
defined as above, we cut
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}\cup\mathbb{V}
as free, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{U}}^{T}
as free from
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{D}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{>\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup(\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}\backslash\mathbb{V})
and cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{D}}^{T}\cup(\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}\backslash\mathbb{V})
into elementary molecules using
DOWN
.

(2)

If a component of
\mathbb{V}
has only one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
, then cut it as fixed, otherwise cut it as free. Then cut each component into elementary molecules using
DOWN
, and then cut
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
into elementary molecules using
UP
.

In Proposition
12.15
we summarize a few properties of the molecule after the cutting process in Definition
12.14
. Parts (
1
) and (
2
) of Proposition
12.15
correspond to the initial cumulant/initial link case (discussed in Section
11.7.4
(a)) and weakly degenerate case (where we apply
Option 2
), which are easy to deal with. The main part of Proposition
12.15
is (
3
), in which case
Option 1
reduces
\mathbb{M}
to the 2-layer model
\mathbb{M}_{UD}:=\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
as in Proposition
11.24
, but with possible fixed ends as discussed in Section
11.7.4
(b). For precise statements for properties of
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
, see Proposition
12.15
(
3
); the proof is essentially the same as Proposition
11.24
, except the complications due to the set
S
etc. coming from the extra step of picking
\zeta_{U}
.

Proposition 12.15
.

Recall
G
defined in Definition
12.14
. For
Options 1 and 2
in Definition
12.14
, we have the followings.

(1)

The contribution of cuttings in
Option 1
to
\#_{\{4\}}
is at most
100(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. If
\zeta_{D}
is absent, then see
Figure
54
, after
Option 1
, we can choose at least
(\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/2)
particle lines
{\bm{p}}\in S
such that

(a)

there exists a subset
T({\bm{p}})
of
\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}=Y
which is either empty or a component of
Y
;

(b)

if
T({\bm{p}})\neq\varnothing
then it is a tree and has only one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
, which is along
{\bm{p}}
;

(c)

consider the set
\mathcal{E}_{0}
of all bottom free ends
e
at atoms in
T({\bm{p}})
(or along
{\bm{p}}
if
T({\bm{p}})=\varnothing
) for all the chosen
{\bm{p}}
, then for at least
\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/4
chosen particle lines
{\bm{p}}
, there exist
e\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
at an atom in
T({\bm{p}})
or along
{\bm{p}}
, such that an initial link exists between
e
and
e^{\prime}
, where
e^{\prime}\neq e
either belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
or has an atom in some component already cut in
Option 1
.

Figure 54.
Proposition
12.15
(
1
). Here
{\bm{p}}_{j}\in S
are the particle lines in Proposition
12.15
(
1
) colored in red, with the corresponding set
T({\bm{p}}_{j})
and initial links.

(2)

If
\zeta_{D}
exists, then after
Option 2
,
\mathbb{M}
is cut into elementary molecules with
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
. We also have
\#_{\{4\}}\leq 100(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}+G
(note here
G
equals the number of components of
\mathbb{V}
that are cut as free in
Option 2
(ii)). Moreover, each atom in
\mathbb{V}
belongs to a {3} or {4} molecule.

(3)

If
\zeta_{D}
exists, then after
Option 1
, the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
consists of parts of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
, parts of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
, and
Y
. Define
\mathbb{M}_{U}
to be the union of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
and
Y
, and all the components in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
that are full (after
Option 1
) and have exactly one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup Y
. Let also
\mathbb{M}_{D}:=\mathbb{M}_{UD}\backslash\mathbb{M}_{U}
(which is a subset of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
). Then we have the following properties for
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
:

(a)

Each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms. No atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a parent of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{D}
) has no C-top (resp. C-bottom) fixed end.

(b)

The number of full (i.e. all deg 4) components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is at most
10(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. The number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is
\geq G
. Each component in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
either has at
\geq 2
bonds connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, or has one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and one deg 3 atom.

(c)

Define the set of
UD connections
to be the set of bonds connecting an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. We say two UD connections are
connected via

\mathbb{M}_{D}
, if the
\mathbb{M}_{D}
endpoints of them belong to the same connected component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Then, there exist at least
(\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/4)
many UD connections, such that each UD connection is either connected to another UD connection via
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, or has its
\mathbb{M}_{D}
endpoint belonging to component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contains a deg 3 atom.

Proof.

We first prove the easier statements, and leave the proof of the hardest (
3c
) to the end.

Proof of (
1
).
The statement about
\#_{\{4\}}
follow from Proposition
12.13
(
1
)–(
3
) and Proposition
12.2
(
2
) (and Remark
10.6
about full components of
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{D}}^{T}
after cutting everything else as free). Now suppose
\zeta_{D}
is absent (so
v_{(1,0)}^{*}\leq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}
in Definition
12.12
(
3
); the case
\ell_{1}=1
is similar, in which case each
T({\bm{p}})
will be empty). Note that
|S|=\rho^{\prime\prime}=\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
by Definition
12.12
. Choose all the particle lines
{\bm{p}}\in S
such that
{\bm{p}}
either intersects a component of
Y
or does not intersect
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}^{T}
at all, then all particle lines
{\bm{p}}\in S
are chosen with at most
(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
exceptions, by Definition
12.12
(
3
) and
v_{(1,0)}^{*}\leq(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}
.

For each chosen
{\bm{p}}
, set
T({\bm{p}})
to be the component of
Y
that intersects
{\bm{p}}
(or
\varnothing
if
{\bm{p}}
does not intersect
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}^{T}
). Next, by Proposition
12.9
(
8
), we know that with at most
(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
exceptions, each chosen
{\bm{p}}
must form an initial link within
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}
. For each such
{\bm{p}}
, define the corresponding bottom free end
e
by the initial link condition; we then claim that these choices satisfy the requirements (
1a
)–(
1c
).

To prove this, note that (
1a
) and (
1b
) follow from properties of
Y
. As for (
1c
), if
{\bm{p}}
forms an initial link with a particle line
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
(this
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
must belong to
H_{0}=r(\mathbb{M}_{0})
), such that the corresponding free end
e^{\prime}
does not belong to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and does not have an atom in a component cut in
Option 1
, then
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
must not intersect
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
after layer refining (indeed, if
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
intersects a component of
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}^{T}\backslash Y
then it contains an atom in a component that is cut; if
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
intersects a component of
Y
, or does not intersect
\mathbb{M}_{<\zeta_{U}}^{T}
and intersects
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
, then
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
must belong to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
). This means that this
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
either contains a deleted O-atom or belong to
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
(by applying Proposition
10.4
(
7
)), which leads to at most
(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
choices for
({\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime})
(in the former case using the upper bound of the number of deleted atoms, and in the latter case using Definition
12.12
(
2
)), and is thus negligible. In summary, at least
\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/2
particle lines
{\bm{p}}\in S
must be chosen, and at least
\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/4
chosen
{\bm{p}}
must satisfy the given restrictions.

Proof of (
2
).
The bound on
\#_{\{4\}}
follows from the above proof, with one extra {4} molecule for each of the
G
components of
\mathbb{V}
cut as free; note that each component cut as fixed will not be full, so they do not contribute {4} molecules. Each atom in
\mathbb{V}
belongs to a {3} or {4} molecule, simply because
\mathbb{V}\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
which is a forest, and each component of
\mathbb{V}
cut as fixed will have exactly one deg 3 atom, so there will not be any {2} or {33} molecule by Proposition

(
1
).

Proof of (
3a
) and (
3b
).
First, each of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
is a forest and does not have deg 2 atoms, by layer refining and the operations of cutting deg 2 atoms in
Option 1
(ii). Moreover
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
) does not have any C-top (resp. C-bottom) fixed end, because cutting as free any deg 2 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
) does not generate any C-top (resp. C-bottom) fixed end in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
) if there is none initially (of course, cutting any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
also does not create any C-top fixed end in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
or C-bottom fixed end in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
). The fact that no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
can be parent of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
is also clear from properties of the thin layers.

All the above properties are also preserved after adding
Y
to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
, and adding to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup Y
a full component in
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
that has only one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}\cup Y
. For example, the forest property is preserved because each component of
Y
is a tree, and each component of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
is also a tree, and each component of
Y
has exactly one bond
15
15

15

This is because, each component of

contains an atom in a particle line in
S
(this atom must belong to some layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\,(\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell_{1}-1)
by definition of

). If it is connected to

; by Proposition

(

, this particle line (and this bond) must be unique.
connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
; the other properties are easy to prove, where we also note that

, or any subsequent deg 2 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
or

, does not create a fixed end in

.

Next, the number of full components of
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
is at most

by Proposition

(

), and this cannot increase after cutting

Finally we prove the number of components of

after

To prove the above claim, note that

has a bond

and remains as a UD connection (see the proof of (

(or a fixed end in each newly created component of

Proof of (
3c
)
. For
{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
and
\zeta_{D}\leq\zeta\leq\zeta_{0}
(recall
\zeta_{0}
is the highest thin layer below
\zeta_{U}
), define
{\bm{p}}\sim_{\zeta}{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
if the particle lines
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
are connected via
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta:\zeta_{0}]}^{T}
. It is easy to see that
\sim_{\zeta}
is an equivalence relation, which again follows from Remark
10.5
.

Recall the definition of
S
in Definition
12.12
(
3

to be the set of
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
that do not contain any atom in

for

. Define

to be the set of
{\bm{p}}\in S
that is either connected to a cycle within
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{0}]}^{T}
or satisfies
{\bm{p}}\sim_{\zeta_{D}}{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
for some other
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
, but does not have the same property if
\zeta_{D}
is replaced by any
\zeta>\zeta_{D}
. By (

) and (

), we have
|S_{1}|<(10C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
|S_{2}|\geq(0.9)\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\varnothing
. Then define
S_{3}
same as

, but with the extra restriction
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\not\in S_{1}
in the relation
{\bm{p}}\sim_{\zeta_{D}}{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
above; since
\sim_{\zeta_{D}}
is an equivalence relation, we know that
|S_{3}|\geq|S_{2}|-|S_{1}|\geq(0.8)\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
(indeed, if

for some

, then each
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
would lead to a unique
{\bm{p}}
, unless two

are

with the same
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, in which case these to
{\bm{p}}
are

with each other and they will belong to

).

Now we claim that after
Option 1
, for each
{\bm{p}}\in S_{3}
, there exists a UD connection

in
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
such that (i) it is either connected to some other UD connection

{\bm{p}}
, or its

endpoint belongs to a connected component of

intersecting
{\bm{p}}
. Once this is proved, is follows from (ii) that the UD connection obtained for different

must be different (because each
Y
intersects a unique

), so the desired result (
3c
) follows from (i).

To prove the above claim, first assume there exists
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\neq{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\not\in S_{1}
such that
{\bm{p}}\sim_{\zeta_{D}}{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
. Since
{\bm{p}}\in S_{3}\subseteq S_{2}\subseteq S
and
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\not\in S_{1}
, we know that

intersects
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
, and either does not intersect

, or intersects a unique component

contained in

; moreover
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\not\in S_{1}
must either intersect
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
, or intersect a unique component

of
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
. Since
{\bm{p}}\sim_{\zeta_{D}}{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
, we consider a path going from an atom on
{\bm{p}}
to an atom on
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
within
\mathbb{M}_{[\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{0}]}^{T}
. This path must enter
\zeta_{D}
because
{\bm{p}}\not\sim_{\zeta}{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
for the layer
\zeta>\zeta_{D}
; moreover, it must then exit
\zeta_{D}
because in the end it reaches
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
which contains an atom in either
\mathbb{M}_{(\zeta_{D}:\zeta_{U})}^{T}
or
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{U}}^{T}
.

Consider the first entry and first exit to
\mathbb{M}_{\zeta_{D}}^{T}
, then we can find two bonds

and

before

Option 1
, (ii) either

{\bm{p}}
or the other endpoint of

{\bm{p}}^{\prime}

Figure 55.
Proof of Proposition

(3c). Here the two particle lines

Finally, if the above
{\bm{p}}^{\prime}
does not exist, then

and note that

∎

Now we can prove the next case of Proposition
9.7
, namely Proposition

Definition 12.16
.

Define the atom pair
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
to be
weakly degenerate
if they are ov-adjacent C-atoms, and we have the restriction (by indicator functions)
|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
. Define also one atom
\mathfrak{n}
to be weakly degenerate if it is C-atom and we have the restriction
|v_{e}-v_{e^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
for two distinct edges
(e,e^{\prime})
at
\mathfrak{n}
.

Proposition 12.17
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
satisfies the assumptions in Reduction
1
, and we apply the layer selection process in Definition

is absent.

12.16

(by inserting an indicator function

Proof.

Recall our goal is to prove (
12.11
), see Reduction
1
.

Proof part 1.

\zeta_{D}

(

, and

(

As such, we know that with at most

defined above must belong to either a {33A} molecule (which can be treated as good, see Reduction
1
) or a {3} molecule
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathfrak{m}\}
. Moreover, if

\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/4

in Proposition

(

good molecules (each bottom end may be involved in two initial links). This then leads to

\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/8,\qquad\#_{\{4\}}\leq 200(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime};\qquad\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq(C_{7}^{*})^{-10\mathfrak{L}}\cdot\rho

by the above arguments and Proposition

(

).

Proof part 2.
Now assume
\zeta_{D}
exists, and
G\leq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and there exist at least
\mathfrak{B}
weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs in
\mathbb{V}
. By splitting into
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\mathfrak{B}}
sub-cases (by decomposing 1 into the corresponding indicator functions), we may fix one choice of these atoms and pairs. Then we apply

of Definition

and cut

into elementary molecules. Clearly

, and

\#_{\{4\}}\leq 100(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}+G\leq 2(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}

. In either case, the

which implies (

∎

Now, summarizing Propositions

,

,

and

, we may assume that the assumptions in Reduction

in Definition

We then apply
Option 1
in Definition

and reduce to the molecule

that satisfies (3a)–(3c) of Proposition

satisfies

by (3b) of Proposition

of

), we see that Proposition

(i.e. (

)) now follows from the following

Proposition 12.18
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
be a molecule satisfying the properties (3a)–(3c) in Proposition
12.15
with some
\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq(C_{8}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho
. Moreover assume that either the number of components of

satisfies

, or (we can restrict by some indicator function such that) the number of weakly degenerate atoms and pairs in

into elementary molecules such that

and each {33B} molecule is good, and moreover

(12.26)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot(\#_{\{4\}})\geq(C_{4}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

where we again understand that all {33A} molecules are good as in Reduction
1
.

13.
Cutting algorithm II: analyzing UD molecules

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
12.18
for the two-layer molecule
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
, by introducing the various algorithms corresponding to the toy models in Section
11
. To simplify notation in this section, if a molecule
\mathbb{M}_{UD}=\mathbb{M}_{U}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
satisfies that (a) each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms, and (b) no atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is parent of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, then we say it is a
UD molecule
. If in addition
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has no C-top fixed end and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no C-bottom fixed end, then we say
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
is
canonical
.

Reduction 2
.

Our proof below relies on the properties (3a)–(3c) in Proposition
12.15
. For convenience we record them here:

(1)

Each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms. No atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a parent of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{D}
) has no C-top (resp. C-bottom) fixed end.

(2)

The number of full components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is at most
10(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Each component in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
either has at least two bonds connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, or has one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and one deg 3 atom.

(3)

There exist at least
(\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/4)
many UD connections, such that each UD connection is either connected to another UD connection via
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, or has its
\mathbb{M}_{D}
endpoint belonging to component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contains a deg 3 atom.

13.1.
The
2CONNUP
algorithm and toy model III

We start by introducing the full
2CONNUP
algorithm corresponding to toy model III. Compared to the toy version (Definition
11.21
), the main extra ingredient is Definition
13.1
(
1
); see the proof of Proposition
13.2
for more explanations.

Definition 13.1

(The algorithm
2CONNUP
)
.

Define the following cutting algorithm
2CONNUP
. It takes as input any canonical UD molecule
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
, such that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no full component that has no bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. Define
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}
to be the set of atoms
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
that have deg 3 and are ov-adjacent to one atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
, such that no other
\mathbb{M}_{D}
atom on the ov-segment between
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3. Define
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
to be the set of atoms
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
that are ov-adjacent to two atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
along two different particle lines, and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}\cup\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
. For any such
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{UD}
, we define the cutting sequence as follows:

(1)

If any (maximal) ov-segment contains at least
A:=32\upsilon^{-2}
atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
, then we choose
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
to be any one of these atoms, and cut it as free. Repeat until no such ov-segment exists.

(2)

If
\mathbb{M}
contains any deg
2
atom
\mathfrak{n}
, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg
2
atom left.

(3)

Choose a lowest deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
among all remaining deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(or a lowest atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
if
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has no deg 3 atom). Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
; this
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
will be fixed until the end of (
4
).

(4)

Now, starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, each time choose a highest atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3, and
\mathfrak{m}
is ov-adjacent to an atom
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}
that also has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{p}\}
as free. Otherwise just cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. Repeat until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
has been cut. Then go to (
2
).

(5)

Repeat (
2
)–(
4
) until all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
has been cut. Then apply algorithm
DOWN
to cut the rest of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into elementary molecules.

We also define the dual algorithm
2CONNDN
, by reversing the roles of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
and the notions of parent/child etc., just as how we define
DOWN
from
UP
in Definition
12.1
.

The key estimate for the algorithm
2CONNUP
is stated in the following proposition, which is the full version of Proposition
11.22
in Section
11
.

Proposition 13.2
.

For the algorithm
2CONNUP
, we have
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
, and

(13.1)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\{33A\}}-d\cdot\#_{\{4\}}\geq C^{-1}(|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}|+|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|)-C\cdot\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})},

where
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
is the number of
full components
of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(note the difference with
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
, i.e. the number of
all components
). Recall also the notion of constant
C
in Definition
1.6
(
2
). Similar results hold for the dual algorithm
2CONNDN
, if we reverse the roles of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Proof.

We first explain the relevance of Definition
13.1
(
1
), see
Figure
56
. Note that, in Definition
11.21
where there is no O-atom, each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
is
adjacent
to two atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, so there cannot be a
bond
containing two atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
; however, now an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
is only
ov-adjacent
to two atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, so there might be other atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
within the ov-segment between it and atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
.

Figure 56.
Illustration of Definition
13.1
(
1
). Here
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j}^{\prime})
are the ov-segments in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
connecting an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. They intersect at different
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
atoms; see also the auxiliary graph
G
on the right (G-edges correspond to intersections of these ov-segments in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
). Now
\lambda_{0}
contains many atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
(i.e. has large degree in
G
), so we choose one atom (the one with
\lambda_{3}
here) and cut it, breaking both
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{3}
but not the others. The point is that (i) the number of such operations is negligible, (ii) each operation breaks two ov-segments, so the total number of ov-segments broken is also negligible, and (iii) after Definition
13.1
(
1
), each ov-segment contains at most
O(1)
atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
, so the same algorithm
2CONNUP
will suffice. See Part 1 of the proof below.

If we still argue naively as in Definition
11.21
, it might happen that we break an ov-segment containing many atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
, turning them into deg 2 simultaneously. This is undesirable, as we expect each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
to contribute a {33A} molecule in the proof of Proposition
11.22
. To resolve this, we would like that each ov-segment contains at most
O(1)
(more precisely
A=32\upsilon^{-2}
) atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
; this leads to the process in Definition
13.1
(
1
), where we cut and break any ov-segments not satisfying the above assumptions, so that the same arguments in Definition
11.21
will be sufficient after such cuttings.

On the other hand, since
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no cycle, it is easy to see that we can define an auxiliary graph
G
in Part 1 below (with G-nodes being ov-segments and G-edges being atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
that are intersections of these ov-segments) which is a forest, so the
average
number of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
atoms on all ov-segments is
less than
2
. As such, the number of ov-segments broken in Definition
13.1
(
1
) will be negligible and does not affect the subsequent proof, see the calculations in Part 2 below.

Proof part 1.
We claim that throughout the algorithm
2CONNUP
, the (MONO) property in Proposition
12.2
is preserved in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no C-bottom fixed end. In fact, the (MONO) property is proved in the same way as in Proposition
12.2
(which is also not affected by cutting the
\mathbb{M}_{D}
atoms in Definition
13.1
(
1
), as they do not create C-top fixed end in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
). Regarding
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, note that cutting atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, or atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
which has two ov-segments connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, will not introduce C-bottom fixed end in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
; as for cutting
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{p}\}
as free in Definition
13.1
(
4
), it can be viewed as first cutting
\mathfrak{m}
and then cutting
\mathfrak{p}
, and
\mathfrak{p}
must be O-atom or have two top fixed ends (cf. Remark
8.7
) when it is cut, so cutting
\mathfrak{p}
also does not introduce C-bottom fixed end in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. It then follows that we only have {2}, {3}, {4} and {33A} molecules, thus
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
. Now we only need to prove (
13.1
).

Consider the set
\mathcal{L}
of all the maximal ov-segments
\lambda
that contain at least one atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and at least one atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}
. Clearly each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{j}\,(j\in\{1,2\})
belongs to exactly
j
ov-segments in
\mathcal{L}
; we define
\lambda\sim\lambda^{\prime}
if they intersects at an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
(note that they cannot intersect at two atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
due to Proposition
10.4
(
2
)). There cannot exist
\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{q}\in\mathcal{L}
such that
\lambda_{j}\sim\lambda_{j+1}
with
\lambda_{q+1}=\lambda_{1}
(otherwise, say
\lambda_{j}
intersects
\lambda_{j+1}
at atom
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
, then the ov-segments between
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
along
\lambda_{j+1}
forms a cycle in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, contradicting that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a tree). In other words, if we define the auxiliary graph
G
with G-nodes
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}
and G-edges
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
where
\lambda\sim\lambda^{\prime}
, then this
G
is a forest, see
Figure
57
.

Figure 57.
The auxiliary graph
G
. The sets
\mathcal{L}_{0}
,
\mathcal{L}_{1}
,
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1})
, and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
are shown. Here
|\mathcal{L}_{0}|=1
,
|\mathcal{L}_{1}|=2
,
|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1})|=7
,
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=6
and
|\mathcal{P}_{2}|=10
; they satisfy (
13.2
) with
A=6
.

Recall by Definition
8.3
that, if any atom (or any two atoms) in
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}
is cut as free, then we either get a {33A} molecule, or get a fixed end at each remaining atom in
\lambda
. In this case we say
\lambda
is
broken
. Note that each cutting in Definition
13.1
(
1
)–(
4
) breaks at most two
\lambda
, and each cutting in Definition
13.1
(
2
) does not break any
\lambda
. Moreover, if a cutting in Definition
13.1
(
2
)–(
4
) breaks some
\lambda
, then this breaking must be due to cutting an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(this is because, in Definition
13.1
(
4
), if we cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{p}\}
as free, then this is equivalent to first cutting
\mathfrak{m}
and then cut
\mathfrak{p}
, and
\mathfrak{p}
will have deg 2 when it is cut, so cutting
\mathfrak{p}
will not break any
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}
). In particular, if a cutting in Definition
13.1
(
2
)–(
4
) breaks both
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
, then they must intersect at an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, so we must have
\lambda\not\sim\lambda^{\prime}
by Proposition
10.4
(
2
).

Now suppose
\lambda\sim\lambda^{\prime}
, and
\lambda
is broken before
\lambda^{\prime}
, and
\lambda^{\prime}
is broken in a cutting operation in Definition
13.1
(
4
), then this cutting operation must involve some
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
that belongs to
\lambda^{\prime}
. Suppose
\mathfrak{m}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is as in Definition
13.1
(
4
)
and does not have deg 4
, since
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is a forest, we know that
\mathfrak{m}
has at most one parent in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
, so
\mathfrak{m}
must have deg 3 when it is cut in Definition
13.1
(
4
) (because
\mathfrak{m}
has two bottom non-fixed edges and one top non-fixed edge corresponding to its parent not in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
, just as before). Let
\mathfrak{p}=\lambda\cap\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
, then the previously broken
\lambda
provides a fixed end at
\mathfrak{p}
, so
\mathfrak{p}
also has deg 3 at the time when
\mathfrak{m}
is cut. By construction in Definition
13.1
(
4
), we get a {33A} molecule with higher atom
\mathfrak{m}
, such that
\mathfrak{m}\in\lambda^{\prime}
. In the same way, if
\lambda
contains an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{conn}}^{1}
and is broken in a cutting operation in Definition
13.1
(
4
) with
\mathfrak{m}
not having deg 4, then we also get a {33A} molecule with higher atom
\mathfrak{m}
, such that
\mathfrak{m}\in\lambda
.

Proof part 2.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
be the set of ov-segments selected in Definition
13.1
(
1
), and
\mathcal{L}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{L}_{0}
be the set of all ov-segments
\lambda
that are broken in Definition
13.1
(
1
), then we have
|\mathcal{L}_{1}|\leq 2|\mathcal{L}_{0}|
. Recall the auxiliary graph
G
defined above; let
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1})
be the set of G-nodes in
\mathcal{L}\backslash\mathcal{L}_{1}
that are adjacent to a G-node in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{1}
be the set of G-edges connecting a G-node in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
to a G-node in
\mathcal{L}\backslash\mathcal{L}_{1}
. We claim that

(13.2)

|\mathcal{P}_{1}|\leq|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1})|+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|,\quad|\mathcal{P}_{1}|\geq A|\mathcal{L}_{0}|-2|\mathcal{L}_{1}|\geq(A/2-2)|\mathcal{L}_{1}|.

Here, the first inequality in (
13.2
) follows because the subgraph
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1})\cup\mathcal{L}_{1}\subseteq G
of the auxiliary graph contains no cycle (and is a forest); the second inequality is because each G-node
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
has at least
A
different G-edges, and that each G-edge in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is counted twice when adding up the G-degrees of G-nodes in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and their number is bounded by
|\mathcal{L}_{1}|
. Moreover, let
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{s})
be the permutation of
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}\backslash\mathcal{L}_{1}
ordered by time of breaking in Definition
13.1
(
2
)–(
4
), then by construction in Definition
13.1
(
1
), each
\lambda_{j}
is adjacent to at most
A
different G-nodes
\lambda_{k}\,(k\neq j)
in
G
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{2}
be the set of G-edges among these
\lambda_{j}
, then
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|+|\mathcal{P}_{2}|\geq|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|-|\mathcal{L}_{1}|
(where
|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|
is the total number of G-edges, as each G-edge corresponds to an intersection of two
\lambda_{j}
).

Now, by the discussion in Part 1 above, for each
\lambda\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1})
, and each
\lambda
containing an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}
, and each
\lambda=\lambda_{j}
that is adjacent to some
\lambda_{k}\,(k<j)
in
G
, if
\lambda
is broken in a cutting operation in Definition
13.1
(
4
) with
\mathfrak{m}
not having deg 4, we get a {33A} molecule corresponding to
\lambda
when it is broken, and each {33A} molecule can be so obtained from at most two such
\lambda
(as shown in Part 1, this
\lambda
must contain the higher atom
\mathfrak{m}
in this {33A} molecule, so it has at most 2 choices). In the case
\lambda=\lambda_{j}
, each
j
also corresponds to at most
A
different G-edges
(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{k})
. This implies (using also the first inequality in (
13.2
)) that

(13.3)

\#_{\{33A\}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\max\bigg(|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{L}_{1}|,\,|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}|,\,A^{-1}|\mathcal{P}_{2}|\bigg)-\#_{\mathrm{exc}}.

Here we use
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{L}_{1}|\leq|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1})|
, and that the number of
\lambda=\lambda_{j}
adjacent to some
\lambda_{k}\,(k<j)
in
G
is at least
A^{-1}|\mathcal{P}_{2}|
. Moreover
\#_{\mathrm{exc}}
is the number of atoms cut in Definition
13.1
(
1
) plus the number of deg 4 atoms cut in Definition
13.1
(
4
) (each of them may break two
\lambda
which count as exceptions; also cutting any deg 2 atom in Definition
13.1
(
2
) will not break any
\lambda
).

On the other hand, the number of deg 4 atoms cut in Definition
13.1
(
4
) does not exceed the number of full components
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and also

\max(\#_{\mathrm{exc}},\#_{\{4\}})\leq\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|,

because (i) any new component of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
generated in the cutting sequence cannot be full (cf. Remark
10.6
, and (ii) any component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
also cannot be full when all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
are cut. By combining this with the second inequality in (
13.2
) and (
13.3
), and the inequality
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|+|\mathcal{P}_{2}|\geq|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|-|\mathcal{L}_{1}|
and using the definition
A:=32\upsilon^{-2}
, we conclude that (recall that all {33A} molecules are viewed as good)

\displaystyle(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\{33A\}})-d\cdot(\#_{\{4\}})

\displaystyle\geq(\upsilon/4)\max\big(|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{L}_{1}|,|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}|,A^{-1}|\mathcal{P}_{2}|\big)-(d+1)(\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|)

\displaystyle\geq(\upsilon/16)(|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-8\upsilon^{-2}|\mathcal{L}_{1}|)+(\upsilon/16)(|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}|+A^{-1}|\mathcal{P}_{2}|)-(d+1)\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}

\displaystyle\geq(\upsilon/16)A^{-1}(|\mathcal{P}_{1}|+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|)+(\upsilon/16)(|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}|+A^{-1}|\mathcal{P}_{2}|)-(d+1)\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}

\displaystyle\geq(\upsilon/16)A^{-1}(|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}|+|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|)-(d+1)\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})},

which proves (
13.1
).
∎

Now we can prove the first case of Proposition
12.18
, namely Proposition
13.3
. This corresponds to toy model III in Section
11.5
, where there exist (many components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
or) many 2-connections in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
.

Proposition 13.3
.

Proposition
12.18
is true if either
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\geq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, or if
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\leq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
|\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|\geq(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, where
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
is defined as in Definition
13.1
, but with the roles of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
reversed.

Proof.

Our goal is to prove that

(13.4)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot(\#_{\{4\}})\geq(C_{4}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}.

Proof part 1.
Suppose
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\geq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Recall the definition of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
in Definition
13.1
(where we do not reverse the roles of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, which is different from
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
defined above). Consider all components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, if at least half of them contain an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}:=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}\cup\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
, then we have
|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}|\geq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}/2
. In this case we apply
2CONNUP
to
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
, by Proposition
13.2
we have

(13.5)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot(\#_{\{4\}})\geq C^{-1}|\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}|-C\cdot\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}\geq\big(C^{-1}(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}-C(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\big)\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}

(where the upper bound of
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
follows from Reduction
2
(ii)), which implies (
13.4
).

If at least half of the components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
do not contain an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}
, then we shall cut
\mathbb{M}_{U}
as free and cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
. Then, for each of these at least half components
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{D}
, it will not contain any deg 2 atom (because this component does not contain any atom in either
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{1}
or
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
); moreover, this
X
may be divided into new components due to the broken ov-segments, but each new component will contain at least one deg 3 atom (cf. Remark
10.6
), and at least one new component will contain at least two deg 3 atoms. This last fact is because at least two atoms in
X
will become deg 3 after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(i.e. has deg 3 or has a bond with
\mathbb{M}_{U}
) by Reduction
2
(ii); by considering a path in
X
between these two atoms before cutting and whether or where this path breaks after cutting, we can obtain a component of
X
after cutting that contains two deg 3 atoms (as the atom where the path breaks must have a fixed end).

We then cut each resulting component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into elementary molecules using
DOWN
. By Proposition
12.2
, and note that all {33A} molecules are treated as good, we get that
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}/2
and
\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
(this is because
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no full component after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, due to Remark
10.6
and Reduction
2
(ii)), so (
13.4
) follows in the same way as (
13.5
).

Proof part 2.
Assume
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\leq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
|\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|\geq(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. We first cut as free each full component in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
that has no bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(and then cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
), to make
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
satisfy the assumptions of Definition
13.1
after reversing the roles of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Then we apply
2CONNDN
to
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
. Proposition
13.2
then gives that (note
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\leq\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}
)

(13.6)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot(\#_{\{4\}})\geq C^{-1}\cdot|\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|-C\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}+\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})})\\
\geq\big(C^{-1}(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}-C(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\big)\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

which implies (
13.4
). Here, we used the bound on
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
provided by Reduction
2
(ii).
∎

Reduction 3
.

With Proposition
13.3
, in the remaining proof of Proposition
12.18
, we may now assume for
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
, in addition to Reduction
2
(i)–(iii):

•

That
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\leq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and the number of weakly degenerate atoms and pairs in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is less than
(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

•

We have
|\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|\leq(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, with the set
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
defined in Proposition
13.3
.

13.2.
The
3COMPUP
algorithm

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
12.18
under the assumption of Reductions
2
and
3
. This proof will be presented, following
Stages 1–6
, in Section
13.5
. Before this, we first introduce the remaining ingredients needed in the proof; in this subsection we define the full
3COMPUP
algorithm (see Definition
13.6
) corresponding to toy model II ((see
Stage 5
in Section
13.5
), which is a direct extension of the toy version in Definition
11.17
by including O-atoms and translating into the ov-segment language (cf. Section
11.7.1
). Apart from this translation, Definition
13.6
and Proposition
13.7
are exactly the same as Definition
11.17
and Proposition
11.19
in Section
11
.
Before proceeding, we state and prove the full version of Lemma
11.6
in Section
11
in the presence of O-atoms (by translating adjacency to ov-adjacency etc.).

Lemma 13.4
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule that is a tree, and
S\subseteq\mathbb{M}
is an
ov-connected
set. Suppose we cut
S
as free from
\mathbb{M}
. Then, for each atom
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash S
that is
ov-adjacent
to an atom in
S
before cutting, there is a unique component
X_{\mathfrak{q}}\subseteq\mathbb{M}\backslash S
after cutting that contains
\mathfrak{q}
. This
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
is in
bijection
with
\mathfrak{q}
, and when viewed in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, the operation of cutting
S
creates
only one
fixed end in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
at
\mathfrak{q}
, see
Figure
58
.

Figure 58.
Illustration of Lemma
13.4
. The main differences with Lemma
11.6
are that (i) now
S
is allowed to be ov-connected instead of connected (with possible O-atoms in between which also belong to different components after cutting
S
as free), and (ii) each ov-adjacent atom
\mathfrak{q}
(not necessarily adjacent) now leads to its own component. For example, the
\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
will belong to different components after cutting
S
as free, but again each component has exactly one new fixed end.

Proof.

Consider all the ov-segments connecting an atom in
S
to another atom (in
S
or not in
S
