), and all atoms (including the intermediate O-atoms) on these ov-segments. Let the set of all such ov-segments (and the bonds contained in these ov-segments) be
\Lambda
, and the set of all these atoms be
Z\supseteq S
, then the atoms in
Z\backslash S
are precisely all the atoms
\mathfrak{q}
as stated. Note that all the bonds in
\Lambda
with an endpoint in
\mathbb{M}\backslash S
are broken after cutting
S
as free; we claim that at this time, any two different atoms
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in Z\backslash S
must be in two different components of
\mathbb{M}\backslash S
. In fact, if not, then they are connected in
\mathbb{M}\backslash S
by a path of bonds
not containing any bond in
\Lambda
. However,
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in Z
are also connected by another path
containing only bonds in
\Lambda
, using that
S
is ov-connected. This then produces a cycle in
\mathbb{M}
, contradiction.

Now we have proved that
\mathfrak{q}\mapsto X_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a bijection, and for each
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, the only atom in it that is ov-adjacent to an atom in
S
is
\mathfrak{q}
. As such, cutting
S
as free only creates fixed end at
\mathfrak{q}
and not at any other atom in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
. If we get
more than one
fixed end at
\mathfrak{q}
, then
\mathfrak{q}
must be ov-adjacent to two atoms in
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in S
along
two different
ov-segments (note: breaking two bonds at
\mathfrak{q}
that belong to the same ov-segment creates only one fixed end at
\mathfrak{q}
, see Definition
8.3
). Note that
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
is connected to
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
by a path of bonds in
\Lambda
; since
\mathfrak{q}\not\in S
, we know that
\mathfrak{q}
either does not occur in this path, or occurs in this path but as one of the intermediate O-atoms (so the two bonds at
\mathfrak{q}
in this path
must be serial
). However, the two ov-segments connecting
\mathfrak{q}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
also form a path between
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
, in which the two bonds at
\mathfrak{q}

are not serial
. Thus these two paths cannot coincide, which again produces a cycle in
\mathbb{M}
. This contradiction then proves that cutting
S
as free produces only one fixed end at
\mathfrak{q}
(and in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
), as desired.
∎

Lemma 13.5
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a molecule that is a tree, contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms and has no C-top fixed end. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
be
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
and assume
|\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|\geq 2
. Define a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
to be
no-bottom
if any child of any atom in
A
must also be in
A
. Then, there always exists a connected, no-bottom subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
with
|A\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|=2
.

Proof.

For any
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
, let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
, then it is always connected and no-bottom. If there exists
\mathfrak{n}
such that
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|\geq 2
, then choose a lowest such
\mathfrak{n}
. Since
\mathfrak{n}
is either O-atom or has no top fixed end, we know that either (i)
\mathfrak{n}
has one child
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
, or (ii)
\mathfrak{n}
has deg 4 and has two children
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}
. In either case, by assumption we have
|S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|\leq 1
for each
j
, which then implies
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|\leq 2
because
S_{\mathfrak{n}}=(\cup_{j}S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
. This implies
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|=2
, as desired.

Now assume
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|\leq 1
for each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
. If
|S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|=1
, there is a unique
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n})\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
; now consider all atom pairs
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
such that
\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n})
and
\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
both exist and
are different
(which exist because
|\mathbb{M}_{(3)}|\geq 2
), and one such pair
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
that are connected by
a shortest path
in
\mathbb{M}
. Let this path be
(\mathfrak{n}_{0},\mathfrak{n},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
, then for each
0<i<j
we must have either
S_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}}\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}=\varnothing
or
\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}_{i})=\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n})
(otherwise the pair
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}_{i})
corresponds to a shorter path, contradiction). Now let
A=\cup_{i=0}^{j}S_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}}
, it is clear that
A
is connected, no-bottom and
A\cap\mathbb{M}_{(3)}=\{\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}),\mathfrak{p}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})\}
, as desired.
∎

Definition 13.6

(The
3COMPUP
algorithm)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule which is a forest, contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms and has no C-top fixed end, and assume that each component of
\mathbb{M}
contains at least two deg 3 atoms. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
be
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
. Define the following cutting sequence:

(1)

Apply Lemma
13.5
to one component of
\mathbb{M}
and select a connected no-bottom subset
A
. This set is fixed until the end of
5
.

(2)

Choose a lowest deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{n}
in
A
that has not been cut. Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
(note that
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\subseteq A
). This
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
are fixed until the end of
3
.

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, each time choose a highest atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
that has not been cut. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3 and has an ov-parent
\mathfrak{m}^{+}
or ov-child
\mathfrak{m}^{-}
that also has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{\pm}\}
as free; otherwise cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. Repeat until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut.

(4)

If
\mathbb{M}
has deg 2 atom, then cut it; repeat until
\mathbb{M}
has no deg 2 atoms. Then go to
2
.

(5)

When all atoms in
A
have been cut, we subsequently cut all remaining deg 2 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
as in
4
, and then cut all
components of
\mathbb{M}
with only one deg 3 atom
using
UP
.

(6)

Go to
1
and select the next
A
from one of the remaining components of
\mathbb{M}
, and so on.

Proposition 13.7
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be as in Definition
13.6
. Define
\#_{\mathrm{3ovcp}(\mathbb{M})}
to be the number of ov-components of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
(the det of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
), and
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}
to be the number of components of
\mathbb{M}
. Then
\mathbb{M}
is cut into elementary molecules after
3COMPUP
(Definition
13.6
), and we have

(13.7)

\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=\#_{\{4\}}=0;\qquad\#_{\{33A\}}\geq\frac{1}{10}\cdot\#_{\mathrm{3ovcp}(\mathbb{M})}+\frac{1}{5}\cdot\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}.

Proof.

Note that steps
2
–
3
in Definition
13.6
are exactly the same as those
UP
(see Definition
12.1
(
2
)–(
3
), note also the step of cutting deg 2 atoms is done in Definition
13.6

5
), but applied to
A
only. Since
A
is no-bottom which means
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\subseteq A
whenever
\mathfrak{n}\in A
, it is easy to see (following the same proof as Proposition
12.2
) that the (MONO) property is preserved, and thus
\mathbb{M}
is cut into elementary molecules only, and
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=\#_{\{4\}}=0
. We now focus on the proof of (
13.7
).

Note that the algorithm
3COMPUP
consists of finitely many loops where one loop is formed by steps
1
–
5
in Definition
13.6
, and (say) turns
\mathbb{M}
into
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
. Define
\lambda(\mathbb{M})
to be the right hand side of the last inequality in (
13.7
), then we only need to consider each single loop
1
–
5
in Definition
13.6
, and prove that:

(
\clubsuit
)

If
\mathbb{M}
is a forest, has no top fixed end and contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms with each component containing at least two deg 3 atoms, then the result
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
after executing the loop
1
–
5
in Definition
13.6
satisfies the same property. Moreover for this loop we have

(13.8)

\#_{\{33\}}\geq\lambda(\mathbb{M})-\lambda(\mathbb{M}^{\prime}).

Note that adding up (
13.8
) leads to the desired lower bound of
\#_{\{33\}}
, thus it suffices to prove (
\clubsuit
) for a given loop. We divide the proof into 4 parts, which exactly correspond to the 4 parts of the proof in toy model II (Proposition
11.19
).

Proof part 1.
We prove that each component of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
satisfies the desired properties. This is obvious, as the lack of top fixed ends follows from the same proof as Proposition
12.2
, and the lack of deg 2 atoms and components with only one deg 3 atom follows from the cuttings done in Definition
13.6

5
.

Proof part 2.
Now we need to prove (
13.8
). We start by making some preparations. Note that
A
is an ov-connected subset of
\mathbb{M}
; by Lemma
13.4
, each atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A
that is ov-adjacent to an atom
\mathfrak{n}\in A
by a pre-loop ov-segment corresponds to a unique component
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
after cutting the atoms in
A
as free (the loop will in fact cut as free more atoms). Note also that
\mathfrak{m}
must be an ov-parent of
\mathfrak{n}
as
A
has no bottom.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
be the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}
(before executing the loop), and consider all ov-components of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
that intersect
A
or are ov-adjacent to
A
by an ov-segment (we call them
good
). We know
A
has exactly two atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
; apart from the one or two ov-components that contain these two atoms, for any atom in any other good ov-component, it must belong to
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
for a unique
\mathfrak{m}
, and this
\mathfrak{m}
must have deg 3 (otherwise the ov-component will not be ov-adjacent to
A
), with the corresponding
\mathfrak{n}
having deg 4. This is similar to the scenario in toy model II, see
Figure
59
. Let
B
be the union of all good ov-components and
A
. We claim that after steps
1
–
4
in Definition
13.6
and cutting the deg 2 atoms in Definition
13.6

5
, exactly those atoms in
B
have been cut as free.

Figure 59.
The set
A
and ov-components of deg 3 atoms forming
B
, in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
13.7
. This is basically the same as in toy model II (
Figure
35
), except now the notion of adjacency and components are replaced by ov-adjacency and ov-components.

In fact, the atoms
A
must have been cut by definition. For any atom in a good ov-component of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
, by definition it is connected to an atom in
A
by a sequence of deg 3 atoms with each consecutive ones being ov-adjacent; once the atom in
A
has been cut, the atoms on this chain will successively become deg 2, so they must have been cut (either in Definition
13.6

4
–
5
, or as part of {33} molecule in Definition
13.6

3
). On the other hand, each atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash B
has at most one ov-segment connecting to
B
(as
B
is ov-connected and
\mathbb{M}
is a tree, cf. the proof of Lemma
13.4
), and must be deg 4 if it has one such ov-segment (otherwise it would belong to one of the good ov-components of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
), so each atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash B
will have deg 3 or 4 after cutting
B
as free, thus it will not be cut in steps
1
–
4
in Definition
13.6
or in cutting the deg 2 atoms in Definition
13.6

5
.

Proof part 3.
Next we obtain a lower bound on
\#_{\{33\}}
. As
A
contains two deg 3 atoms, we can show that
\#_{\{33\}}\geq 1
in the same way as Proposition
12.2
(
3
). Moreover, since
A
contains
exactly
two deg 3 atoms, by arguing as in the proof of Proposition
12.2
(
3
) and calculating the quantity
\sigma
defined there but within
A
, we can show that, apart from at most two exceptions, each deg 4 atom in
A
will have deg 3 when it is cut.

Now let
\lambda
be the number of good ov-components of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
(i.t. those forming
B
). By the discussions in Part 2 above, with at most two exceptions, each such ov-component must contain some
\mathfrak{m}
which has deg 3 and is an ov-parent of an atom
\mathfrak{n}\in A
of deg 4; consider then the maximal ov-segment
\sigma
containing
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
, note that each
\sigma
corresponds to at most one good ov-component (if
\sigma
is given, then any deg 3 atoms on
\sigma
must be ov-adjacent to each other and thus belong to the same ov-component), so the number of such ov-segments
\sigma
is at least
\lambda
. We claim that with at most two more exceptions, the first atom
\mathfrak{p}\in\sigma
that is cut, must belong to a {33A} molecule. In fact, we must have
\mathfrak{p}\in A
by Definition
13.6

3
; with at most two exceptions we may assume
\mathfrak{p}
has deg 3 when it is cut. At this time the
\mathfrak{m}
(which is ov-parent of
\mathfrak{p}
) also has deg 3 (because the only way
\mathfrak{m}
is ov-connected to
A
is by the ov-segment between
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
, cf. Lemma
13.4
), so by Definition
13.6

3
, we know that
\mathfrak{p}
must belong to a {33} molecule (which may or may not be
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{m}\}
). Since each {33} molecule can be so obtained from at most two
\sigma
, we thus obtain that

(13.9)

\#_{\{33\}}\geq\max\bigg(1,\frac{\lambda-2}{2}-2\bigg).

Proof part 4.
Now consider the components of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
and ov-components of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}^{\prime}
(set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
). Note that
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is formed from
\mathbb{M}
by cutting
B
as free and then removing all components with only one deg 3 atom. Since
B
is ov-connected, by applying Lemma
13.4
again, we see that each component
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
corresponds to a unique atom
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash B
that is ov-adjacent to an atom in
B
. Let
\mu
be the number of such atoms (i.e. corresponding to those new components with at least two deg 3 atoms after cutting
B
), then

(13.10)

\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}=1-\mu.

Finally, consider the set of deg 3 atoms, i.e.
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
and
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}^{\prime}
, and their ov-components. The ov-components of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
includes those
\lambda
ov-components forming
B
as in Part 3 (which have been cut when forming
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
), and the other ov-components which are contained in some
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
defined above. Note that for each new component
Y
formed after cutting
B
that contains only one deg 3 atom, it will be removed and will not occur in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
; however, all atoms in
Y
originally have deg 4 before any cutting, so initially
Y
also has no contribution to
\#_{3\mathrm{ovcp}(\mathbb{M})}
, and thus we can ignore such
Y
.

By Lemma
13.4
, when viewed in each
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, the effect of cutting
B
exactly turns one free end into fixed end (turning one deg 4 atom into deg 3). Therefore the part of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}^{\prime}
within
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
is formed by
adding one deg 3 atom
into the existing part of
\mathbb{M}_{(3)}
within
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
. This new deg 3 atom may
decrease

\#_{3\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{\mathfrak{q}})}
(i.e. the number of ov-components of the set of deg 3 atoms in
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
) by joining existing ov-components together, but this decrease is
at most 2
because this new deg 3 atom can join at most 3 ov-components into 1, see
Figure
60
. From this, and accounting for the
\lambda
ov-components removed when cutting
B
, we deduce that

(13.11)

\#_{3\mathrm{ovcp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{3\mathrm{ovcp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}\leq\lambda+2\mu.

Putting together (
13.9
)–(
13.11
), and using the definition of
\lambda(\mathbb{M})
, we deduce that

(13.12)

\displaystyle\lambda(\mathbb{M})-\lambda(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{5}\big(\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}\big)+\frac{1}{10}\big(\#_{3\mathrm{ovcp}(\mathbb{M})}-\#_{3\mathrm{ovcp}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{5}+\frac{\lambda}{10}\leq\max\bigg(1,\frac{\lambda-6}{2}\bigg)\leq\#_{\{33\}}.

This proves (
13.8
) and completes the proof of Proposition
13.7
.
∎

Figure 60.
Part 4 of the proof of Proposition
13.7
, where turning a deg 4 atom into deg 3 may decrease the number of ov-components of the deg 3 set by at most 2. This is exactly the same as the toy model II counterpart (
Figure
37
), as long as we change “components” to “ov-components” etc.

13.3.
Pre-processing

In this subsection we introduce the
pre-processing
step, discussed in Section
11.7.4
(d). We start with the notion of
ov-proper
, which is the extension of proper in Definition
11.3
.

Definition 13.8
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule, we say it is
ov-proper
, if it is a forest, contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms, and the ov-distance (Definition
8.4
) between any two deg 3 atoms is at least 3.

Note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
may have fixed ends as the result of the cuttings in Definition
12.14
(and further cuttings of 2-connections, see
Stage 1
in Section
13.5
), and might not be ov-proper. The idea of pre-processing is to turn
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into ov-proper again by cutting as free a suitable set
S
. Namely, given a subset of atoms in
Z\cup Y\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{D}
(which in practice will include the deg 3 atoms and weakly degenerate atoms (Definition
12.16
)), we define a function
SELECT
(Definition
13.10
) which outputs a set
S\supseteq Z\cup Y
, such that cutting
S
as free turns
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into ov-proper, see Proposition
13.11
(1). Moreover, since the
UD connections
are important in the algorithm
MAINUD
below, we also prove a result on how cutting
S
as free may affect the UD connections, see Proposition
13.11
(
2
).

The idea of the
SELECT
function is a simple greedy algorithm. Start with the set
Z\cup Y
and its ov-components. If cutting
Z\cup Y
as free would create (deg 2 atom or) two deg 3 atoms with ov-distance
\leq 2
, then there must exist two ov-components
A,B\subseteq Z\cup Y
with ov-distance
\leq 4
. We then add to
Z\cup Y
the intermediate atoms in the shortest path of ov-segments between
A
and
B
to bring them together, and repeat this process until it is no longer possible. In the end we get
S=\textbf{SELECT}(\mathbb{M}_{D},Z,Y)
such that cutting
S
as free makes
\mathbb{M}_{D}
ov-proper; see Definition
13.10
and Proposition
13.11
(1). As for Proposition
13.11
(2), the proof relies on Lemma
13.9
below, which is in the same spirit as the proof of uniqueness of lowest atom in Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
11.7
in Section
11
.

Lemma 13.9
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
be a UD molecule such that
\mathbb{M}_{D}=\varnothing
, and
\mathbb{M}_{U}
does not have C-top fixed end. Suppose
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{U}
is ov-connected within
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(cf. Definition
8.4
) such that each atom in
A
has deg 3. Then
A
contains a
unique
lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}
, such that (i) each atom
\mathfrak{m}\in A
is obtained from
\mathfrak{n}
by iteratively taking ov-parents in
A
, and (ii) each ov-child of
\mathfrak{m}\in A
is either one of the terms preceding
\mathfrak{m}
in the ov-parent sequence defined in (i), or an O-atom between two consecutive terms preceding
\mathfrak{m}
, or an ov-child of
\mathfrak{n}
.

Similarly, if we assume
\mathbb{M}_{D}\neq\varnothing
,
A
is ov-connected within
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, each atom of
A
is C-atom, and each deg 4 atom of
A
has at most one ov-child in
A
, then conclusion (i) is still true.

Proof.

Choose a lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}\in A
. We will prove (i), which also establishes the uniqueness of
\mathfrak{n}
, and prove (ii) in the process. For any
\mathfrak{m}\in A
, since
A
is ov-connected, there exists a sequence
\mathfrak{p}_{j}\in A\,(0\leq j\leq r)
such that
(\mathfrak{p}_{0},\mathfrak{p}_{r})=(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m})
and
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}
is ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
; we may assume this sequence is shortest. Since
\mathfrak{n}
is lowest,
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
must be ov-parent of
\mathfrak{p}_{0}=\mathfrak{n}
; since
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
has deg 3 (thus it is O-atom or has one bottom fixed end, cf. Remark
8.7
, we know that
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
must be ov-parent of
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
(otherwise
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
is ov-child of
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
along the same maximal ov-segment containing
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
, which means
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
is ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
and we can skip
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
to get a shorter sequence), and for the same reason
\mathfrak{p}_{3}
must be ov-parent of
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
, and so on. This proves (i). Then (ii) follows from the same proof (any ov-child of
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
either belongs to the ov-segment between
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{j-1}
, or is an ov-child of
\mathfrak{p}_{j-1}
so we can keep iterating). The proof of (i) in the case
\mathbb{M}_{D}\neq\varnothing
is similar, where we note that
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
has at most one ov-child in
A
in the case when
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
has deg 4, so
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}
still cannot be ov-child of
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
if
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}\in A
and is C-atom.
∎

Definition 13.10

(The function
SELECT
)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be any UD molecule, such that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has no deg 2 atoms, and has no C-bottom fixed end. Let
Z
be the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and
Y
be a subset of the set of deg 4 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Given
(\mathbb{M}_{D},Z,Y)
, we define a set
S:=\textbf{SELECT}(\mathbb{M}_{D},Z,Y)
as follows, see
Figure
61
:

Figure 61.
Top: the process of constructing the
SELECT
function in Definition
13.10
(for two ov-components
A
and
B
with ov-distance
\leq 4
, add the intermediate atoms in the ov-segments connecting them, until this cannot be done anymore). Bottom: proof of Proposition
13.11
(
1
); if
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
belong to the same component
A
, then we get a cycle from
\mathfrak{m}\to\mathfrak{n}\to\cdots\to\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\to\cdots\to\mathfrak{m}
(the path from
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
to
\mathfrak{m}
belongs to
A
).

(1)

Consider all the ov-components of
Z\cup Y
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(this means we only consider the O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
when defining ov-connectedness in Definition
8.4
), which is a finite collection of disjoint subsets of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

(2)

If any two of these subsets, say
A
and
B
, have the shortest ov-distance (within
\mathbb{M}_{D}
) which is at most 4, then we choose the shortest path between an atom in
A
and an atom in
B
, which can be divided into at most 4 ov-segments. Write this path as
(\mathfrak{n}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{q})
, where
q\leq 4
and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\in A
,
\mathfrak{n}_{q}\in B
, and each
(\mathfrak{n}_{j},\mathfrak{n}_{j+1})
is an ov-segment. Then replace the two sets
A
and
B
by a single set
A\cup B\cup\{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{q-1}\}
.

(3)

Repeat (
2
) until this can no longer be done (i.e. when only one set is left, or any two sets have of distance at least 5). Then output
S:=\textbf{SELECT}(\mathbb{M}_{D},Z,Y)
as the union of all the current sets.

Proposition 13.11
.

Let the function
SELECT
be as in Definition
13.10
. Then we have the followings:

(1)

After cutting
S:=\textbf{SELECT}(\mathbb{M}_{D},Z,Y)
from
\mathbb{M}
as free, then the set
\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
in the resulting molecule
\mathbb{M}\backslash S
is ov-proper (Definition
13.8
). Note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
may have both C-top and C-bottom fixed ends, however this is compensated by the good property of being ov-proper.

(2)

Consider all the maximal ov-segments in
\mathbb{M}
that contains at least one atom in
S
and one atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. Then the number of these maximal ov-segments does not exceed
10(\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(Z)}+|Y|)
, where
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(Z)}
is the number of ov-components of
Z
(i.e. the set of all deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
), where the ov-components are determined by considering
\mathbb{M}_{D}
only.

Proof.

Proof of (
1
).
At the end of the construction in Definition
13.10
(
3
), we get a number of disjoint subsets, such that each one is ov-connected and the ov-distance between each two of them is at least 5, and
S
is the union of all these subsets. Note also that the sub-molecule
\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
of the molecule formed by cutting
S
as free from
\mathbb{M}
, is equal to the molecule formed by cutting
S
as free from
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Now suppose after cutting
S
as free, there exist two deg 3 atoms
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
of ov-distance at most 2; the case of a deg 2 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}\backslash S
can be treated similarly. Since
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\not\in S
, they must have deg 4 initially in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. As they become deg 3 after cutting
S
as free, we know that for each of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, it must be ov-adjacent to an atom in
S
. Assume
\mathfrak{n}
is ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{m}\in S
, and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\in S
. If
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
belong to two different subsets
A
and
B
defined above, then the ov-distance between
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
(and hence the ov-distance between
A
and
B
) is at most
1+2+1=4
, contradicting the assumption about these subsets.

Therefore, we know that
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
belong to the same set
A
defined above. But then there exists a path
\mu_{1}:\mathfrak{m}\to\mathfrak{n}\to\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
(composed of ov-segments), and another path
\mu_{2}
between
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
, which is composed of ov-segments connecting atoms in
S
(because
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
belong to the same ov-component
A
of
S
). The path
\mu_{1}
cannot be expressed in this way (because the atoms
\mathfrak{n}
,
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
separating the different ov-segments, as well as the possible atom ov-adjacent to both
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, all belong to
\mathbb{M}\backslash S
), unless this whole path belongs to a single ov-segment connecting
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
(meaning the atoms
\mathfrak{n}
,
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
etc., all belong to this single ov-segment connecting
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
), but in this latter case the path
\mathfrak{n}\to\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
would no longer exist after cutting
S
as free (see Definition
8.3
). Consequently, we know that the paths
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}

cannot coincide
, but then they will form a cycle, which contradicts the fact that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest.

Proof of (
2
).
In each step in Definition
13.10
(
2
) we add at most 3 atoms to the union of the current subsets. Moreover, the number of such steps that can be carried out cannot exceed the initial number of subsets (as each step reduces the number of subsets by at least 1), which is the number of ov-components of
Z\cup Y
, and is at most
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(Z)}+|Y|
. This means that the number of newly added atoms in the construction of
S
(starting from
Z\cup Y
) is at most
3(\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(Z)}+|Y|)
.

Therefore, we only need to consider the required maximal ov-segments that contains an atom in
Z
. In each such maximal ov-segment, we have an atom
\mathfrak{m}
belonging to an ov-component
W
of
Z
, which has an ov-parent in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
. By (the dual version of) Lemma
13.9
(ii), this ov-parent of
\mathfrak{m}
, which does not belong to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, must also be an ov-parent of the unique highest atom
\mathfrak{n}
of
W
; therefore this maximal ov-segment must contain
\mathfrak{n}
. As each ov-component
W
contains a unique highest atom
\mathfrak{n}
, and each atom belongs to at most two maximal ov-segments, we conclude that the number of such maximal ov-segments does not exceed
2\cdot\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(Z)}
(as
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(Z)}
is the number of ov-components of
Z
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
), as desired.
∎

13.4.
The
MAINUD
algorithm

In this section we introduce the last ingredient, which is also the central algorithm in our proof, namely the
MAINUD
algorithm corresponding to toy model I plus (see
Stage 6
in Section
13.5
). In Definition
13.12
below, we present the full version of this algorithm. Compared to the toy version (Definition
11.13
), the main difference (apart from standard translations into ov-segment language) is that, the set
X
in Definition
11.13
is replaced by the set
X_{1}
(see Definition
13.12
below). The reason has to do with the main dichotomy between toy models I plus and II in Section
11
(basically
X_{1}
is the set of deg 3 atoms after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free, which plays the same role as the
X
in Section
11
); see the discussion before
Stages 1–6
in Section
13.5
. Apart from this, Definition
13.12
, Proposition
13.13
and its proof are essentially the same as their toy versions (Definition
11.13
, Proposition
11.14
) in Section
11
.

Definition 13.12

(The algorithm
MAINUD
)
.

Define the following cutting algorithm
MAINUD
. It takes as input any UD molecule
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
that satisfies the following assumptions:

(1)

\mathbb{M}_{U}
contains no C-top fixed end, and
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}=\varnothing
, where
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
is defined as in Proposition
13.3
.

(2)

\mathbb{M}_{D}
is ov-proper (Definition
13.8
), and does not have any full component that is not connected to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
by a bond.

For any such
\mathbb{M}
, we define the sets
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
of C-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
as follows (see
Figure
62
):

•

Define
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{0}^{0}
if
\mathfrak{n}
has deg 3 and has one ov-child in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

•

Define
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{0}^{q}
, if
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{0}^{q-1}
, or
\mathfrak{n}
has deg 3 and has one ov-child in
X_{0}^{q-1}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
, or if
\mathfrak{n}
has two ov-children in
X_{0}^{q-1}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
along two ov-segments in two different particle lines. Then define
X_{0}=\cup_{q\geq 0}X_{0}^{q}
.

•

With
X_{0}
fixed, define
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{1}
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}\not\in X_{0}
, and
\mathfrak{n}
either has deg 3 or has one ov-child in
X_{0}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Figure 62.
The sets
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
in Definition
13.12
, constructed by definition. The main point (which will be used in
Stage 5
in Section
13.5
) is that, after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free, (i) all atoms in
X_{0}
(pink) will be subsequently cut as deg 2, (ii)
X_{1}
(yellow) is precisely the set of deg 3 C-atoms, (iii) the number of ov-components does not decrease if we add the new deg 3 O-atoms (blue) to
X_{1}
.

Note these sets are fixed throughout the cutting sequence below. Define the following cutting sequence:

(1)

If
\mathbb{M}_{U}
contains any deg
2
atom
\mathfrak{n}
, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg
2
atom left.

(2)

Consider the set of all (remaining) atoms that either belong to
X_{0}\cup X_{1}
, or has deg 3; choose
\mathfrak{n}
to be a lowest atom in this set (if this set is empty then choose
\mathfrak{n}
to be any lowest atom). Let
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and fix
\mathfrak{n}
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
until the end of (
6
).

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, choose a highest atom
\mathfrak{p}
in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
; if
\mathfrak{p}
is ov-adjacent to a deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
, then cut
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
as free, and cut
\mathfrak{q}
as free from
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
is
\mathfrak{p}
has deg 4. Otherwise cut
\mathfrak{p}
as free.

(4)

If any two deg 3 atoms
(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes ov-adjacent, then choose
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}\}
such that this ov-segment contains the fewest bond, and cut
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}\}
as free. Repeat until no such instances exist.

(5)

If the ov-distance of any two deg 3 atoms
(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes 2 (within
\mathbb{M}_{D}
), say
\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
are both ov-adjacent to some
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
, then cut
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
as free and then cut
\mathfrak{r}
as free from
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
. Go to (
4
). Repeat until
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes ov-proper.

(6)

When
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes proper, go to (
3
). Repeat until all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut.

(7)

When all atoms in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut, go to (
1
). Repeat until all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
have been cut.

(8)

When all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
have been cut, choose any deg 3 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
and cut it as free. If the ov-distance between any two deg 3 atoms becomes at most 2, go to (
4
) and repeat (
4
)–(
5
) until
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes proper again. Then choose and cut the next deg 3 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and so on.

Proposition 13.13
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be any UD molecule that satisfies (i)–(ii) in Definition
13.12
. Consider the number of UD connections
e
that are either (i) connected to some other UD connection via
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, or (ii) connected to a component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contains deg 3 atoms. We call such UD connections
good
and let the number of them be
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}
.

Let
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
be the number of full components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and let
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}
be the number of ov-components of the set
X_{1}
defined in Definition
13.12
. Then, for the algorithm
MAINUD
, we have
\#_{\{44\}}=0
, and each {33B} molecule must have both its atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and that

(13.13)

\#_{\{33A\}}+\#_{\{33B\}}\geq\frac{1}{5}\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{UD}}-\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}-\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}),\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}.

Proof.

We divide the proof into 4 parts, which exactly correspond to the 4 parts of the proof in toy model I plus (Proposition
11.14
); the presentation of Part 4 is slightly different, but essentially it is the same counting argument (involving the same quantity
\#_{\mathrm{deg3}}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}}
).

Proof part 1.
We prove the (MONO) property as in Proposition
12.2
: at any time in the cutting sequence, there is no C-top fixed end in
(\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash S_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
and no C-bottom fixed end in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
. In fact, when we choose
\mathfrak{n}
as in Definition
13.12
(
2
), every atom
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
must have deg 4. In the process of Definition
13.12
(
3
)–(
6
), if we only consider the cutting within
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, then the desired property follows from the same proof as in Proposition
12.2
. Now suppose an arbitrary set of atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
are also cut in the process, then this will only create C-bottom fixed ends, and only at C-atoms
\mathfrak{t}
that are ov-adjacent to some atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. By definition we must have
\mathfrak{t}\in X_{0}\cup X_{1}
, and thus
\mathfrak{t}\not\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
by our choice, so it does not affect the desired (MONO) property. Similarly, for any {33} molecule
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
cut in (
3
), the
\mathfrak{p}
must either be O-atom or
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{n}
, so this {33} molecule must be {33A} (as
\mathfrak{p}
has not C-top fixed end).

Proof part 2.
We prove that each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
belongs to a {3} or {33} molecule (which can be either {33A} or {33B}). In fact, note first that initially
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is ov-proper (thus has no deg 2 atom), and does not have full components not connected to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
by a bond (thus will not have any full component after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{U}
as free), so the cuttings in Definition
13.12
(
8
) will not create any {4} molecule.

Next, note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
initially is a forest, and will remain a forest in the whole cutting process (cutting an atom will not create any cycle, but may cause a tree to become a disconnected forest). Now consider each cutting step in Definition
13.12
(
3
) involving
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
, at which time
\mathbb{M}_{D}
must be proper by the construction in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
6
). This
\mathfrak{p}
has
at most one
ov-segment connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, because
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}=\varnothing
by Definition
13.12
(i). If no such ov-segment exists, then cutting
\mathfrak{p}
does not affect
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. If
one
such ov-segment exists, then the effect of the cutting step in Definition
13.12
(
3
)
on
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, has the following two possibilities:

(1)

If we cut
\mathfrak{p}
as free (which means all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{p}
have deg 4), then this step breaks
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into connected components
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
which are in bijection with atoms
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{p}
, and this cutting creates only one fixed end at
\mathfrak{q}
when viewed within
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, turning it from deg 4 to deg 3.

(2)

If we cut
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
as free (which means
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
is ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{p}
and has deg 3), then when viewed in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, this step equivalent to cutting
\mathfrak{q}
(which has deg 3) as free in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
.

Here
1
follows from the same proof in Lemma
13.4
(i.e.
\mathfrak{q}
in bijection with
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, in which only one fixed end is created at
\mathfrak{q}
) as
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest. In
2
, the effect of cutting
\{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\}
as free to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is equivalent to cutting
\{\mathfrak{q}\}
as free, because
\mathfrak{p}
has no other ov-segment connecting to other atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(apart from those on the ov-segment containing
\mathfrak{q}
).

Now, regarding the effects described in
1
–
2
, we have the following lemma, which is extension of Lemma
11.8
in the proof of Proposition
11.7
:

Lemma 13.14
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is proper at some point of the algorithm, and becomes non-proper after either
1
or
2
. Then,
\mathbb{M}_{D}
will once more become proper after applying Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
), via cuttings of {3} and {33} molecules only, and
without seeing any deg 2 atom
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is always a forest, and our proof will crucially rely on Lemma
13.4
. Consider first
1
: when viewed within each resulting component
X_{\mathfrak{q}}
, its effect is simply
switching one free end at a deg 4 atom into a fixed end, turning it into deg 3
. We call the latter step
(\ast)
. As for
2
, right before performing it at a deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{q}
, any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that is ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{q}
must have deg 4 (because
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is ov-proper). By Lemma
13.4
,
\mathbb{M}_{D}
will break into finitely many components after performing
2
, and the effect of
2

when viewed within each component
is again step
(\ast)
. Therefore we only need to prove Lemma
13.14
for step
(\ast)
.

Assume now
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes non-proper, with two ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms (say
\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
), after step
(\ast)
. We may assume
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
has deg 3 before
(\ast)
, while
\mathfrak{r}
has deg 4 before
(\ast)
and is turned into deg 3 after
(\ast)
. Then,
before
(\ast)
, any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{r}
or
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}

must have deg 4
, because otherwise we either have two ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms before
(\ast)
, or have two deg 3 atoms ov-adjacent to the same deg 4 atom before
1
, contradicting properness. Therefore, by Lemma
13.4
, we see that cutting the {33} molecule breaks
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into finitely many components, and the effect of this cutting
when viewed in each component
is again equivalent to step
(\ast)
. We then induct on the size of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
and apply induction hypothesis to each of these components, to conclude the proof of Lemma
13.14
.

Now suppose
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes non-proper, without two ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms, after
(\ast)
. In this case there must be two deg 3 atoms, say
\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}
, that are both ov-adjacent to a deg 4 atom
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
. We may assume
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}
respectively have deg 4 and 3 before
(\ast)
, and
\mathfrak{r}
has deg 4 before
(\ast)
and is turned into deg 3 after
(\ast)
. Then,
before
(\ast)
, any atom ov-adjacent to
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
or
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}

must have deg 4
for the same reason as above thanks to properness. Moreover, before
(\ast)
, any atom adjacent to
\mathfrak{r}
must also have deg 4, because otherwise we would get two ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms after
(\ast)
, contradicting our assumption. Therefore, by Lemma
13.4
, we see that cutting
\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
breaks
\mathbb{M}_{D}
into finitely many components, and the effect of this cutting
when viewed in each component
is again equivalent to
(\ast)
. We then conclude the proof by the same induction as above. This completes the proof of Lemma
13.14
.
∎

Now, with Lemma
13.14
, we can finish Part 2 of the proof, i.e. each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is contained in a {3} or {33} molecule. In fact, those cut in Definition
13.12
(
3
) clearly belong to {33} molecule. Those cut in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
) also belong to {3} or {33} molecules by Lemma
13.14
. Finally, those cut in Definition
13.12
(
8
) either belong to {3} molecule, or are cut in the process of applying Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
) and thus belong to {3} or {33} molecules by Lemma
13.14
.

Proof part 3.
We prove that the number of deg 4 atoms
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{U}
cut in Definition
13.12
(
3
) is at most
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}
. In fact, such
\mathfrak{n}
must be the atom chosen in Definition
13.12
(
2
) (the
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
in (
3
) will not be cut as deg 4 because it has an ov-parent in
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
which must be cut before
\mathfrak{p}
). Moreover, there are two possibilities when this
\mathfrak{n}
can has deg 4: either the component of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
containing
\mathfrak{n}
contains only deg 4 atoms, or
\mathfrak{n}
is the lowest remaining atom in
X_{0}\cup X_{1}
and has deg 4. The former case leads to at most
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}
choices for
\mathfrak{n}
.

In the latter case,
\mathfrak{n}
must have deg 4 and must not have any ov-child in
X_{0}\cup X_{1}
(otherwise this ov-child must have been cut before
\mathfrak{n}
as
\mathfrak{n}
is the lowest remaining atom in
X_{0}\cup X_{1}
, so
\mathfrak{n}
would not have deg 4), which means that
\mathfrak{n}\not\in X_{0}
by definition of
X_{0}
(and that
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}=\varnothing
). Therefore
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{1}
, and it must belong to one of the initial ov-components of
X_{1}
, say
A
. Since
\mathfrak{n}
has no ov-child in
A
, by Lemma
13.9
(i), it must be the unique lowest atom in
A
(here
A
satisfies the assumption of Lemma
13.9
, because each deg 4 atom in
X_{1}
must have an ov-child in
X_{0}\cup\mathbb{M}_{D}
, thus has at most one ov-child in
X_{1}
), which leads to at most
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}
choices for
\mathfrak{n}
due to the same number of choices of
A
. Putting together, this finishes Part 3 of the proof.

Proof part 4.
We now complete the proof of (
13.13
). The bound for
\#_{\{4\}}
follows directly from Part 3, as no deg 4 atom can be cut in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
), or Definition
13.12
(
8
) (as shown in Part 2). To prove the lower bound for
\#_{\{33\}}
(which is
\#_{\{33A\}}+\#_{\{33B\}}
), note that with at most
\mu:=\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{2})}
exceptions, each {3} molecule in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
created in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
) is always paired with a {33} molecule (the exceptions correspond to those atoms
\mathfrak{p}
in Definition
13.12
(
3
) with deg 4, which may cause
\mathfrak{q}
to be cut as a {3} molecule without a pairing {33} molecule), so the number of atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
cut in these steps is at most
3\cdot\#_{\{33\}}+\mu
.

Now consider the
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}
good UD connections and the corresponding atoms
\mathfrak{r}\in\mathbb{M}_{D}
. If any such
\mathfrak{r}
is not cut in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
), then in the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{D}
after Definition
13.12
(
7
), this
\mathfrak{r}
must be a deg 3 atom (it cannot be deg 4 as it belong to a UD connection, and cannot be deg 2 because
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is ov-proper by Part 2), and must belong to a component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contains at least two deg 3 atoms (by considering the pre-algorithm path connecting
\mathfrak{r}
to a deg 3 atom or another UD connection, and the possible place where a bond of this path is broken; note that a broken bond creates a fixed end and thus deg 3 atom). We collect all these atoms
\mathfrak{r}
(which are not cut in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
)), with the number of them being
\geq\#_{\mathrm{UD}}-(3\cdot\#_{\{33\}}+\mu)
by the above proof, and consider all the components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(after Definition
13.12
(
7
)) containing them; let the number of deg 3 atoms in each of these components be
m_{j}\geq 2
, then after Definition
13.12
(
7
) we have that (note that each component in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
will have at least one deg 3 atom after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, see Remark
10.6
):

(13.14)

\#_{\mathrm{deg3}}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}}\geq\sum_{j}(m_{j}-1)\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}m_{j}\geq\,(\#_{\mathrm{UD}}-3\cdot\#_{\{33\}}-\mu)/2,

where
\#_{\mathrm{deg3}}
and
\#_{\mathrm{comp}}
are the number of deg 3 atoms and number of components respectively. Then, consider the increment of the left hand side of (
13.14
) in each cutting in Definition
13.12
(
8
). Using Definition
8.3
, and noting that each such cut can only create one fixed end at one deg 4 atom (adding only one deg 3 atom) in each resulting (extra) component, we can verify that this is invariant after cutting one deg 3 atom, and decreases by 1 after each cutting in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
). Since this value should be come
0
after all atoms of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
have been cut, we conclude that

\#_{\{33\}}\geq\frac{1}{2}(\#_{\mathrm{UD}}-3\cdot\#_{\{33\}}-\mu),

and therefore

\#_{\{33\}}\geq\frac{1}{5}\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{UD}}-\mu).

This completes the proof.
∎

13.5.
Toy model I plus/II dichotomy, and finishing the proof

We now put everything together and finish the proof of Proposition
12.18
. Now we may assume (i)–(iii) in Reduction
2
, as well as Reduction
3
(otherwise Proposition
12.18
already follows from Proposition
13.3
); namely, we assume that

•

Each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms. No atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is a parent of any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{D}
) has no C-top (resp. C-bottom) fixed end.

•

The number of full components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
is
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}\leq 10(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Each component in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
has either at least two bonds connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, or one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and one deg 3 atom.

•

The number of good UD connections (i.e. UD connections that are connected to either another UD connection or a deg 3 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, see Proposition
13.13
) satisfy
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/4
.

•

The number of components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is
\#_{\mathrm{comp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\leq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and the number of weakly degenerate atoms and pairs in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is less than
(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

•

We have
|\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|\leq(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, with the set
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
defined in Proposition
13.3
.

Proof of Proposition
12.18
.

Before proceeding with
Stages 1–6
below, we first briefly summarize our strategy, how the different toy models (I plus and II) in Section
11
occur in the proof, and how the different algorithms (
3COMPUP
and
MAINUD
) are used. First note that, from the above assumptions, each of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is essentially a tree (with negligible full components), with negligible number of 2-connections in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
, and sufficiently many good UD connections (all relative to
\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
; same below).

In
Stage 1
, we first cut as free all 2-connections (i.e. atoms in
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
) in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and subsequently cut any deg 2 atoms. This allows to completely get rid of 2-connections in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
at a loss of negligible {4} molecules. The quantity
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}
(i.e. the number of
good UD connections
) may decrease, but the decrease is again negligible, so we still have
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\gtrsim\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
after this stage.

Now, let
\overline{Z}
be the set of deg 3 atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(in reality this needs slight modification to avoid components with only one deg 3 atom, which we omit here). Consider
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z})}
which is the number of ov-components of
Z
; if
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z})}\gtrsim\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then in
Stage 2
, we can apply
3COMPDN
(the dual of
3COMPUP
) to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, which is already sufficient by Proposition
13.7
.

Therefore, we may assume
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z})}\ll\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Now in
Stage 3
, we perform pre-processing to get rid of
\overline{Z}
as well as the set
\overline{Y}
of weakly degenerate atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, by cutting as free the set
S=\textbf{SELECT}(\mathbb{M}_{D},\overline{Z},\overline{Y})
defined in Definition
13.10
. By assumption we have
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z})}\ll\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
|\overline{Y}|\ll\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, so by Proposition
13.11
,
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes ov-proper after cutting
S
as free, and the decrease of
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}
is still negligible, so we still have
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\gtrsim\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
after this stage.

At this point we can see the dichotomy that distinguishes the cases of toy model I plus (where we apply
MAINUD
) and toy model II (where we apply
3COMPUP
). Let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be defined as in Definition
13.12
; by definition, it is easy to see that if we cut
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free, then the C-atoms in
X_{0}
are subsequently cut as deg 2, and the deg 3 C-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
are
exactly those in
X_{1}
(the O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
will not matter in this discussion). As such, this
X_{1}
plays the role of the set
X
in toy model II (see the discussion below Definition
11.15
), so our dichotomy will be based on the value of
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}
, the number of ov-components of
X_{1}
(which is the analog of the number of components of
X
in Section
11
).

Before proceeding, however, we need one more step: in order to apply
3COMPUP
, we need that each component of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free (and subsequently cutting deg 2 atoms) to contain
at least two
deg 3 atoms. If a component
A
violates this, it is easy to see that
before cutting
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, the set
A
has only one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
. This leads to
Stage 4
, where we get rid of these sets
A
by cutting them as fixed, which allows us to apply
3COMPUP
when needed. It is easy to see that we still have
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\gtrsim\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Now we can consider the dichotomy discussed above: if
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}\gtrsim\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then in
Stage 5
we cut
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free and apply
3COMPUP
to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(as in toy model II), which is sufficient by Proposition
13.7
. Finally, if
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}\ll\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then in
Stage 6
we apply
MAINUD
to
M_{UD}
(as in toy model I plus). Note that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is ov-proper and has no weak degeneracy atom thanks to
Stage 3
, so any {33} molecule will be good. We then apply Proposition
13.13
, and use that
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\gtrsim\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}\ll\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, to conclude the proof.

Stage 1.
We cut each atom in
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
as free (see
Figure
63
). If any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
becomes deg 2, we also cut it as free, until
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
contains no deg 2 atoms. Note that the contribution of these operations to
\#_{\{4\}}
is at most
|\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|\leq(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Figure 63.
Cutting atoms in
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
(red) and subsequent deg 2 atoms (blue) in
Stage 1
. Note that cutting each red breaks at most 2 UD connections (cutting each blue breaks none), and any good UD connection that is not broken shall remain good.

By definition of
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
, cutting any atom in it as free does not create any C-top fixed atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
or any C-bottom fixed atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and neither does any subsequent operations cutting deg 2 atoms (because, when we subsequently cut any deg 2 atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
(resp.
\mathbb{M}_{D}
), it must be O-atom or has two bottom (resp. top) fixed ends, so cutting it will not create any top (resp. bottom) fixed end). Moreover, consider any UD connection
e
and the maximal ov-segment containing
e
; cutting each atom in
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}
breaks at most two such ov-segments (where breaking means cutting an atom in this ov-segment as free), and any subsequent cutting of deg 2 atoms cannot break any such ov-segment (for the same reason as above).

If the ov-segment containing
e
is not broken, then
e
will remain a good UD connection if it is initially so. In fact, consider the component
X
of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
containing the
\mathbb{M}_{D}
endpoint of
e
; if
X
is not affected in the cutting (i.e. no atom in
X
is cut and no fixed end is created in
X
) then
e
will remain good as it is; if
X
is affected then the new component containing the
\mathbb{M}_{D}
endpoint of
e
must contain a deg 3 atom.

Therefore, after
Stage 1
, we still have

(13.15)

\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/4-2|\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}|\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/5.

Moreover, after
Stage 1
,
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
is still a canonical UD molecule, and now
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}=\varnothing
. The number of full components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
still satisfy that
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\leq 4(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Stage 2.
Now consider all the components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contain at least two deg 3 atoms, and all the deg 3 atoms contained in one such component. Let the set of all such deg 3 atoms be
\overline{Z}_{0}
, and let
\overline{Y}
be the union of all weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
(see Proposition
12.17
). Let the number of ov-components of
\overline{Z}_{0}
in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
be
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z}_{0})}
as in Propositions
13.7
and
13.11
.

Now, if
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z}_{0})}\geq(10/\upsilon)^{4}(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then we cut
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free. For each component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
containing at least two deg 3 atoms, we cut it into elementary molecules using
3COMPDN
(the dual of
3COMPUP
in Definition
13.6
); for other components we cut them into elementary molecules using
DOWN
. Then cut
\mathbb{M}_{U}
into elementary molecules using
UP
.

In this case, by Proposition
13.7
we get
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
, and

(13.16)

\#_{\{33A\}}\geq(1/10)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z}_{0})}\geq 100\upsilon^{-4}\cdot(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

(13.17)

\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}+(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq 2\cdot(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

which already proves (
12.26
) (recall that all {33A} molecules are treated as good).

Stage 3.
Now suppose
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z}_{0})}\leq(10/\upsilon)^{4}(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Define
\overline{Z}
to be the union of
\overline{Z_{0}}
, together with the deg 3 atoms in those components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contain only one deg 3 atom and at least one atom in
\overline{Y}
. Then for the set
\overline{Z}
we have
\#_{\mathrm{3ovcp}(\overline{Z})}\leq\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z}_{0})}+|\overline{Y}|
. Note also
|\overline{Y}|\leq 10(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
by Reduction
3
.

We then define
S:=\textbf{SELECT}(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{D},\overline{Z},\overline{Y})
as in Definition
13.10
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{D}
contains only those components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that contain an atom in
\overline{Z}\cup\overline{Y}
(but this does not affect Definition
13.10
). We then cut
S
as free and subsequently cut it into elementary molecules using
DOWN
. If any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
becomes deg 2, we also cut it as free, until
\mathbb{M}_{U}
contains no deg 2 atoms.

By Proposition
13.11
(
1
), we know that
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
is still a UD molecule with
\overline{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{2conn}}^{2}=\varnothing
, and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
becomes ov-proper after cutting
S
(those components of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that are not in
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{D}
will contain at most one deg 3 atom by definition of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and are thus automatically ov-proper). Now
\mathbb{M}_{D}
may contain C-bottom fixed ends, but
\mathbb{M}_{U}
still does not contain C-top fixed end. Moreover, by Proposition
13.11
(
2
), we know that the number of ov-segments corresponding to UD connections
e
, that are broken in the process of cutting
S
as free (such an ov-segment must contain an atom in
S
; again subsequent operations cutting deg 2 atoms will not break any such ov-segments), is at most

(13.18)

10\cdot(3\cdot\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\overline{Z})}+|\overline{Y}|)\leq C\cdot(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

therefore we still have

(13.19)

\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/5-C\cdot(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/6,

after
Stage 3
(note that the good UD connections that are not broken will remain good, as proved in
Stage 1
above). The contribution to
\#_{\{4\}}
of these cutting operations is also bounded by (
13.18
). Note that any component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that is not
connected to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
after cutting
S
cannot be full.

Stage 4.
Now the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
satisfies the assumptions (i)–(ii) in Definition
13.12
, and has
\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/6
. Moreover, since
\overline{Y}
has been cut, we know that
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is ov-proper and does not contain any weakly degenerate atom or atom pair. Next we perform the following operations, and repeat until this can no longer be done:

•

If a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{U}
is not connected to
\mathbb{M}_{UD}\backslash A
by any bond, then we cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
.

•

If a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{U}
has only deg 4 atoms, and is connected to
\mathbb{M}_{UD}\backslash A
by only one bond (this bond can be connected to either
\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash A
or
\mathbb{M}_{D}
), then we cut
A

as fixed
and subsequently cut it into elementary molecules using
UP
or
DOWN
(depending on the direction of this only bond).

In the above operation, there are three possibilities: (i) when there is no bond connecting
A
to
\mathbb{M}_{UD}\backslash A
, (ii) when there is only one such bond connecting
A
to an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}\backslash A
, or (iii) when the only bond connects
A
to an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
. Note that cases (i) and (ii) will not affect any good UD connections; in case (iii),
A
is a connected component of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
by itself, so cutting
A
as fixed just removes one full component of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
; in particular case (iii) can occur at most
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}\leq 10(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
times, and each time reduces the number of good UD connections by at most 2 (it may break one UD connection and
e
, and turn at most one more good UD connection into not-good; note that if
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
are both connected to
e
via
\mathbb{M}_{D}
before cutting
A
, then they are also connected to each other via
\mathbb{M}_{D}
after cutting
A
, and thus remain good).

As such, after
Stage 4
, we have that no subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{U}
as described above, and we still have

(13.20)

\#_{\mathrm{UD}}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/6-20(C_{5}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}/7;

The assumptions (i)–(ii) in Definition
13.12
are still preserved (if needed, we can further remove any full component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
that is not connected to
\mathbb{M}_{U}
by a bond, and cut it into elementary molecules
following Definition
13.12
(
8
)
; the number of {4} molecules produced is at most
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}\leq(C_{3}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
and is negligible). Now define the sets
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
as in Definition
13.12
, and consider the value of
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}
as in Proposition
13.13
.

Stage 5.
If
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}\geq 10^{-6}\upsilon^{2}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then we cut
\mathbb{M}_{D}
as free and cut it into elementary molecules
following Definition
13.12
(
8
)
(except that if a component of
\mathbb{M}_{D}
is full, then we first cut any deg 4 atom as free). If any atom in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
becomes deg 2 then we cut it as free, and repeat until
\mathbb{M}_{U}
contains no deg 2 atoms. This produces {2}, {3}, {4}, {33A} and {33B} molecules (with {33B} molecule only in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
and {2} molecule only in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
), with the number of {4} molecules bounded by
\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}
; moreover all {33B} molecules are good due to absence of weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs.

By definitions of
X_{j}
in Definition
13.12
, it is clear that all the C-atoms in
X_{0}
have been cut, and all the C-atoms in
X_{1}
now have deg 3, and all the other C-atoms now have deg 4, see
Figure
62
(all atoms in
X_{0}
will subsequently become deg 2 and be cut when its ov-children have been cut, and cutting all atoms in
X_{0}
leaves all atoms in
X_{1}
as deg 3; on the other hand, if an atom does not belong to
X_{0}\cup X_{1}
then it is not affected by cutting atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}\cup X_{0}
, or cutting any subsequent deg 2 O-atoms, cf. Remark
8.7
).

In addition, we claim that after the above cuttings, each atom
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{1}
must belong to a component of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
with at least two deg 3 atoms. In fact, let the component of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
containing
\mathfrak{n}
be
A
, then before the cuttings,
A
must have one bond connecting to
\mathbb{M}_{UD}\backslash A
, and either has another such bond or a deg 3 atom (due to the operations in
Stage 4
). In either case, after the cutting operations, these two bonds (or one bond and one deg 3 atom) will lead to two fixed ends in
A
(or a fixed end at an O-atom), hence the result.

Now consider all the components of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
after the above cutting operations, that contain an C-atom in
X_{1}
(i.e. ignore the other components, which can always be cut into elementary molecules using
UP
, in view of absence of C-top fixed ends in
\mathbb{M}_{U}
); denote the union of these components by
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{U}
. In
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
before cutting, the number of ov-components of
X_{1}
is
\mu:=\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}\geq 10^{-6}\upsilon^{2}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
. After the cutting operations, the set of deg 3 atoms in
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{U}
equals
X_{1}\cup X_{O}
, where
X_{O}
is the set of deg 3 atoms in
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{U}
that are O-atoms (see
Figure
62
). Now note that, the number of ov-components of
X_{1}\cup X_{O}
is no less than the number of ov-components of
X_{1}
(which is obviously no less than
\mu
, because these cuttings can only break ov-segments but not create them). This is because for each deg 3 O-atom, it must have two edges (free ends or bonds) that are serial, and one free end that is serial with one fixed end; in other words one cannot change to a different ov-segment by going through this O-atom. Therefore, if two atoms in
X_{1}
are ov-connected within
X_{1}\cup X_{O}
, they must also be ov-connected within
X_{1}
.

Then, we can apply
3COMPUP
to
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{U}
(as in toy model II), and use Proposition
13.7
to get

(13.21)

\#_{\{33A\}}\geq(1/10)\cdot\mu\geq C^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

(13.22)

\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{D})}+C(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\leq C(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

which already proves (
12.26
) (with all {33A} molecules treated as good).

Stage 6.
Finally, if
\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}\leq 10^{-6}\upsilon^{2}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then we apply
MAINUD
to cut
\mathbb{M}_{UD}
into elementary molecules (as in toy model I plus). By Proposition
13.13
, and note that all {33B} molecules are good (as they must have both atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{D}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
does not contain weakly degenerate atoms or atom pairs; note also that all the {33B} molecules must have both atoms being C-atoms, because the fixed end at any O-atom cannot be serial with the bond), we get that

(13.23)

\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq\frac{1}{5}\cdot\big(\#_{\mathrm{UD}}-\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}-\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})})\geq\frac{1}{35}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}

(13.24)

\qquad\#_{\{4\}}\leq\#_{\mathrm{fucp}(\mathbb{M}_{U})}+\#_{\mathrm{ovcp}(X_{1})}+C(C_{2}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq 10^{-5}\upsilon^{2}\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime\prime},

which proves (
12.26
). This completes the proof of Proposition
12.18
, and thus proves Proposition
9.7
, in the case when
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
.

The finale: the case when
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
.
Finally we prove Proposition
9.7
when
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
. First, note that we can cut
\mathbb{M}
using
UP
which leads to
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq 0
and
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}\leq|H|
(see Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
12.3
). In view of the extra term
C^{*}|H|
on the right hand side of (
9.51
), we see that (
9.51
) is true if
\rho\lesssim C^{*}|H|
.

Now, suppose
\rho\gg C^{*}|H|(\gg C_{12}^{*}|H|)
, then we can repeat exactly the same proof in Sections
11
–
13
. The only place where Proposition
10.4
(
9
) is used (beyond Proposition
12.3
which is already taken cared of), is in layer selection (Definition
12.12
). However, in this case we always have
\ell_{1}-1\leq\ell-1
, so Proposition
10.4
(
9
) remains true for the set
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1}-1})
, therefore the proof goes exactly the same way as in the
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
case.
∎

14.
Proof of the
f^{\mathcal{A}}
estimates

In this section, we prove Proposition
6.1
. By induction, we only need to prove (
6.1
)–(
6.2
) for
\ell\tau
, assuming that they are already true for
(\ell-1)\tau
. For convenience, below we will denote
f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau):=g
and
(f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau))_{\mathrm{rem}}:=g_{\mathrm{rem}}
, so
g=f((\ell-1)\tau)+g_{\mathrm{rem}}
.

Start from the expansion (
3.16
), where
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
(or
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
) is an unlabeled molecule with one fixed root particle line, and
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|
are defined in (
3.11
)–(
3.12
). Since we need to estimate these quantities with
fixed
value
z
, in this section we will understand that, the unique top end
e
for the root particle line
{\bm{p}}
will be a
fixed end
16
16

16

Note that this
\mathbb{M}
might not be regular (Definition
8.2
), but this does not affect our proof until we get to the cutting algorithm in Section
14.3
, at which time we will make
\mathbb{M}
regular again by a special operation, see
Point 4
in Section
14.3
.
, and all other ends of
\mathbb{M}
will be free.

For each molecule
\mathbb{M}
with single layer
\ell
, define the operator
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
as in (
7.6
), and define

(14.1)

Q_{\mathbb{M}}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}})\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}g(x_{e}+(\ell-1)\tau\cdot v_{e},v_{e}),

where relevant notations are in Definition
7.3
. Then we have the following equalities (note also that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q)
is a function of the single variable
z
which corresponds to the unique fixed end
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
):

(14.2)

\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}(x,v)=(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})(x-\tau v,v),\quad|\mathcal{I}\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{M}}|(x,v)=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}|Q_{\mathbb{M}}|(x-\tau v,v),

where
|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}
is the number of O-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
.

To prove (
14.2
), note that
\mathbb{M}
has single layer
\ell
, so
\ell_{1}[{\bm{p}}]=\ell
for all particle lines
{\bm{p}}
. We may make the change of variables
t_{\mathfrak{n}}\mapsto t_{\mathfrak{n}}-(\ell-1)\tau
and
(x_{e},v_{e})\mapsto(x_{e}+(\ell-1)\tau\cdot v_{e},v_{e})
in the integral (
7.6
), and reduce to the case
\ell=1
. Then (
14.2
) follows from the same proof as in Proposition
7.5
, by replacing
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
with
Q_{\mathbb{M}}\cdot\bm{\delta}(z_{e_{\mathrm{tr}}}-(y,v))
, where
(y,v)=(x-\tau v,v)
represents the final state vector of the root particle transported backwards to time
0
.

In the rest of this section, we will perform the above change of variables and thereby assume
\ell=1
. Now, using (
14.2
), we can rewrite the sum (
3.16
) as

(14.3)

f^{\mathcal{A}}(\ell\tau)=f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}+f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{sub}},

where (recall
|O_{1}(A)|\leq|A|
)

(14.4)

\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}(x,v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}\\
\rho(\mathbb{M})=0,\,|\mathbb{M}|_{p}<\Lambda_{\ell}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})(x-\tau v,v),

\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{sub}}(x,v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}},\,\rho(\mathbb{M})>0\\
\mathrm{or\ }|\mathbb{M}|_{p}\geq\Lambda_{\ell},\mathrm{\ or\ }\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}\end{subarray}}O_{1}\big(\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}|Q_{\mathbb{M}}|\big)(x-\tau v,v)

are the leading and sub-leading terms in
f^{\mathcal{A}}
respectively.

We will study the leading term
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
in Section
14.1
, and establish its asymptotics (see (
14.27
)) in Section
14.2
. In Section
14.3
, we will study the sub-leading term
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{sub}}
and prove it vanishes in the limit. Putting these together, we can then complete the proof of Proposition
6.1
at the end of Section
14.3
.

14.1.
The leading terms

Start with the term
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
in (
14.4
). Here
\mathbb{M}
is a tree as
\rho(\mathbb{M})=0
and
\mathbb{M}
is connected (by Definition
3.20
(
1b
), note
\mathbb{M}
has only one root particle line); moreover, it is straightforward to verify that
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}<\Lambda_{\ell}
actually implies
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
. Therefore, the summation in
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
in (
14.4
) is taken with
only
the assumptions that
\mathbb{M}
is a tree and
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}<\Lambda_{\ell}
. Note also that
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}=|\mathbb{M}|+1
(i.e. number of collisions equals number of particles minus
1
, in the absence of recollisions).

Now we introduce the following definition.

Definition 14.1
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule with single layer
\ell
and one root particle line, which we denote by
{\bm{p}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
. Define the highest atom in
{\bm{p}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
to be
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{rt}}
, and the top end in
{\bm{p}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
to be
e_{\mathrm{rt}}
, which is the unique fixed end of
\mathbb{M}
.

Define
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
to be the set of trees
\mathbb{M}
with
\rho(\mathbb{M})=0
and
|\mathbb{M}|=n
(with
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}=n+1<\Lambda
). Define also
\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}
to be the subset of
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
formed by those
\mathbb{M}
such that every atom in
\mathbb{M}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{rt}}
.

It is known in
[
7
]
that the contribution of the molecules
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}\backslash\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}
to
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
in (
14.4
) is zero, due to a simple cancellation between the terms. We now present this argument, in a similar manner to
[
7
]
.

Proposition 14.2
.

There is an involution
\mathbb{M}\mapsto\mathbb{M}^{\mathrm{twi}}
from
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}\backslash\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}
to itself, such that
|\mathbb{M}^{\prime}|_{\mathrm{O}}=|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}\pm 1
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}})=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
for
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}^{\mathrm{twi}}
. In particular, this implies that

(14.5)

\sum_{\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}\backslash\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}}(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})=0.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}\backslash\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}
, then
\mathbb{M}\neq S_{\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}}
(where
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
, see Definition
3.5
). Choose a highest atom
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash S_{\mathrm{tr}}
. If there is more than one
\mathfrak{m}
, we can select one in an arbitrary way (for example by introducing the artificial total ordering in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
7.5
and select the highest
\mathfrak{m}
). Then define
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}^{\mathrm{twi}}
to be formed from
\mathbb{M}
by turning
\mathfrak{m}
into a C/O atom
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
if
\mathfrak{m}
is originally an O/C atom.

Clearly
|\mathbb{M}^{\prime}|_{\mathrm{O}}=|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}\pm 1
. To prove
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}})=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
, note that
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{M}
(and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
) has no parent, thus it has two top free ends
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}
(the fixed end
e_{\mathrm{rt}}\not\in\{e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime}\}
). Now we compare the integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}})
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
as in (
7.6
). Note that
Q_{\mathbb{M}}=Q_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
does not depend on
z_{e_{1}^{\prime}}
and
z_{e_{2}^{\prime}}
, thus the only difference in the two integrals occurs in the
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
factors. However, by (
7.15
), if we integrate out the variables
(z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{m}})
, then the result would be the same for C and O-atoms, provided that the
Q
in (
7.15
) does not depend on
z_{e_{1}^{\prime}}
and
z_{e_{2}^{\prime}}
(which is true in our case). This proves
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}})=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
, then (
14.5
) follows consequently.
∎

Thanks to (
14.5
), we can rewrite the sum in
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
in (
14.4
) as

(14.6)

f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}(x,v)=g(x-\tau v,v)+\sum_{1\leq n<\Lambda_{\ell}-1}\sum_{\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}}(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})(x-\tau v,v).

Note that, for
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}
, we have
\mathbb{M}=S_{\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}}
is a binary tree in the sense that each atom other than
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}
has a unique parent (and one top fixed end), and two bottom free ends/children in total. As such, it will be convenient to rename the variables at the node
\mathfrak{n}
as

(14.7)

(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}},z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}})\leftrightarrow(z_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}})

where
z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is the variable corresponding to the top free end at
\mathfrak{n}
,
z_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is the one corresponding to the bond connecting
\mathfrak{n}
to its parent (or the free end
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
when
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}
),
z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is the one corresponding to the bottom edge at
\mathfrak{n}
that is serial to the top free end, and
z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is the remaining variable. Given the symmetries of
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
, we always have
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}(z_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}})=\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}(z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}},z_{e_{1}^{\prime}},z_{e_{2}^{\prime}})
.

Next, we will split
g=f((\ell-1)\tau)+g_{\mathrm{rem}}
for each input factor in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
in (
14.6
). We expect those terms containing at least one
g_{\mathrm{rem}}
should vanish in the limit, in view of (
6.2
) satisfied by
g_{\mathrm{rem}}
; to prove this, we need the next proposition.

Proposition 14.3
.

Let
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}
, and let
\widetilde{Q}
be defined as in (
14.1
) but with the input factors
g
replaced by
h_{e}
which depends on
e
. Recall also the
\beta_{\ell}
as in (
1.34
), and define
\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}:=(\beta_{\ell}+\beta_{\ell-1})/2
. Then we have

(14.8)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q})(x-\tau v,v)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}}}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot C^{n}\tau^{n/2}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{-}_{\mathrm{end}}}\|h_{e}\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta_{\ell-1}}}.

Proof.

Recall that
\widetilde{\beta_{\ell}}=\beta_{\ell-1}-(\beta/40\mathfrak{L})
by definition. We divide the proof into 3 parts.

Proof part 1: adjusting Maxwellian weights.
Without loss of generality, we may assume
h_{e}\geq 0
and
\|h_{e}\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta_{\ell-1}}}=1
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}^{-}_{\mathrm{end}}
. Recall
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{\pm}
is the set of all top/bottom ends of
\mathbb{M}
, and
e_{\mathrm{tr}}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}
is the unique fixed end; by energy conservation we have
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{-}_{\mathrm{end}}}|v_{e}|^{2}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{+}_{\mathrm{end}}}
. Using also the notion
z_{j}^{\mathfrak{n}}
in (
14.7
), this implies that

(14.9)

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\beta_{\ell-1}|v_{e}|^{2}=\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v|^{2}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}\frac{\beta}{40\mathfrak{L}}|v_{e}|^{2}+\sum_{\mathfrak{n}}\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}|^{2},

where
v=v_{e_{\mathrm{tr}}}
is the output variable in the function
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q})(x-\tau v,v)
. Since
\|h\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}}=\|e^{\beta|v|^{2}}h\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{0}}
by (
1.15
), we can replace
h_{e}
by
e^{\beta_{\ell-1}|v_{e}|^{2}}h_{e}
and apply (
14.9
), to reduce (
14.8
) to the following estimate:

(14.10)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{Q})(x-\tau v,v)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{0}}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot C^{n}\tau^{n/2},\quad\mathrm{given\ }\|h_{e}\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{0}}=1,

where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{Q})=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}\left(\widetilde{Q}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}e^{-\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}|^{2}}\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}e^{-(\beta/40\mathfrak{L})|v_{e}|^{2}}\right)
.

Proof part 2: decomposition of
\widetilde{Q}
.
By the exponential decay in the formula of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{Q})
, it is easy to see that we can restrict the support of
Q
to
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
for each
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}
, and therefore for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
by energy conservation, in the same way as in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
6.2
. Moreover, by repeating the same proof there (in Section
9.2
), we can also decompose

(14.11)

\widetilde{Q}=\sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\widetilde{Q}_{\alpha},\quad\sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\leq C^{n},

where each
\widetilde{Q}_{\alpha}
is uniformly bounded and is supported in the set
|x_{e}-x_{e}^{*}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
for each
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}
, with some choice of
(x_{e}^{*})
depending on
