\alpha
.

Since
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
, this support condition implies that
|x_{e_{\mathrm{tr}}}-x_{e_{0}}^{*}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
where
e_{0}
is the bottom end of the root particle line (
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
is its top end). As we have
x=x_{e_{\mathrm{tr}}}+\tau\cdot v_{e_{\mathrm{tr}}}
for the variable
x
in (
14.8
), we know that for each
\alpha
, this
x
belongs to a fixed ball of radius
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
. This ball can be divided into
\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
unit cubes, so under this support condition we have
\|f\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{0}}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\cdot\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}
due to (
1.15
).

Summing up, using (
14.11
) and the above discussions, we see that (
14.10
) would follow if we can prove the following estimate (uniformly in
\alpha
):

(14.12)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{Q}_{\alpha})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq(C\tau^{1/2})^{n},\quad\mathrm{given\ }\|\widetilde{Q}_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}=1.

Proof part 3: estimating the integral.
To prove (
14.12
), we first invoke (
9.90
) in Proposition
9.8
with
v_{e}
replaced by
\mathfrak{L}^{-1/2}v_{e}
, which allows us to bound the product of the cross-sections in the
\bm{\Delta}(\cdots)
factors by

\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}(1+|v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}|)\leq\mathfrak{L}^{n/2}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}(1+|\mathfrak{L}^{-1/2}v_{e_{1}}-\mathfrak{L}^{-1/2}v_{e_{2}}|)\leq C^{n}\mathfrak{L}^{n/2}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{M}}^{5/6}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{\beta}{40\mathfrak{L}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}|v_{e}|^{2}\bigg),

where the products are taken over all atoms
\mathfrak{n}
with
(v_{e_{1}},v_{e_{2}})
being the two bottom edges at
\mathfrak{n}
, and
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{M}}
is defined in (
9.86
) with
T
replaced by
\mathbb{M}
(which is also a binary tree as in Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
9.8
). Here we have used the elementary inequality
\exp(C|v|^{4/3})\leq C\exp((\beta/40)|v|^{2})
. This allows to bound

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{Q}_{\alpha})\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{n}\mathfrak{L}^{n/2}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{M}}^{5/6}\cdot\bigg(\int_{\mathcal{D}}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}\bigg)\cdot\sup_{\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathcal{D}}\|V_{n}(z,\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}})\|_{L^{\infty}},

\displaystyle V_{n}(z,\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-(d-1)n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*}|}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}e^{-\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}|^{2}}\bm{\Delta}^{\dagger}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\,\mathrm{d}z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}},

where
\mathcal{D}
is as in (
7.5
) but with
\ell^{\prime}
replaced by
1
, and

\bm{\Delta}^{\dagger}
is as in (
7.3
)–(
7.4
) but without the
[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega]_{-}
factor, and we have relabeled the variables at each atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
using the variables
z_{j}^{\mathfrak{n}}
as in (
14.7
). Note also that
|\mathcal{E}_{*}|-2|\mathbb{M}|=n
in (
7.6
) using Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
7.5
(and
|\mathcal{E}|-|\mathcal{E}_{*}|=1
and
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}=n+1
).

Since
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{M}}\geq 1
and
\int_{\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}=\tau^{n}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{M}}^{-1}
by (
9.86
), and recall that
\tau\mathfrak{L}=t_{\mathrm{fin}}\leq C
, we see that (
14.12
) will follow once we show that
\sup_{\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathcal{D}}\|V_{n}(z,\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C^{n}
. The latter is straightforward as one calculates the integrals from top to bottom in the binary tree
\mathbb{M}
as follows: for each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
, we fix
z_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}
and integrate in the variables
(z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}})
, noting that for any node
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
we have

\varepsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{2d})^{3}}e^{-\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}|^{2}}\cdot\bm{\Delta}^{\dagger}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\,\mathrm{d}z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}\leq C

by a simple variant of (
9.5
). This finishes the proof.
∎

Now, using Proposition
14.3
, we may split
g=f((\ell-1)\tau)+g_{\mathrm{rem}}((\ell-1)\tau)
for each input factor in in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(Q_{\mathbb{M}})
in (
14.6
). Define
f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
to be the same as in (
14.6
) but with all input factors
g=f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)
replaced by
f((\ell-1)\tau)
. By Proposition
14.3
and (
6.2
) and (
1.29
), it is easy to see that

(14.13)

\big\|f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}-f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}\big\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta_{\ell}}}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}\sum_{n\geq 0}(C\tau^{1/2})^{n}\cdot\varepsilon^{\theta_{\ell-1}}\leq\varepsilon^{\theta_{\ell}}/10.

Here we have used the fact that the number of molecules
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}
is bounded by
C^{n}
, which follows from the well-known upper bound for the number of binary trees.

14.2.
Approximation by Boltzmann iterates

In view of (
14.13
), now we only need to estimate
f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
. In this subsection, we will approximate it by the iterates, up to order
\Lambda-2
, of the Boltzmann equation. To do this, we first perform a change of variables in the integral appearing in the definition of
f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
. We adopt the same relabeling of the variables
z_{j}^{\mathfrak{n}}
at each atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
, then there are two cases of
\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\Delta}_{\mathfrak{n}}
in (
14.6
):

•

When
\mathfrak{n}
is C-atom, we have
\bm{\Delta}_{+}
defined by

(14.14)

\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}_{+}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{\delta}\big(x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3})\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4})\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}+[(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-[(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2})\cdot\omega]\omega\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(|x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+};

•

When
\mathfrak{n}
is O-atom, we have
\bm{\Delta}_{-}
defined by

(14.15)

\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}_{-}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{\delta}(x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3})\cdot\bm{\delta}(x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4})\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}\big)\cdot\bm{\delta}\big(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\times\bm{\delta}\big(|x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1})|-\varepsilon\big)\cdot\big[(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}.

Here we have used that
\big[(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}=\big[(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4})\cdot\omega\big]_{-}=\big[(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}
for the given relabeling in (
14.7
); also recall that
\omega:=\varepsilon^{-1}(x_{e_{1}}-x_{e_{2}}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{e_{1}}-v_{e_{2}}))=\varepsilon^{-1}(x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}))
. Now, recall that the integration of the
\bm{\Delta}_{\pm}
factor at each atom is performed by first integrating in
(z_{3}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{4}^{\mathfrak{n}})
(with
(z_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}},z_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}})
fixed), followed by integrating in
z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}
followed by that in
z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}
and
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Since the integral over
x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}
involves a
\bm{\delta}
measure supported on a sphere centered at
x_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-v_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}})
, we can make the change variables
x_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}=x_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-v_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}})+\varepsilon\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
as in the proof of Proposition
9.1
, and write

\bm{\delta}\big(|x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-x^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}+t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}-v^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1})|-\varepsilon\big)=\varepsilon^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bm{\delta}\big(x_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}-x_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-v_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}})+\varepsilon\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}.

As a result, we can now rewrite the integral defining
f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
as follows:

(14.16)

\displaystyle f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}(x,v)

\displaystyle=f\big((\ell-1)\tau,x-\tau v,v\big)+\sum_{1\leq n<\Lambda_{\ell}-1}\sum_{\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}^{*}}(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(x-\tau v,v),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*}|}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},\omega_{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}f(({\ell-1})\tau,z_{e})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}}\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}},

where
\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{\mathcal{M}}:=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}
is the same as
{\bm{\Delta}_{\pm}}
in (
14.14
)–(
14.15
) except that
\omega
is replaced by
\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
and the last
\bm{\delta}
in both expressions is replaced by
\bm{\delta}(x_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}-x_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-v_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}})+\varepsilon\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
.

Next, we will explain the relation of the above expansion in (
14.16
) with the Duhamel expansion of the solution
f(t,z)
to the Boltzmann equation on the interval
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]
.

Proposition 14.4
.

The solution
f(s,z)
of the Botlzmann equation on the interval
[(\ell-1)\tau,\ell\tau]

can be written as
f(s,z):=F(s-(\ell-1)\tau,x-sv+(\ell-1)\tau v,v)
where
F(t,z)
is defined for
t\in[0,\tau]
as

(14.17)

\displaystyle F(t,z)

\displaystyle:=F^{\mathrm{(app)}}+F^{\mathrm{(rem)}},

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{(app)}}

\displaystyle:=f((\ell-1)\tau,z)+\sum_{1\leq k<\Lambda_{\ell}-1}F^{(k)}(t,z),\qquad F^{\mathrm{(rem)}}:=\sum_{k\geq\Lambda_{\ell}-1}F^{(k)}(t,z),

and
F^{(k)}(t,z)
is defined by

(14.18)

\displaystyle F^{(k)}(t,z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{k}^{*}}(-1)^{|\mathbb{M}|_{\mathrm{O}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(t,z),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{D}^{t}}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*}|}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(0)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},\omega_{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}f(({\ell-1})\tau,z_{e})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}}\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}.

Here
\mathcal{D}^{t}
is the same as
\mathcal{D}
in (
7.5
) but with
\ell^{\prime}
replaced by
1
and with the extra restriction that
t_{\mathfrak{n}}<t
for all
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}
, and
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}^{(0)}_{\pm}
is the same as
{\bm{\Delta}_{\pm}}
in (
14.14
) but with
\omega
replaced by
\omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
and the last
\bm{\delta}
in both expressions replaced by
\bm{\delta}(x_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}-x_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-v_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}))
. Note that this makes
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})
independent of
\varepsilon
. Moreover, the following estimate holds for any
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{k}^{*}
:

(14.19)

\big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(x-\tau v,v)\big\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}}}\leq C^{k}\tau^{k/2}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{-}_{\mathrm{end}}}\|f((\ell-1)\tau)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}}.

Proof.

First notice that
F
satisfies the following integral equation on the interval
[0,\tau]
:

(14.20)

\displaystyle F(t,z)

\displaystyle=f((\ell-1)\tau,z)+\mathcal{C}(F,F)(t,z),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(f,g)(t,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big[(v-v_{1})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\cdot\big(f(s,x+vs-v^{\prime}s,v^{\prime})g(s,x+vs-v_{1}^{\prime}s,v_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle-f(s,x,v)g(s,x+vs-v_{1}s,v_{1})\big)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{1}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\pm)}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6d}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(0)}(z,z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},s)\cdot f(s,z_{3})\cdot g(s,z_{4})\prod_{i=2}^{4}\mathrm{d}z_{i}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}s,

where
v^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime}
are defined from
(v,v_{1})
as in (
1.14
).

Next, notice that
F^{(k)}
defined in (
14.18
) satisfies the following rrecurrence relation:

F^{(k)}=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k-1}\mathcal{C}(F^{(k_{1})},F^{(k_{2})}),\qquad F^{(0)}(t,z):=f((\ell-1)\tau,z),

which can be checked readily by induction using the last equation in (
14.20
). Here we note that each molecule
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{k}^{*}
consists of one highest atom
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}
which is either C-atom (corresponding to
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{+}^{(0)}
in (
14.20
)) or O-atom (corresponding to
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{-}^{(0)}
in (
14.20
)), and two sub-molecules
\mathbb{M}^{i}\in\mathfrak{M}_{k_{i}}^{*}
which are the sets of descendants of the two children
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
of
\mathfrak{n}^{*}
, with
k_{1}+k_{2}=k-1
. The cases when some of these two children are absent are modified trivially (with
k_{1}=0
or
k_{2}=0
).

The convergence of the infinite series
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F^{(k)}
then follows, once we establish (
14.19
), in view that
\mathfrak{M}_{k}
contains
O(C^{k})
molecules (by counting binary trees), and our choice of
\tau
. The fact that this sum solves (
14.20
) follows by writing down the equation satisfied by
F^{\leq k}=\sum_{j\leq k}F^{(i)}
, which reads

(14.21)

F^{(0)}+\mathcal{C}(F^{\leq k},F^{\leq k})-F^{\leq k}=\sum_{k+1\leq i\leq 2k}\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}=i-1}\mathcal{C}(F^{(i_{1})},F^{(i_{2})}),

and noticing that
\mathcal{C}(F^{(i_{1})},F^{(i_{2})})
is an algebraic sum of at most
C^{i}
terms
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(t,z)
where
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}_{i}^{*}
. This gives that the right hand side of (
14.21
) can be bounded by
C^{k}\tau^{k/2}
using (
14.19
) again, which converges geometrically to zero. This gives that
F=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F^{(k)}
.

As such, it remains to justify (
14.19
). But, this follows from exactly the same proof of Proposition
14.3
, only with
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
replaced by
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}
.
∎

Now we can compare
f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
with
f(\ell\tau)
. The key step is contained in the next proposition.

Proposition 14.5
.

With the expressions defined in (
14.16
) and (
14.18
), we have that

(14.22)

\|\big[\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})-\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(\tau)\big](x-\tau v,v)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}}}\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}C^{k}\tau^{k/2},

for any
\mathbb{M}\in\mathfrak{M}^{*}_{k}
with
k<\Lambda_{\ell}-1
.

Proof.

First define
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(1)}:=\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}-\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(0)}
. Then we can write

(14.23)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(z)-\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(\tau,z)=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M},\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(z),

where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M},\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})
is an expression of the following form:

(14.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M},\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*}|}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathfrak{n}_{0}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(*)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle\times\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(1)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}}f(({\ell-1})\tau,z_{e})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}}\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}},

where
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(*)}\in\{\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm},\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(0)}\}
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{0}=S_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}=\{\mathfrak{n}_{0}\}\cup\mathbb{M}_{1}\cup\mathbb{M}_{2}
, where
\mathbb{M}_{j}=S_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
are the children of
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
(with trivial modifications if any
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
is absent), and
\mathbb{M}_{3}=\mathbb{M}\backslash\mathbb{M}_{0}
. Note in particular that the parent
\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{\mathrm{pr}}
of
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
belongs to
\mathbb{M}_{3}
, and the bond
e_{\mathrm{pr}}
between
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{\mathrm{pr}}
in
\mathbb{M}
becomes a bottom free end at
\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{\mathrm{pr}}
in
\mathbb{M}_{3}
, and a top fixed end at
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
. We then have
\mathbb{M}_{i}\in\mathfrak{M}_{k_{i}}^{*}
, where
k=k_{0}+k_{3}
and
k_{0}=k_{1}+k_{2}+1
. Define also the same notions
\mathcal{D}
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{*})
etc. for the sub-molecules
\mathbb{M}_{i}
, but with subscript
i
(the fixed ends will be specified below).

By first integrating in the
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}
variables, then in
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
and finally in
\mathbb{M}_{3}
variables, we get

(14.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M},\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{3}}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{|\mathbb{M}_{3}|}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*3}|}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{3}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(*)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{Q}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(t_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{\mathrm{pr}}},z_{e_{\mathrm{pr}}})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{3,-}\backslash\{e_{\mathrm{pr}}\}}f(({\ell-1})\tau,z_{e})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*3}}\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}_{3}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}_{3}},

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}
is defined as (with the fixed top end in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
being at
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
)

(14.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(t,z):=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}^{t}}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{|\mathbb{M}_{0}|}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*0}|}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{0}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}_{0}\}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(*)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle\times\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(1)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{0,-}}f(({\ell-1})\tau,z_{e})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*0}}\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}_{0}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6d}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(1)}(z,z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},s)\cdot\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}})(s,z_{3})\cdot\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}})(s,z_{4})\prod_{i=2}^{4}\mathrm{d}z_{i}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}s.

Here in (
14.26
), the
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})
is defined in the same way as
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})
in (
14.18
), but allowing arbitrary choices between
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}
and
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(0)}
at each factor. If we define
f^{(j)}(z):=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}_{j}})(s,z)
, then we obtain, after integrating in the
x
variables, that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(t,x,v)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big[f^{(2)}(x+s(v-v_{4})+\varepsilon\omega,v_{4})-f^{(2)}(x+s(v-v_{4}),v_{4})\big]

\displaystyle\times f^{(1)}(x+s(v-v_{3}),v_{3})\cdot\big[(v-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\,\mathrm{d}v_{2}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}s,

where
(v_{3},v_{4})
equals
(v,v_{2})
if
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is O-atom, and equals
(v^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime})
defined by (
1.14
) from
(v,v_{2})
if
\mathfrak{n}
is C-atom. By mean value theorem, we can rewrite this expression of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(t,x,v)
as

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f^{(1)}(x+s(v-v_{3}),v_{3})\cdot(\omega\cdot\nabla_{x})f^{(2)}(x+s(v-v_{4}))+\theta\varepsilon\omega,v_{4})\cdot\big[(v-v_{2})\cdot\omega\big]_{+}\,\mathrm{d}v_{2}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6d}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(\theta)}(z,z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},s)\cdot\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}})(s,z_{3})\cdot(\omega\cdot\nabla_{x})\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}})(s,z_{4})\prod_{i=2}^{4}\mathrm{d}z_{i}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(\theta)}
is the same as
{\bm{\Delta}_{\pm}}
in (
14.14
)–(
14.15
) except that the last
\bm{\delta}
in both expressions is replaced by
\bm{\delta}(x_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}}-x_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}}(v_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}}-v_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}})+\theta\varepsilon\omega_{\mathfrak{n}})
.

Now, given the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})(s,z_{4})
similar to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})
, it is clear that its
\nabla_{x}
gradient can be written as a sum of at most
|\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{2,-}|
terms, each of which having the same form as
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}^{*}(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{M}})
itself, but with one of the input factors
f((\ell-1)\tau,z_{e})
replaced by
\nabla_{x}f((\ell-1)\tau,z_{e})
. As a result, we can bound (
14.23
) by at most
\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
expressions of the following form: for some fixed
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
and some bottom free end
e_{0}
at a descendant atom of
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
, the expression is given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{|\mathbb{M}|}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d|\mathcal{E}_{*}|}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathfrak{n}_{0}}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(*)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}})\cdot\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}_{\pm}^{(\theta)}(z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{1},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{2},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{3},z^{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}_{4},t_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}\backslash\{e_{0}\}}|f(({\ell-1})\tau,z_{e})|\cdot|\nabla_{x}f(({\ell-1})\tau,z_{e_{0}})|\,\mathrm{d}\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}}\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}}\mathrm{d}\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}}\mathrm{d}\theta.

Now the integral in
(\bm{z}_{\mathcal{E}_{*}},\bm{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}},\bm{t}_{\mathbb{M}})
is uniformly bounded in
\theta\in[0,1]
, following exactly the same proof of Proposition
14.3
, using the
\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}
bound for both
n
and
\nabla_{x}n
in (
1.29
). This proves Proposition
14.5
.
∎

Now, by combining (
14.13
), (
14.16
), (
14.17
)–(
14.18
), we conclude that

(14.27)

\big\|f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}-f(\ell\tau)\big\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta_{\ell}}}\leq\varepsilon^{\theta_{\ell}}/5.

Here the difference between
f_{1}^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{led}}
and
F^{(\mathrm{app})}
is bounded using (
14.22
) and counting binary trees, and the contribution of
F^{(\mathrm{rem})}
is bounded using (
14.19
) and our choice of
\Lambda_{\ell}
.

14.3.
The sub-leading terms

We now turn to
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{sub}}
in (
14.3
)–(
14.4
). This is a sum over molecules
\mathbb{M}
. Let
|\mathbb{M}|=n
and
\rho(\mathbb{M})=\rho
, note that either
\rho>0
or
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}\geq\Lambda_{\ell}
by definition of
f^{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{sub}}
(if
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
then the extra O-atom creates a cycle). The same proof of Proposition
7.2
allows us to bound the number of molecules
\mathbb{M}
by
C^{n}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
, so it suffices to bound each individual term
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}|Q_{\mathbb{M}}|
. Just as in the proof of Proposition
6.2
(see Section
9.2
), it suffices to construct an operation sequence (after turning
\mathbb{M}
into regular, see
Point 4
below) that produces at most
C^{n}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
sub-cases, and prove that each sub-case satisfies (cf. (
9.53
))

(14.28)

\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}|Q^{\prime}|(x-\tau v,v)\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}}}\leq\tau^{n/9}\cdot\varepsilon^{(\upsilon/3)+(C_{14}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho}.

The proof of (
14.28
) will follow the same arguments as in Sections
9
–
13
. We will only elaborate on the few places where the proof needs to be adapted to the current setting.

Point 1: adjusting Maxwellian weights.
Start with the input factors
g=f^{\mathcal{A}}((\ell-1)\tau)
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}|Q_{\mathbb{M}}|
. We decompose
g=f((\ell-1)\tau)+g_{\mathrm{rem}}
as before, where
f((\ell-1)\tau)
and
g_{\mathrm{rem}}
satisfy (
1.29
) and (
6.2
) respectively. Let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}
be such that we have
g_{\mathrm{rem}}
for
e\in\mathcal{V}
and
f((\ell-1)\tau)
for
e\not\in\mathcal{V}
, then we already gain a factor
\varepsilon^{\theta_{\ell-1}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|}
from the inputs in
\mathcal{V}
.

Next, we use
\|h\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}}=\|e^{\beta|v|^{2}}h\|_{\mathrm{Bol}^{0}}
to reduce the
\mathrm{Bol}^{\beta}
norms to
\mathrm{Bol}^{0}
norms. By conservation of energy
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{-}_{\mathrm{end}}}|v_{e}|^{2}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{+}_{\mathrm{end}}}
we have

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{V}}\beta_{\ell-1}|v_{e}|^{2}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}\backslash\mathcal{V}}\beta|v_{e}|^{2}\geq\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v|^{2}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{V}}(\beta/40\mathfrak{L})|v_{e}|^{2}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}\backslash\mathcal{V}}(\beta/2)|v_{e}|^{2}+(\beta/40\mathfrak{L})|v|^{2}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}\backslash\{e_{\mathrm{tr}}\}}\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v_{e}|^{2},

so arguing as in Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
14.3
, we can reduce
Q_{\mathbb{M}}
to
\widetilde{Q}
where the input factors are bounded in
\mathrm{Bol}^{0}
norm, and we replace
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}
with

(14.29)

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}}|\widetilde{Q}|:=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}\bigg(|\widetilde{Q}|\cdot\varepsilon^{\theta_{\ell-1}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot e^{-(\beta/40\mathfrak{L})|v|^{2}}\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathcal{V}}e^{-(\beta/40\mathfrak{L})|v_{e}|^{2}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{-}\backslash\mathcal{V}}e^{-(\beta/2)|v_{e}|^{2}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{end}}^{+}\backslash\{e_{\mathrm{tr}}\}}e^{-\widetilde{\beta}_{\ell}|v_{e}|^{2}}\bigg).

Define the post-operation quantity
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}|\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}|
in the same way.

Point 2: decomposition of
\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}
.
This is the same as in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
14.3
. We decompose
\widetilde{Q}
(and consequently
\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}
by decomposing the input functions as in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
6.2
, to reduce to estimating a single
\widetilde{Q}_{\alpha}^{\prime}
under the support condition that
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
and each
x_{e}
belonging to a fixed ball of radius
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
. By the same arguments in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
14.3
, we can also bound the
\mathrm{Bol}^{0}
norm of
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}|\widetilde{Q}_{\alpha}^{\prime}|
by its
L^{\infty}
norm up to a factor
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
.

Point 3: applying Propositions
9.8
and
9.9
.
In view of the weights in (
14.29
), we will choose
\gamma=\beta/(10^{5}\mathfrak{L})
in Proposition
9.8
, then the
\exp(\cdots)
factor on the right hand side of (
9.84
) is acceptable and can be absorbed by the decay factor in (
14.29
), so we can apply Proposition
9.8
in the same way as in Part 3 of the proof of Proposition
14.3
, except that we now have an additional factor
\gamma^{-n/2}\leq(C\mathfrak{L})^{n/2}
on the right hand side of (
9.84
). Note that
\tau\mathfrak{L}\leq C
, this additional factor can then be absorbed by the time integral in (
9.85
), which provides a power
\tau^{n}
.

As for Proposition
9.9
, let
e
be any (top or bottom) free end of
\mathbb{M}
. Thanks to the decay factor in (
14.29
), if we restrict to
|v_{e}|\sim X_{e}
, then we will gain a factor
e^{-\beta X_{e}^{2}/4}

unless

e\in\mathcal{V}\cup\{e_{\mathrm{tr}}\}
, which we treat as exceptional cases. In non-exceptional cases, the gain
e^{-\beta X_{e}^{2}/4}
absorbs the power
X_{e}^{d}
in (
9.110
) leading to a constant
\leq C
; in exceptional cases we simply apply the rough bound
|v_{e}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
. This leads to the loss of
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}(|\mathcal{V}|+1)}
, but can be absorbed by the gain
\varepsilon^{\theta_{\ell-1}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|}
in (
14.29
) (if
|\mathcal{V}|=0
then we only lose
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
which is acceptable in view of the gain in (
14.28
)).

Point 4: turning
\mathbb{M}
to regular.
Note that
\mathbb{M}
has a fixed end
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
at an atom
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}
. By Definition
8.2
, this might not be regular if
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}
is O-atom and
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
is serial with a bond. In this case, we then turn it into a regular molecule as follows: take the maximal ov-segment containing
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
, then remove every bond in
\sigma
, draw a bottom free end at
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}
, draw a simple pair (Definition
8.2
) at each intermediate O-atom in
\sigma
, and draw a top fixed end at the lowest atom of
\sigma
(
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}}
is the highest atom), see
Figure
64
.

This operation is akin to the cutting operation in Definition
8.3
, and it is easy to prove (similar to Proposition
8.14
) that we can match the edges before and after the operation (similar to Definition
8.12
), such that the value of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{M}}|Q_{\mathbb{M}}|
is unchanged after the operation. For convenience, we still call the molecule
\mathbb{M}
after the operation, which is now regular.

Figure 64.
Left: operation in
Point 4
. Middle: this operation may break a cycle and create two deg 3 atoms. Right: this may also disconnect different clusters, creating one deg 3 atom in each component.

Point 5: the cutting algorithm.
By all the above preparations, and by repeating the same proof as in Sections
9
–
13
, it now suffices to construct an operation sequence that produces at most
C^{n}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
sub-cases, and for each sub-case
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
contains only elementary molecules, and that

(14.30)

\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{4\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0,\quad\textrm{and }\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq(10\Gamma)^{-1}\cdot\rho.

Here we may assume
\rho>0
(otherwise
|\mathbb{M}|_{p}\geq\Lambda_{\ell}
and thus
n=|\mathbb{M}|\geq\Lambda_{\ell}-1
using also
\rho=0
, in which case the power
\tau^{n}
coming from time integration (
9.85
) provides sufficient gain, in view of our choice of
\Lambda_{\ell}
).

First assume
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{err}}
, then it is obtained from some
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
by creating one more O-atom. If
\rho(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})>0
, we can delete this O-atom and reduce to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
which will be discussed below; if not, then
\rho(\mathbb{M})=1
.
It is easy to see that, after the operation in
Point 4
,
\mathbb{M}
has no full component, no deg 2 atom and no C-bottom fixed end, and at least one component must contain a cycle or two deg 3 atoms (the two deg 3 atoms case may occur if the operation in
Point 4
breaks a cycle). We then apply
DOWN
to
\mathbb{M}
, so we get
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{4\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
, and
\#_{\{33A\}}\geq 1
by Proposition
12.2
(
3
). Moreover, the two fixed ends of this {33A} molecule cannot both belong to the ov-segment containing
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
(this follows from Proposition
10.4
(
2
)), so one of them must come from cutting as free another deg 3 atom. This then leads to at least one
good
molecule, by repeating the proof of Proposition
12.2
(
4
).

Now let us assume
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}
. Let
\mathbb{M}_{j}
be the clusters of
\mathbb{M}
, with
\mathbb{M}_{1}
containing the unique fixed end
e_{\mathrm{tr}}
(before the operation in
Point 4
). Then, after the operation in
Point 4
, there is only one deg 3 atom in the component containing
\mathbb{M}_{1}
, and any other component of
\mathbb{M}
also contains exactly one deg 3 atom
\mathfrak{o}_{j}
on an ov-segment within some
\mathbb{M}_{j}
. For convenience we call such
\mathbb{M}_{j}

lovely
.

We then follow the strategy in
Choice 2
in the proof of Proposition
12.5
(and formally allow clusters to be empty ends as in that proof). Namely, we cut
\mathbb{M}_{1}
as free and cut it into elementary components using
DOWN
; for any lovely
\mathbb{M}_{j}
, we also cut
\mathbb{M}_{j}\cup\{\mathfrak{o}_{j}\}
as free and cut it into elementary components using
DOWN
. This then creates more lovely
\mathbb{M}_{j}
and then we repeat this process until all atoms have been cut. Here each
\mathbb{M}_{j}
(or
\mathbb{M}_{j}\cup\{\mathfrak{o}_{j}\}
) contains exactly one deg 3 atom when it is cut, which is proved using Proposition
10.4
(
3
), as in
Choice 2
in the proof of Proposition
12.5
.

As such, by Proposition
12.2
(
3
), we get a good component for each
\mathbb{M}_{j}
with
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})>0
(i.e. with a cycle). Since
\rho=\sum_{j}\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})
and
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
by Proposition
10.4
(
4
), we know that the number of
j
with
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})>0
, and hence the number of good components, is
\geq\rho/\Gamma
. This proves (
14.30
), which then completes the proof of Proposition
6.1
.

15.
Proof of the error term estimates

In this section we prove Proposition
6.3
. The key ingredient here is a cutting algorithm specifically designed for the layer
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{trc.err}}
for the molecule
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{trc.err}}
. This is stated in Proposition
15.1
below.

Proposition 15.1
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a connected molecule with only C-atoms, with
|\mathbb{M}|\leq|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
and
\Gamma<\rho(\mathbb{M})\leq 2\Gamma
. Then there exists an operation sequence (formed by cutting and splitting) which produces at most
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}}
sub-cases, such that in each sub-case,
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
consists of good, normal, and bad elementary molecules, and the numbers
\#_{\mathrm{good}}
and
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}
of good and bad molecules satisfy that

(15.1)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{good}})-d\cdot(\#_{\mathrm{bad}})\geq\upsilon/2.

Proof.

The proof will occupy Section
15.1
.
∎

We now prove Proposition
6.3
assuming Proposition
15.1
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
assuming Proposition
15.1
.

Start with (
3.20
). The term
\mathrm{Err}((\ell-1)\tau)
is already controlled using (
3.19
) and (
6.4
), so we only need to estimate the summation over
\mathbb{M}
. Note that only the root particles of
\mathbb{M}
are labeled.

Let
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bm{\Lambda}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{trc.err}}
and
r(\mathbb{M})=[s]
be fixed. We will assume
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})>\Gamma
in (
3.7
); otherwise
|\mathbb{M}_{\ell}|_{p}>\Lambda_{\ell}
, in which case we can argue similarly as below, and the powers of
\tau^{\Lambda_{\ell}/10}
coming from time integration will provide enough gain, similar to the proof of (
6.4
).

Define
\rho
as in (
7.1
), but only summing
s_{\ell^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1
. Define also
\kappa
to be the number of root particle lines that have an atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
(the other root particle lines occur as empty ends in
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
). By Proposition
7.2
, the number of choices for layers
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}\,(\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell-1)
, initial links and merging top and bottom ends, is bounded by
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
.

As for
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, by Definition
3.20
(
4
), we know it equals
s-\kappa
empty ends (with fixed labels) plus a graph, which equals a tree plus
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})\leq 2\Gamma
edges. Note also that the
\kappa
root particles have fixed labels and must be identified in the graph. By the same arguments as in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition
7.2
, the number of choices of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
is bounded by
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}(1+\kappa)}
(the factor
|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\kappa}
comes from identifying the
\kappa
root particles in the graph).

With the number of choices for
\mathbb{M}
under control, now fix one
\mathbb{M}
. By the same proof of Proposition
6.2
(in Section
9.2
), we know that Proposition
6.3
holds, provided that there exists an operation sequence on
\mathbb{M}
that produces at most
C^{|\mathbb{M}|}|\log\varepsilon|^{C^{*}\rho}
sub-cases, and for each sub-case we have only good, normal, and bad elementary molecules in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, and the following analog of (
9.51
) in Proposition
9.7
holds:

(15.2)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+(d-1)(s-\#_{\mathrm{bad}})\geq(100d^{2}C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot(1+\rho+\kappa).

Now we focus on the proof of (
15.2
). If a particle line
{\bm{p}}\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell})=[s]
is an empty end in
\mathbb{M}
and
{\bm{p}}\not\in r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell-1})
, then we remove it from
\mathbb{M}
, which does not change the value of (
15.2
) (an empty end counts as a bad molecule by Definition
9.4
), so below we will assume no such particle line exists. Consider two cases.

Case 1
: assume the sole component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
has no particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell-1})
. In this case,
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
and
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell}
are two disconnected components of
\mathbb{M}
, which we can cut separately. Note that
|r(\mathbb{M}_{<\ell})|=s-\kappa
. By applying Proposition
9.7
to
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell}
and Proposition
15.1
to
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
, we get

(15.3)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+(d-1)(s-\#_{\mathrm{bad}})\geq(d-1)\kappa+(\upsilon/2)+(C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho,

which implies (
15.2
).

Case 2
: assume the sole component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
has at least one particle line in
r(\mathbb{M}_{\ell-1})
. In this case, we can verify that Definition
3.20
(
1d
) actually holds for
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
. Moreover Definition
3.20
(
1a
) and (
1c
) also hold for
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
with slightly worse constants (which do not matter in the proof in Sections
9
–
13
), as does Definition
3.20
(
1b
) if we artificially add a particle line in the sole component of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
to
r(\mathbb{M})
(this increases
s
by
1
, so we should replace
s=|H|
in (
9.51
) by
s+1
). Therefore, by Proposition
9.7
, we have

(15.4)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+(d-1)(s-\#_{\mathrm{bad}})\geq(C_{13}^{*})^{-1}\cdot\rho-(d-1),

where the
-(d-1)
term is due to the change
s\mapsto s+1
.

Alternatively, we can also perform the following cutting sequence: first cut
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
as free, then cut it using Proposition
15.1
, then cut
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell}
using
DOWN
. In this case, note that after cutting
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}
as free, the number of empty ends plus full components of
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell}
does not exceed
s-\kappa
(this is because, by the (PL) argument in Part 1 of the proof of Proposition
12.3
, each such component must intersect a particle line in
r(\mathbb{M})=[s]
, and this particle line must not have an atom
\mathbb{M}_{\ell^{\prime}}
if this component is to be full; same for empty ends). By Proposition
12.2
, we know that the number of bad molecules produced in cutting
\mathbb{M}_{<\ell}
using
DOWN
, is at most
s-\kappa
. Therefore for this alternative we have

(15.5)

(\upsilon/2)\cdot\#_{\mathrm{good}}+(d-1)(s-\#_{\mathrm{bad}})\geq(\upsilon/2)+(d-1)\kappa.

Finally, by optimizing between (
15.4
) and (
15.5
) we easily get (
15.2
). This completes the proof of Proposition
6.3
, assuming Proposition
15.1
.
∎

15.1.
The proof of Proposition
15.1

Now we prove Proposition
15.1
. First we recall the theorem of Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko
[
15
]
, which establishes an upper bound for the number of collisions with fixed number of particles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; we restate it below in a more convenient form.

Proposition 15.2
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a full molecule of C-atoms and
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
is an atom set, such that for any particle line
{\bm{p}}
and any atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}\prec\mathfrak{n}_{2}\prec\mathfrak{n}_{3}
on the particle line
{\bm{p}}
(in the ordering in Definition
3.5
) with
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{3}\in A
, we must have
\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in A
. Let
q
be the total number of bottom edges at atoms of
A
that are not bonds between two atoms of
A
(i.e. these edges can be bottom ends of
\mathbb{M}
or bonds connecting an atom in
A
to an atom not in
A
), then we must have
|A|\leq G(q)
, where
G(q):=(2q+1)\cdot\binom{q}{2}\cdot(32q^{3/2})^{q^{2}}
.

Proof.

We may identify particles with the corresponding particle lines, see
Table
3
. Let
B
be the set of particle lines
{\bm{p}}
that contain at least one atom (collision) in
A
. Each such particle line must contain a bottom edge at an atom of
A
that is not a bond between two atoms of
A
, which implies that
|B|\leq q
.

For each particle
{\bm{p}}\in B
, consider the collision
\mathfrak{n}_{\bm{p}}^{-}
immediately before the first collision of the particle
{\bm{p}}
in
A
, and the collision
\mathfrak{n}_{\bm{p}}^{+}
immediately after the last collision of the particle
{\bm{p}}
in
A
. Note that one or both of
\mathfrak{n}_{\bm{p}}^{\pm}
may be absent, but the total number of such collisions is at most
2q
. We may fix a total time ordering of these collisions, and consider each of the at most
2q+1
time intervals between two adjacent collisions time
t_{\mathfrak{n}_{\bm{p}}^{\pm}}
. For any such time interval
I
, define the set

(15.6)

B^{\prime}=\big\{{\bm{p}}\in B:\textrm{$t_{\mathfrak{n}_{\bm{p}}^{-}}$ is either absent, or is on the left of $I$, or is the left endpoint of $I$,}\\
\textrm{$t_{\mathfrak{n}_{\bm{p}}^{+}}$ is either absent, or is on the right of $I$, or is the right endpoint of $I$}\big\}.

We also shrink
I
a little bit to
\widetilde{I}
such that
I\backslash\widetilde{I}
contains no collision (other than the ones at the endpoint of
I
, which do not belong to
A
). It is then easy to see that (i) any collision in
A
that happens within time interval
\widetilde{I}
must be between two particles in
B^{\prime}
, and (ii) any collision that happens within time interval
\widetilde{I}
and involves an atom in
B^{\prime}
must belong to
A
. In particular, within this time interval
\widetilde{I}
, no particles in
B^{\prime}
collides with any other particle, so the restriction of the full dynamics to these particles is equal to the partial duynamics with only atoms in
B^{\prime}
. Let
\lambda_{I}
be the number of collisions in
A
that happen within time interval
\widetilde{I}
, then the collision dynamics for the
|B^{\prime}|
particles in
B^{\prime}
within interval
\widetilde{I}
produces at least
\lambda_{I}
collisions. By the BFK theorem, we know that
17
17

17

The extra factor
\binom{q}{2}
is due to some extra considerations needed for extended dynamics; see Appendix
A
.

\lambda_{I}\leq G_{0}(q)
for
G_{0}(q)=\binom{q}{2}\cdot(32q^{3/2})^{q^{2}}
. Summing in
I
we then get
|A|\leq(2q+1)G_{0}(q):=G(q)
, as desired.
∎

Before getting to the proof of Proposition
15.1
, we first briefly explain the strategy.

Cutting deg 3 and deg 2 atoms.
A naive idea to exploit the recollisions is to keep cutting deg 3 atoms (the first being deg 4), until hitting a recollision and getting a {33} molecule. The main difficulty here, also the main difficulty in general, is that we may be forced to cut long sequences of deg 2 atoms without any gain. Let the set of deg 3 atoms being cut be
A
, then the set of subsequent deg 2 atoms being cut is obtained by starting from
A
and iteratively taking atoms with 2 bonds with previous atoms, which is just
X(A)
in the notion of Definition
15.3
.

We note that each atom in
A
is cut as deg 3, so it has only one bond connecting to previously cut atoms; in particular keep adding atoms in
A
will not create any new cycle, and we can think of these sets
A
as close to being trees (i.e. with
\rho(A)
controlled). On the other hand, adding each atom in
X(A)
will create one new cycle and increase the
\rho
value by
1
. Finally, in choosing the set
A
we can optimize this process by each time cutting the highest (or lowest) deg 3 atom as in
UP
, which will ensure this set
A
is
transversal
18
18

18

The intuition is as follows. Suppose we apply
UP
until getting the first deg 2 atom (or {33} molecule), and let the set of deg 3 atoms cut be
A
. Then
A
basically consists of the union of sets of
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
for some
\mathfrak{n}
(which has no bottom in the sense of Lemma
11.16
), and a subset of the next
S_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
which will have no top (as we keep cutting the highest atom in
S_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
). Note that no-bottom (resp. no-top) subsets are transversal sets where
A^{-}=\varnothing
(resp.
A^{+}=\varnothing
), so the general notion of transversality then arises naturally as combination of both.
as in Definition
15.3
.

The sets
A_{j}
and reduction to toy models.
Now, suppose we keep cutting sequences of deg 3 and deg 2 atoms as above, then we get sets
A_{j}
such that
A_{j+1}\supseteq A_{j}\cup X(A_{j})
. Note that forst each sequence of deg 3 and the next sequence of deg 2 atoms, we get a {33} molecule at the transition of the two sequences; thus, we may assume there are only
O(1)
sequences. Note also that
\rho(A_{j+1}=\rho(A_{j}\cup X(A_{j})=\rho(A_{j})+|X(A_{j})|
(cf. Proposition
15.4
(
4
)), so in order for
A_{J}
to reach
\mathbb{M}
and include many (
>\Gamma
) recollisions for some
J=O(1)
, it must occur at some step that for some
A=A_{j}
, the size of
X(A)
is significantly larger than
\rho(A)
. Now, by applying
the BFK theorem
, we know that the number of bonds between
X^{+}(A)
and
A
must also be sufficiently larger than
\rho(A)
(Proposition
15.2
).

Effectively, we can now think
A
as almost a tree, compare it to
\mathbb{M}_{D}
in the 2-layer model, and compare
X(A)
(or more precisely
X^{+}(A)
where we only take parents in the construction of
X(A)
) to the
\mathbb{M}_{U}
in the 2-layer model (this set is not clse to a tree, but this will not be used in the algorithms below). The role of UD connections is now played by the bonds between
X^{+}(A)
and
A
. We then repeat the same proof for (part of) Proposition
12.18
: if there are many 2-connections (corresponding to a subset
X_{0}^{+}(A)\subseteq X^{+}(A)
) then apply
2CONNDN
as in toy model III; if not, then we cut these 2-connections as in
Stage 1
, then perform pre-processing as in
Stege 3
, then get rid of weak degeneracies and finally apply
MAINUD
as in toy model I plus.

We now define the
transversal
subsets
A
of
\mathbb{M}
, and prove some properties.

Definition 15.3
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a full molecule of C-atoms, and
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
is an atom set. We say
A
is transversal, if we can decompose
\mathbb{M}\backslash A
into two disjoint subsets
A^{+}
and
A^{-}
, such that no atom in
A^{-}
is parent of any atom in
A\cup A^{+}
, and no atom in
A
is parent of any atom in
A^{+}
.

For any transversal set
A
, define the set
X_{0}(A)
such that, an atom
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{0}(A)
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}\not\in A
and
\mathfrak{n}
has two bonds connected to two atoms in
A
. Inductively define
X_{q}(A)
such that,
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{q}(A)
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}\not\in A
, and either
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{q-1}(A)
, or
\mathfrak{n}
has two bonds connected to two atoms in
X_{q-1}(A)\cup A
. Define
X(A)=\cup_{q\geq 0}X_{q}(A)
, see
Figure
65
.

Figure 65.
Example of sets
X(A)
and
X_{0}(A)
for a specific connected transversal set
A
. Note that if we cut
A
as free, then those atoms in
X_{0}(A)
(blue) will immediately become deg 2; if we keep cutting deg 2 atoms, then those atoms in
X(A)
(red) will eventually become deg 2 and be cut.

Proposition 15.4
.

Let
A
be a connected transversal subset and
\mathbb{M}
is a connected molecule.

(1)

We can always choose
(A^{+},A^{-})
in Definition
15.3
such that each component of
A^{+}
has at least one bond connected to
A
.

(2)

Define the sets
X_{q}^{\pm}(A)
in the same way as
X_{q}(A)
, but with the sentence “
\mathfrak{n}
has two bonds connected to two atoms in
X_{q-1}(A)\cup A
” replaced by “
\mathfrak{n}
has two
children
(or
two parents
) in
X_{q-1}^{\pm}(A)\cup A
”. Then we have
X_{q}^{\pm}(A)=X_{q}(A)\cap A^{\pm}
. Define
X^{\pm}(A)=\cup_{q\geq 0}X_{q}^{\pm}(A)
, then
X^{\pm}(A)=X(A)\cap A^{\pm}
.

(3)

The set
X(A)\cup A
is also connected and transversal. Moreover any atom in
\mathbb{M}\backslash(X(A)\cup A)
has at most one bond with atoms in
X(A)\cup A
, so
X_{0}(X(A)\cup A)=\varnothing
.

(4)

Recall
\rho(A)
defined in Definition
3.13
. Then there exists a connected transversal set
B\supseteq A
, such that
\rho(B)=\rho(A)
, and either
B=\mathbb{M}
or
X_{0}(B)\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

Proof of (
1
).
Suppose
A^{+}
has a component
U
that is not connected to
A
by a bond, then we replace
A^{+}
by
A^{+}\backslash U
and
A^{-}
by
A^{-}\cup U
, which still satisfies the requirements for
(A^{+},A^{-})
in Definition
15.3
, but the number of components of
A^{+}
decreases by
1
. Repeat until each component of
A^{+}
is connected to
A
by a bond (or
A^{+}
becomes empty).

Proof of (
2
).
First
X_{q}^{\pm}(A)\subseteq X_{q}(A)\cap A^{\pm}
, which easily follows from definition and induction. To prove the opposite direction, first if an atom
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{0}(A)
belongs to
A^{\pm}
, then
\mathfrak{n}
must have two children (or two parents) in
A
so
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{0}^{\pm}(A)
. We then proceed by induction: suppose
X_{q-1}^{\pm}(A)=X_{q-1}(A)\cap A^{\pm}
, now consider
\mathfrak{n}\in(X_{q}(A)\cap A^{+})\backslash X_{q-1}(A)
which has two adjacent atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
in
X_{q-1}(A)\cup A
. If
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
belongs to
A^{-}
, then
\mathfrak{n}
must be child of
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
(it is easy to see that both parents of
\mathfrak{n}_{1}\in A^{-}
must be in
A\cup A^{-}
) and thus
\mathfrak{n}\in A^{-}
which is impossible. This means
\mathfrak{n}_{1}\in A^{+}\cup A
and same for
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
. Since
\mathfrak{n}_{1,2}\in X_{q-1}(A)\cup A
, we know by induction hypothesis that
\mathfrak{n}_{1,2}\in X_{q-1}^{+}(A)\cup A
, thus
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{q}(A)
by definition. Same is true for
A^{-}
.

Proof of (
3
).
Connectedness is obvious by definition. To prove transversality, we simply decompose
\mathbb{M}\backslash(A\cup X(A))=B^{+}\cup B^{-}
, where
B=A^{+}\backslash X^{+}(A)
and
B^{-}=A^{-}\backslash X^{-}(A)
. This then satisfies the requirements (using the fact that any child (or parent) of any atom
\mathfrak{n}\in X^{\pm}(A)
must belong to
X^{\pm}(A)\cup A
). The second statement follows from the definition of
X(A)
in Definition
15.3
.

Proof of (
4
).
Suppose
A
is connected and transversal; we may assume
A\neq\mathbb{M}
and
X_{0}(A)=\varnothing
(otherwise choose
B=A
). Since
\mathbb{M}
is connected, there must exist an atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A
that is either a parent or child of an atom in
A
. We may assume it is a parent, and then choose a lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{M}\backslash A
among these parents. Since
X_{0}(A)=\varnothing
, we know that
\mathfrak{n}
has only one bond with atoms in
A
; let
A_{1}=A\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
, then
A_{1}
is connected and
\rho(A_{1})=\rho(A)
, see
Figure
66
.

Figure 66.
Proof of Proposition
15.4
(
4
):
\mathfrak{n}
is a lowest parent of atom in
A
, which is natural in terms of the
UP
or
DOWN
algorithm;
C
is the set of descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
in
A^{+}
.

Now we claim that
A_{1}
is transversal. To see this, define
C
to be the set of atoms
\mathfrak{m}\in A^{+}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
that are descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
, and decompose
\mathbb{M}\backslash A_{1}=A_{1}^{+}\cup A_{1}^{-}
, where
A_{1}^{+}=A^{+}\backslash(C\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\})
and
A_{1}^{-}=A^{-}\cup C
. It is clear by checking all cases that no atom in
A_{1}
can be parent of any atom in
A_{1}^{+}
, and no atom in
A^{-}
can be parent of any atom in
A_{1}\cup A_{1}^{+}
. If an atom
\mathfrak{m}\in C
is parent of some atom
\mathfrak{p}\in A_{1}^{+}
, then
\mathfrak{p}\in A^{+}\backslash C
, but
\mathfrak{m}\in C
and
\mathfrak{p}
is child of
\mathfrak{m}
, so we also have
\mathfrak{p}\in C
, contradiction. Finally if
\mathfrak{m}\in C
is parent of
\mathfrak{p}\in A_{1}=A\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
, clearly
\mathfrak{p}\in A
, but then
\mathfrak{m}
is also parent of an atom in
A
, contradicting the lowest assumption of
\mathfrak{n}
.

Now we know that
A_{1}
is also connected transversal; by replacing
A
by
A_{1}
and repeating the above discussion, we eventually will reach some
B
such that either
B=\mathbb{M}
or
X_{0}(B)\neq\varnothing
, as desired.
∎

Now we define the algorithm
TRANSDN
, which is analogous to the
2CONNDN
algorithm and is applicable in the case when
|X_{0}^{+}(A)|
(corresponding to the 2-connections in toy model III) is large.

Definition 15.5

(Algorithm
TRANSDN
)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is connected full molecule and
A
is a connected transversal subset. We may choose
(A^{+},A^{-})
as in Proposition
15.4
(
1
). Define the following algorithm:

(1)

If
A
contains any deg
2
atom
\mathfrak{n}
, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg
2
atom left.

(2)

Choose a
highest
atom
\mathfrak{n}
in the set of all deg 3 atoms in
A
(or a highest atom in
A
, if
A
only contains deg 4 atoms). Let
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
be the set of ancestors of
\mathfrak{n}
in
A
.

(3)

Starting from
\mathfrak{n}
, choose a
lowest
atom
\mathfrak{m}
in
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
that has not been cut. If
\mathfrak{m}
has deg 3, and is adjacent to an atom
\mathfrak{p}\in X_{0}^{+}(A)
that also has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{p}\}
as free; otherwise cut
\mathfrak{m}
as free. Repeat until all atoms in
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}
have been cut. Then go to (
1
).

(4)

Repeat (
1
)–(
3
) until all atoms in
A
have been cut. Then cut the remaining part of
A^{+}
as free and cut it into elementary components using
UP
, then cut
A^{-}
into elementary components using
DOWN
.

We also define the dual algorithm
TRANSUP
by reversing the notions of parent/child etc. Note that we replace
A^{+}
and
X_{0}^{+}(A)
by
A^{-}
and
X_{0}^{-}(A)
, and also replace
A^{+}
by
A^{-}
in Proposition
15.4
(
1
).

Proposition 15.6
.

For the algorithm
TRANSDN
(and same for
TRANSUP
), we have
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
, and
\#_{\{33A\}}\geq|X_{0}^{+}(A)|/2-\rho(A)-1
.

Proof.

It is clear, in the same way as in the proof of Proposition
12.2
, that during the process, there is no bottom fixed end in
(A\backslash Z_{\mathfrak{n}})\cup\{\mathfrak{n}\}
or in
A^{-}
, and there is no top fixed end in
Z_{\mathfrak{n}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{n}\}
or in
A^{+}
, so
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
. Moreover
\#_{\{4\}}=1
because no component of the remaining part of
A^{+}
after Definition
15.5
(
3
) can be full when it is first cut in Definition
15.5
(
4
) (thanks to Proposition
15.4
(
1
)), and no component of
A^{-}
can be full when it is first cut in Definition
15.5
(
4
) (thanks to
\mathbb{M}
being connected).

To prove the lower bound for
\#_{\{33A\}}
, note that only one atom in
A
belongs to a {4} molecule (as
A
is connected). Let the number of atoms in
A
that belongs to a {2} molecule be
q
, then by considering the increment of the same quantity
\sigma:=\#_{\mathrm{bo/fr}}-3|\mathbb{M}|
and applying the same arguments in the proof of Proposition
12.2
(but restrected to
A
), and noticing that initially all atoms in
A
have deg 4, we deduce that

\#_{\{2\}}+\#_{\{33\}}-\#_{\{4\}}=-\sigma_{\mathrm{init}}=\#_{\mathrm{bond}}-|A|=\rho(A)-1,

and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
, hence
q\leq\rho(A)
. Then, for each
\mathfrak{n}\in X_{0}^{+}(A)
, which is adjacent to two atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in A
by two bonds, assume say
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
is cut after
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
. The total number of such
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
is at least
|X_{0}^{+}(A)|/2
as each
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
can be obtained from at most two
\mathfrak{n}
. For each such
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
, the corresponding
\mathfrak{n}
must have deg 3 when it is cut, so by Definition
15.5
(
3
), it must belong to either {4}-, or {2}-, or {33A} molecule. Using the upper bound for the number of {4} and {2} molecules, this completes the proof.
∎

Next we define the
SELECT2
function, which is an extension of the
SELECT
function in the case when
A
may contain cycles (but with
\rho(A)
bounded). The proof follows similar ideas, except for the extra step (
3
), which is needed because of the possible cycle in
A
.

Definition 15.7

(The function
SELECT2
)
.

Let
A
be a connected molecule with only C-atoms, no bottom fixed end, and no deg 2 atoms. Let
Z
be the set of deg 3 atoms in
A
, and let
Y
be a subset of atoms in
A
such that
A
becomes a forest after removing the atoms in
Y
. We define the function
\textbf{SELECT2}=\textbf{SELECT2}(A,Z,Y)
as follows, see
Figure
67
.

(1)

Consider all the components of
Z\cup Y
in
A
, which is a finite collection of disjoint subsets of
A
.

(2)

Since
A
only has C-atoms, the shortest ov-distance between sets (Definition
8.4
) coincides with the standard definition of distance on a graph. If any two of the subsets in (
1
), say
U
and
V
, have the shortest distance (within
A
) which is at most 4, then we choose one shortest path between an atom in
U
and an atom in
V
, and let the atoms on this path be
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
. Then replace the two sets
U
and
V
by a single set which is
U\cup V\cup\{\mathfrak{n}_{j}\}
.

(3)

Repeat (
2
) until this can no longer be done. Next, if a single subset
U
contains two atoms (which may be the same) that are connected by a path of length at most 4 with none of the intermediate atoms belonging to
U
or any other subset, then add the atoms on this path to
U
. If this causes the shortest distance between two subsets to be
\leq 4
, then proceed to (
2
) and repeat it as above.

(4)

When no scenario in (
2
) or (
3
) occurs, we output
S:=\textbf{SELECT2}(A,Z,Y)
as the union of all the current sets.

Figure 67.
The
SELECT2
function, which is basically the same as the
SELECT
function (Definition
13.10
,
Figure
61
). The left arrow: Definition
15.7
(
2
(same as Definition
13.10
), where we add new atoms to connect two components. The right arrow: Definition
15.7
(
3
), where we add the new atoms that form cycles.

Proposition 15.8
.

The molecule
A
becomes a proper forest after cutting
S
as free, and we also have
|S|\leq 10(|Y|+|Z|+\rho(A))
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as Proposition
13.11
. First
A
becomes a forest after cutting
S
as free, because
Y\subseteq S
. Also
A
becomes proper after cutting
S
as free, due to the same reason in the proof of Proposition
13.11
; in the language of that proof, the absence of the scenario in Definition
15.7
(
2
) prevents the case when
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
belong to two different components
U
and
V
, and the absence of the scenario in Definition
15.7
(
3
) prevents the case when
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
belong to the same components
U
.

As for the upper bound for
|S|
, simply note that each single step in Definition
15.7
(
2
)–(
3
) adds at most
3
atoms to
S
. Moreover each single step in Definition
15.7
(
2
) decreases the number of subsets by 1 (as it connectes
U
and
V
together) and does not decrease
\rho(S)
(as
\rho(S)
is the number of independent cycles, so adding more atoms cannot decrease
\rho(S)
). Each single step in Definition
15.7
(
3
) does not change the number of subsets, but
adds one more cycle to
S
; in particular adding this cycle increases the value of
\rho(S)
by at least 1. Putting together, we know that the value of

\gamma:=(\textrm{number of sets in }S)-\rho(S)

decreases by at least 1 in each step Definition
15.7
(
2
)–(
3
). Since we always have
\rho(S)\leq\rho(A)
because
S\subseteq A
(again it is easy to see that adding one atom or arbitrarily many atoms does not decrease the
\rho
value), we know that the initial value of
\gamma
is
\leq|Y|+|Z|
and the final value of
\gamma
is
\geq-\rho(A)
, which means that the total number of steps in Definition
15.7
(
3
) is bounded by
|Y|+|Z|+\rho(A)
, hence the result.
∎

Now we can define the algorithm
MAINTRUP
, which is the main algorithm in this section. It has two options,
Option 1
, which is applied only in the (simple) weakly degenerate case, and essentially trivially cuts most atoms in
A
as {3} molecules, so one can gain from extra restrictions from weak degeneracies. In
Option 2
, which is the main part, we basically combine
Stage 1
(cutting 2-connections in
X_{0}^{+}(A)
) and
Stage 3
(pre-processing) in the proof of Proposition
12.18
at the end of Section
13
, and the
MAINUD
algorithm in toy model I plus, but wih the roles of
\mathbb{M}_{U}
and
\mathbb{M}_{D}
played by
X^{+}(A)
and
X
respectively.

Definition 15.9

(Algorithm
MAINTRUP
)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{M}
is a connected full molecule and
A
is a connected transversal subset. We may choose
(A^{+},A^{-})
as in Proposition
15.4
(
1
). Let the number of bonds connecting an atom in
X^{+}(A)
to an atom in
A
be
\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}
. It is easy to prove that there exists a set
Y_{0}\subseteq A
of at most
\rho(A)
atoms, such that
A
becomes a forest after cutting atoms in
Y_{0}
as free. Moreover, by splitting into at most
C^{|A|+\rho(A)}
sub-cases, we can identify a set of weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs in
A
in the sense of Proposition
12.17
; let
Y_{1}\subseteq A
be the atoms involved in these weak degeneracies. We define the following algorithm, which contains two Options that we can choose at the beginning.

In
Option 1
we do the followings:

(1)

Cut
A
as free, then cut it into elementary components using
UP
.

(2)

Then cut
A^{+}
as free and cut it into elementary components using
UP
, then cut
A^{-}
into elementary components using
DOWN
.

In
Option 2
we do the followings:

(1)

Cut all atoms in
X_{0}^{+}(A)
as free. If any atom in
A
becomes deg 2, also cut it as free until
A
has no deg 2 atom.

(2)

If
A
remains connected after the above step, let
Z
be the set of deg 3 atoms in
A
, and
Y
be those atoms in
Y_{0}\cup Y_{1}
that have not been cut. Define
S=\textbf{SELECT2}(A,Z,Y)
as in Definition
15.7
, then cut
S
as free and cut it into elementary components using
DOWN
. If
A
is not connected, apply this step to each connected component of
A
.

(3)

If not all atoms in
X^{+}(A)
have been cut, then choose a lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}
in
X^{+}(A)
that has not been cut. If
\mathfrak{n}
is adjacent to an atom
\mathfrak{p}\in A
that has deg 3, then cut
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{p}\}
as free; otherwise cut
\mathfrak{n}
as free. If
A
becomes non-proper (Definition
11.3
) then repeat the steps in Definition
13.12
(
4
)–(
5
) until
A
becomes proper again.

(4)

Repeat (
3
) until all atoms in
X^{+}(A)
have been cut. Then repeat the step in Definition
13.12
(
8
) until all atoms in
A
have been cut.

(5)

Finally, cut (the remaining parts of)
A^{+}
as free and cut it into elementary components using
UP
, and then cut
A^{-}
into elementary components using
DOWN
.

We define the dual algorithm
MAINTRDN
in the same way (so
\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}
is replaced by
\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{-}
etc.).

Proposition 15.10
.

In
Option 1
of
MAINTRUP
in Definition
15.9
(same for
MAINTRDN
), we have

(15.7)

\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0,\quad\#_{\{4\}}=1;\qquad\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq\frac{1}{10}\cdot|Y_{1}|-\rho(A)-1.

In
Option 2
of of
MAINTRUP
in Definition
15.9
, we have
\#_{\{44\}}=0
and all {33B} molecules are good, and moreover

(15.8)

\displaystyle\#_{\{33A\}}+\#_{\{33B\}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{10}\cdot\big(\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}-10^{5}(|Y_{1}|+\rho(A)+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|)\big),

\displaystyle\#_{\{4\}}

\displaystyle\leq|Y_{1}|+\rho(A)+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|.

Proof.

The case of
Option 1
is easy;
\#_{\{33B\}}=\#_{\{44\}}=0
is obvious by definition, and
\#_{\{4\}}=1
because
A
is connected and
A^{+}
satisfies the assumption in Proposition
15.4
(
1
), in the same way as in the proof of Proposition
15.6
. Moreover, each atom in
A
belongs to a {4} or{3} or {2} or {33A} molecule, and the total number of {2} and {33A} molecules is equal to
\rho(A)
(see the proof of Proposition
15.6
). For any atom
\mathfrak{n}
that belongs to a {3} molecule, if it is a weakly degenerate atom, or if it is part of a weakly degenerate pair and is cut after the other atom
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
of the pair, then
\{\mathfrak{n}\}
must be a good component by Definition
9.4
(
3a
). This gives at least
|Y_{1}|/10-\rho(A)-1
good components, as desired.

In the case of
Option 2
, note that in the whole process, there is no top fixed end in
A^{+}
and no bottom fixed end in
A^{-}
; this is because for the lowest atom
\mathfrak{n}
in
X^{+}(A)
chosen in Definition
15.9
(
3
), any child of
\mathfrak{n}
must either belong to
A
or belong to
X^{+}(A)
(and thus will have already been cut). Moreover, any
\mathfrak{n}
chosen in this step either has deg 2 and no bond connecting to
A
or has deg 3 and exactly one bond connecting to
A
(because all atoms in
X_{0}^{+}(A)
have been cut in Definition
15.9
(
1
)). Also any {33} molecule
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{p}\}
cut in this way must be {33A} molecule, and no full component can be cut (hence no {4} molecule created) in Definition
15.9
(
5
). Therefore, all {33B} molecule must have both atoms in
A
, and the only {4} molecules created are those created in Definition
15.9
(
1
) (contributing at most
|X_{0}^{+}(A)|
many {4} molecules) and Definition
15.9
(
2
) (contributing at most
|Y_{1}|+\rho(A)
many {4} molecules), hence the upper bound
\#_{\{4\}}\leq|Y_{1}|+\rho(A)+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|
.

Now we prove the lower bound on
\#_{\{33\}}
. Note that after Definition
15.9
(
1
), there is no bottom fixed end in
A
(and hence none in
S
), so we can cut
S
into elementary components using
DOWN
as in Definition
15.9
(
2
). By definition of
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
, and by Proposition
15.8
, we know that after Definition
15.9
(
2
) is finished,
A
will become a forest which is proper, and contains no weakly degenerate atoms or atom pairs. Next we prove an upper bound on
|S|
; let
Z_{0}
be the set of atoms in
A
cut in Definition
15.9
(
1
), then
|Z|\leq 4(|Z_{0}|+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|)
(because the deg 3 atoms in
Z
must be adjacent to one of the atoms in
X_{0}^{+}(A)
or
Z_{0}
that has been cut) and
|S|\leq 10(4|Z_{0}|+4|X_{0}^{+}(A)|+|Y_{1}|+2\rho(A))
by Proposition
15.8
. By Definition
15.9
(
1
), we know that each atom in
Z_{0}
must have two parents with each of them in either
Z_{0}
or
X_{0}^{+}(A)
. This then leads to
\rho(Z_{0})\geq|Z_{0}|-2|X_{0}^{+}(A)|
(by counting the total number of top bonds at all atoms of
Z_{0}
, note that each bond is counted only once, and the number of such bonds that do not connect two atoms in
Z_{0}
is at most
2|X_{0}^{+}(A)|
). Since also
\rho(Z_{0})\leq\rho(A)
(because
Z_{0}\subseteq A
), we know
|Z_{0}|\leq\rho(A)+2|X_{0}^{+}(A)|
, thus

|S|\leq 2\cdot 10^{2}(\rho(A)+|Y_{1}|+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|).

Now, after cutting
X_{0}^{+}(A)\cup Z_{0}\cup S
as free, the number of bonds connecting an atom in
A
to an atom in
X^{+}(A)
is still at least

(\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+})^{\prime}=\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}-2(|X_{0}^{+}(A)|+|Z_{0}|+|S|)\geq\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}-10^{4}(\rho(A)+|Y_{1}|+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|).

At this point we can apply the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
13.13
(especially using Part 2 of the proof and (
13.14
)) to show that

(15.9)

\#_{\{33A\}}+\#_{\{33B\}}\geq\frac{1}{10}\cdot\big(\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}-10^{5}(\rho(A)+|Y_{1}|+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|)\big).

Here the role of UD connections is played by the
(\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+})^{\prime}
bonds connecting an atom in
A
to an atom in
X^{+}(A)
. Note that after cutting
S
as free, the number of components of
A
is at most

\#_{\mathrm{comp}(A)}\leq 1+4(|X_{0}^{+}(A)|+|S|+|Z_{0}|)\leq 10^{5}(\rho(A)+|Y_{1}|+|X_{0}^{+}(A)|),

so among these
(\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+})^{\prime}
bonds (we denote this set by
Q
), there exists a subset
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
of at least
(\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+})^{\prime}-\#_{\mathrm{comp}(A)}
bonds, such that each bond in
Q^{\prime}
is connected to some other bond in
Q
via
A
(which matches the definition of UD connections). The same proof for Proposition
13.13
then applies, which leads to (
15.9
). This completes the proof.
∎

Now, we can finally prove Proposition
15.1
.

Proof of Proposition
15.1
.

First assume
\mathbb{M}
contains at least one strongly degenerate primitive pair
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n})
, then we can cut
\{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\}
as free and cut the rest of
\mathbb{M}
into elementary components using either
UP
or
DOWN
(cf. proof of Proposition
12.5
,
Choice 1
). This creates one good {44} molecule and no {4} molecule (as
\mathbb{M}
is connected), so
\#_{\mathrm{good}}\geq 1
and
\#_{\mathrm{bad}}=0
, which implies (
15.1
).

Now assume
\mathbb{M}
contains no strongly degenerate primitive pair, then we can show (by splitting into two sub-cases) that each {33A} molecule is either good or can be cut into one {2} and one good {3}-atom. The proof is similar to Lemma
12.6
; suppose this {33A} molecule is
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
where
\mathfrak{n}
is parent of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, then we may assume this is strongly degenerate and
|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{\upsilon}
otherwise this is already good by Definition
9.4
. Now our assumption implies that
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
must be non-primitive; in the absence of C-atoms, this implies that there exists another chain of atoms going from
\mathfrak{n}
to
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
by iteratively taking children. If any atom
\mathfrak{q}
in this chain is cut before
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
, then the inequality
t_{\mathfrak{n}}\geq t_{\mathfrak{q}}\geq t_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}
allows us to argue as in the proof of Lemma
12.6
to get a good molecule; if not, this means that at the time
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is cut,
\mathfrak{n}
still has two children (i.e.
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
and a child on the chain), and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
still has two parents (i.e.
\mathfrak{n}
and a parent on the chain), which means that
\mathfrak{n}
must have a top fixed end and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
must have a bottom fixed end, but this contradicts that
\{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\}
is {33A} molecule.

Therefore, from now on, we will treat {33A} molecules as good. We start by choosing a connected transversal subset
A_{1}
of
\mathbb{M}
which is a tree (i.e.
\rho(A_{1})=0
) and either
A_{1}=\mathbb{M}
or
X_{0}(A_{1})\neq\varnothing
; the existence of such
A_{1}
follows in the same way as in Proposition
15.4
(
4
) starting from a single atom with no children. If
A_{1}\neq\mathbb{M}
, let
X_{1}=X(A_{1})
, then
A_{1}\cup X_{1}
is connected transversal by Proposition
15.4
(
3
), so we can find
A_{2}\supseteq A_{1}\cup X_{1}
starting from
A_{1}\cup X_{1}
, using Proposition
15.4
(
4
). If
A_{2}\neq\mathbb{M}
, then let
X_{2}=X(A_{2})
and find
A_{3}
by Proposition
15.4
(
4
), then define
X_{3}=X(A_{3})
and so on, see
Figure
68
.

Figure 68.
Sets
A_{j}
and
X_{j}
in the proof of Proposition
15.1
where
X_{j}=X(A_{j})
and
A_{j+1}\supseteq A_{j}\cup X_{j}
. Note that if we cut
A_{1}
, then
X_{1}
, then
A_{2}\backslash(A_{1}\cup X_{1})
, then
X_{2}
etc., then we get a {33} molecule when cutting each
X_{j}
.

Recall the function
G(q)=(2q+1)\cdot\binom{q}{2}\cdot(32q^{3/2})^{q^{2}}
as in Proposition
15.2
. Define
D=K_{1}:=(60d)^{60d}
and
K_{j+1}=(60d\cdot G((60dK_{j})^{60d}))^{60d}
for
j\geq 1
. We will assume
\Gamma>(60d)^{60d}K_{D}
, and consider several cases.

(1) Suppose
A_{j}
exists and
X_{0}(A_{j})\neq\varnothing
for all
j\leq D
. By construction
\rho(A_{j})=\rho(A_{j-1}\cup X_{j-1})
, so we can list the elements of
B_{j}:=A_{j}\backslash(A_{j-1}\cup X_{j-1})
as
\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{j},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{|B_{j}|}^{j}
such that
\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{j}
has only one bond with
A_{j-1}\cup X_{j-1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{i}^{j}
has only one bond with
A_{j-1}\cup X_{j-1}\cup\{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{j},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{i-1}^{j}\}
. Similarly we can list the atoms in
B_{1}:=A_{1}
. Choose also an element
\mathfrak{m}_{j}\in X_{0}(A_{j})
for each
j
, then this
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
is adjacent to two atoms in
A_{j}
, with at least one being in
B_{j}
. We then perform the following cutting sequence:

Starting from
j=1
, for each
j
, cut the atoms
(\mathfrak{n}_{i}^{j}:i\leq|B_{j}|)
as free in the increasing order of
i
. However, if
\mathfrak{n}_{i}^{j}
is adjacent to
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
has deg 3 when we cut
\mathfrak{n}_{i}^{j}
, then we cut
\{\mathfrak{n}_{i}^{j},\mathfrak{m}_{j}\}
instead of
\mathfrak{n}_{i}^{j}
. After all atoms in
B_{j}
have been cut, the atom
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
will also have been cut; we then cut the remaining atoms in
X_{j}
as free (starting from the lowest ones; each will have deg 2 when we cut it), and proceed with
B_{j+1}=A_{j+1}\backslash(A_{j}\cup X_{j})
and so on.

In this way, it is clear that each
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
must belong to a {33} molecule, which also has to be {33A} molecule because
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
will have two top (or two bottom) free ends when it is cut, assuming
\mathfrak{m}_{j}\in X_{0}^{+}(A_{j})
(or
\mathfrak{m}_{j}\in X_{0}^{-}(A_{j})
). This produces at least
D
many {33A} molecules (which are treated as good), while
\#_{\{4\}}=1
, which implies (
15.1
).

(2) Suppose
A_{j}=\mathbb{M}
for some
j<D
, then
\rho(A_{j})\geq\Gamma
for this
j
. Therefore we may choose the smallest
j
such that
\rho(A_{j+1})\geq K_{j+1}
. Let
A=A_{j}
, then we have

\rho(A)<K_{j},\quad K_{j+1}\leq\rho(A_{j+1})=\rho(A\cup X(A))\leq\rho(A)+10|X(A)|,

so
|X(A)|\geq(20)^{-1}\cdot K_{j+1}
. We may assume
|X^{+}(A)|\geq(40)^{-1}\cdot K_{j+1}
; consider the set
X^{+}(A)
, which obviously satisfies the assumptions in Proposition
15.2
, so by Proposition
15.2
we conclude that
\#_{\mathrm{conn}}^{+}>(60d)^{60d}\cdot K_{j}
in Definition
15.9
. We then

•

Run algorithm
TRANSUP
and apply Proposition
15.6
if
|X_{0}^{+}(A)|\geq(30d)^{30d}\cdot K_{j}
;

•

Run algorithm
MAINTRUP
,
Option 1
and apply Proposition
15.10
if
|Y_{1}|\geq(30d)^{30d}\cdot K_{j}
;

•

Run algorithm
MAINTRUP
,
Option 2
and apply Proposition
15.10
, if
|X_{0}^{+}(A)|\leq(30d)^{30d}\cdot K_{j}
and
|Y_{1}|\leq(30d)^{30d}\cdot K_{j}
.

In any case it is easy to verify that (
15.1
) is true, so the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix A
Some auxiliary results

In this appendix, we first prove some basic properties of the extended (E-) and truncated (T- or
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-) dynamics, in Proposition
4.5
.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

We need to prove the following statements (1)–(5). Note that the flow property for
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)
in (
4.3
) follows from the same property for
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)
and the definition (
3.4
) of
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)
, since

\sum_{\bm{z}^{0}:\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t+s)\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}}f(\bm{z}^{0})=\sum_{\bm{z}^{1}:\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)\bm{z}^{1}=\bm{z}}\,\sum_{\bm{z}^{0}:\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(s)\bm{z}^{0}=\bm{z}^{1}}f(\bm{z}^{0});

also the equality
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}(t)f=\mathcal{S}_{N}(t)f
in Proposition
4.5
(
2
) follows from the previous statement concerning
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)
and
\mathcal{H}_{N}(t)
, in the same way as in the proof of (
4.7
) in Proposition
4.7
.

(1)

For E- and T-dynamics, the initial configurations in which two collisions happen at the same time, or an infinite number of collisions happen in a finite time interval, or some collision happens at a fixed time, form a Lebesgue zero set.

(2)

Apart from Lebesgue zero sets, the mapping
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)
defined by the E-dynamics (Definition
4.1
, Proposition
4.5
(
2
)) satisfies the flow property in (
4.3
).

(3)

If the initial configuration
\bm{z}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
, then the E-dynamics and the O-dynamics coincide.

(4)

In the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-dynamics, the size and recollision number of any cluster
\mathbb{M}_{j}
of the topological reduction
\mathbb{M}
always satisfy that
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|\leq\Lambda
and
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})\leq\Gamma
.

(5)

The equality (
4.4
) holds for both E- and T-dynamics.

(6)

For O-, E- and T-dynamics, the total number of collisions has an absolute upper bound that depends only on
d
and
N
.

Proof of (1).
This follows from the same proof as in
[
1
]
.

Proof of (2)–(3).
Notice that by Definition
4.1
, the motion of the particles starting from any time
t
depends only on the configuration of the system
at time

t
but
not on the collision history before time
t
. The flow property in (2) then follows. Moreover, by Definition
4.1
, we know that if the distance between any two particles
i
and
j
ever becomes
\geq\varepsilon
along the E-dynamics, then their distance will remain
\geq\varepsilon
for the rest of time; therefore, if the distance between any two particles is
>\varepsilon
initially, then all these distances will remain
\geq\varepsilon
forever, so the E-dynamics coincide with the O-dynamics, which proves (3).

Proof of (4).
This follows from the definition of
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-truncated dynamics (Definition
4.3
): it is easy to see that any collision that is allowed cannot cause
|\mathbb{M}_{j}|>\Lambda
or
\rho(\mathbb{M}_{j})>\Gamma
, due to the definition of the condition
\mathtt{Pro}
in (
4.2
).

Proof of (5).
We prove it for
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{E}
, and the
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\Lambda,\Gamma}
case is the same. By listing all the collisions and time ordering them, we can divide
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
into a countable union of disjoint open sets
(O_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
, where on each
O_{\alpha}
the E-dynamics contain only a fixed set of collisions between fixed particles with a fixed time ordering. The mapping
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{E}(t)
, when restricted to
O_{\alpha}
, is injective and volume preserving; we denote it by
\mathcal{H}^{t}
. Then

(A.1)
